# 2 Samuel

# ANNOTATIONS ON THE SECOND BOOK OF SAMUEL

## THE ARGUMENT

The history concerning the state of the church and commonwealth of Israel is continued in this book from the death of Saul to the end of David’s reign, and is the story of forty years. In it is declared the infallible truth of God’s promises made and accomplished unto David concerning the kingdom; who having passed through many difficulties and dangers, at last, after those storms of trouble and afflictions, arrives at the wished haven, and quietly enjoys the crown and scepter: yet not all at once, but by degrees. For at first he is opposed by Ishbosheth Saul’s son, counseled and assisted by Abner the captain-general of his host, and has only Judah his own tribe adhering to him; over which alone he reigned in Hebron for the space of seven years, till, he growing, and they daily decreasing, at last by the treacherous death of both his chief enemies (of which he was innocent) the war was finished, and by the general choice of all the tribes he was with applause crowned and established in his royal throne. In him is set forth unto all posterity the lively picture and pattern of a truly royal king, every way qualified with such rich graces, piety, justice and heroical endowments, as approved him to be a man according to God’s own heart. His piety towards God was singular, and not to be paralleled with any that either went before, or succeeded him. For he chiefly devotes himself to God’s service, and the performance of all religious duties, continually meditating on God’s law, composing and singing of psalms, which breathe nothing but piety and holiness; praying, and praising of God; excellently settling the ecclesiastical government, and promoting God’s public service to a high degree of perfection in all the parts of it; ordering the tabernacle, and making and pitching a tent for the ark of the covenant near unto his own dwelling, that he might more readily upon all occasions resort unto it for comfort, counsel and direction. And thinking this too mean to be the place of God’s glorious presence, his whole care and study is taken up about the building him a temple. Which his pious zeal being accepted, and yet the work itself interdicted by God, he uses all his endeavors in preparing for it; and does not only leave to his son Solomon a model and pattern of the building, being inspired by God’s Spirit; but provides beforehand a vast mass of gold, silver, and all other materials, for perfecting the work in most sumptuous and magnificent manner. His justice also towards men appears in many passages, but especially in the punishment of the Amalekite who brought tidings of Saul’s death, and of Ishbosheth’s servants, who had treacherously murdered him. For when they expected from him some royal reward for slaying his enemies, and so preparing a way for his present and secure enjoying of the crown and scepter, he abhorring their treason, though attended with a kingdom, caused them instantly to be put to death. He was also excellently endowed by the Spirit of God with heroical virtues and graces fit for a general and great commander; as wisdom, courage, strength and magnanimity; whereby he was enabled to fight the Lord’s battles against all his and the church’s enemies, as the Philistims, Ammonites, Moabites, and Syrians; all which he vanquished, and either wholly destroyed, or brought them under tribute; whereby the kingdom of Israel was extended to the utmost limits which God had promised. Yet as he was endowed with all these excellencies, he had also his failings, yea, strong corruptions, as exorbitant lusts, uncleanness, cruelties, which discovered themselves in the matter of Uriah; pride, in numbering of the people; partial injustice, in passing an unrighteous sentence against innocent Mephibosheth; lying, dissembling: which the Holy Ghost has also recorded, not to encourage any to do the like, but as sea marks, that we may avoid those rocks against which so choice a vessel dashed, and had surely split and perished, had not God’s grace and Holy Spirit, as a timely gale of wind, blown him off, and reduced him into his right course by unfeigned repentance. Upon which though his sin was pardoned, yet to show how horrible it was in itself, and how odious and displeasing unto God; and that also the enemies of the church might not by his impunity take occasion to blaspheme, God severely corrected his sins, as it were, by the law of taliation, like for like, so as he might plainly read his sins in his punishment.

Thus sinning by adultery, his own daughter was incestuously ravished by his own son, and his concubines defiled by his son Absalom, in the sight of the sun. And causing Uriah to be murdered by the sword of the children of Ammon, the sword departed not from his house; but Absalom murders his brother Amnon, and after rises in rebellion against himself, and unnaturally seeks his death that preferred his life before his own; in which ambitious treason himself perished, to the intolerable grief of his over-loving father: besides all other wars, both foreign and intestine, with which he was continually vexed to the end of his reign.

And finally, his pride and confidence in the number of his people and the arm of flesh, was punished with a grievous plague, that consumed threescore and ten thousand of them in the space of three days. Yet for all this the Lord did not, as his sins merited, utterly forsake him, but loving him with a love unchangeable, he does by these fatherly, though severe, chastisements, thoroughly humble him in the sight and sense of his sins; and so raising him from these grievous falls by unfeigned repentance, he forgives his sins, and receives him into former favor, (to encourage all that have so sinned, to follow his example;) renews his covenant with him, and ratifies and (as it were) seals unto him all his gracious promises, especially that which was chief of all, concerning the perpetuity of his kingdom, which lasted long in a numerous posterity, but was accomplished in the eternity of Christ’s kingdom, who came, according to the flesh, out of his loins; of which, David and his kingdom were a type and figure.

## CHAPTER 1

**Verse 1.** {*Now it came to pass after the death of Saul, when David was returned from the slaughter of the Amalekites}* i.e. When he had vanquished his many and malicious enemies, and was come back in triumph, laden with spoils, and the joyful trophies of a glorious victory, within two days after his return he is encountered with the sad news of that great defeat given by the Philistims to God’s people, which seemed to hazard the ruin of the whole land. Thus the wise providence of God mixes and tempers prosperity with afflictions, and the joys of his saints and servants with their griefs, that they may neither be too much exalted with the one, nor dejected with the other.

**V. 2.** {*on the third day*} After David’s victory, and Saul’s defeat and death.

*{with his clothes rent, and earth upon his head*} Hereby in their mourning they testified their great grief and contrition of heart, their shame, unworthiness, and deep humiliation; acknowledging they were unworthy to be above ground, and deserved to be under it among the dead. See on Gen. 37. 29. Josh. 7. 6. 1 Sam. 4. 12. Job. 2. 12.

**V. 4.** {*And David said unto him, How went the matter?*} i.e. Relate unto me the news of the chief things that happened in that battle. By which earnest inquiry David showed how careful he was of the welfare of God’s people, and how forward and willing to use all good means to repair any loss they had sustained.

*{and he answered, That the people are fled from the battle}* In this relation he speaks divers truths, but mixes and interlaces with them many lies, intending them for his own advantage, though they brought him to ruin: which will appear if we compare his words with the former relation of Saul’s defeat and death, 1 Sam. 31.

**V. 5.** {*How knowest thou that Saul and Jonathan his son are dead?*} He does in a more especial manner inquire after this, because above all other passages in the battle, this chiefly concerned him and the whole commonwealth.

**V. 6.** {*on mount Gilboa*} See on 1 Sam. 28. 4.

*{Saul leaned upon his spear*} Being sore wounded by the archers, and so unable to get away, though hotly pursued by the chariots and horsemen. Others understand it, that Saul was fallen upon his spear, which he had assayed to thrust into his body; but it being hindered that it could not enter and do the deed, he called to this Amalekite, and willed him to kill him. But in the true story it is plainly said, that he took a sword and fell upon it, and immediately died; which his armor-bearer seeing, fell likewise upon his sword, and died with him, 1 Sam. 31. 4, 5.

**V. 7.** {*He saw me, and called unto me*} It is not likely that being so sore wounded both by the archers, and also (as he would imply) with a wound which he had given himself with his own sword or spear, whereof he was ready to die, that he should call unto this Amalekite, and spend so many words with him.

**V. 8.** {*I am an Amalekite*} i.e. Of a nation no less accursed and devoted to destruction than the uncircumcised Philistims; and therefore, if upon this ground Saul abhorred to be slain by the one, what reason had he to make choice to be killed by the other?

**V. 9.** {*For anguish is come upon me, because my life is yet whole in me*} That is, as the Amalekite would be understood, I am much grieved, that, being in this desperate condition, I cannot die as I desire, to prevent the disgrace that may befall me, if I come alive into my enemies’ hands. Others read it thus, *My coat of mail, or embroidered upper garment*, has hindered me, that though I have attempted to kill myself by falling upon my lance, or sword, yet, to my grief, I cannot do it, and therefore do you do it, and rid me out of my pain, and from my fear of being disgraced.

**V. 10.** {*So I stood upon him*} To wit, that I might with my strength force the spear into his body, and so kill him outright.

*{because I was sure that he could not live after that he was fallen*} i.e. My intention in this act was not to kill him, if there had been any hope of his recovery; but his case being desperate by reason of his mortal wounds, given both by the archers and himself, I did what I did out of mere pity, and at his earnest request, to rid him out of pain, and prevent his disgrace. This his relation is for the most part feigned, to find grace with David, and to get some great reward, as may plainly appear, if we compare his speeches with the true story, 1 Sam. 31.

*{And I took the Crown that was upon his head*} This was true, seeing he now brought it with him: but it is not likely that he wore it upon his head in the battle, because it would have made him the fairer mark for his enemies, at whom they chiefly aimed, (a thing which wise generals avoid, 1 King. 22. 30.) and have cumbered him in the fight; but we may rather think it was carried before him as an ensign of honor, by his armor-bearer, or some other.

**V. 11.** {*took hold on his clothes, and rent them*} See on ver. 2.

**V. 12.** {*And they mourned and wept, and fasted until even}* David had good cause thus to mourn; not only for Jonathan, who was a virtuous prince, his dear and faithful friend, and a valiant captain and chief pillar of the commonwealth, both in peace and war, but also because the enemies of God had given his people so great a defeat, and slaughtered so many of them; yea even for Saul himself in divers respects: for though he were his desperate and implacable enemy, who continually persecuted him and sought his life, yet he was the Lord’s anointed (under which notion he had twice spared him) and a valiant general, who had fought God’s battles against the enemies of his people with continual good success; over whom they would now insult and triumph, and take occasion to blaspheme God’s holy name, and prefer their idols before him, who had given them this great victory. Besides, in him he had cause to bewail the common misery and mutability of all human conditions; in that he, who lately was a great and potent king, now was made a spectacle of misery, in his fearful and ignominious death. And finally, there was great cause of mourning and lamentation, in that this grievous overthrow was an evidence of God’s wrath against his people for their many and grievous sins.

**V. 13.** {*And David said to the young man that told him, whence art thou?*} Though he had said before that he had told Saul, asking the same question, that he was an Amalekite, yet it seems David’s mind was so disturbed and distracted with passion and grief that he took no notice of it, and therefore he asks him again of what nation he was: or if he did remember it, then like a wise judge he makes this diligent inquiry, doubting of the truth of his relation, to see if he would agree with himself in the same tale.

**V. 15.** {*And David called one of the young men, and said, Go near, and fall upon him}* Though there were no witnesses of his fact, yet having his own testimony against himself, he might justly proceed to execution; seeing there is no other use of witnesses but to convince the offender, if he deny the fault, and plead not guilty; but if the party voluntarily confess the fact, it is instead of many witnesses. And David might lawfully thus proceed against him, because he was anointed king, and now, Saul being dead, was come into actual possession of the royal rights. Besides, this malefactor was apprehended at Ziklag, where he had the sole jurisdiction.

**V. 16.** {*Thy blood be upon thy head*} See on Josh. 2. 19.

**V. 17.** {*And David lamented with this lamentation over Saul, &c.*} To wit, in his own name, and in the name of all the people; not only because he was fittest to do it in respect of his abilities, having a prophetical spirit and gift of poesy, but also because he had the greatest interest and right to perform it, seeing near allied unto Saul, his son-in-law, and successor in the kingdom, and also a dear and faithful friend unto Jonathan, who was an actor and patient also in this woeful tragedy, and a chief subject of this lamentable elegy.

**V. 18.** {*Also he bade them teach the children of Judah the use of the bow*} i.e. That he might put courage into the hearts of God’s people, which by this great overthrow were much dejected, he gave order to have them instructed in the right use of their arms and weapons, especially in the skill of shooting, because thereby the Philistims had most prevailed against them. And this in a parenthesis is prefixed before this mournful funeral song, that by singing it in all parts of the land they might be put in mind of their duty, and practice it with more diligence. See on Psal. 60. tit.

*{Behold, it is written in the book of Jasher*} See on Josh. 10. 13. It seems to have been a book of annals, or chronicle, containing a relation of the civil affairs of the commonwealth. In it this song was inserted, that it might be preserved. And to this book reference is made as being commonly known and usually read. But it is now lost, as many other of the Jewish histories concerning the affairs of that state.

**V. 19.** {*The beauty of Israel is slain*} i.e. Saul, Jonathan, and all the flower of chivalry, and valiant men of Israel. See on ch. 7. 23.

*{upon thy high places*} i.e. The mountains of Gilboa, where the battle was fought, v. 21. & 1 Sam. 31. 8. Though the Israelites had made choice of these as places of great advantage, yet there they suffered this great defeat: which was an evidence of God’s high displeasure against them.

*{how are the mighty fallen?*} i.e. What a wonderful thing is this that so many great worthies, of such incomparable strength and valor, should thus fall before these uncircumcised Philistims, whom they had formerly so oftentimes vanquished! And this is thrice repeated, as the foot of this sad song or epitaph, here, v. 25, & 27.

**V. 20.** {*Tell it not in Gath, publish it not in the streets of Askelon*} These were two of the five chief cities of the Philistims, in which they were most likely to triumph upon the hearing of this news. Now this is not spoken as though David thought it possible that this great overthrow should be concealed from them; for their people were chief actors in this war, and Saul’s head was carried through the land, and set up in the temple of Dagon, and his arms kept in the house of Ashtaroth: but this is only an emphatical expression of David’s desire in a rhetorical and poetical manner, what he would have done, had it been possible, namely, that this matter might be concealed from the enemies of God and his people, lest they should triumph and insult over them, Judg. 16. 23. See Mic. 1. 10.

*{lest the daughters of the Philistines rejoice}* Because the women used to celebrate victories with songs and dances, Exod. 15. Judg. 11. 34. 1 Sam. 18. 6.

**V. 21.** {*Ye mountains of Gilboa, let there be no dew——upon you*} He curses these mountains in a poetical manner, that he might pathetically and hyperbolically express his grief for the great loss which the whole land sustained in the death of Saul and Jonathan, with all the gallant worthies that died with them; wishing that those mountains by their barrenness might be a perpetual monument of this great calamity. So Job 3. 3, 4, 5. Jer. 20. 14.

*{nor fields of offerings*} Heb. *of elevations*, or *high lands*, i.e. Fertile fields, bringing forth fruits fit for offerings in the tabernacle.

*{For there the shield of the mighty is vilely cast away*} Here he expresses the dishonorable manner of the overthrow of Saul, Jonathan, and the rest, seeing it is a great disgrace to soldiers, when they cast away their shields. And hereby he signifies, either, that their shields were as good as nothing, serving for no use, or that indeed, when they were routed in the field, they cast away their arms, that they might the faster fly away, and save themselves by flight.

*{as though he had not been anointed with oil*} i.e. As though he had been a common ordinary man, and not a king.

**V. 22.** {*From the blood of the slain, from the fat of the mighty the bow of Jonathan turned not back, &c.*} i.e. Saul and Jonathan never attempted their enemies in vain with their arms, but were always victorious, piercing and killing the best and valiantest of them, and satiating their weapons with blood and slaughter, even as a hungry man gluts himself by greedy devouring his food.

**V. 23.** {*Saul and Jonathan were lovely and pleasant in their lives*} i.e. Both in respect of others, in respect of their persons and parts, 1 Sam. 9. 2. and also mutually between themselves. For though Saul was enraged against Jonathan in his frantic fits, yet no doubt he loved so worthy a son when his fit was over; yea, even his wrath and rage against him proceeded from his love, because having made a league with David, he would not join with him in seeking his ruin, who would banish him from the kingdom, if he were suffered to live.

*{and in their death they were not divided*} This he added to clear Jonathan of that suspicion, that he conspired with David against his father; seeing he approved his love and faithfulness by adhering unto him both in life and death.

*{they were swifter than eagles, they were stronger than lions}* i.e. They were nimble and active in pursuing their enemies, and strong and valorous in subduing them. And hereby he signifies in a rhetorical and hyperbolical manner that they were complete soldiers and commanders, in whom nothing was wanting that could be desired.

**V. 24.** {*Ye daughters of Israel, weep over Saul, who clothed you in scarlet*} Partly by the peace you enjoyed through his government, (and peace usually is accompanied with plenty and abundance of all things both for necessity and delight) and partly by enriching the land with the spoil of the enemy: and here speaking of bravery in apparel, he addresses himself to the daughters of Israel rather than the sons, because that sex more than the other are delighted with costly attire, and so mourn more when they are deprived of it.

**V. 25.** {*How are the mighty fallen in the midst of the battle}* i.e. Not like cowards slain in their flight, but as valiant soldiers, who forsake not their colors, but keep their ground and fight it out to the death even in the very face of the enemy.

*{O Jonathan, thou wast slain in thy high places*} i.e. Upon the mountains and places of advantage in your own country.

**V. 26.** {*I am distressed for thee, my brother Jonathan*} He terms him brother, either in respect of alliance, being his brother-in-law by marriage, or rather in regard of that nearer bond of most intimate love and friendship contracted between them.

*{Thy love towards me was wonderful, passing the love of women*} i.e. The love either of one woman to another, or which the tender mother bears to her only son, or the most loving wife to her dearest husband. Now women’s affections being usually much stronger than men’s, by this expression David shows that Jonathan’s love towards him far exceeded the dearest love or nearest friendship.

**V. 27.** {*How are the mighty fallen, and the weapons of war perished!*} i.e. Either Saul and Jonathan, who were the swords and shields of the commonwealth, its choicest and chiefest strength; in which sense Elisha calls Elijah, and Joash Elisha, *the chariots and horsemen of Israel*, 2 King. 2. 12. & 13. 14. Or he means simply the arms and munition that were lost in the battle, and become a spoil to the Philistims, to the dishonor and weakening of God’s people.

## CHAPTER 2

**Verse 1.** {*And it came to pass after this, that David enquired of the Lord}* i.e. By the priest putting on the breastplate with Urim and Thummim, Num. 25. 21. Having from God a promise of the kingdom, he would do nothing of himself for the obtaining of it, but addressed himself to God for direction, what course he should take, and what means he should use for the accomplishment of his promise. For though he knew he should be king, yet he was ignorant of many circumstances which belonged to the managing of the business, as when he should set upon the work, and what place he should first attempt for the promoting of his cause.

*{saying, Shall I go up into any of the cities of Judah?*} He was weary of continuing in the Philistims’ country, or in that city which he had by Achish his donation, as lying upon the uttermost borders of the kingdom, and so unfit to settle in, being in too great a distance from the place where he was to reign; and therefore he desires (if it would stand with God’s good pleasure) to go up to some of the cities of Judah, his own tribe, where he was born and bred, and where his kindred, allies and friends living, he was likely to find best acceptance and most assistance.

*{And he said, Unto Hebron*} A chief and ancient city in the tribe of Judah, built seven years before Zoan in Egypt, Num. 13. 22. Hither Joshua and Caleb came when they were sent by Moses to search out the land, and it was afterwards given to Caleb and his posterity, Josh. 14. 13. It was one of the cities of refuge given to the priests, Josh. 20. 7. & 21. 11. and chiefly renowned, because the patriarchs Abraham, Isaac and Jacob were there buried, unto whom the land of Canaan by promise belonged, especially to Jacob, to whom it was revealed that the kingdom should be established in Judah, Gen. 49. 10. In all these respects it was the fittest place for David to go unto first when he was to enter upon the possession of the kingdom.

**V. 2.** {*So David went up thither, and his two wives also*} That as they had been comforts to him in his troubles, and partakers in all his afflictions, so they might be consorts with him in his prosperity, and communicate in his high honor and preferment. And herein they were types of Christ’s church, which when it has suffered with him, shall also reign with him, 2 Tim. 2. 12. Luke 22. 28, 29.

**V. 3.** {*And his men that were with him did David bring up*} i.e. His servants and soldiers that had accompanied him in his afflictions and persecutions.

*{and they dwelt in the Cities of Hebron*} i.e. Such as belonged to Hebron, which was the metropolis or chief city, Josh. 21. 11, 12.

**V. 4.** {*And the men of Judah came, and there they anointed David King over the house of Judah*} David was thrice anointed: first by Samuel, when God elected and designed him to be king, 1 Sam. 16. 13. secondly, by the tribe of Judah; whereby they testified their approbation and consent unto the election, actually receiving him to be their king; and lastly, by the whole body of the people, ch. 5. 3. to testify likewise their assent and approbation. By which anointing was signified not only God’s calling him to the royal dignity, but also the inward unction of the Spirit, whereby he was qualified with heroic gifts and graces necessary for that calling. Now it pleased God thus to reiterate David’s anointing for the further confirmation of his faith in this assurance, that God, who had called him to the kingdom, would establish him in it, and bless and prosper him in his government. And of such supports our weak faith stands in need, in respect of the many temptations unto which we are subject, and our frailty and inability to make any resistance. Now though it is not to be doubted but that many of other tribes did join in this action, even all those who being convinced of David’s just title from God, came unto him in Ziklag, and offered their service to bring him to the crown, 1 Chr. 12. yet Judah is only named, because that whole tribe unanimously joined in this action, whereas there were but some alone out of the other tribes that favored his title; and the rest, which were the greatest part, addicted still to the house of Saul, and desired to settle the crown and kingdom upon his posterity. Thus David through many tribulations at last comes to the crown, and was herein a lively type of our Savior Christ, who was first crowned with thorns before he was crowned with glory, and after many persecutions and afflictions was exalted to his throne and kingdom.

*{And they told David saying, That the men of Jabesh were they that buried Saul*} To wit, either upon his inquiry what was done with the bodies of Saul and Jonathan, as being willing to have them buried in an honorable manner, 1 Sam. 31. 13. or else they reported it of their own accord, thinking it would be ill taken at their hands that they bestowed so much care and cost in the burial of Saul, who was David’s professed enemy; or at least they gave him this warning, that taking notice of their affection to Saul and his family, he should take heed of them, if they proved his enemies, or use the best means he could to make them his friends, who were worthy the having, seeing they were so faithful to their former lord and king.

**V. 5.** {*And said unto them*} To wit, by these messengers which he sent.

*{Blessed be ye of the Lord*} i.e. The Lord multiply upon you all his blessings, as a reward of your faithful service.

*{that ye have shewed this kindness unto your lord, even unto Saul}* Who as lord and king over the whole land, was generally to be respected and honored, but in a more peculiar manner by you, who received from him deliverance out of desperate danger, 1 Sam. 11.

**V. 6.** {*And now the Lord shew mercy and truth unto you*} i.e. *Mercy*, in making gracious promises of rewarding your faithful service; and *truth*, in his constant performance of them unto you, who have in this action approved yourselves such loyal and grateful subjects to your lord and sovereign.

*{And I also will requite you this kindness}* i.e. Seeing your lord, who is dead, cannot do it, I as his successor will pay his debt, and recompense the love and thankfulness which you have showed unto him.

**V. 7.** {*Therefore now let your hands be strengthened, and be ye valiant*} Heb. *be ye sons of valour*, i.e. Be not dismayed, though you have lost your lord: for I, whom the house of Judah by God’s appointment have anointed king, will supply unto you all the good things you have lost by his death. And this he speaks both to take off from them all jealousies and fears, that he would deal the worse with them for their expressions of love to their deceased lord; and withal that he might use all lawful policy and good means to gain the people’s love, though he had assurance from God that he should be king. He would not use any hostile means to obtain the kingdom (though he were certain he should have prevailed) until he was compelled thereunto by Abner and Ishbosheth. And this he did, partly, as a gracious king, who abhorred to shed the blood of his subjects; and partly, in respect of his oath to Saul, that he would not destroy his posterity, and abolish his name, 1 Sam. 24. 21, 22.

**V. 8.** {*But Abner the son of Ner, Captain of Saul’s host, took Ishbosheth}* Called also Eshbaal. 1 Chron. 8. 33. See on ch. 11. 21. God being pleased yet more to exercise David’s faith and patience, before he came to the full possession of the crown, leaves Abner to the ambition and pride of his heart, to cross him in his proceedings, by setting up one of Saul’s sons to be king for his own ends. For being himself of Saul’s family, and near of kin unto him, and by him advanced to be general of the army, (a high place of honor and command) he desires that the sovereignty might remain in the house of Saul, that so he might still retain his dignity, which he had no hope to do if David came to be king, seeing he had many worthy friends and near kindred of his own to advance. And he makes choice of Ishbosheth, who was no martial man, but of a weak spirit and small parts; knowing he could rule him at his pleasure, who was chief ruler of the kingdom, and so in effect make himself king, the other serving only for state, or shadow, to color his actions: of which power and regency he had no hope, if David, a prudent and valiant prince, should wield the scepter. And because Mephibosheth was but a child, and lame of his feet, and so not fit to color his ends and aims, though the right heir by lineal descent, and makes choice of his uncle, to make him king. And all this he did out of pride and ambition, against the light of his conscience: for he knew well enough that by God’s appointment Samuel had anointed David to be king over Israel, and to succeed Saul, whom God had rejected; and he plainly acknowledges it in his rage, ch. 3. 9, 10, 18.

*{And brought him over to Mahanaim*} A city in Gilead, in the tribe of Gad, beyond Jordan, on the river Jabbok, near Jabesh-Gilead; which had its name, signifying *two hosts* or *camps*, from the vision of angels that there appeared unto Jacob, Gen. 32. 1, 2. Of this place Abner made choice, either because being a remote part of the kingdom, and far distant from Judah, where David’s chief strength lay, it was fittest for the raising and forming of an army; or it may be he was jealous of those parts, especially of Jabesh-Gilead, lest they should fall to David’s party, being won by his kind message, and therefore he meant to reside there with his strength about him, to keep them from defection.

**V. 9.** {*And he made him king over Gilead*} i.e. All the country beyond Jordan.

*{And over the Ashurites*} i.e. The tribe of Asher, on the north of Canaan.

*{and over Jezreel*} Situate almost in the middle of Canaan, on the borders of Zebulun, Manasseh, and Issachar, in which tribe it was, Josh. 19. 18.

*{And over all Israel*} The tribe of Judah only excepted, and those that resorted unto David out of the other tribes: though it seems that the whole people were well-affected to David, and inclined to choose him for their king, had they not been overawed by Abner, who was general of the army, and had a great strength under his command.

**V. 10.** {*Ishbosheth, Saul’s son, was forty years old when he began to reign}* And therefore was old enough to have accompanied his father with the rest of his brethren in the battle, had he been a martial man, and of parts fit for such a service.

*{and reigned two years*} To wit, before the wars between him and David began, and chiefly that by the pool of Gideon. For he reigned over Israel all the time that David reigned over Judah in Hebron, before these wars; though it is not like that they lived in peace all the time before; howsoever this was the chief war that was between them in these two years. For otherwise it could not have been said that *there was long war between the house of Saul and the house of David*, if Ishbosheth reigned but two years in all, ch. 3. 1.

*{followed David*} i.e. Took his part, and followed him as their king and general. And herein they could not be condemned for making a schism in the commonwealth, and being the cause of a civil war by separating from their brethren, because they did it in obedience to God, knowing that he had appointed David to be their king. Yea they deserved great praise, in that they were so ready to expose themselves to all the dangers and miseries of such a war rather than they would transgress God’s known will.

**V. 12.** {*And Abner——went out from Mahanaim to Gibeon*} To wit, his army attending him, to wage war against David and Judah.

**V. 13.** {*And Joab the son of Zeruiah*} i.e. David’s sister. Joab was entrusted in this expedition, to lead the armies, though he was not yet chosen chief general, 1 Chron. 11. 6.

*{by the pool of Gibeon*} This pool was on the south side without Gibeon, a city of Benjamin. See on Josh. 9. 9. Some think it was the same that was called Gibeah-Saul, where he kept his court and residence, 1 Sam. 11. 4.

**V. 14.** {*And Abner said to Joab, Let the young men arise, and play before us*} i.e. Try their valor and skill in arms, to make us some sport by acting their bloody tragedy. This challenge he makes, in hope that by the good success of his gallant soldiers he should dismay and daunt the courage of David’s men, and so obtain a more easy victory. And this challenge is no sooner made by Abner than accepted by Joab, they both delighting and conspiring in cruelty and shedding of blood, as men often fleshed with these horrid acts and aspects, and accounting it but a play and sport to see their followers massacre and murder one another. But how abominable this is in the sight of God, appears as many other ways, so by the fearful issue of this barbarous and bloody challenge.

**V. 16.** {*every one his fellow*} i.e. His antagonist or adversary.

**V. 17.** {*And there was a very sore battle that day}* For either party being enraged against the other by the death of their champions, joined their main battles, and furiously fought together as mortal enemies, till at last Abner and his party were beaten by David’s servants, and being routed were put to flight.

**V. 18.** {*And Asahel was as light of foot as a wild roe}* A hyperbolical expression of his swiftness in running: as Saul and Jonathan are said to be *swifter then Eagles*, ch. 1. 23. Josephus says of Asahel, that he not only exceeded other men in running, but also contended with horses in swiftness. Unto which he trusting too much, it became the occasion of his death, by his example verifying the saying of Solomon, Eccles. 9. 11. *That the race is not to the swift, nor the battel to the strong.*

**V. 19.** {*And Asahel pursued after Abner*} Ambitiously affecting the glory of taking prisoner the general of the field. For as he was swift of foot, so he was valiant, and a man of his hands; and therefore reckoned among David’s worthies, 1 Chr. 11. 26.

**V. 21.** {*Lay thee hold on one of the young men, and take thee his armour*} Or *his spoil*, q.d. If you do affect the glory of the victory, I will not be against it; but then you must choose such an antagonist as is your equal, and a young man like yourself, and not provoke an old experienced soldier, to your extreme danger and dishonor.

**V. 22.** {*And Abner said again to Asahel——wherefore should I smite thee to the ground?*} q.d. I am very loath to kill you, if you do not too much press and provoke me, for your brother’s sake.

*{how then should I hold up my face to Joab thy brother?*} i.e. With what confidence can I look for any peace and reconciliation with him having killed his brother. And of this, Abner was careful even in the very heat of war, being guilty to himself that he fought in a bad cause, and doubting that David in the end would prevail: and when that came to pass, he well knew that Joab who was so potent and in so great favor with David, would either be a heavy adversary, if incensed against him; or a good friend, to make his peace.

**V. 23.** {*Wherefore Abner with the hinder end of the spear*} i.e. With the pike of the spear in the lower end; for their spears were double pointed.

*{smote him under the fifth rib*} i.e. Under the lowest of the short ribs, (which are five in number) near the belly, out of which wound his guts came forth. So ch. 3. 27. and 4. 6. and 20. 10. And in that place he smote him, because in all likelihood it was below his armor, and so a sure killing place, if his spear hit upon it.

*{As many as came to the place where Asahel fell down and died, stood still*} And so were hindered in the pursuit; by gazing upon this sad spectacle, and lamenting his death.

**V. 24.** {*Joab also and Abishai pursued after Abner*} Like wise and brave commanders; not regarding so much their private interest and losses as the common good of the people.

*{and the sun went down when they were come to the hill of Ammah*} Hither Abner retreated, that his men being rallied into a body, and having the advantage of the upper ground, might the better stand upon their defense.

**V. 25.** {*And the children of Benjamin gathered themselves together after Abner*} To wit, into one main body of battalia, *on the top of an hill*.

**V. 26.** {*Shall the sword devour for ever?*} i.e. Will you put no end to slaughter? He seems to allude to a metaphor usual in the Scriptures, calling the edge *the mouth of the sword*, which destroys men, as wild beasts devour their prey: q.d. There ought to be some mean and measure in these acts of hostility, seeing war is not a thing good in itself, (for in such things there are no bounds, but the more the better, and never enough) but good only as it is referred to its end, i.e. the settling of a firm and just peace.

*{Knowest thou not that it will be bitterness in the latter end?}* i.e. It will prove like such meats, as are sweet in the mouth, and breeding choler, are bitter in the stomach. For though in the fury of war revenge seems sweet, yet the effects and fruits of it are bitter and distasteful, as rapines, spoil, effusion of blood, and innumerable other miseries, especially in a civil war, wherein not only men of the same country, but also of the same family, kindred and allies, yea even nearest friends, and dearest brethren, spoil and murder one another. And that being the condition and nature of this present war, Abner uses it as a reason to persuade Joab to desist from it. Otherwise it might prove bitter in another respect, namely, if by too eager and merciless pursuance they cut off all hope, and brought their enemies into a desperate condition, and so made them resolute to sell their lives at the dearest rate, and to turn again upon them, and renew the fight. This in the chance of war sometimes proves fatal to them who a little before seemed to have gotten the victory.

*{bid the people return from following their brethren*} He uses this word *brethren* as a strong argument to make him desist from the pursuit; seeing they were, not aliens, heathens and idolaters whom they pursued, but all of the same nation with themselves, even the Israel of God; children of the same Father, and professors of the same true religion.

**V. 27.** {*unless thou hadst spoken}* i.e. Sent us a challenge, and provoked us to a combat between our young men, we had never engaged ourselves in this fight, but were rather resolved to have marched away, and returned quietly to our own homes that so it might have been prevented.

**V. 28.** {*So Joab blew a trumpet, and all the people stood still*} i.e. Caused his trumpeter to sound a retreat; upon the hearing whereof, his army desisted from pursuing their victory. And herein Joab showed much wisdom and moderation in his affections and passions. For though he could not choose but be much moved and grieved for the death of his brother, yet he respects not his own private revenge, but shows how unwilling he was to shed the blood of his brethren; thinking that it would be a good means to make Abner desire peace, and for the settlement thereof, to apply himself to David as his lawful king. So ch. 18. 19.

*{neither fought they any more*} Namely, at that time; for otherwise the war continued between the house of David and the house of Saul for some time after this, ch. 3. 1.

**V. 29.** {*Bithron*} Called *the mountains of Bether*, Cant. 2. 17. i.e. *of separation*, because lying in Gilead they were by Jordan separated from Canaan.

**V. 31.** {*had smitten of Benjamin and Abner’s men}* i.e. Had slain of his party, both of Benjamin, and all the other tribes that adhered to the house of Saul.

**V. 32.** {*And they took up Asahel, and buried him in the sepulchre of his father, which was in Bethlehem*} Here we have a transposition of the words in this verse. For they did not bury Asahel that night after the battle was fought, but traveled all that night to Hebron, to bring unto David with as much speed as could be the good success of the battle, and carrying the corpse of Asahel along with them; they afterwards solemnized his funeral when they were at better leisure. Others, not willing to admit of such a transposition, hold that they did not go to David the night after their victory, but stayed a while to bury their dead slain upon the place, and then carried the body of Asahel to Bethlehem, and there buried it, and the night following went unto Hebron to carry the news of their victory to the king. But by that time it was like to be stale news, if they stayed till Asahel’s funeral was solemnized. And I see no reason why a great worthy slain in field in the king’s cause and quarrel, should be so suddenly and obscurely buried in the night. Besides, it seems unto me, that as Abner and his men went all the night after the battle to their king at Mahanaim, v. 29. so Joab and his men are here likewise said to have gone the same night to their king at Hebron.

## CHAPTER 3

**Verse 1.** {*Now there was long war between the house of Saul and the house of David*} For it lasted five years and odd months, from the first fight, ch. 2. 12. to the death of Ishbosheth. For David’s reign over Judah in Hebron was seven years and six months, two of which were spent before that fight, ch. 2. 11.

*{But David waxed stronger and stronger, and the house of Saul waxed weaker and weaker*} David’s kingdom had a sure foundation, God’s never-failing promises, upon which he grounded his faith, waiting with patience for the accomplishment of his word; but Ishbosheth’s kingdom was founded upon the arm of flesh, human policy and power, and had no word of God for the continuance of it, but rather on the contrary for its ruin and abolition. Here we have a lively type of the kingdom of Satan and the kingdom of Christ. That is strong and ample in its first beginnings, but grows lesser and weaker by degrees; this, though small and weak at the first, is daily enlarged, and grows in strength, until at last Satan’s kingdom be wholly ruined and abolished, and Christ’s set up and enlarged over the whole world. And this appears in Christ’s kingdom, both generally considered in the whole church, against which Satan raises daily war by his chief general Antichrist, as Ishbosheth by his Abner, yet shall never prevail, but continually grow weaker and weaker, being daily *consumed by the Spirit of his mouth*, the preaching of the word, until he be at last wholly *destroyed and abolished by the brightnesse of his coming*, 2 Thess. 2. 8. And also particularly considered in every Christian, in that fight which is between the flesh and the Spirit; the one, as Satan’s lieutenant-general, attended with innumerable lusts, seeming invincibly strong in the first beginnings of the Christian warfare, grows daily weaker, till it be wholly abolished; but the other as Christ’s lieutenant, accompanied with his spiritual graces, waxes stronger continually, till at last getting a full and final victory, the kingdom of Christ is erected and established both in grace and glory.

**V. 2.** {*And unto David were sons born in Hebron*} Whilst he was exiled and persecuted by Saul, God gave him no children, though he had two wives with him, because in his wise providence he saw that children then would have been burdensome and troublesome unto him; but no sooner is he chosen king over Judah, and settled in Hebron, than he is made the father of six sons. Yet God did not multiply his posterity as he multiplied his wives, but of his six wives gave him only six sons, to teach him and us, that polygamy, howsoever it was tolerated in those times, yet was not allowed as his ordinance to make a fruitful progeny. And experience also teaches us, that one man joined with one wife, according to God’s first institution, have been blessed with a more numerous posterity than they who have had many. But especially this is evident in Solomon, who of his thousand wives and concubines had only one son, and he none of the best; even as his father before him, though he had divers sons, yet was not much blessed in them, seeing they were many of them rather corrosives, to eat out his heart with grief and vexation, than comforts and causes of joy; as Amnon, Absalom, and Adonijah, 1 Chr. 3. 1, 2. See on 1 Kin. 1. 5.

**V. 3.** {*And his second, Chileab*} Called also Daniel, 1 Chro. 3. 1.

*{the third, Absalom the son of Maacah, the daughter of Talmai King of Geshur*} A city situate in the north, on the frontier of Gilead, at the entrance of the valleys of Syria Trachinitis, Deut. 3. 14. Josh. 12. 5. and 13. 8. Hence some think David had this wife, when being at Ziklag he invaded and foraged the country of the Geshurites and Amalekites, and taking her captive, did marry her, becoming first a proselyte to the Jews’ religion. Which after some ceremonial observations performed, it was lawful for him to do, Deut. 21. 12, 13. (though not very expedient, seeing he had choice enough among his own people) because she was not of any of those seven cursed nations with whom marriage was interdicted. But it is more probably thought that David married her after his departure from Ziklag, for there he had only two wives, Ahinoam and Abigail, 1 Sam. 30. 5. and that with her father’s liking and consent, when he was chosen king of Judah, and dwelt at Hebron; that he might by this alliance secure his kingdom from the like invasion. And if David and Talmai had not been in league and friendship, it is not like that Absalom would have fled to him for protection, when for murdering his brother Amnon he was in his father’s displeasure, ch. 13. 37, 38.

**V. 5.** {*And the sixth, Ithream, by Eglah David’s wife}* All the rest were his wives, yet she only is so called; either because her parentage being obscure, she was no otherwise known than by her title of being *Davids wife*, as all the rest were; or to distinguish her from some other that then lived of the same name; or, being the last that is named, she in particular is called his *wife*, to imply that all the rest were his wives likewise, by whom he had these children, besides those which he had by his concubines, 1 Chr. 3. 9.

**V. 6.** {*Abner made himself strong for the house of Saul*} i.e. He furnished himself with arms, munition and riches, and increased his army, to settle the crown upon Ishbosheth.

**V. 7.** {*And Ishbosheth said to Abner, Wherefore hast thou gone in unto my father’s concubine?}* i.e. Have lain with and defiled her? With this fault he charges him, either because it was so reported unto him by others, or out of his own groundless suspicion and jealousy, seeing it appears not whether it was so or no. Abner in his answer neither confessing nor denying it. But whether it were true or false, it seems Ishbosheth’s discontent did arise, not only from this, that Abner had dishonored his father, who had been his king and sovereign, in lying with his concubine; but chiefly because, observing unto what great strength he was grown, being general of the army, and in high favor with the people, he suspected that he aspired to the kingdom, and made way thereunto by making use of the king’s concubine, who having two sons might give some color to his claim, when Ishbosheth was taken out of the way. For it seems in those days the having of the king’s wives and concubines after his decease was thought not only a sign of ambition and aspiring to the crown, but some help also to further such a design; as appears in the example of Adonijah, with whom Solomon was implacably displeased for desiring to marry Abishag David’s wife, as supposing that thereby he intended to get the kingdom, 1 Kin. 2. 22. See ch. 12. 8. & 16. 21.

**V. 8.** {*Then Abner——said, Am I a dog’s head}* Or, *a dog-keeper*, i.e. Am I so base and contemptible in your eyes that you should suspect me guilty of so foul a fact? Or suppose I were guilty of it, am I in your esteem of such a low and poor condition, even no better than a dog, or dog-keeper, that you hold it so great a disparagement unto you for me to lie with this woman, who was not your father’s wife, but only his concubine? Whereas by my place I am next unto the king, and have done therein such faithful and profitable service both to your father, yourself, and your whole family? See chap. 9. 8. & 16. 9. 1 Sam. 24. 14. Deut. 23. 18.

*{which against Judah do shew kindness this day to the house of Saul}* q.d. Have you any reason to slight and vilify me as some mean groom or scoundrel, from whose support both yourself and all your father’s house have your whole subsistence, and who have power, when I please, to deliver you into your enemies’ hands, who would depose you and seek your ruin?

**V. 9.** {*So do God to Abner, and more also*} See on Ruth 1. 17.

*{except, as the Lord hath sworn to David, even so I do to him*} i.e. Translate the kingdom from the house of Saul, and make him king. Hereby it appears that he knew that God had chosen and appointed David to be king, and that all this while he had opposed him against his knowledge and conscience, for his own worldly and carnal ends.

**V. 10.** {*from Dan even to Beersheba*} See on Judg. 20. 1.

**V. 11.** {*And he could not answer Abner a word, for he feared him*} Being overawed with the greatness of his power which he had with the people, especially with the army. This was David’s case, ver. 39.

**V. 12.** {*And Abner sent messengers to David on his behalf*} Heb. *instead of himself*. Or, *in his own name*. Not daring as yet to go unto him in his own person, till he had from him a safe conduct, and might be entertained with acceptance, and upon good conditions. Others read it, *Because of this*, i.e. being enraged by this affront.

*{saying, Whose is the land?*} This may be either meant thus, Whose is it to bestow? Or in whose power is it to confer upon whom he pleases, but in mine, who am ready to give it you? Or thus, Unto whom by right does it appertain but unto you only, whom God, who alone has the right to dispose of kingdoms, has chosen and appointed unto it? And here, like a hypocrite, he pretends it was God’s designation that moved him to tender his service unto David, whereas indeed it was his indignation towards Ishbosheth, and his self-ends, to secure him from danger of punishment for his former demerits, and his hopes by this means either to be advanced by David’s favor, or to retain his present power and dignity, at least that he might not be rejected and cast out with dishonor and disgrace.

*{Make thy league with me*} i.e. Enter into covenant with me, that you will remit all that is past, receive me into favor, and give me such place and preferment as befits my person and parts.

*{to bring about all Israel unto thee*} i.e. Cause them to leave Ishbosheth, and become your subjects.

**V. 13.** {*And he said, Well, I will make a league with thee*} i.e. David returned Abner this answer, I like and accept of your propositions.

*{Thou shalt not see my face, except thou first bring Michal Saul’s daughter}* There were two principal causes which made David so desirous to have Michal brought back unto him, that he might enjoy her as his wife, though she had been so long detained from him, and married to another: I. Because she was his first wife, and had formerly loved him dearly, and approved her love by using means to save his life when he was in extreme danger, though thereby she incurred the displeasure of the king her father. And howsoever he might make some doubt of the continuance of her affection, seeing she had given her consent to marry another man, yet he imputed that, not to her want of love to him, but to her father’s authority and importunity, by which she was overruled. II. He might have therein a politic end, namely to settle peace, and secure the possession of his kingdom, by ingratiating himself, and gaining the love of all Saul’s kindred and allies, having his daughter to live with him as his wife, freely remitting that great wrong offered unto him in marrying her to another man.

**V. 14.** {*And David sent messengers to Ishbosheth Saul’s son, saying, Deliver me my wife Michal}* He desires no favor but only that he would do him right, by using his authority for restoring unto him his lawful wife. And he himself sends this message, and does not only use the mediation of Abner, because Abner being lately but upon ill terms with him, he might suspect he made the motion for some sinister ends, and self-interest; and also that it might be the more easily effected, having Ishbosheth’s authority to countenance it, and Abner’s power to back and execute it. And it is apparent, that Abner, as he did help it on with his persuasion, so he was the king’s agent to effect it. For he went to Phaltiel her husband to demand her in the king’s name; and having obtained her, he brought her away, and accompanied her to Bahurim, Phaltiel following her weeping, till Abner caused him to return, ver. 16.

*{which I espoused to me for an hundred foreskins of the Philistines}* He pleads his right unto her, not by Saul’s free gift, but in a way of justice, because he had purchased her at a dear and dangerous rate, venturing his life for the procuring of the Philistims’ foreskins. And here he names a hundred only, though he brought two hundred unto Saul, because the covenant between them was only for a hundred, 1 Sam. 18. 25, 27.

**V. 15.** {*even from Phaltiel*} He is called Phalti, 1 Sam. 25. 44.

**V. 16.** {*And her husband went with her along weeping*} Heb. *going and weeping*. i.e. Expressing his great grief with tears, because a wife so noble and beautiful was taken away from him.

*{to Bahurim*} A city of Benjamin, on the frontiers of Judah, ch. 19. 16. See on Josh. 21. 18.

**V. 17.** {*And Abner had communication with the Elders of Israel*} According to his covenant made with David.

*{Ye sought for David in times past to be King over you*} Hereby it appears that many or most of the people of Israel did so much honor David, that they much desired long ago to have had him to be their king, had not Abner, having the *militia* in his hands, overpowered them, and compelled them to take part with Ishbosheth.

**V. 18.** {*Now then do it; for the Lord hath spoken of David, saying, &c.*} We do not read in the sacred story where this is expressly spoken; but Josephus says it was spoken by Samuel, and commonly known amongst the people. Here Abner like a politic hypocrite, pretends he endeavored to set up David king, in obedience to God, and for the good and salvation of the people, whereas he did it merely out of self-love, and to advance his private interest.

**V. 19.** {*Abner also spake in the ears of Benjamin*} i.e. Having received a satisfactory answer from the rest of the tribes, condescending to his motion, that they would receive David for their king, he addresses himself to the tribe of Benjamin, speaking unto them to the same purpose he had spoken to the elders. And this he does in an especial manner to them, because Saul being of their tribe, was chiefly in their favor; and therefore, if they gave way to David’s title, and submitted to his government, little doubt was to be made of the other tribes.

*{And Abner went also to speak in the ears of David in Hebron*} i.e. Having received such an answer from the elders of Israel and Benjamin as satisfied him, he himself, to show his industrious diligence and faithfulness in the agitating David’s business which he had undertaken, went to carry the news of his good success to him at Hebron. And this he did either secretly, that it might not come to Ishbosheth’s ear, having all about him as his creatures at his command; or rather (because this could hardly be done, he having twenty men in his company to attend him, ver. 20.) it is probable that he carried Michal with him to color his action, he having acquainted Ishbosheth with it, and obtained his consent.

**V. 20.** {*And David made Abner and the men that were with him, a feast*} As it was usual in those days to do when they made solemn contracts and covenants, Gen. 26. 30. & 31. 44, 46. Herein David is thought to have failed: but, not, as I conceive, because he feasted Abner, though, (as they say) he was wicked in himself, and now acted treachery against his king; seeing it may be lawful to eat with wicked men, when as we do it not out of free choice and love of their company, but for civil ends and the better transacting and composing of our worldly business and important affairs. Neither could this be counted treachery in Abner, seeing he had openly professed that he would thus do, even to Ishbosheth’s own face: and if it were unlawful to set up Ishbosheth, being contrary to God’s express will, then it was lawful to put him down, although he might fail in his ends, and manner of doing it. But here was David’s fault, that he thus far proceeded in his own politic plots, and never consulted with God for his direction: and that might well be the cause of the ill success of this transaction, and that it had such a tragical conclusion.

**V. 21.** {*And Abner said unto David——I will gather all Israel unto my lord the King*} i.e. All the tribes, or the greatest part of all, or all the chief of all estates and conditions. So Mat. 3. 5. Phil. 2. 21.

**V. 22.** {*Joab came from pursuing a troop*} It is most likely of the Philistims, who were sent out to spoil the country.

**V. 24.** {*Then Joab came to the king, and said, What hast thou done?*} In a proud and insolent manner he taxes the king of imprudence and indiscretion, for sending away a dangerous enemy, when he had him in his hands.

**V. 25.** {*Thou knowest Abner the son of Ner, that he came to deceive thee*} i.e. You might well have known, if you had been so considerate, that Abner, being so subtle and politic a man, came not for any good that he intended towards you, but only to deceive you, and like a crafty spy to discover all your counsels and courses, your actions and proceedings. And thus he sweetens his saucy reproof, by pretending nothing but love and care of David’s good; whereas in truth he did all out of self-love, pride and envy. He feared lest Abner by this important service should creep into the king’s favor, and be corrival with him in honors and preferments; and was therefore vexed because he had escaped out of his hand, when such a fit opportunity was offered of revenging himself on him for the death of Asahel his brother.

**V. 26.** {*And when Joab was come out from David*} From whom he flung in a fury and discontent, not waiting for his reply, as he would have David conceive it, but indeed making all haste to put his design in execution.

*{he sent messengers after Abner*} Using, it is likely, David’s name to countenance his action, and pretending that he had some weighty and important business to treat further with him about. And this perhaps made David afterwards the more earnest and careful in clearing himself, and wiping away the suspicion that he had some hand in Abner’s murder.

*{which brought him again from the well of Sirah*} Situate on the north of Hebron.

**V. 27.** {*Joab took him aside in the gate to speak with him quietly*} 1 King. 2. 5. Heb. *peaceably*. i.e. To treat with him about the king’s affairs in secret, and as it were in his ear, that no man might hear him. And because he would be the less suspected, he made choice of the gate of the city to consult in, which was the place of judicature, and of their public and solemn meetings.

*{and smote him there under the fifth rib, that he died*} As he had done before to his brother Asahel. See on ch. 2. 23.

*{for the blood of Asahel his brother*} This was one cause which made Joab take this revenge, though he had little reason to do it, seeing Abner shed Asahel’s blood, in a sort unwillingly, and not before he was much provoked to do it by his importunity. But God herein had his ends, as well as Joab his. I. He thus justly punished Abner, as for all other sins, so for his rebellion against David his lawful king, contrary to his own knowledge and conscience, to compass his worldly and wicked ends; and for being the cause of shedding so much blood in this unlawful war, at which he so little scrupled, that he counted it but a sport or play, ch. 2. 14. II. By this means he took Abner out of the way, that David might not be beholding to him for bringing Israel under his government, whom he would have come to the kingdom by a better way.

**V. 28.** {*I and my Kingdom are guiltless before the Lord for ever}* i.e. I am wholly innocent of having any hand in this heinous crime of shedding Abner’s blood; and therefore the Lord, who knows it, will never lay it to my charge, to punish me for it, nor my kingdom for my sake, seeing it is alike guiltless.

**V. 29.** {*Let it rest on the head of Joab, and on all his father’s house}* Though we are bound by God’s law to abstain from all private revenge, to overcome evil with goodness, and to pray for our enemies that hurt and persecute us, yet in this public cause, that concerned not David alone, but the welfare of the whole kingdom, he is fain to use these fearful imprecations both against Joab and his whole family, that so he might the better clear himself from having any hand in this horrid murder, of which otherwise he was likely to be suspected, by Abner’s friends at least, that he was consenting to it. Which would have been a cause of raising all his party against him, and the occasion of a bloody war, which would have disturbed the peace of the commonwealth, caused much effusion of blood, and quite unsettled him in the kingdom, in which now by general consent they were about to establish him.

*{And let there not fail from the house of Joab*} i.e. As long as his family or any of it continued, let all or some of these evils befall them. So 2 Kin. 5. 27.

*{one that hath an issue*} i.e. Some incurable running sore, or flux of seed: for which they were ceremonially excommunicated, and debarred from entering into the congregation, and partaking of the public ordinances, Lev. 15. 21.

*{or that leaneth on a staff}* By reason of feebleness and lameness.

**V. 30.** {*So Joab and Abishai his brother slew Abner*} i.e. Both the brothers conspired together and plotted this murder, though Joab alone did act it.

*{at Gibeon in the battle}* This is added as an aggravation of this foul fact: Abner slew Asahel like a brave soldier, though unwillingly, in the open field; but Joab slew Abner desperately and willingly, and that in a base, cowardly and treacherous manner, when he pretended peace and amity.

**V. 31.** {*And David said to Joab and all the people that were with him*} He proclaimed a solemn and public funeral mourning for the death of Abner, and for that heinous sin that was committed in his murder, that thereby he might show he was innocent, and had no hand in it. And he would have Joab to join with all the rest, because he was the chief actor, that (if it were possible) he might come to a sight and sense of his sin, and to unfeigned repentance for it, when he was convinced of the heinousness thereof, by this public lamentation of the king and all the people, though they had no hand in the fact.

*{rent your clothes and gird you with sackcloth*} That, to testify the renting of their hearts with grief; and this, to afflict their bodies, and to intimate they thought themselves unworthy to put on better clothing. See on ch. 1. 2. & Gen. 37. 29.

*{And King David himself followed the bier*} i.e. That he might the more honor Abner’s funeral, he laid aside his kingly dignity, and attended upon the corpse. For among all nations kings have been ever exempted from such expressions of their mourning.

**V. 32.** {*The King lift up his voice, and wept*} i.e. Did not only express his grief by shedding of tears, but also did testify it to the people by vocal lamentations.

**V. 33.** {*Died Abner as a fool dieth?*} i.e. As a base coward, that foolishly yields himself to be slaughtered by his enemy, making no resistance? q.d. No surely, he did not die so, but was suddenly and treacherously surprised, having no opportunity to show his valor, as the most courageous man on earth might have been in the like case.

**V. 34.** {*Thy hands were not bound, nor thy feet put into fetters*} i.e. You did not die like a base weak captive taken in war, having neither courage nor strength to make resistance, nor as a malefactor bound in chains and fetters, and so led out to execution: no, you were truly valiant in all your enterprises, as Joab should have found it, if he had fought with you on equal terms; but you were suddenly slain, when you did least suspect it, by one that pretended peace and friendship.

*{as a man falleth before wicked men, so fellest thou*} i.e. As it might be the case of the most wise and valiant man in the world, that has to do with false, faithless and graceless men, who have no respect to true valor, honor or honesty, and of whom he has no suspicion, because they make show of love and friendship; so was it with you. And this David publishes before Joab’s face, and brands him with this dishonor and reproach, in the audience of all the people, as a part of his punishment for this wicked fact.

**V. 35.** {*And when all the people came to cause David to eat meat*} i.e. The tribunes, colonels and chief commanders of the people and army hearing that David refused to eat, came and entreated him not to lay the matter so much to heart as to forbear his food, especially there being a feast provided to refresh and cheer them in the time of their mourning, as the manner was then, and is to this day, in solemn funerals. See Jer. 16. 7, 8. Ezek. 24. 17.

*{So do God to me, and more also*} See on Ruth 1. 17.

*{If I taste bread, or ought else, till the Sun be down*} He joined fasting with his mourning, to express his grief for Abner, and to clear himself of being any way guilty of his death, that thereby he might give satisfaction to the people seeing his innocency.

**V. 36.** {*And all the people took notice of it, and it pleased them*} Heb. *was good in their eyes*. This is said, to intimate that David attained unto the end at which he prudently aimed in the carriage of this business, that is, to clear himself, and please the people.

*{As whatsoever the King did, pleased all the people*} i.e. He was so well accepted, and so gracious in the eyes of the people, through his wise, fair and just demeanor in all his courses, that not only his carriage of all this business concerning Abner, but generally of all things else, was exceeding pleasing unto them.

**V. 37.** {*that it was not of the King*} i.e. Not by his will, counsel or instigation, directly or indirectly, that Abner was slain.

**V. 38.** {*Know ye not that there is a Prince and a great man fallen this day in Israel?*} i.e. You cannot choose but take notice of it, that there is a great peer and pillar of the land fallen, and that therefore they that killed him should be punished as murderers, were there power to do it.

**V. 39.** {*And I am this day weak, though anointed King*} i.e. Unsettled in my kingdom, like a tender plant that has not taken root, and therefore of small power to see justice executed, especially on these murderers who are in such favor with the people, and command the army. This excuse was below pious and valiant David, and too much savored of carnal policy and ungrounded fear. For he had God’s infallible promises, that he would not only bring him to the kingdom, but also establish him on his throne, and his posterity after him: and this promise could not have been frustrated and made void by all the power and plots of all wicked traitors combining together and conspiring against him; and therefore he needed not to have feared to have executed justice upon such a heinous malefactor, maugre the might and malice of him and all his allies and adherents. Again, where there is from God an absolute command, there is no reasoning with flesh and blood. *He that sheddeth mans blood, must by man have his blood shed*, Gen. 9. 6. And the willful murderer must be taken from the altar, if he flee to it for shelter and sanctuary, and be put to death, Exod. 25. 14. And though there were never so much danger in it, yet let justice be done, though heaven fall. Neither was there so much peril in the execution as there was in the neglecting of it. For at this time David was highly in the people’s favor: and Joab’s abominable fact must needs render him odious to all men that were not as wicked as himself. And if now justice had been duly executed, Amasa’s death had been prevented, whom Joab, being fleshed with cruelty and impunity, did in like manner take away. Nor is it sufficient to say, that his punishment was only respited, not remitted, seeing all that while the guilt of blood lay upon the king and the whole land and cried for vengeance, till it was expiated by the blood of the murderer, 1 Kings 2. 31, 33. Yea, but the command of executing the murderer is an affirmative precept; *always* binding to obedience, but *not at all times* for the acting of it. I answer, Though some delay were allowed for punishing willful murderers, (for which we have no warrant in the Scriptures) yet this would not excuse David for his not executing justice upon Joab, when he was established in his kingdom, even to the very end of his reign, seeing it was more than probable that in so long time Joab might have died a natural death, or being a martial man and in continual wars, have been slain by his enemies, and so justice should never have been executed upon him.

*{the Lord shall reward the evil doer*} i.e. Though I be unable to inflict condign punishment on these potent offenders, yet the Lord in his due time will do it himself; and I refer it unto him, and desire him to do it; 1 Kings 2. 5, 6, 34.

## CHAPTER 4

**Verse 1.** {*And when Saul’s son heard that Abner was dead in Hebron, his hands were feeble}* i.e. When Ishbosheth heard that Abner was taken away, upon whom he wholly relied, he was so daunted and disanimated that he became weak both in courage and counsel, and was utterly disabled for action, and managing the great affairs of the commonwealth. So ch. 17. 2. Ezra. 4. 4. Neh. 6. 9. Isai. 13. 7.

*{And all the Israelites were troubled*} i.e. All the tribes that adhered to his party were in great fear and perplexed with the suddenness of this news, because they knew not what would become of the treaty of peace and reconciliation, Abner being dead, who did negotiate it: and if it should now break off for want of his prosecution, and the kingdom be embroiled in a new war, they should be at a great loss, upon whose counsel and conduct they chiefly relied.

**V. 2.** {*Captains of bands*} Which were used to come and spoil, ch. 3. 22.

*{a Beerothite*} i.e. Born in Beeroth, a city of Benjamin, Josh. 18. 25. and 9. 17.

*{Beeroth was reckoned to Benjamin*} Though now the Philistims possessed it, the Benjamites after Saul’s discomfiture being fled out of it, 1 Sam. 31. 7.

**V. 3.** {*And the Beerothites fled to Gittaim*} A town of Benjamin Neh. 11. 33. whither they fled for fear of the Philistims. Of which flight mention is here made, that these captains might be known to be Beerothites, though they now sojourned in Gittaim, and their city at this time was inhabited by the Philistims. Others think there were two cities of this name, one in Benjamin, the other in the south frontiers of Judah, whither these Beerothites fled, and there inhabited, liking it so well that they returned not to their own city to the time that this story was written.

**V. 4.** {*And Jonathan Saul’s son had a son that was lame}* The drift of this narration concerning Mephibosheth is to show what induced these two captains to murder Ishbosheth, that they might thereby ingratiate themselves with David; to wit, because there was none remaining of Saul’s house likely to vindicate his death. For Mephibosheth only was left, and he but a child, and lame of his feet; and therefore as he was unlikely to revenge his uncle’s death, being young and impotent, especially when as he could not look otherwise upon him than as an usurper, who had put him by the crown to which he had title by lineal succession; so he was as far unable to hinder David from quiet possession of the kingdom, being a lame child, whom no man much respected. Hereupon they conclude that the taking away of Ishbosheth would be so advantageous to David, that he could not choose, in common humanity, but richly reward the contrivers and actors of it; whereas on the other side, now that Abner was dead, Ishbosheth’s kingdom was in such a declining and tottering condition, that there was no hope of advancement by him, though they should be never so faithful in his service.

*{when the tidings came of Saul*} i.e. Of his overthrow and death, 1 Sam. 31.

*{out of Jezreel*} Where the battle was fought and lost, 1 Sam. 29. 11.

*{and his name was Mephibosheth*} See on chap. 11. 21.

**V. 5.** {*And the sons of Rimmon——went*} i.e. Seeing that David was likely to be king of all Israel, that all things grew worse and worse with Ishbosheth they resolved to slay him, and to this end now came, thinking hereby to gratify David, and to endear their service, presuming of indemnity, because there was none but a lame child to revenge his death.

*{and came about the heat of the day to the house of Ishbosheth, who lay on his bed at noon*} Though there were a common custom in those hot countries to take their ease in the heat of the day, which made them unfit for action and employment, yet it seems to be spoken here in a special manner of Ishbosheth, because he was a dull unactive man, who having put off all business to Abner, gave himself over to sloth and ease: a thing unsuitable for a king at any time, especially for him in his present condition, full of desperate danger, Abner his chief strength being taken away. And therefore it was just that he should die in his sloth who had lived slothfully all his days.

**V. 6.** {*And they came thither into the midst of the house, as though they would have fetched wheat*} Having disguised themselves in the habit of such as came to buy corn, whereof Ishbosheth’s lands (it seems) yielded great store, or of porters, that came to carry away some that had been bought; or else, as it is more probable, being commanders in Ishbosheth’s army, and well acquainted in his house, they came thither under pretense of fetching corn for provision of the camp, or for themselves and the soldiers under their command; and by this color having free access into the chamber where he lay, and finding him asleep upon his bed, they murdered him, and cutting off his head, took it away with them, and carried it to David, expecting some great reward.

*{under the fifth rib*} See on ch. 2. 23.

**V. 7.** {*and gate them away through the plain all night*} Making all possible haste for fear of being pursued and overtaken, they traveled all night from Mahanaim, where they did commit this bloody act, unto Hebron, through the plain between Jericho and Hebron, chap. 2. 29.

**V. 8.** {*Behold the head of Ishbosheth the son of Saul thine enemy which sought thy life*} This speech is cunningly composed. To commend their service in killing Ishbosheth, they put David in mind of Saul’s enmity and bloody persecution, which they in this fact had revenged upon his son.

*{and the Lord hath avenged my Lord the King*} Though this were true in respect of the secret workings of God’s providence, using this as a means of settling David in the quiet and peaceable possession of the whole kingdom, and though it were just with the Lord to bring Ishbosheth to such an end, having usurped the kingdom contrary to God’s revealed will, yet it excused not at all these murderers of their lord, seeing they had no respect to accomplish God’s will, which expressly forbids all treachery and murder, but only aimed at their own private profit and advancement.

**V. 9.** {*As the Lord liveth, who hath redeemed my soul out of all adversity*} i.e. As sure as the Lord lives, who has always hitherto delivered me out of all my troubles by just and lawful means, without the help of any base villainy or wicked treachery, I will inflict upon you, according to your demerits, severe punishment. The which latter part he abruptly suppresses, as it is usual in oaths.

**V. 10.** {*When one told me*} i.e. The Amalekite, chap. 1. 2, &c.

*{saying, Behold, Saul is dead*} Saying withal that upon his request he did help to kill him, and rid him out of his pain, chap. 1. 10.

**V. 11.** {*How much more when wicked men have slain a righteous person?*} i.e. How much better reason have I to execute severe justice on such bloody and treacherous assassins as you, who have murdered one that was just and innocent, though not simply in himself, yet in reference to you, having done you no wrong to provoke you to this horrid act, but contrariwise well deserved at your hands by advancing you to places of command in his army?

*{in his own house, upon his bed*} i.e. In a place where he had reason to think himself most secure and free from danger. By all these circumstances David aggravates their horrible crime, as being in many respects far more heinous than that of the Amalekite, which he had punished with death. He was an Amalekite, and in respect of nation and birth an enemy to God’s people, from whom no better was to be expected, especially by Saul, who not long before had slain almost all their nation; but these were Israelites, and of the same religion with Ishbosheth, and thereby obliged to perform all good duties to their brother; yea, they were of the same tribe with Ishbosheth, and his servants, and therefore bound to be loyal to their master. He did but hasten Saul’s death, being deadly wounded, and out of all hope of recovery, to ease his pain and prevent his shame and dishonor; but these took away Ishbosheth’s life when he was in perfect health and suspected no danger.
