He did it (as he pretended) at Saul’s earnest request, out of mere pity, to free him from torment; but these murdered Ishbosheth in a violent manner against his will, when he was taking his repose, and was at ease. He slew Saul, when he was pursued of his enemies in the field, and had no hope to escape their fury: but these killed Ishbosheth in his own house, sleeping on his bed, and in no danger to be surprised by any other enemies but such treacherous villains as they, who ought to have been his guard to secure his person against the attempts of any other.

*{shall I not therefore now require his blood of your hands?*} i.e. Ought I not much more in justice to punish you for this horrid crime with all severity, seeing neither the example of him that slew Saul, nor the innocency of the person, nor duty to your master, nor reverence of the place, nor time, could deter you from committing such a treacherous and bloody fact?

**V. 12.** {*And they slew them, and cut off their hands and their feet, and hanged them over the pool in Hebron*} They were dismembered of their hands and feet, because these were the special instruments in acting this villainy, their feet in carrying them to it, which were swift to shed blood, and their hands in stabbing him and cutting off his head. The murderer by the letter of the law was appointed only to be put to death; but when the crime was more heinous and horrible, it was in the magistrate’s power to aggravate and increase the punishment, and make the malefactor exemplary, to deter others from the like wickedness. And though as the Jewish writers observe, it was not lawful to lessen the punishment which the law had appointed, as to adjudge one to imprisonment, whipping, loss of goods, instead of death, yet they might add unto it according to the nature of the sin; because God in mercy had appointed the least degrees of punishment which in justice could be required. Now these hands and feet David caused to be hanged over the pool of Hebron, (their bodies, it is likely, after sun-setting being taken down according to the law, Deut. 21. 23.) both to be a public spectacle and example to others, and to show his detestation of such treacherous villainy; that so it might appear to all the world that he was clear of having any hand in this bloody fact, either by conniving it, or giving any encouragement to the actors of it.

*{but they took the head of Ishbosheth, and buried it in the sepulchre of Abner in Hebron*} Honoring him, though an enemy, with an honorable burial in that city where the great patriarchs did lie interred; and making them partners in the same sepulcher who had been partakers in the same cause, and had both alike fallen by treacherous hands.

## CHAPTER 5

**Verse 1.** {*Then came all the tribes of Israel to David unto Hebron*} i.e. Not only the elders and chief of the tribes, as v. 3. but with them their tribunes, captains and commanders, with a great army gathered together out of all Israel, armed and well appointed for the war, as it is specially expressed 1 Chr. 12. 23. where the number of the several tribes that came to David at Hebron, being summed up together, amounts to above three hundred and forty thousand. All which continued three days with him, feasting and rejoicing at his inauguration and coronation, their brethren of the tribe of Judah dwelling at Hebron, and all the country adjoining, having made liberal provision for their entertainment, with the help also of the tribes of Issachar, Zebulun and Naphtali, who were near unto them, and brought in likewise their provisions, 1 Chr. 12. 39, 40. This feast being ended, David takes the opportunity of this great assembly, before he dismissed them, to assault the Jebusites’ strong fort, which was thought impregnable, and therefore was never attempted hitherto by any of the judges, Samuel, or Saul. Now this free coming in of all the tribes, after their long reluctancy and opposition, and their peaceable submission of all nations, Jews and Gentiles, called and gathered by the preaching of the gospel to the kingdom of Christ, and his gracious acceptance of them as his subjects, notwithstanding all their former reluctancy in the times of their ignorance, according to Psal. 110. 3.

*{Behold, we are thy bone, and thy flesh*} i.e. All of us Israelites, both you and we, descended from the same father, Jacob, and therefore all brethren. This they allege, not only as an argument to move David to receive them graciously, as his subjects, notwithstanding their former oppositions, seeing they were all brethren; but also and chiefly, to show upon what ground they chose him to be their king, namely, because he was an Israelite, and so one of their brethren, from amongst whom they were bound by God’s law to choose their king, Deut. 17. 15. And herein also David did typify Christ our king, who is head of that body, whereof we are members, *bone of our bone, and flesh of our flesh*, Ephes. 5. 30. 1 Cor. 12. 12. Heb. 2. 14–18. See Gen. 9. 14.

**V. 2.** {*Also in time past, when Saul was King over us, thou wast he that leddest out and broughtest in Israel*} i.e. You then were our chief captain, that did lead out our armies against our enemies, and did bring them back, crowned with victory and laden with spoil: therefore as by the former benefits received by your prudent and valiant conduct we are much obliged unto you, so having had so much experience of your parts, prowess and prosperous success in all your enterprises, we cannot think any so fit to be our king, who are not only prudent to govern in time of peace, but also expert in war, and every way able to lead an army, Num. 27. 17.

*{and the Lord said to thee, Thou shalt feed my people Israel*} i.e. Has already anointed you by Samuel to be king over all Israel, and to be both as a good shepherd to provide food for them, and to guide and govern them in time of peace, and as a good captain, to go out and in before them in time of war, 1 Sam. 16. 11, 12. Psal. 78. 71. In both which respects also he was a type of Christ, Psal. 2. 6. Joh. 10. 3, 4, 10, 11.

**V. 3.** {*made a league with them in Hebron before the Lord*} Namely, that he would, as a good king, govern them according to the prescript rule of God’s law, and that they would obey him, as his loyal and liege people. Hereunto both parties bound themselves by solemn covenants, confirmed by a sacred oath, calling God to witness, and invocating his name for a blessing on their proceedings. In which respect it is said that they *made this league before the Lord*. See on Judg. 11. 11.

*{And they anointed David King over Israel*} This was his third anointing: see on ch. 2. 4. And herein also David was a type of Christ, who began to execute his kingly office, like David, at the age of thirty years, and was thrice anointed by God, and, as it were, proclaimed king over his church: first by the secret unction of his Holy Spirit, Psal. 45. 7. and 2. 6, 7. Secondly, at his baptism, by a voice from heaven, Matth. 3. 16, 17. And thirdly, at his transfiguration, Matth. 17. 5.

**V. 5.** {*In Hebron he reigned over Judah seven years and six months}* See ch. 2. 11.

*{and in Jerusalem he reigned thirty and three years over all Israel and Judah*} The last year being begun, but not finished. But he reigned precisely but thirty and two years and six months; but the last year, though not complete but current, is added, to make up the number round and full, as is usual in the Scriptures, Judg. 20. 46. See 1 Kings 2. 11.

**V. 6.** {*And the King and his men*} Having such a great number of the choice and chief men of his kingdom assembled, he took this opportunity to assault and vanquish the Jebusites in their strong fort.

*{went to Jerusalem unto the Jebusites*} Jerusalem was before called *Jebus*, Judg. 19. 10. and *Salem*, Gen. 14. 18. Psal. 76. 2. It was situate on the uttermost borders of two tribes, Judah and Benjamin. One part of it fell to the lot of Judah, called Shalem; which they conquered and inhabited, Judg. 1. 8. The other, standing on the north side, afterwards called *the fort of Sion*, and exceeding strong, fell to Benjamin’s portion; but they not being able to vanquish it, though assisted by Judah, Josh. 15. 53. the Jebusites did still inhabit it, Judg. 1. 23. and 19. 10, 11. even to this present, till David assaulted and took it. In this part was the hill Moriah, where Abraham being appointed to sacrifice his son, was afterwards inhibited, the Lord having provided him another offering: and from thence it seems this city had its name, being divided from *Jireh*, *God will be seen in the mount*, and *Shalem* signifying *peace*, both conjoined in *Jerusalem*, *The vision of peace*. This fort David at the very entrance into the rule of the whole people first attempted, intending to make it the metropolis of the kingdom where the temple was afterwards to be built, on the hill Moriah; and the rather because Melchizedek *the Priest of the most high God* had dwelt and reigned there, Heb. 7. 1. And it was also the most commodious place to be the chief seat of the kingdom, being situate in the midst of the land, and in his tribe whom they had chosen to be their king.

*{which spake unto David, saying, Except thou take away the blind and the lame, thou shalt not come in hither*} These words, and the verses next following, being obscure, intricate and very perplexed, are diversely understood by expositors. But letting pass the most of them, I will only produce those which seem most probable. Some understand them as a proud and scornful brag of the Jebusites, who presumed their fort was so strong and impregnable that though they had none to defend it but impotent men, and such as were blind and lame, yet it would be in vain, and but mere lost labor, for David and his army to attempt the assaulting of it, and out of this confidence by way of contempt and derision, they set only such to guard it. Others think that by *the blind and lame* they meant their idols and images, in which they put their trust, as their tutelary gods, and whom they thought sufficient, being placed there, to make good the place against all the power of Israel without any other help. Now these they called *the blind and lame*, not out of that opinion which themselves had of them, who highly esteemed them as their patrons and protectors, but as they were upbraided by the Israelites, who thus conceived of them; q.d. Though you have such a base opinion of our gods, and account them blind and lame idols, yet they being placed on this impregnable fort, you shall find, that if you dare attempt the surprising of it, you shall never be able to do it, though you found no other resistance.

**V. 7.** {*Nevertheless David took the strong hold of Zion. The same is the City of David}* i.e. Notwithstanding all their confidence, expressed in their proud brags and scorns, David caused this their stronghold, in which they put their trust, to be assaulted and taken, and called it *the city of David*. The manner of the surprise is expressed in the next verse.

**V. 8.** {*and David said on that day, Whosoever getteth up to the gutter*} Or thus, *For David said*. Here the manner how, and the means whereby, are expressed, of taking the stronghold: namely David’s plotting and contriving the best and readiest way, which was to scale the walls, and get into the gutter, and kill the Jebusites, with the lame and blind, who were placed there by way of scorn to man and defend it; and II. Joab’s executing of his design, by first entering into the fort, and slaying all there: which he undertook, partly moved thereunto by his desire of recovering David’s favor, which he had lost by his killing of Abner, and partly encouraged by the reward promised, of being made, for this service, general of the army; which is here only intimated in general terms, but plainly and particularly expressed 1 Chron. 11. 6.

*{and the lame and the blind, that are hated of David’s soul}* If we understand this literally, it is not simply meant that David hated the lame and blind because of their imperfections, who ought rather to be pitied and relieved, but respectively, as they were by the Jebusites in way of scorn and derision presented unto him to disgrace and dishonor him and his whole army. But if we take it in a symbolical sense, to be meant of the idols and images, which have eyes, and see not, and feet, and walk not, then it is to be understood absolutely, that they were hated of David’s soul.

*{Wherefore they said, The blind and the lame shall not come into the house*} Or, *Because they had said, Even the blind and the lame, He shall not come into the house*. On this occasion they took it up as a common proverb, saying, *The blind and the lame shall not come into the house*, q.d. The blind and lame were but bad guardians of this place in the time of the Jebusites, therefore such shall be entrusted no more, but abler men employed to defend it. By *house*, some understand, and most probably, the royal house or palace of David, from entering whereof the blind and lame were interdicted, not for pride and state, as the Persian kings would have none enter their courts in sackcloth, because they would not offend their eyes with beholding mournful objects, Esth. 4. 2. but that it might be a perpetual memorial of this victory, to have the blind and lame secluded from entering the king’s house, because they were used by the Jebusites as instruments to put such a foul scorn upon him. Neither can it hinder us from taking the words in this sense, that Mephibosheth, though lame, was admitted to sit at the king’s table, seeing no rule is so general, as that it may not have some one exception. And the presence of Mephibosheth had another use, namely, to put David in mind of his dearly loving Jonathan, and to be unto him a cause of joy and content, when by showing kindness to the son, he expressed his love and thankfulness to his deceased father. But they who would have the blind and lame idols to be here understood, think that hereby is meant that idols and images should be wholly excluded both out of the king’s house, and God’s. But I see no reason why that should need a new interdiction in remembrance of this victory, seeing always before they were forbidden to be received into either of these places, on any other. Now as this proverb was taken up among the people for a memorial of this great victory, for which they had always cause to praise God, so it was applied according to special occasions; as when they would intimate that a thing seeming impossible might come to pass, as was the taking of this impregnable tower: or, to reprove such as were proud and presumptuous of their own strength, either in respect of place and fortifications, or of the arm of flesh, by telling them they might be deceived as well as the Jebusites: or, to give men warning that they commit not the like error with them, presuming too much of their own strength, lest they fail in their hopes as they did; seeing oftentimes what begins in confidence ends in shame; and that they take heed lest that befall themselves which they intend to others; as the Jebusites thinking to make sure that David should never enter their fort, were themselves expelled, and never reentered. Others read these words, as we have them in our margin, *because they had said, &c.* i.e. David promises this reward to him that should take away the blind and lame, because they had put this scorn upon him.

**V. 9.** {*And David built round about, from Millo and inward*} Millo was a deep and broad valley or ditch, dividing Sion from the lower city; from whence David raised his wall of defense about Sion. See 1 King. 9. 15. Others think it was the town-house in the city of David, where the people had their solemn assemblies, so called from *filling*, *replenishing*. Others, and very probably, think it was some tower and place of munition belonging to the city, because it is said that *Hezekiah repaired Millo in the City of David, and made darts and shields in abundance*, 2 Chr. 32. 5. A place of the same name and for the like use we read of Judg. 9. 6, 20, 46. From this tower or fortress David is said to have built the city *round about, even from Millo and inward*; and Joab repaired the rest of the city that was without. Compare this place with 1 Chron. 11. 8.

**V. 10.** {*went on and grew great*} Heb. *went going and growing*. i.e. Prospered in all his attempts, because *the Lord* by his special favor *was with him*, and assisted him.

**V. 11.** {*And Hiram King of Tyre*} Tyre was a royal, potent and famous city of merchandise, situate on and in the sea, by the west limits of Asher, Josh. 19. 19. It was built by the Phoenicians, who had formerly dwelt by the Red Sea, but being driven thence by earthquakes, frequent in those countries, and other calamities, they transplanted themselves into this island, and built a city there at first on a low ground which was of little note, for fishermen to dwell in; but afterwards on a promontory, which became stately and famous, sending out of it divers much ennobled colonies, into Carthage, Leptis, Utica, and the islands of Gades. See on Isa. 23. 1. and Amos 1. 9.

*{sent messengers to David*} Hearing of his fame and virtues, and his growth and greatness in glory, and being much taken therewithal, he sent ambassadors unto him, to congratulate his happy settlement in his kingdom, thereby showing his great respect and kindness towards him.

*{and cedar trees, and Carpenters, and Masons}* i.e. After Hiram’s messengers had been with David, and were returned to their master, and had acquainted him with David’s suit unto him, that he would accommodate him with men and materials for the building of him a house: seeing there was the greatest plenty of the choicest cedars in Lebanon, which for the most part was situate in Hiram’s country, and most excellent artisans both in wood and stone at Tyre above any other place; according to his request Hiram sent unto him.

**V. 12.** {*And David perceived that the Lord had established him*} i.e. By God’s blessing and prospering him in all his affairs, he experimentally found the truth of his promises concerning his settlement in the kingdom.

**V. 13.** {*And David took him more Concubines and wives}* This it seems he did to multiply his friends and allies, for the strengthening of him in his kingdom. But herein he showed much human frailty and infirmity, in using that as a means to establish him, which God had expressly forbidden, Deut. 17. 7. Concerning concubines, see on Judg. 19. 1, 2. 1 Chron. 3. 5.

**V. 14.** {*Shammua*} Called also Shimea, 1 Chr. 3. 5.

**V. 15.** {*Elishua*} Or, *Elishama*, 1 Chron. 3. 6.

**V. 16.** {*And Eliada*} Called Beeliada, 1 Chr. 14. 7.

**V. 17.** {*But when the Philistines heard that they had anointed David King over Israel}* 1 Chron. 11. 16. and 14. 8. i.e. When they understood that there was a cessation of the civil war between the houses of Saul and David, and that the whole kingdom was now settled upon David, and he peaceably enjoyed it; and it may be hearing also that he had vanquished the Jebusites, and taken their strong fort; and that Hiram, a rich king of a neighbor nation, was in league and friendship with him: they became jealous of his greatness, and thought it high time to look unto him, and keep him down, seeing they were now out of that hope in which they lately were whilst the civil wars lasted, that the Israelites would destroy one another, and so save them a labor, and not put them to the cost and hazard of war. And perhaps they did all that while forbear to take up arms against them, lest a foreign enemy invading their country, they should make peace one with another, and join together with united forces to keep off a common danger, which otherwise would involve both parties in a universal ruin.

*{All the Philistines}* This came to pass by God’s good providence, so disposing of it for preserving David’s honor, and to keep his name from the brand and blemish of inhumanity and ingratitude. For the Philistims having in the time of his trouble and exile given him kind and courteous entertainment, aspersions of that nature would have been cast upon him, if he had raised war against them before they had first pressed and provoked him unto it.

*{all the Philistines came up to seek David}* To wit, that they might fight with him, before he were thoroughly settled in his kingdom, lest he should grow too strong for them, if they let him alone.

*{and David heard of it, and went down to the hold*} Not to the fort of Zion, as some conceive: for that standing on a high hill, he should not have been said *to have gone down*, but rather *to have gone up*, or *ascend*, unto it. But either it was the stronghold of Adullam, or some other near unto it; unto which he resorted, that he and his camp might be safe and free from the attempts of his enemies, till he had direction from God what to do.

**V. 18.** {*in the valley of Rephaim*} i.e. *Of Giants*, Gen. 14. 5. situate on the north limits of Judah, in Benjamin, not far from Jerusalem, and lying westward from it, Josh. 15. 8. See on Isa. 17. 5.

**V. 19.** {*And David enquired of the Lord*} Namely, by Urim and Thummim, Num. 27. 21. See ch. 2. 1. 1 Sam. 23. 9. Though he had a great army, and had been always victorious, yet he relied not on his own strength, but seeks to God for direction, and without it would do nothing.

**V. 20.** {*And David came to Baal-Perazim*} So called by anticipation, on this occasion.

*{The Lord hath broken forth upon my enemies before me, as the breach of waters*} This may either be referred to his enemies, which were dispersed like weak waters, that are easily divided with the foot or finger, or being shed on the ground are not easily recollected; or to God’s powerful scattering them, like waters suddenly breaking their banks and bounds, which violently sweep all away that lie in their passage.

*{Therefore he called the name of that place Baal-Perazim*} Or, *The plain of breaches*, Isa. 28. 21.

**V. 21.** {*And there they left their images*} They had brought them with them, as their chief patrons and protectors, in whom they hoped for victory: but failing thereof, and being put to flight, they left them there, either out of fear, flying for their lives; or out of neglect, because they had given them no better success.

*{burnt them*} According to God’s law, Deut. 7. 25. and by David’s commandment, 1 Chr. 14. 12.

**V. 22.** {*And the Philistines came up yet again}* Though a little before they had been beaten, put to flight, and slaughtered, yet such was their inveterate malice against the Israelites, that they recruited their scattered army, and came again to provoke them to fight in the very same place where they had received their former defeat, though David had not incensed them by pursuing his victory.

**V. 23.** {*And when David enquired of the Lord*} Though David had lately gotten a famous victory over his enemies, yet he is not thereby lifted up into an opinion of his own strength, but still relies wholly upon the Lord, and now again seeks unto him for direction.

*{Thou shalt not go up*} i.e. In an open manner, as before, but fetch a compass behind them secretly; that coming upon them suddenly and unexpected, their hearts might be smitten with the greater fear and amazement.

**V. 24.** {*And let it be when thou hearest the sound of a going in the tops of the mulberry trees}* Not an ordinary sound caused by the winds, but as if it were of an army marching or going on the tops of the trees; and, as it is probable, a noise of chariots and horsemen, like that 2 King. 7. 6. which was no other than the Lord’s host of angels sent to destroy the Philistims. And this sign was given unto David to encourage him to the fight, and assure him of victory, seeing God and his angels were present to assist him. The same noise perhaps was also heard of the Philistims, as that other by the Syrians, whereby being terrified they were put to flight.

*{for then shall the Lord go out*} Or, *then is the Lord gone out*.

**V. 25.** {*from Geba*} Called also *Gibeah in Benjamin*, Judg. 19. 13. and 20. 10. and *Gibeon* 1 Chr. 14. 16.

*{until thou come to Gazer}* Situate in the west of Ephraim, Josh. 16. 10. but then possessed by the Philistims.

## CHAPTER 6

**Verse 1.** {*Again David gathered together all the chosen men of Israel*} In the first book of the Chronicles, first the fetching of the ark is set down ch. 13. and the victories over the Philistims ch. 14. Wherefore some have thought that here is some disorder and transposition in the story. But if we understand it aright, the just order is here observed: David having first vanquished his enemies, and settled peace, and does in the next place take order for promoting and setting up of religion; and first of all, for the fetching of the ark from the house of Abinadab to the tent he had prepared for it. And for the reconciling of that in the Chronicles with this place, in respect of the order of things there related differing from this here, we are to know that David did twice assemble the chief of Israel that he might consult with them about fetching home the ark: first, when he had taken the Jebusites’ fort; which consultation was interrupted by the Philistims’ invasion: and they being overthrown and put to flight, he called them the second time to consult about the same business; and therefore it is plainly said in this verse, that *again he gathered Israel*. And both these gatherings of Israel are expressed 1 Chron. 13. 1, 5. The first was of *chosen men*, officers, colonels and captains; the other, *of all Israel, from Shihor in Egypt, even to the entring of Hamath*, that is the whole land from south to north, Josh. 13. 3. Now if it be further objected that the doubt is not quite cleared, because all that is said to be done here, to v. 10. is in the Chronicles set down, as done before the battle with the Philistims; to this it is answered, That mention being made there of that consultation which David had before the battle, that narration is finished together, notwithstanding other things intervened, as it is usual in the Scripture, which more respects the coherence of the matter related than the order of times.

**V. 2.** {*with all the people that were with him*} Not only the elders and magistrates, but also the colonels, captains and soldiers, which he summoned to appear and join with him in so great a number from all parts of the land, both that the action might be done with general consent and more solemnity, and also that they might have sufficient strength at hand to bear them out, if there should happen any attempt to disturb and hinder their proceedings.

*{from Baal of Judah*} i.e. Kirjath-jearim, 1 Chr. 13. 6. called also Kirjath-baal, Josh. 15. 9. and Baalah of Judah. Here the ark was placed after it was brought from Beth-shemesh, in the house of Abinadab, 1 Sam. 7. 1. and had there ever since continued, above forty years. And from hence David now fetched it; because perhaps it was not so much respected, being in a private house, as it ought to be; and brought it to Jerusalem, because it was the chief city in the kingdom, and also situate in the heart of the land, and so more convenient for the people to resort unto from all parts for divine worship, and to consult with God in all difficult and important cases; and because also, it being the regal seat where himself resided, he might frequently and with more ease enjoy the benefit of the ark upon all occasions.

*{whose name is called by the name of the Lord of hosts*} Or, *at which the Name, even the name of the Lord of hosts, was called upon*. i.e. *Whose name is called on it*, 1 Chr. 13. 6. Or, *at which the name of the Lord was called upon*; to wit, when by the high priest they consulted with him, 1 king. 8. 29.

*{that dwelleth between the cherubims*} He is said to sit between the cherubims, though he be everywhere present, because he was there in a special manner by the presence of his grace; from thence delivering his oracles when he was consulted with, as Num. 7. 89. Exod. 25. 22.

**V. 3.** {*And they set the Ark of God upon a new cart*} To wit, that they might save themselves a labor in carrying it so far upon their shoulders as from Kirjath-jearim to Jerusalem; propounding the example of the Philistims as their precedent, who had done it before without any damage. But herein they transgressed the express law of God, which enjoined the Kohathites to carry it upon their shoulders, though they might not touch it, upon pain of death, Num. 4. 15. And though the act of the Philistims was not punished, they being heathens, and ignorant of God’s law, which was not given to them; yet being done by the Levites, to whom the law was given, and who knew, or ought to have known and also have taught others all that was contained in it, it was a great sin in them, and very displeasing to God, and accordingly severely punished. And this David himself, being taught better by lamentable experience, acknowledges to have been the cause of that severe judgment executed upon Uzzah, though there were another cause concurring with it, to wit, his touching it with his hand, being only a Levite, and no priest. Compare 1 Chr. 15. 12, 13. with v. 6, 7. of this chapter.

*{that was in Gibeah*} Or, *the hill* not in Gibeah the city so called properly; but appellatively, *upon an high place*, which was in or by Kirjath-jearim. See 1 Sam. 7. 1.

**V. 4.** {*And Ahio went before the Ark*} Namely, to drive the oxen, while Uzzah came behind, or by the side of the ark; as appears by his putting forth his hand to stay it when it was shaken.

**V. 5.** {*And David, and all the house of Israel played*} As they used to do at divine services and solemnities in the time of the law, that thereby they might express their joy and cheerfulness in holy duties, or stir up their hearts if dull and drooping, to more alacrity, 2 kings 3. 15.

*{before the Lord*} i.e. The ark, the visible sign of God’s presence.

**V. 6.** {*to Nachon’s threshingfloor}* Called Chidon 1 Chr. 13. 9.

*{and took hold of it: for the oxen shook it*} Or, *stumbled*, 1 Chr. 13. 9. i.e. By their stumbling they so shook the ark that it was in danger of falling, as Uzzah thought, and therefore he put forth his hand to stay it. So the word is taken 2 king. 9. 33. Thus God was pleased by the stumbling of these dumb beasts to convince the Levites of their error, as he did by an ass reprove Balaam for his sin.

**V. 7.** {*And the anger of the Lord was kindled against Uzzah, &c.*} Because he caused his holy ark to be carried in a cart, which they should have borne on their shoulders; and for touching it with his hand, being but a Levite, and no priest. See on Deut. 31. 9.

*{and God smote him there*} To wit, by a violent death; and that (as some think) by a rent or rupture in his body, which gave the name to the place, Perez-Uzzah, v. 8.

*{for his error}* Or, *rashness*. i.e. His sin in touching holy things, Num. 4. 15. Now this was his own sin, peculiar to himself; but that of carrying the ark in a cart was common to all the rest, v. 3. And here it pleased God to make Uzzah an example of his severe justice against all that swerve aside from the rule of his word in the least things that concern his worship, and follow their own wills and inventions, though they have never so good a pretense for it. The like we see in the example of Nadab and Abihu, struck with present death for *offering strange fire before the Lord, which he commanded them not*, Lev. 10. 1.

**V. 8.** {*And David was displeased*} i.e. Much grieved and disquieted in his mind both in respect of Uzzah’s sin and punishment. Yea, considering human frailty, which is apt to slight and extenuate sin, and to amplify and aggravate the judgments of God inflicted for it, it may be probably thought that with David’s sorrow there was mixed some pettishness and peevishness, impatience and discontent, as though this punishment were not only too severe for a small error and oversight committed out of a good intention, but now also unseasonable, when they were acting such a pious work; seeing thereby it was discountenanced and wholly interrupted, and so all the joy of the people turned into grief and lamentation.

*{Perez-Uzzah*} That is, *The breach of Uzzah*.

*{to this day*} To wit, when this story was written; or, for a long time.

**V. 9.** {*And David was afraid of the Lord that day*} Because of this severe judgment he feared to proceed any further in the action, lest himself through some error might miscarry and perish in it.

*{How shall the Ark of the Lord come to me?*} i.e. How shall I with any safety go on in this action, seeing the Lord is not pleased (as it seems) that I should carry the ark to the tent I have prepared for it? Therefore it were better for me to desist, than proceed with so much danger.

**V. 10.** {*So David would not remove the Ark of the Lord unto him*} Herein David discovered great infirmity. For either he had thus far proceeded of his own head, (as afterwards he did in resolving to build the temple) having no warrant from God; and then, though his zeal and good intention were commendable, yet the action itself was not, being not done in faith, but without ground: or else he had command from God at this time to remove the ark to Jerusalem; and then it was his fault thus to desist upon this discouragement. He should rather have considered, that though the action were good, there had been some failing in the manner of doing it; and so finding out and reforming what was amiss, he should have proceeded, having God’s word and warrant for what he did.

*{But David carried it aside into the house of Obed Edom*} Not forcing the ark upon him against his will, that he might so put off the danger from himself, but having, no doubt, his consent and approbation. For though Obed-edom knew what had befallen the Philistims and Bethshemites whilst the ark was among them, and what had happened to Uzzah also at this time, yet, being a wise and holy man, he did not impute these disasters to the having of the ark, which was a gracious sign of God’s presence, but to their miscarriages and abuses, their want of due reverence, and other faultiness. And being resolved that there could come no ill, but much good, by entertaining it, if abuses and irreverence were avoided, as he purposed they should be, he does willingly and cheerfully give it entertainment.

*{the Gittite*} Not by nation, (for he was an Israelite, of the tribe of Levi, and one of the porters and singers, 1 Chr. 15. 18, 21.) but he is so called because he was of Gath-Rimmon, a city of the Philistims near Gath, appropriated to the Levites, Josh. 21. 24, 25. Here it seems he was born. Some think he was a proselyte, and by nation a Philistine; as ch. 15. 18, 19.

**V. 11.** {*blessed Obed-Edom and all his household}* i.e. Caused him and all that he had to prosper; thereby showing that they shall be no losers, but great gainers, that give him, or any that belong unto him, due entertainment: as we see also in the examples of Potiphar, and the jailer, entertaining Joseph; Laban, Jacob; the widow-woman, Elijah; the Shunammite, Elisha; and Zaccheus, Christ.

**V. 12.** {*So David went and brought up the Ark of God*} To wit, by the ministry of the priests and Levites. Having found by experience that there was no danger in removing and entertaining the ark, but only in the miscarriages about it, which he now purposed to reform, he sets afresh upon the action, and takes order to have it brought from Obed-Edom’s house to Jerusalem with all solemnity, appointing the priests and Levites to their several charges, and how they should demean themselves in them, as is more fully expressed, 1 Chron. 15. & 16.

**V. 13.** {*When they that bare the Ark of the Lord had gone six paces, he sacrificed oxen and fatlings*} i.e. He took order to have them sacrificed by the priests upon an altar erected for this service. See 1 Chr. 15. 26. And this he did to testify his thankfulness unto God for blessing their beginning and proceeding hitherto, and to move him to continue his favor. See on 1 Sam. 9. 12.

**V. 14.** {*And David danced before the Lord with all his might*} Not vainly and carnally; but being excited with inward joy, he expressed it by outward gestures and motions in a grave and sober manner and measure. And it was usual with God’s servants in those times so to express their spiritual ravishments of rejoicing for some special favor received. So Miriam, Exod. 15. 20. and Jephthah’s daughter, Judg. 11. 34.

*{And David was girded with a linen Ephod*} i.e. An upper garment of linen in fashion like that of the priests, which he used at this time, not only as being more light and fit for motion, but also to show his devotion in this religious service. See on 1 Sam. 2. 18.

**V. 16.** {*she despised him in her heart*} Looking upon David with carnal eyes and a carnal heart, she judged also carnally of his holy actions and intentions, and so vilified his person and deportment.

**V. 17.** {*And they brought in the Ark of the Lord and set it in his place*} To wit, which David had prepared for it before he went to fetch it, 1 Chron. 15. 1. All which was done by God’s special inspiration and direction, that it might continue there till a temple were built for it; which being unlikely to be done, David had now prepared for it, not a permanent building, but a tabernacle or tent. As for the tabernacle which Moses made, at their coming into Canaan, it was first erected at Shiloh, Josh. 18. 1. and there it long continued, 1 Sam. 1. 3. & 14. 3. After, it was set at Nob, 1 Sam. 21. At this time it was at Gibeon, 1 Chron. 16. 39. where it remained till Solomon’s reign, 2 Chron. 1. 3, 4. & 1 Chro. 21. 29. and then all the holy things were brought from thence, and placed in the temple, 1 Kin. 8. 4. See the notes there.

**V. 18.** {*he blessed the people*} 1 Chro. 16. 2. i.e. As a godly king and holy prophet, he prayed for their peace and prosperity, both spiritual and temporal.

**V. 19.** {*And he dealt among all the people——to every one a cake of bread, &c.*} He does not only give them his benediction, but bountifully rewards them. Thus he is not said to have done at his coronation, but now, when he had brought home the ark; to show that he more rejoiced in God’s coming to him, than in his own coming to the crown and kingdom.

**V. 20.** {*Then David returned to bless his household}* i.e. To pray with and for his family, as he had done for the people; as thinking it not enough as a king, to care for the commonwealth, unless also he performed the duty of a good governor in his own house.

*{And Michal the daughter of Saul came out to meet David*} Being no longer able to contain the proud swelling thoughts of her heart, she hasted out to ease and empty her mind by venting bitter and scornful reproaches against him. She is called here, *the daughter of Saul*, because she resembled her father’s disposition, who was a proud hypocrite, neglecting God’s service when it did not promote his own greatness and glory; contrary to David’s practice in this place.

*{How glorious was the King of Israel to day*} She speaks this ironically, by way of derision and contempt, implying the contrary, that his carriage was loose and unbeseeming such a king as the king of Israel. q.d. You did this day behave yourself as one of the vain and light common people, and not as did become the state and gravity of a king. And it may be she had respect not only to his dancing, but also to his habit, being clothed like one of the Levites (of whom she had but a mean opinion) in a linen ephod. See on Gen. 9. 21.

*{who uncovered himself in the eyes of the handmaids}* Her meaning may be, either that he used open immodesty in his dancing, without any respect to his regal majesty, or modesty, his garments, which hung loose upon him flying open, and discovering some bare parts of his legs or thighs. And this is not unlikely to have been her intention, seeing she terms it most imperiously and reproachfully as she could, saying, *He uncovered himself shamelesly, as one of the vain fellows*, and that *in the eyes of the hand-maids of his servants*, who jeered and scorned him for it. Or else, though no part of his body in truth appeared bare, yet she conceived he exposed himself to be derided as a naked man, whilst he discovered his vanity and lightness to all the beholders, like a fool, that puts off his clothes, and shows his nakedness, to make others sport.

**V. 21.** {*And David said to Michal, It was before the Lord*} i.e. I did thus demean myself in God’s presence, and to his glory, that by this abasement to myself, I might show my thankfulness unto him, and the joy of mine heart in doing him service.

*{which chose me before thy father, and before all his house*} Here David shows great displeasure in his reply, upbraiding Michal with her father’s rejection for his sins, and the miseries of the whole family that did follow upon it: but her insolent reproaches extorted it from him, contrary to his sweet nature; and the rather, because she cast upon him these disgraces and scorns for showing his zeal for God’s glory, and faithfulness in his service; which in some sort trenched upon God himself.

**V. 22.** {*And I will yet be more vile than thus}* i.e. I am so far from repenting of that I have done, that I will be ready still to abase myself, when as I may thereby glorify God.

*{of them shall I be had in honour*} i.e. They can lose no honor that become vile in their own eyes to glorify God, seeing he has in his hand the hearts of all men, and has promised that *he will honour them that honour him*, 1 Sam. 2. 30.

**V. 23.** {*Therefore Michal——had no child}* Though she hitherto had no children that we read of, yet she might by God’s blessing obtained by David’s prayers have afterwards become fruitful, Gen. 20. 17. & 25. 21. whereas now she is punished with perpetual barrenness for deriding her husband: for *she had no childe to the day of her death*, i.e. she never had any; as this phrase is taken 1 Sam. 15. 35. Mat. 1. 25. Gen. 8. 7.

## CHAPTER 7

**Verse 1.** {*And it came to pass}* Compare this whole chapter with 1 Chron. 17.

*{when the King sat in his house}* To wit, in peace and safety.

*{and the Lord had given him rest round about from all his enemies*} This may be understood either of the time present, that he then lived in peace when he spoke and did that which followed, being not molested by any bordering enemies; or, that now he lived in such a peace as was not interrupted by the invasions of neighboring nations, as formerly. And from the time of his second victory over the Philistims, both they, and all other people round about, were so overawed by his prosperous success that they durst not make war against him; though contrariwise he after this fought many battles against the Ammonites, Syrians, Moabites and other nations, and obtained many victories. Or it may be understood of his quiet possession and settling of his kingdom without any opposition.

**V. 2.** {*That the King said to Nathan the Prophet*} No sooner is he peaceably settled in his kingdom than he takes care for promoting true religion; and to this end he sends for Nathan a holy prophet, to advise with him about some special means tending thereunto, as the building of the temple, and settling the ark in it.

*{See now, I dwell in an house of Cedar, but the Ark of God*} q.d. It is a most unfitting thing that I should dwell in a more fair, costly and settled habitation than the ark of God, the visible sign of his glorious presence. See Hag. 1. 4. Whilst the Israelites were in the wilderness in a flitting condition, it was fit the public place of God’s service should not be fixed and permanent, but a tent or tabernacle, which might be removed with them: but now they were settled in Canaan, David thought it more suitable to their present estate, that the house allotted to God’s service should no longer be a movable tabernacle, but a standing temple; especially considering that the Lord had promised, that when they were come into the land of their inheritance, he would choose out a place for them *to cause his name to dwell there, &c.* Deut. 12. 10, 11, 14. And therefore in zeal to God’s glory, he desires to build a temple for his public worship. Yet though he were a wise king, yea, and a pious prophet, he did not in this weighty matter wholly rely upon his own counsel, but before he would attempt anything, he consulted with Nathan the prophet of the Lord about it; by his example teaching kings and princes what they ought to do in the like case.

*{dwelleth in curtains*} i.e. In a tabernacle covered with skins and curtains.

**V. 3.** {*And Nathan said to the King, Go*} This he spoke as a friend and counselor, out of his private opinion, and not by revelation from God; which he should have waited for in a matter of such importance, before he gave his sentence. Hereby it appears that the prophets did not always speak as prophets, having upon them the spirit of prophecy, but sometimes as private men; as we see also in the example of Samuel, 1 Sam. 16. 6. and Elisha, 2 king. 4. 27. Yea, sometimes they spoke like frail men, unadvisedly and sinfully, as Moses Psal. 106. 33. and Jeremiah, Jer. 20. 14. See on Gen. 37. 9.

*{Do all that is in thy heart: for the Lord is with thee*} i.e. Build a house to the Lord, as you desire: and he that has moved you to do it, will prosper your work.

**V. 4.** {*And it came to pass, that night, that the word of the Lord came to Nathan}* God delayed not to bring David out of his error, both because it proceeded from simple ignorance, and out of a good intention; and also because, having encouragement from Nathan, he was so zealous and serious to have his desire presently acted; that as some probably conjecture, he bound himself by a solemn vow that he would never be at rest till he had set himself upon the accomplishment of it, Psal. 132. 2, 3, 4. But others expound that place of his bringing the ark into that tent which he had pitched for it. Howsoever, God was able to dispense with such an oath as was not agreeable to his will, much more than the father or husband had power to disannul the vow of his daughter, or wife, Num. 30. 5, 8.

**V. 5.** {*Go, and tell my servant David,——shalt thou build me an house for me to dwell in?*} i.e. Assuredly you shall not do it. Because God had promised he would have such a house built when the people were settled in the land of promise, Deut. 12. 10, 11. David thought that now the time was come, and that he was the man designed to the work: but God by his prophet tells him, that though he rightly guessed at the thing, yet he was mistaken in the man who must effect it. It was not he, but his son, that was appointed to do it. And though his pious intention was very commendable, and God would not only graciously accept it, 1 king. 8. 18. but also bountifully reward it, as if he had done it, ver. 10, 11, &c. yet it was not his pleasure to use him for the effecting of this great and glorious work, for divers reasons; some whereof are here expressed, and others to be gathered out of the books of the kings and Chronicles. As first, Because, being a martial man, he had shed much blood. This reason God renders unto him, why he would not use him in this service, 1 Chro. 28. 3. not because it was unlawful for him to fight the battles of the Lord, and to shed the blood of his enemies; or because, having shed it, he was thereby defiled; for this was acceptable and pleasing to God, as a sacrifice in which he delighted, Isa. 34. 6. Jer. 46. 10. but the building of the temple being a type of the spiritual building of the church by Christ the prince of peace, Isa. 9. 6. it was fit it should be built by peaceable Solomon, that the shadow might be suitable to the substance, 1 Chro. 22. 9. The second reason is mentioned by Solomon, 1 king. 5. 3. Because also for the time to come his father David was to have war with the nations about him, not yet subdued, and so could have no leisure to go through with so great a work as the building of the temple. The other reasons given, why God would not have him build it, do follow in the text.

**V. 6.** {*Whereas I have not dwelt in any house, since the time that I brought up the children of Israel out of Egypt*} i.e. I have not made choice of any standing permanent house, wherein to show my gracious presence, to this day; but have manifested myself in a tabernacle flitting and removing from place to place. Here another reason is given why God would not have David build the temple, taken from himself: because there was yet no need of it: he had dwelt in no house since their coming out of Egypt, yet all that while he had been present with them, as their God, all-sufficient in himself. q.d. There is no present haste or necessity of building a house for me, who have all this while been content with a tent; and so will be still, till my own time be come; and who also have been as efficacious by my presence in a tabernacle, as in a temple; and so will be still, as long as I please to make use of it.

**V. 7.** {*Spake I a word with any of the tribes of Israel*} Or, *any of the Judges*, 1 Chron. 17. 6. i.e. Did I ever complain in the time of the judges that my house was not built? I had a purpose it should be done in due time, yet I required not any of them to go about it. Here, though he accepted of David’s zeal and care, yet he secretly taxes him for not waiting his leisure, and for thinking of undertaking the work, having had no special command for it, no more than the judges had in their time.

*{to feed my people Israel*} i.e. As faithful shepherds, to govern and provide for my people.

**V. 8.** {*I took thee from the sheepcote, from following the sheep}* 1 Sam. 16. 11. Psal. 78. 70, 71. Here God recounts his former and future benefits, not to upbraid David with them, or to tax him for ingratitude, and not walking worthy of such high favors; as he afterwards did in the case of Uriah, ch. 12. 7. but that he might not be discouraged and doubt of God’s love, as though for want of it his service was rejected in building him a temple, he tells him, that as already he had multiplied many blessings upon him, so he was still ready to do for the time to come.

**V. 9.** {*And I was with thee whithersoever thou wentest*} i.e. Prospered you by my gracious assistance in all your enterprises, as in the combat with Goliath, and many battles against the Philistims. So ch. 8. 6, 14.

*{and have cut off all thine enemies*} Saul, Doeg, the Philistims, and all the rest.

*{and have made thee a great name*} i.e. I have made you famous and formidable among all the nations round about you.

*{like unto the name of the great men which are in the earth*} i.e. The great princes and potentates of other nations.

**V. 10.** {*Moreover, I will appoint a place for my people Israel, &c.*} He breaks off from recounting the blessings he had conferred upon David, and inserts a promise of what good things he was purposed to do to the whole nation under his government, for his greater comfort and encouragement; because the happiness of a king does much consist in the happiness and prosperity of his people.

*{and will plant them*} i.e. Establish and settle them in their own land allotted unto them. These promises, though in part fulfilled in David’s and Solomon’s reign, yet were to have their full accomplishment in the spiritual kingdom of the Messiah. Neither must this promise be understood of their bare habitation in the land of Canaan, seeing they had dwelt in it ever since the time of Joshua; but of their peaceable and quiet possession thereof, which had been often interrupted and disturbed, not only of old in the time of the judges, when their enemies prevailed against them, but of late also in Saul’s time, by the incursions and invasions of the Philistims.

*{and move no more*} i.e. They shall no more as in former times, be tumbled and tossed up and down, and be compelled to fly out of their cities and habitations, and leave them for their enemies to dwell in: 1 Sam. 31. 7.

*{Neither shall the children of wickedness afflict them any more, as before time}* i.e. Howsoever their malicious enemies may make some attempts against them, yet they shall not so far prevail as to lay upon them such grievous pressures as they have formerly endured. Now all this is to be understood, not absolutely, but upon condition of their continuing in obedience, and in the pure service of God, Jer. 18. 9. 2 king. 8. 12. 1 Chron. 22. 12, 13. For when afterwards they fell to idolatry, God gave them over to be afflicted by the Assyrians, Egyptians and Chaldeans, by which last they were conquered and led into captivity.

**V. 11.** {*And as since the time that I commanded Judges to be over my people Israel, and have caused thee to rest from all thine enemies*} i.e. As they have been often afflicted in the days of the judges, and even unto the time of your reign: but now I have given you and them full victory over all your enemies.

*{Also the Lord telleth thee that he will make thee an house*} i.e. Establish your kingdom in your posterity from one generation to another, until it be fully and perfectly established in the Messiah that shall come out of your loins; hereby graciously rewarding your pious intention to build him a house. See Exod. 1. 21. & 1 king. 2. 24.

**V. 12.** {*I will set up thy seed after thee that shall proceed out of thy bowels*} i.e. Solomon, and the Messiah prefigured by him: both which be respected in the following promises, some whereof are peculiar to Solomon, others peculiar to Christ, and some belonging to them both, as the type and antitype.

**V. 13.** {*He shall build an house for my name*} i.e. For the advancement of my glory in the means of my public worship and service to be performed in the temple, according to Deut. 12. 11. This is literally to be understood of Solomon: for he it was who was appointed to build the temple. David only prepared materials, and left the pattern to his son which he had received from God, but had no hand in the building, 1 Chron. 22. 14. & 28. 11. It was Solomon only that did both begin and finish it, 1 king. 5. 15. & 6. 14. But this is to be understood spiritually of Christ, who was to build, to the glory of God’s name, a spiritual and everlasting house, that is, his church, of which the temple was but a type, Luk. 1. 32, 33. 1 Pet. 2. 5.

*{And I will establish the throne of his Kingdom for ever}* This is proper and peculiar to Christ’s kingdom alone, and cannot be understood of Solomon’s, seeing the kingdom was only continued to Zedekiah in Solomon’s posterity.

**V. 14.** {*I will be his father, and he shall be my son*} This belonged both to Solomon and Christ, Solomon was God’s son by grace and adoption, Christ, by nature and eternal generation, and so his only-begotten one, John 1. 18. Psalm 2. 7. Acts 13. 33. 1 John 4. 9. Heb. 1. 5.

*{If I commit iniquity*} This is to be understood of Solomon, not of Christ. For though our sins were imputed unto him, and he suffered and satisfied for them; in which regard the prophet says, *He was wounded for our transgressions*, Isa. 53. 5. and the apostle, *He was made sin for us*, 2 Cor. 5. 21. yet he himself did not commit any iniquity or sin, *neither was there any guilt found in his mouth*, Isa. 53. 9. 1 Pet. 2. 22. & 1. 19.

*{I will chasten him with the rod of men*} So Psal. 89. 30, 31, 32. i.e. Either by temporal corrections, using men as rods and instruments; so he corrected Solomon in the latter end of his reign, when he fell to idolatry, 1 king. 11. 9, 14, 23, 26. and the people of Israel, Isa. 10. 5. Or else the meaning is, that he would correct him for his sins as a loving father his beloved son, with fatherly chastisements for his amendment; and not in wrath to his perdition. For as when he speaks of his destroying punishments inflicted on his enemies, he says, he will *not meet them as a man*, but as a God, who is *a consuming fire*, Hebr. 12. 29. Isa. 33. 14. so when he says he will correct them as a loving father, he thus expresses it, that he will draw them unto him *with the cords of men, and with the stripes of the children of men*, i.e. not in rigor, but with a gentle and fatherly hand, with much pity and moderation, Jer. 30. 11.

**V. 15.** {*But my mercy shall not depart away from him, as I took it from Saul*} i.e. I will not quite cast him out of my favor, and deprive him of his kingdom, as I did Saul. Neither can this be understood of God’s eternal love, wherewith he loves his elect; seeing Saul had never any interest therein: for those *whom God loveth, he loveth to the end*, Joh. 13. 1.

**V. 16.** {*And thine house, and thy Kingdom shall be established for ever before thee*} i.e. I will begin your kingdom in your own days, in yourself, and in Solomon your son in your own sight, 1 king. 1. 48. to assure you it shall be continued in your posterity for a long time, and be established forever, and completely accomplished in Christ, who shall descend of you. If this be applied to Solomon, then we are to understand the words *for ever*, of a long time; namely, that David should enjoy the kingdom himself to his death, and before his departure see it settled upon Solomon in respect of title and right, and that it should so continue in his posterity, till the scepter departed from Judah, Gen. 49. 10. before the coming of Shiloh, or Christ, in whom it was to be *established for ever*. But how can Christ’s kingdom be said to *be established for ever*, seeing the apostle says, that at the end of the world, after the last judgment, *Christ shall deliver up his Kingdom to his Father*, 1 Cor. 15. 24. I answer, that is to be understood of his economical kingdom committed unto him as Messiah and Mediator, which he exercises in subduing his and the church’s enemies, in making intercession for his chosen ones, given him out of the world, and perfecting his members and mystical body. For when all things are done and finished, there shall be no use of that kingdom in respect of this administration, and therefore it shall cease, though the effect and fruit of it shall never have end: but then Christ, as he is God and man, shall reign forever with his Father, and even in his human nature everlastingly enjoy that glory and majesty which by virtue of the hypostatical union of the two natures belongs unto it; though that outward administration of the mediatorship shall cease.

**V. 17.** {*According to all these words, and according to all this vision, so did Nathan speak to David*} i.e. He made a true and faithful relation of all God commanded him to speak, though it were to his own disparagement, being contrary to the advice he had before privately given. And herein he carried himself piously and prudently. For as it is the first point of wisdom so to speak as to have no cause to repent afterwards of what we have spoken, so it is the second point, when we have spoken amiss to retract and revoke it; as Augustine excellently says in excuse of his own practice. Neither does David upbraid Nathan, as though he had not carried himself as the Lord’s prophet herein, saying, and unsaying what he had formerly said. Thus Isaiah, having denounced the sentence of death upon Hezekiah, afterwards, being appointed by God, revokes it, and promises that his life should be prolonged fifteen years. But Jonah, who out of pride and self-seeking neglected to deliver the message which God had put into his mouth, is justly taxed and condemned for it. But seeing David himself was a prophet, why did not God immediately inform him what he was to do in this great business, but uses the ministry of Nathan to teach and instruct him? I answer, Though there were no reason hereof to be given but his own good pleasure, that alone were sufficient: but besides, we must consider that God looks upon us as members of the same mystical body, who are linked together in love and service, and need mutual help: and as every member has its employment, so it tends to the increasing of love and affection, and to the common good of the whole. So it is in the body natural; all its members, as the eye, ear, hand, foot, perform mutual service to one another; and thereby the whole body is preserved, 1 Cor. 12. 16, 21.

**V. 18.** {*Then went King David in, and sat before the Lord}* To wit, in the tent before the ark, that he might pour forth his soul in holy soliloquies and meditations. Here it is said that he *sate*, not in respect of the posture of his body, but because he there remained and continued to perform this devout service. So the word is often taken, as Gen. 27. 44. Lev. 14. 8. 1 Sam. 1. 22. & 20. 19. Yet is it not simply unlawful to use this posture of sitting in prayer and meditation. For the Lord having nowhere tied us to any gesture of body, but only requiring a fit disposition of soul, and that we should call upon him with humble hearts, with contrite and broken spirits, and in sincerity and truth, the saints have taken liberty, and prayed unto God in all postures, as lying in bed, walking, standing, and sitting, as we see in the example of Moses, Exod. 17. 12. and Elijah 1 king. 19. 4. Notwithstanding the gesture of kneeling, and prostrating of the body in time of deepest humiliation, being fittest to express the disposition of a humble soul, are sometimes required, and most commonly practiced by God’s saints and servants, Psal. 95. 6. 1 king. 8. 54. And so it is probably thought David did in this place; first sat, whilst he meditated on God’s grace and goodness towards him, and afterwards kneeled, when he addressed himself to God by prayer.

*{Who am I, O Lord God, and what is my house, that thou hast brought me hitherto?*} i.e. How base and unworthy am I, and all my parentage and family, that I should be so highly advanced as I am already out of your mere grace and undeserved favor?

**V. 19.** {*Thou hast spoken also of thy servant’s house for a great while to come}* i.e. You have promised also to continue your favor, not only to me, but also to my posterity after me for many generations.

*{and is this the manner of man, O Lord God?*} q.d. How admirable is this grace and goodness which you extend towards me; and how far above not only the practice of men to act, but even their understanding to conceive, that you should show such favor above all desert! And therefore, seeing this is peculiar to yourself alone, to you only be the praise. Or thus; Do men deal so freely and familiarly with their mean inferiors as you have done to me? Surely no: such incomprehensible love is not to be found among mortal men. So Psal. 8. 3. Into this ecstasy of admiration David is chiefly brought by God’s promise of perpetuating his kingdom in Christ, which should come out of his loins, as the apostle applies it, Heb. 2. 5.

**V. 20.** {*And what can David say more unto thee? for thou, Lord God, knowest thy servant*} i.e. Either, What can I ask more of you than you of your free grace have already promised? as which sense that place 1 Chro. 17. 18. seems to aim, *What can David speak more to thee for the honour of thy servant?* Or thus, What need have I to use many words to amplify your goodness, and express my thankfulness, seeing you know my heart, how real I am in this duty?

**V. 21.** {*For thy word’s sake, and according to thine own heart, hast thou done all these great things}* i.e. Not for any desert of mine, but for your truth and promise’s sake, and of your mere grace and love, have you conferred all these benefits upon me. Or, as some take it, for your Christ’s sake, who is your eternal Word, and your mere grace, and free goodwill.

*{to make thy servant know them*} i.e. To acknowledge your benefits with all thankfulness, both which you have conferred and will confer upon your servant in time to come.

**V. 22.** {*according to all that we have heard with our ears*} i.e. According to all that by your word you have made known unto us.

**V. 23.** {*And what one nation in the earth is like thy people*} i.e. So advanced in high and holy privileges, Deut. 4. 7, 34.

*{whom God went to redeem for a people to himself*} i.e. Came as it were down from heaven to manifest his power in redeeming them, Exod. 3. 7, 8. This was the work of the whole Trinity in the unity of the Godhead; and therefore the word *God Elohim* and the verb *went* are both in the plural number; yet David’s speech is directed to one God alone, *before thy people which thou redeemedst*, and, *Thou, Lord, art become their God*. Here God, who is omnipresent, is said to have come to redeem his people; *God went to redeem*. This is usually spoken of God when he applies himself to do any great or strong action, though he is present in all places at all times.

*{and to make him a name*} i.e. Either to make his people renowned and famous among all nations, as it is expounded, 1 Chro. 17. 21. Or, to magnify his own glory by his great works, according to Num. 14. 21. *All the earth shall be filled with the glory of the Lord.* Isa. 63. 14. *So didst thou lead thy people to make thy self a glorious name.*

*{and to do for you great things and terrible*} Not only in their deliverance out of Egypt, but also in subduing their enemies in the land of Canaan; which are therefore joined together, 1 Chro. 17. 21. Here David uses an apostrophe, converting his speech abruptly from God to the people of Israel, *and to do for you*. And in the next words again he converts his speech to God, *for thy land, before thy people*. So in his mourning song for Saul and Jonathan by the like apostrophe he turns his speech to the people, *O noble Israel, he is slain upon thy high places*; as some read it ch. 1. 19.

*{from the nations, and their gods*} From all nations that fought against them, and from their idols in whom they trusted.

**V. 24.** {*For thou hast confirmed to thyself thy people Israel, to be a people unto thee for ever}* i.e. The natural Israel for a long time, even to the coming of Christ; but the spiritual Israel, consisting both of Jews and Gentiles forever. And hereof he assigns no other cause but God’s free election, Gen. 17. 7.

**V. 25.** {*And now, O Lord God, the word that thou hast spoken——establish it for ever*} Though David having God’s infallible promise, was assured he would be as good as his word, yet he earnestly prays that he would fulfill it. God’s promises must not make us neglect the means, but be the more diligent in the use of them, having a good ground to make our prayers with more firm confidence to be heard. This our Savior also has taught us by his example, praying that the Father would *glorifie his Son*, though he were most assured of his glorification, John 17. 5.

**V. 26.** {*And let the house of thy servant David be established before thee*} Here David repeats in his prayer what he had said before, yet not in that vain manner which Christ condemns, Matth. 6. 7. but being ravished with joy by the sweet apprehension of God’s gracious promises made unto him and his posterity, he thought he could not sufficiently express his affection, and so in the fervency of his devotion repeats the same things again and again. So our Savior Christ himself in his bitter agony is said to have *prayed the third time, saying the same words*, to show his earnestness, Matthew 26. 44. and Luke 22. 44.

**V. 27.** {*hast revealed to thy servant*} Heb. *opened the ear*, i.e. Have made him clearly know. See 1 Sam. 9. 15.

*{I will build thee an house*} i.e. Continue the kingdom to you and your posterity after you.

*{hath thy servant found in his heart*} i.e. Has found his heart thus disposed to call upon you, having your promise as a sure ground of his faith and prayer. So Psalms 116. 10. See on Eccles. 5. 2.

**V. 28.** {*Thy words be true*} Joh. 17. 17. i.e. There can be no doubt of obtaining my suit, seeing I have your promise, who are true of your word, and powerful to perform it.

**V. 29.** {*For thou, O Lord God, hast spoken it*} Therefore I firmly believe it shall come to pass.

*{let the house of thy servant be blessed for ever*} i.e. Both now in my temporal kingdom, and to all eternity in the spiritual kingdom of the Messiah, which you have promised shall descend of me.

## CHAPTER 8

**Verse 1.** {*And after this it came to pass that David smote the Philistines, and subdued them}* Compare this whole chapter with 1 Chron. 18. In this and the following chapters a reason is rendered why God would not suffer David to build his temple, namely, because he had no leisure, being by the Lord’s will employed in wars. And withal in the following story is shown how God accomplished his gracious promise made in the former chapter concerning the establishment of David’s kingdom in all prosperity, and the confluence of all blessings, to the joy of himself and all his people.

*{And David took Metheg-Ammah out of the hands of the Philistines}* This was the third war against the Philistims after he was anointed king over all Israel. In which, having vanquished them, he took from them this Metheg-Ammah, i.e. Gath, the chief city of theirs, as appears 1 Chron. 18. 1. and all the small towns under its jurisdiction. Now Gath was called Metheg-Ammah because it was situate on a hill or mountainous tract of ground, and, as a bridle by its great strength it restrained enemies from invading the country adjoining, and also gave laws to all the lesser towns about it, whereby they were governed and kept in awe and order. Afterwards called Die-Caesarea. And it was seated on the frontiers of Palestine, at the entrance into the tribes of Judah and Ephraim. This city of strength David surprises and holds, that thenceforth it might as a bridle restrain the Philistims from invading the land of Canaan.

**V. 2.** {*And he smote Moab*} At the coming of the Israelites out of the wilderness they were forbidden by God to invade the Moabites’ land, or do them any hurt, because he had given it to the children of Lot for a possession, Deut. 2. 9. But now David makes war against them; for what cause, and upon what occasion, it does not clearly appear. The Hebrews think it was because David having, in the time of Saul’s persecution, left his father and mother under their king’s protection, they treacherously murdered them after he was departed from them. But for this we have no good ground in Scripture, why we should believe it. It is rather thought that God at first restrained his people from distressing them, because they had then done them no wrong. But afterwards they proved malicious enemies, and thereupon were interdicted from coming into the congregation *unto the tenth generation*, Deut. 23. 3. They showed their malice, in hiring Balaam to curse Israel; and when that would not take, in following his cursed counsel, tempting them by their women to commit both corporal and spiritual whoredom, whereby a great plague was brought upon them; also in not relieving them with bread in their necessity; and in cruel tyrannizing and oppressing them by Eglon their king, in the time of the judges: for all which wrongs David had just cause to make war against them, they still continuing in their former enmity; though the end of his war was not to dispossess them of their country, but only to make them tributaries, and thereby to revenge the injuries they had offered God’s people.

*{and measured them with a line, casting them down to the ground*} i.e. He vanquished them, and brought them into absolute subjection and servitude, using them at his own pleasure, to kill and keep alive whom he would, and, as conquerors use to do, dividing their land as he thought good. This is metaphorically expressed by a similitude taken from those who measured land by line, or from a carpenter that strikes with a line, the timber he intends to use in his building; and then cuts off what he thinks unnecessary and superfluous. So David having subdued the Moabites by an absolute conquest, used them at his pleasure, leveling their cities and towns with the ground, and laying all open and flat before him, to be measured as it were with a line, and so to be divided and disposed of like a new plantation; as Joshua did the land of Canaan, which he divided by lot among the Israelites. Others thinking the historical and plain sense best when there is no need of metaphors and allegories, do take the words here literally, that David did indeed measure the Moabites’ country with a line, allotting two thirds of the lands and cities, with all the inhabitants to ruin, slaughter and destruction, and reserving one third part of the land for a new plantation, and a third part also of the people alive, to inhabit it, as his tributaries. For though God at first appointed his people to spare them, yet they becoming malicious enemies, David exercises upon them this hostile severity.

**V. 3.** {*David smote also Hadadezer*} Called Hadarezer 1 Chr. 18. 3. See on 1 kings 15. 18.

*{the son of Rehob*} Who reigned in that part of Syria called Beth-Rehob after his name, ch. 10. 6.

*{King of Zobah*} A large country in Syria beyond Taurus, between the two Armenians, inhabited by a people called Sopheni, as Josephus reports. Against these Saul formerly had made war with good success, 1 Sam. 14. 47. But now they being grown again to great strength, David takes notice of it, and makes war against them, knowing how dangerous it was that the Syrians and their king, who were professed enemies to the Israelites, and dwelled so near them, should grow powerful and puissant.

*{as he went to recover his border at the river Euphrates*} i.e. As some understand it, when David went to enlarge and settle his dominion in those utmost limits which God had set his people, even at the river Euphrates, Josh. 1. 4. Deut. 1. 7. & 11. 24. Others very probably understand it of Hadadezer, that when he went to establish his dominion towards Euphrates, and must needs pass through part of David’s country, or near it, which could not be without danger, in respect of the enmity between the nations, David raised forces, and sent them under the conduct of Joab and Abishai, to fight with him and hinder his passage; as appears in the title of the 60. Psalm. This is the more likely, because it is said 1 Chr. 18. 3. that the end of this expedition was *to establish his dominion*; which may well be understood of Hadadezer, that had reigned there; but not so well of David, the bounds of whose kingdom did not as yet extend so far.

**V. 4.** {*seven hundred horsemen*} i.e. Seven hundred decuries or ranks of horsemen, ten going to a rank; which make up that full number of *seven thousand* mentioned 1 Chr. 18. 4. See the like expression ch. 10. 18.

*{and David houghed all the chariots-horses*} And so made them unserviceable for war, though they might be of use for other employments. He reserved only enough for a hundred chariots, four horses going to one chariot, 1 kings 10. 29. And herein he had respect to God’s commandment Deut. 17. 16. forbidding the kings of Israel to *multiply horses to themselves*, lest leaving their trust in God they should place confidence in their own strength and warlike preparations. This also was put in execution by Joshua at God’s command, Josh. 11. 6. David here reserved out of a thousand a hundred only for his own use, a number not exceeding the necessary occasions of a king.

**V. 5.** {*of Damascus*} The metropolis and chief city of Syria.

**V. 6.** {*brought gifts*} i.e. Paid him tribute, by way of homage.

*{preserved David*} i.e. Prospered his attempts, and gave him victory over his enemies.

**V. 7.** {*and brought them to Jerusalem*} Consecrating them unto God, v. 12. Which shows that he did not make these wars out of covetousness, to enrich himself: but that chiefly therein he aimed at God’s glory, seeing he devoted his spoils to the building and beautifying of his temple.

**V. 8.** {*And from Betah, and from Berothai*} Called Tibhath and Chun 1 Chr. 18. 8. Either they had double names, or their old names were changed upon some occasion when that history was written. Ptolemy calls them Tauba and Barathena.

**V. 9.** {*when Toi King of Hamath*} Called Tou 1 Chron. 18. 9. See Num. 13. 21. Hamath was a city of Syria, as some think, Antioch. See on Isa. 10. 9. and on Amos 6. 2.

**V. 10.** {*Then Toi sent Joram*} Named Hadoram 1 Chr. 18. 10.

*{to salute him*} Heb. *to ask him of peace*. i.e. To understand of his welfare, and congratulate his victory, Gen. 43. 27.

**V. 11.** {*Which also King David did dedicate unto the Lord*} i.e. Having received this congratulatory present from Toi, he consecrated it also unto God for the building of his temple. And herein he was a type of Christ, who having vanquished Satan and the world, consecrated the spoils of his conquests to God’s glory and the building and adorning of the church, converting and making some who had been slaves of Satan vessels of grace and his faithful apostles and teachers, and causing those precious arts which had been abused formerly to the advancing of Satan’s kingdom, to be of special use for his church.

**V. 13.** {*And David gat him a name when he returned*} i.e. By his many victories he became renowned for a great warrior and famous conqueror, having all his expeditions and enterprises crowned with prosperous success; but especially he grew famous by his subduing the Syrians: in which service Abishai was his chief commander under Joab. Compare 1 Chron. 18. 12. with the title of the 60. Psalm.

*{From smiting the Syrians in the valley of Salt*} Situate on the east end of Mount Seir, without Syria, on the south of the Dead Sea, 2 king. 14. 7. Here David, in the return of his army, did by his commanders Abishai and Joab obtain another famous victory, in which were slain eighteen thousand of his enemies. Now whereas it is said that *he smote of Edom in the valley of Salt twelve thousand*, in the title of the 60. Psalm, and 1 Chr. 18. 12. and here they are said to be *eighteen thousand*, and called *Syrians*, it is to be conceived that these two nations of Edom and Syria joined together in this expedition. And whereas it is said 1 Chr. 18. 12. that *Abishai slew* these enemies, and in the 60. Psalm this victory is ascribed to *Joab*; and whereas in the Psalm it is said that Joab slew but *twelve thousand*, and here and 1 Chr. 18. 12. also are mentioned *eighteen thousand*, it is thus to be understood; That Joab got this victory by Abishai, who was under his command, and in the beginning of the fight slew six thousand and then Joab coming in with the whole body of the army slew twelve thousand more, in all eighteen thousand: which full number in the Chronicles are said to have been slain by Abishai, because he with that part of the army under his command began the battle and made way for a full victory by routing the ranks of the enemy. Now the victory is ascribed to David, because both Joab and Abishai were but his agents and instruments, and he their king and supreme lord. Thus three seeming differences concerning the persons that got this victory, the people slain, and the number of them may be reconciled. See on the title of Psal. 60.

**V. 14.** {*And he put garrisons in Edom*} i.e. Having conquered them, he placed garrisons in all their strongholds, to keep them still under subjection and tribute; placing over them a viceroy, to govern them as his deputy, which continued unto Joram’s time, 1 kings 22. 47. and 2 king. 8. 21.
