And thus that prophecy Gen. 25. 23. that *the elder should serve the younger*, was fulfilled; even as that other, Gen. 27. 40. that Esau’s posterity *should break the yoke from off his neck*, was accomplished in the days of Joram.

**V. 15.** {*And David executed judgement and justice*} By which his throne was established, Jer. 22. 15. Prov. 14. 34.

**V. 16.** {*was over the host*} i.e. general of the army. To this honor he was preferred for his taking from the Jebusites the strong fort of Zion, 1 Chr. 11. 6.

**V. 16.** {*Recorder*} Or, *remembrancer*; or *writer of chronicles and records*; or, as it is with us, *master of the court of requests*, to take notice of and prefer the suits of the subjects.

**V. 17.** {*And Zadok the son of Ahitub, and Ahimelech the son of Abiathar, were the Priests*} i.e. Zadok, who descended of Eleazar, 1 Chr. 6. 4, 8. and Ahimelech who was of Ithamar, were chief priests in David’s time. Not that there were then two high priests, for that would have been contrary to God’s law, (which appointed only one, that he might be a type of Christ our alone high priest) and therefore would not have been permitted by holy David, as it was afterwards under the corrupt government of Pilate and Herod, when there were two at once, Annas and Caiaphas; but these were two chief priests of several families, having command over the rest of the priests of those families, according to that division which David made 1 Chron. 14. 3, 4. to compound a controversy between them contending about the priesthood. But now Abiathar only was the high priest, who fled from Saul to David 1 Sam. 22. 20. and so continued till Solomon’s time; when for his siding with Adonijah he was deposed, and Zadok put in his place, 1 king. 26, 27, 35. Some think that the father and the son had double names, and the one and the other were called both Ahimelech and Abiathar, and so promiscuously Ahimelech is said to be the father of Abiathar, 1 Sam. 22. 20. and 23. 6. and Abiathar the father of Ahimelech, as here. See Mark. 2. 26.

*{and Seraiah*} He is called Sheva, ch. 20. 25. and Shavsha, 1 Chron. 18. 19.

*{the Scribe*} Or, *secretary*.

**V. 18.** {*And Benaiah the son of Jehoiada was over both the Cherethites and the Pelethites*} These were old, expert, strong and valiant men, like the praetorian soldiers among the Romans: and these David chose for his guard, and made Benaiah captain over them. See 1 king. 1. 38. This plainly appears ch. 15. 18. and 20. 7. 1 king. 1. 38, 44. But why they were thus called, and whence they were, is not so easy to determine. Some think the Cherethites had their name from the Hebrew word *Charath* signifying *to be cut off*, because at the king’s command they executed offenders; and the Pelethites theirs, from the word *Palat*, which signifies either *to defend*, or *such as were excellent*, because they were excellent men that defended the king’s person. But others more probably think they had these names rather from their countries than from their office; and that the Cherethites were such soldiers of the Israelites as were put in garrison into the towns and cities of the Cherethims in the country of the Philistims, after David had subdued them, when dwelling at Ziklag he made inroads into their country, 1 Sam. 30. 14. Ezek. 25. 16. Zeph. 2. 4, 5. and that the Pelethites were also garrison-soldiers living among the Japhletites in the lot of Joseph, Josh. 16. 3. and that from these being valiant and expert soldiers, the king chose his guard to attend his person. Yet did not all of them, and at all times, live at court, but only gave their attendance by course upon the king, as may be gathered, ch. 15. 18. and were also used as garrison-soldiers in warlike expeditions and employments for the defense of the land.

*{And David’s sons were chief rulers}* Or, *princes*; or, as it is 1 Chr. 18. 17. *were chief about the King*. The which policy or paternal affection in David was not crowned with good success, two of his sons at least, Absalom and Adonijah, having their spirits so raised by these preferments that they ambitiously affected sovereignty, and one of them so angled the hearts of the people by casting before them the baits of his favors, that being impatient of any delay, he would needs presently usurp the kingdom, though he could no otherwise step into the throne than by making his father his footstool; nor obtain his desire by any other means than by his father’s utter ruin.

## CHAPTER 9

**V. 1.** {*And David said*} i.e. Inquired of his servants.

*{Is there yet any that is left of the house of Saul*} i.e. Is there any remaining of that family which are fit objects of my love and kindness, for otherwise he could not but know that divers of them were still alive.

*{that I may shew him kindness for Jonathan’s sake}* Now David calls to mind the great love which had been between him and Jonathan, and the covenant of the Lord, contracted in his presence, and confirmed by solemn oath, that he would show kindness to him and his posterity after him, 1 Sam. 18. 3. and 20. 14, 15. And indeed a great wonder it is to me that being a man of such piety and morality, he had thought all this while of that incomparable love which his dear Jonathan had always showed him, and the manifold fruits of this favor received, which had concerned him as much as the preservation of his life; all which he was bound to requite not only by covenant, but even common humanity. But David is not only excused, because till this time, being busied in settling his kingdom at home, and subduing his enemies abroad, he had had no leisure to think of private affairs, but he is also highly commended, in that being settled in his kingdom, he does now at last think of recompensing Jonathan’s love. But, saving other men’s better judgments, and always reserving a most honorable and deserved respect to holy David for his true worth and incomparable goodness, piety and justice, I do impute it unto him as one of the chief errors of his life, that he did not only not answer Jonathan in his love before this time, but afterwards failed not only in friendship but also in justice in his carriage towards poor Mephibosheth the only son of his dearest Jonathan. And the best I can say in excuse of his faults, is, that he was a king and so subject to kingly errors, that is, jealousies and suspicions in things concerning the crown and kingdom. For Ishbosheth Saul’s son; and it is very likely, with him, all that family siding in that cause, had raised war against David, pretending a right to the crown by lineal descent, and impleading his title to it, which God himself had given him: and in that respect David might think all Jonathan’s favors sufficiently recompensed if this rebellion were pardoned. And besides, some old relics of former jealousies still remained, of their plotting to revive their title to the crown, as appears by his easiness and readiness to hearken to the false and foolish calumnies of treacherous Ziba, and by his being so forward in passing that unjust sentence of dividing the land between him and his master, lest poor Mephibosheth, holding the entire interest to Saul’s lands, might in time grow too great in wealth and in the favor of the common people, who are apt to mislike the present government though never so good and just, and so aspire to the crown. These I conceive were the chief causes why David so long delayed to think efficaciously of Jonathan’s posterity, to do them good, and in them to requite his kindness towards him. For Mephibosheth was but five years old when his father was slain, ch. 4. 4. and at this time he was married, and had a son, v. 12. Whereby it appears that this kindness was long deferred, and it is likely had not yet been thought on, had not David now been securely settled in his kingdom, and so wholly freed from fears and jealousies.

**V. 2.** {*And there was of the house of Saul a servant, whose name was Ziba*} Hence some probably gather that Ziba was a Canaanite, or a stranger of some other nation, seeing the Israelites were to serve only for seven years, unless they would serve longer; and then, if they preferred bondage before liberty, their ears were to be nailed to the doorpost, and they to remain servants forever, Exod. 21. 6. Now Ziba had been servant to Saul, and so still continued in that condition; although it seems he was not an ordinary servant, but in some special place of credit and employment, seeing he himself had twenty servants belonging to him, v. 10.

**V. 3.** {*That I may shew the kindness of God unto him}* i.e. Either great kindness, as the phrase is used; or, such kindness as I am bound to show by virtue of that covenant which I contracted in the presence of God with Jonathan; or, such kindness as the Lord uses to show to the fatherless and distressed, whose example we ought to imitate. And thus he expresses himself that he might not conceal it, if he knew any such, as fearing that he thus earnestly inquired after them out of some intention to do them hurt.

**V. 4.** {*Behold, he is in the house of Machir the son of Ammiel, in Lodebar*} This Machir, as it seems was a potent man of the tribe of Manasseh, Num. 26. 29. and 32. 39. Lodebar was a town in Gilead, on the other side Jordan, not far from Mahanaim, ch. 17. 27. where Ishbosheth had reigned. Hither Mephibosheth was brought, and had committed himself to the care and trust of Machir for his better safety and security; and here he lived, as it seems, privately and secretly, as thinking perhaps there was some danger, in respect of the present time and state, to be known to be one of Saul’s posterity.

**V. 5.** {*Then King David sent, and fet him*} i.e. Caused him to be brought unto him by his messengers whom he employed.

**V. 6.** {*Now when Mephibosheth*} He is called Meribbaal 1 Chr. 8. 34. which some think was his proper name, and Mephibosheth his surname, given him because of some deformity in his face or body.

*{was come unto David, he fell on his face*} Using it is likely, this adoration and awful reverence out of fear that David, who had power in his hand to use him as he pleased, had sent for him, intending him some evil. Which David perceiving, speaks comfortably unto him, and promises to show him kindness.

**V. 7.** {*and will restore thee all the land of Saul thy father*} Or *grandfather*. For grandfathers usually in Scripture are called *fathers*, Dan. 5. 13. Herein David showed kindness to Mephibosheth, because Saul’s land was confiscate by reason of that war raised against him by Saul’s family, and in that also he gave him not only the land due to him by inheritance from his father Jonathan, but also the whole lands that belonged to the house of Saul. And hereunto he added a further great kindness, that he would repute him as one of his own sons, and entertain him as his daily guest at his own table.

**V. 8.** {*What is thy servant, that thou shouldst look upon such a dead dog as I am?*} A hyperbolical expression, whereby Mephibosheth humbly acknowledged his base condition in respect of David’s royal eminency; and how far unworthy he was of his high favors; both because his family was rejected of God, and David chosen in their room; and also because they were all dead in law for rebelling against their sovereign king whom God had set over them: and besides, in respect of his own person, he was a lame, contemptible and forlorn man, and so, though a fit object of pity and mercy, yet not of honorable respect and kingly munificence. Thus David had humbled himself formerly to Saul, acknowledging that in comparison of his royal dignity he was but as *a flea*, or *a partridge on the mountains*, or, (as Mephibosheth here calls himself) *a dead dog*, 1 Sam. 24. 14. and 26. 20. See ch. 3. 8.

**V. 9.** {*Ziba, Saul’s servant}* i.e. Who in former times had been servant to Saul, but now belonged to Mephibosheth, chap. 19. 26.

*{I have given to thy master’s son}* i.e. Mephibosheth, the nephew or grandson of Saul.

**V. 10.** {*That thy master’s son may have food to eat}* Either he means Micha Mephibosheth’s son, and those that belonged to him; or else Mephibosheth himself, of whom he speaks under this name, v. 7, 9. He would that Ziba should till Mephibosheth’s land and husband his estate for his use, and for the provision of his family; though in respect of his own person he had taken order that he should continually eat at his own table.

**V. 11.** {*As for Mephibosheth, said the King, he shall eat at my table*} This some expositors understand as the speech of Ziba still continued, because these words *said the King* are not in the text; and because he was lame, he had the king’s provisions sent from his own table to him. But it is plain that it was the king’s speech, and so well expressed in our translation, by adding *said the King* unto the original, seeing it is added in the text; *he shall eat at my table, as one of the Kings sons*. For the king’s sons did not sit at Ziba’s table, but at the court with the king himself.

**V. 12.** {*And Mephibosheth had a young son, whose name was Micha*} Mentioned, 1 Chr. 8. 35. and 9. 40, 41. He had four sons; and those many others, in whom the family of noble Jonathan was continued.

**V. 13.** {*So Mephibosheth dwelt in Jerusalem*} Though his lands and inheritance were at Gibeah-Saul; both because he lived a sedentary life, being unfit to travel to and fro by reason of his lameness, and also was to be David’s daily guest at his table.

*{and was lame on both his feet*} This is added to show what an honorable favor David did him, in that, though he were lame, and so unfit to sit at the king’s table, yet out of his love to Jonathan he gave him this privilege. See on ch. 5. 8.

## CHAPTER 10

**Verse 1.** {*And it came to pass after this}* Compare this chapter with 1 Chr. 19. where the same story is related.

*{the King of the children of Ammon died*} i.e. Nahash, who had formerly besieged Jabesh-Gilead, 1 Chr. 19. 1. 1 Sam. 11. 1.

**V. 2.** {*Then said David, I will shew kindness unto Hanun}* David being peaceably settled in his kingdom, studies how to show kindness to his friends who had been kind to him, and as first at home to Jonathan, in his son Mephibosheth, so now abroad to Hanun, in sending ambassadors to comfort him for the death of his father.

*{as his father shewed kindness unto me}* What this kindness was the Scripture mentions not, nor when it was showed unto him. Hierome thinks that when he fled from Achish king of Gath, Nahash king of Ammon gave him courteous entertainment; though not so much out of love to David as hatred to Saul, who was a common enemy to them both; having given the one a great defeat before Jabesh-Gilead, and driven the other to seek harbor in strange countries for the safeguard of his life.

*{And David sent to comfort him*} It is questioned how David should show himself so officious to him, seeing ch. 8. 12. it is said that among other nations he spoiled *the children of Ammon*. To which the answer is easy; That it is spoken by way of anticipation both of Ammon and some other nations, upon occasion of David’s spoiling Hadadezer, whom he conquered there, that he might sum up together the vanquished nations whose spoils he consecrated for the building of the temple. But the Ammonites were not conquered and spoiled before they put this foul affront upon David and his messengers; for till then the two nations had lived in peace and unity one with another, as appears by their mutual kindness.

**V. 3.** {*and the Princes of the children of Ammon said unto Hanun*} i.e. Out of mere jealousy, having no true ground, they misinterpret David’s kindness to be counterfeit, and not sincere; and say, that though he pretended by his messengers to honor the father, in very deed he intended them as spies against the son, and had sent them to search their country, and discover some advantages for the conquering of them, as he had done other nations. Thus some politicians think they are then most wise when most suspicious: but such wisdom oft proves folly, and brings upon them those evils which they fear, and would prevent; as appears in this place.

*{hath not David rather sent his servants unto thee, to search the City, and to spy it out, and to overthrow it?}* Here they measure David’s foot by their own last. They had false perfidious hearts, full of rancor and malice towards him and his people; (such their ancestors had ever had; as we see in their carriage towards the Israelites in the wilderness, for which they were interdicted the congregation unto the tenth generation, Deut. 23. 3, 4. in the history of the judges, ch. 10, and 11. and in the siege of Jabesh-Gilead) therefore measuring them by themselves, they judged that they were alike malicious towards them, and that therefore there could be no reality in their profession of friendship; and the rather because it may be they knew they were bound by their law *not to seek their prosperity all their days for ever*, Deut. 23. 6. And indeed, these things being true, it may seem strange that David, a holy and strict observer of the law, should show so much respect to Hanun as to send messengers to comfort him: and herein the Hebrew writers think he did ill, and therefore had such ill success in the action. But things must not be judged good or evil by their events, Eccles. 9. 1, 2. And though it were unlawful for David to contract with these nations any near friendship and leagues, yet in common humanity and civility he might return one kindness for another.

**V. 4.** {*Wherefore Hanun took David’s servants, and shaved off the one half of their beards}* Like a foolish young king, he hearkened as Rehoboam afterwards, 1 kings 12. to the ill counsel of his advised princes, and rashly took order to have it put in execution. First he caused one half of their beards to be shaven off, partly to deform and disgrace them, that all who saw them might deride and jeer them; and partly to put a scorn on their religion and profession, seeing by the law they were forbidden, even in the mourning, *to round the corners of their heads, and to mar the corners of their beards*, Lev. 19. 27. In respect of which law the Israelites forbare to shave their beards at all: for if this had been in use among them, these messengers should not have needed to have *stayed at Jericho till their beards were grown*, seeing they might have cut off the other part which they had left, and so have presently returned home. Secondly, whereas they used in those countries to wear long loose garments, both for ease and ornament, comeliness and modesty, to hide their nakedness and keep their uncomely parts from open view, Hanun caused them to be cut off in the middle, even to the buttocks, that those parts which nature itself teaches men to hide, might lie open to their shame; herein also crossing the law of God, which so carefully provided that such nakedness might not be discovered, that they were forbidden *to ascend unto the altar by steps, lest* slipping or falling, *their nakednesse should appear*, Exod. 20. 26. and also commanded to *put on linen breeches, when they ministred in the Priests office*, from the loins unto the thighs, *to cover their nakednesse*, Exod. 28. 41, 42. So that in abusing David’s ambassadors in this malicious and despiteful manner, the Ammonites grievously offended against the law of God, of nature, and of nations; and thereby deserved most severe punishment. The like despite and disgrace the king of Assyria offered unto the Egyptians, though not in so high a nature, seeing they were his captives, but these ambassadors, Isa. 20. 4. And the like was inflicted by God on the Chaldeans, as a heavy, yet just punishment for their pride, cruelty and oppression of God’s people, Isa. 47. 2, 3.

**V. 5.** {*When they told it unto David, he sent to meet them*} i.e. When these poor disgraced men had made their case known unto David by some messengers, he presently dispatched some to comfort and clothe them.

*{and the King said*} Namely by the messengers he sent, *Tarry at Jericho till your beards be grown*, i.e. In some village or private house set up in or near the place where Jericho stood before it was destroyed. For Jericho itself was not rebuilt till the time of Ahab, 1 kings 16. 34. But the more obscure and private the place appointed for their residing was, the more suitable for men in their condition, who would not be seen without shame.

**V. 6.** {*they stank before David*} i.e. Had made themselves odious and abominable, as a loathsome carrion to the sense of smelling, by their horrible fact, in abusing his ambassadors. So Gen. 34. 30.

*{hired the Syrians*} And with them chariots and horsemen out of Mesopotamia, amounting to that vast number of thirty and two thousand chariots, which they hired with a thousand talents of silver, 1 Chron. 19. 6. All these nations were willing to join with the Ammonites in this war, being all enemies to David, he having lately subdued them and brought them under tribute. Now they hoped they had found an opportunity of revenging themselves on him and his people for all the damages and harms received from them, and of freeing themselves from that bondage in which they lived under them. See ch. 8. 9.

*{of Beth-Rehob*} i.e. Out of that part of Syria whose metropolis was Rehob, ch. 8. 3. a city at the foot of Libanus, in the utmost bounds of Syria towards Palestine. Hereof mention is made Numb. 13. 21. See on Judg. 18. 28.

*{and the Syrians of Zoba*} See ch. 8. 3.

*{and of King Maacah*} king of one part Syria, 1 Chron. 19. 6. near Gilead Trachonitis. Deut. 3. 14.

*{and of Ish-tob twelve thousand men*} This was the country where Jephthah dwelt. See Judg. 11. 3. Besides these, the Ammonites had some help from Aram-Naharaim, mentioned in the title of the 60. Psalm.

**V. 7.** {*And when David heard of it, he sent Joab, &c.*} He did not stay till they invaded his land, but prudently sent his army under the command of Joab to prevent them by invading their country, and so to make it the seat of the war.

**V. 8.** {*And the children of Ammon came out, and put the battle in array at the entering in of the gate}* To wit, of Medeba, 1 Chr. 19. 7, 9. a city in the borders of Ammon, of which mention is made Numb. 21. 30. The Ammonites prudently put their battle in array before the gates of their own city, that if they were beaten and put to the worse, they might secure themselves by a safe retreat into it; and they caused the Syrians and their other auxiliaries to pitch their battle in the field, that so they might environ the Israelites, both in the front and in the rear, when they came to fight, as it is, v. 9. Another reason also is given why they were placed in the field in some distance from the city, because they held it dangerous to have them, being mercenaries, too near their city, especially being such a numerous multitude, lest they should surprise it if they got the better of the day.

**V. 9.** {*He chose of all the men of Israel, and put them in array against the Syrians*} Because he thought the Syrians, being but mercenaries, would not stand to it, if hotly charged; and if they were beaten and routed, the Ammonites would quickly be put to flight, being discouraged and discomfited by their example.

**V. 10.** {*And the rest of the people he delivered into the hand of Abishai*} i.e. He appointed them to be under his command and conduct in this battle.

**V. 11.** {*And he said, If the Syrians be too strong for me*} Because the safety and prosperous success of an army does much consist in the concord of the commanders, and their right understanding one of another, he prudently makes this agreement, before the fight with his brother; and lest he should be discouraged or discontented because he had culled out the chief men of valor in all the army to be under his conduct, he engages both himself and him by a mutual promise that in the battle they should relieve one another as occasion should require.

**V. 12.** {*Be of good courage, and let us play the men*} Here (if ever) Joab showed himself a valiant, prudent and pious general, putting life and vigor into the hearts of his army by a well-grounded encouragement; they were to fight in a good cause against the Ammonites and their king, for that they had been so injuriously abused and disgraced by them in their ambassadors, contrary to the law of nations; and for the preservation of their people from the rage of malicious enemies, who would utterly destroy them, if they got the victory; and to keep their cities and native country, given them by God for an inheritance, and which he had also reserved unto himself for his inheritance, from utter ruin and vastation; not doubting, if they did their part, but God would be wanting on his, in giving them victory; but howsoever, quietly submitting themselves to be disposed of at his good pleasure. So David, ch. 15. 26.

**V. 14.** {*So Joab returned from the children of Ammon*} Having by this famous victory brought them under, he did not for the present any further pursue them, the time of the year (as it seems) being past for keeping the field, ch. 11. 1.

**V. 15.** {*And when the Syrians saw that they were smitten before Israel, they gathered themselves together*} Having no hope that David would make peace with them, because they had rebelled and joined with the Ammonites to make war against him, they recruit their army, and recollecting their scattered forces prepare for a new expedition.

**V. 16.** {*Hadarezer sent and brought out the Syrians that were beyond the river*} He sent his agents, and hired the Syrians that inhabited beyond Euphrates.

*{And they came to Helam*} See on 1 Chr. 19. 16.

*{and Shobach*} Called Shophach 1 Chr. 19. 16.

**V. 17.** {*And when it was told David, he gathered all Israel together*} Hearing what a puissant and numerous army the Syrians had raised against him, he also mustered a great army out of all Israel, and in his own person led them forth for their better encouragement.

**V. 18.** {*And David slew the men of seven hundred chariots of the Syrians*} Ten fighting men were in every chariot, which make up the number of *seven thousand* mentioned 1 Chr. 19. 18. See ch. 8. 4. Or, *seven hundred troops of horsemen in chariots, consisting of ten in a troop*, as Junius would have it.

*{and forty thousand horsemen}* Besides *fourty thousand footmen* mentioned 1 Chr. 19. 18. For with such huge multitudes the Syrians and other nations in those days used to go out to battle, 1 kings 20. 10. And for this cause David is said to have *gathered all Israel* to encounter them, v. 17. This great defeat of Hadarezer’s army is probably thought to have given occasion to Rezon, one of his commanders, to revolt from him; who gathering unto him his scattered forces, and making himself their general, afterwards became their king, and reigned in Damascus in Solomon’s time, 1 kings 11. 23, 24.

**V. 19.** {*Who were servants to Hadarezer*} i.e. Who had been in his pay, and fought under him in his quarrel.

*{they made peace with Israel, and served them*} i.e. Came under their subjection, and paid them tribute.

## CHAPTER 11

**Verse 1.** {*After the year was expired*} Heb. *after the return of the year*. To wit, after the former battle was fought with the Ammonites, and the winter of that year ended.

*{at the time when Kings go forth to battle}* So 1 king. 20. 22. 2 Chro. 36. 10. The former year being expired, the year following is said to *begin* or *return*, because the sun then returns to the same place where it began its course the former year. Now David having been hindered from prosecuting his victory by the approach of winter, when the spring came on, he sent again Joab, with his chief commanders and the whole army of Israel, against the children of Ammon, to complete his conquest, because in that season they had all provisions fit for an army, food and forage for man and horse, and had time all the summer long to besiege cities, and exercise all warlike exploits for subduing of their enemies.

*{and they destroyed the children of Ammon, and besieged Rabbah*} 1 Chron. 20. 1. i.e. They wasted their country with fire and sword, and so having removed all stops out of their way, they laid siege to Rabbah. This was afterwards called Philadelphia, and was now the chief city of the Ammonites, lying on the mountains of Gilead beyond Jordan, by the head of the river Jabbok, ch. 12. 26, 27.

*{But David tarried still at Jerusalem*} This is added, as I conceive, to imply that David began now to grow slack in executing the duty of a king, and in going out and in before the people in time of war, whereby formerly he had purchased such fame and honor both at home and abroad, and began to give himself to ease and pleasure.

**V. 2.** {*And it came to pass in an eveningtide, that David arose from off his bed}* He did not content himself to take a noon’s nap in the heat of the day, (which they usually did to refresh themselves in those hot countries, ch. 4. 5, 7.) but giving himself to sloth and sleep, he continued his lying and lazing on his bed till towards evening. Such ease and idleness does not beseem a king at any time, upon whom lies the care of the commonwealth, and various employments for the good of the whole state; much less did it become David at this time, when the army of Israel was in the field, and daily exposed to danger, to give himself to security and sloth. And this is here related as a chief cause of David’s fearful fall into his sin of adultery. In his former days, while he was either persecuted by Saul, or afterwards being king, employed himself in the wars, and in settling the state and government ecclesiastical and civil, he was never tainted with any scandalous sin, but walked in God’s fear, having respect to his commandments, Psal. 119. 6. but now entertaining one of the sins of Sodom, idleness, Ezek. 16. 49. he soon after falls into their other sin of lust and uncleanness, which are seldom severed one from the other; even as the heathen poet, who was skillful in the very art of loving and lusting, did observe, *Otia si tollas &c.*

*{And walked upon the roof of the King’s house}* Houses in those countries were flat-roofed. See on Deut. 22. 8. Josh. 2. 6. 1 Sam. 9. 25.

*{And from thence he saw a woman washing herself}* To wit, that she might be purified from her legal uncleanness contracted by her monthly disease, according to the law, Levit. 15. 19. and 18. 19. So, v. 4. Now he saw her washing herself, either in her garden near adjoining to his palace, (otherwise he could not have discerned her beauty, if he had been at a further distance) and then she was not free from faultiness, in taking no more care to hide those beauties which being discovered were a bait for lust; especially being so near the king’s court, where usually men are at most leisure, and take most liberty to gaze after wanton objects, their ease and idleness being accompanied with fullness of bread, Ezek. 16. 49. Or, as others think, (judging her to be a virtuous, wise and modest woman, as the Scriptures report of her, Pro. 1. 8. and 6. 20. and 31. 1.) she washed herself in her chamber, and some casement opposite to the palace being accidentally open, David with the quick sight of his lustful eye gazed upon her, and was ensnared with her beauty, so forgetting his own prayer, Psal. 119. 37. and Job’s practice, Job. 31. 1. Thus in the old world before the flood *the sons of God*, which were of the posterity of Sheth, giving their eyes liberty to gaze upon the beauties of *the daughters of men*, which were of Cain’s progeny, were ensnared with lustful desires, *and took them to be their wives*, though never so wicked, Gen. 6. 2.

**V. 3.** {*And David sent and enquired after the woman*} When his wanton eyes had betrayed his heart, and inflamed it with unlawful lust, he should presently have quenched this wildfire, or suddenly cast it out by meditating on the greatness of the sin and the grievousness of the punishment, how displeasing it would be to his good God, and how dishonorable to himself; but contrariwise he revolves it in his mind, and so gives his consent unto it with delight, he first commits adultery with her in his heart, Matth. 5. 28. and never is at rest, in plotting and contriving, till at last he practices it into act.

*{And one said, Is not this Bathsheba?*} i.e. One of the servants, of whom he inquired, strongly affirmed, and peremptorily told him, that it was Bathsheba whom he had seen. 1 Chr. 3. 5. she is called Bathshua.

*{the daughter of Eliam*} Called Ammiel 1 Chron. 3. 5. He is thought by Hierome to be that worthy of David mentioned 2 Sam. 23. 34. who was the son of Ahithophel the Gilonite David’s great counselor.

*{the wife of Uriah the Hittite*} So called, because he was so by nation, and now a proselyte to the Jewish religion. See on 1 Sam. 26. 6. Others think he was born in a place called Heth, near the place of Abraham’s burial, and called a Hittite, though he were a natural Israelite, because born in that country, Gen. 23. 3. He also was one of David’s worthies, 2 Sam. 23. 39. So Bathsheba was an honorable woman both by parentage and also by marriage; and therefore David more faulty in abusing her to satisfy his wanton lust, especially she being another man’s wife, and he having so many of his own, and being at liberty to have taken more, as Nathan afterwards, speaking from God, does aggravate his sin, ch. 12. 3, 4, 8.

**V. 4.** {*And David sent messengers, and took her*} i.e. Caused her to be brought unto him; not signifying, it is like, to his messengers the end of his sending for her; for this had been to proclaim his shame, but pretending some other business. But being come, he allured and persuaded her to commit folly and filthiness with him; and she wanting grace and the fear of God, and that conjugal love and faithfulness which she owed to her worthy husband, consented unto him, being better pleased to be the harlot of a king than the loyal wife of an honest subject.

*{for she was purified from her uncleanness}* Or, *when she had purified her self*. She cleansed herself from her menstrual sickness, (whereby she was apter to conceive) but now defiles herself with moral filthiness of body and soul.

**V. 5.** {*And the woman conceived, and sent and told David, and said, I am with child}* Either she wrote unto him, because letters blush not; or she sent before to signify her coming, that she might have the more free and private access, and when she came, she told him secretly that she was with child. This she did that they might advise together of the likeliest means whereby they might hide their sin and shame; and also that she might avoid the rage of her husband at his coming home, and escape the punishment by law due unto her, which was no less than death to be inflicted on the adulteress.

**V. 6.** {*And David sent to Joab, saying, send me Uriah the Hittite*} Instead of repenting for his sin, that obtaining pardon, he might have peace with God, he falls, in the hardness of his heart, to plot and contrive means to conceal it from the knowledge of man in the meantime not regarding the all-seeing of God, which beheld his close-acted sin, nor his justice and power ready to punish it. Therefore he sends for Uriah, that coming home and lying with his wife he might father his bastardly brood and so clear him from all disgrace; not scrupling most unjustly to thrust in his own son to be Uriah’s heir, and to rob his children, if after he had any lawfully begotten, of their inheritance.

**V. 7.** {*David demanded of him how Joab did, and how the people did, and how the war prospered*} Which questions, though they had some weight and worth in themselves, yet were too idle to be the occasion for which he caused Uriah to be sent unto him. For why should he withdraw so great a worthy from the service of the war to answer such questions as he might be (and no doubt was) daily resolved in by letters and posts that passed to and fro between him and the camp? And these frivolous inquiries might justly make Uriah suspect (and who knows but that they did?) that there was some other cause of his sending for, besides this which was pretended, and so to grow jealous of his beautiful wife, and that that might be the true cause why he could not be persuaded to go home and accompany her.

**V. 8.** {*And David said to Uriah, Go down to thy house, and wash thy feet*} After he had answered his questions, he dismissed him under this pretense, that it was fit for him after such a journey to refresh himself and take his ease; but intending indeed to have him go home and lie with his wife, that so his fault might be cloaked and covered. So that when he bids him *go and wash his feet*, which after a journey they used to do in those hot countries, it was as much as if he had said, Go, recreate and refresh yourself after your travel.

*{and there followed him a mess of meat from the King}* This he sent after him in show of kindness, but with a purpose that being made frolic and jolly by receiving such a favor from the king, and also by faring more plentifully on such dainties, he might be the more apt to desire his wife’s company.

**V. 9.** {*But Uriah slept at the door of the King’s house, with all the servants of his Lord}* Which were appointed to sit there to guard the king’s person. Now God by his secret and all-ruling providence inclined Uriah’s heart to do thus that David’s sin might come to light, notwithstanding all his policies and persuasions to the contrary. And besides, it may more than probably be thought that his fair but false wife came herself, and used many entreaties and much importunity to draw him to his own house, seeing it did so much concern her to enjoy his company to cover her fault and hide her shame.

**V. 10.** {*And when they had told David*} To wit, they who were appointed by him to observe his course and carriage, and so to make relation to him of what they saw.

*{Camest thou not from thy journey?*} When his counsel took not, he further presses him to hearken thereunto by this expostulation; q.d. It might well have beseemed you to have taken mine advice, seeing I not only required you as your king, but as a friend persuaded you to do nothing but that which was for your own ease and welfare, namely to refresh yourself after so long a journey. For Rabbah (as some of the learned think) was so far distant from Jerusalem that it was a journey for footmen of thirty hours.

**V. 11.** {*And Uriah said unto David, The Ark, and Israel and Judah abide in tents*} It is very probable that the ark was at this time with Joab in the camp, seeing they used in great and dangerous wars to carry it along with them, and that both for their better encouragement, it being a visible sign of God’s presence, and also that they might consult with God in their great doubts and difficulties; 1 Sam. 4. 4. and 14. 18. Neither (as I conceive) does he understand it of the ark’s being in the tent which David had prepared for it; seeing it being fixed there, not only at this time, but also before and afterwards, he should upon the same reason have forborne to lie at his own house when the war was ended: and saying jointly, that *the Ark and Israel and Judah did abide in tents*, it seems to imply that they were all alike in tents, and in the same place; though it is not much material of what tent we understand it.

*{and my lord Joab*} Some think he so calls him in a more peculiar relation he had to him above others, as being his armor-bearer. But because for this we have no Scripture grounds, it may be understood that he calls *his Lord Joab* in a common relation, as he was general of the army.

*{shall I then go into mine house, to eat and to drink, &c.*} q.d. How unfitting is it, when my lord and general, so far my superior, and the whole army of the God of Israel, yea the ark of God itself, does abide in tents in the open fields, that I should go home to my mansion and dwelling house, and there solace myself with variety and delights? This speech of Uriah’s might have been a loud peal to awaken David out of his deep sleep of sin and security: he wallowed in unlawful lust and pleasure in the time of common danger, when as Uriah on this occasion made conscience of enjoying those delights which otherwise in themselves were honest and lawful. But it pleased God to leave David to himself, both that by his fearful fall he might be humbled, and also that by his example others might be admonished not to trust in their own strength, (for if cedars fall, what will become of shrubs?) or, being fallen, might be comforted, and encouraged to rise with him by true repentance, and have hope and assurance that they also, as well as he, should have their sin pardoned, seeing God is no accepter of persons, but alike gracious to all that are alike qualified to receive mercy and forgiveness.

*{As thou livest, and as thy soul liveth, I will do this thing*} This is not a double oath, but an earnest asseveration, or more pathetical expression of the same thing, q.d. It is as certain as you are alive, or as sure as I desire that you might still live, that no persuasions shall ever move me to do this thing. All which pious and conscionable care and carriage of Uriah did but tend to aggravate David’s sin in practicing against him; seeing he was nothing affected with it, nor moved at all to desist from his purpose. See on 1 Sam. 20. 3.

**V. 12.** {*And David said to Uriah, Tarry here to day also*} Having his heart hardened through the deceitfulness of his sin, he observes not the passages of God’s providence in crossing him in his design, but still strives against the stream, and will not suffer Uriah to depart till he have tried another sinful conclusion for the attaining to his end.

**V. 13.** {*And when David had called him, he did eat and drink before him, and made him drunk*} Hereby intending both to make him forget his oath: That he would not go home to his house; and also more apt to desire his wife’s company, being inflamed and intoxicated with wine, to which he could not persuade him whilst he was sober. And here we have in Uriah an example of human frailty, if man be left to himself. No doubt, he who so resolutely refused to enjoy his wife’s company, and to take his ease and lawful pleasure in his own house, did much abhor to use excess, and drink himself drunk, in the presence of the king; but now he is made so frolic and jovial by the apprehension of this special favor from his sovereign, that forgetting his former austere resolution, he falls into the sin of drunkenness, David, as it seems, pressing him to drink more than he desired, seeing it is said, not that he allowed him plenty of wine, but that *he made him drunk*, by causing him to be plied with cup after cup. And we have in David an extreme and horrid wickedness which even God’s dearest servants are apt to fall into, if he leave them in the time of temptation to their own strength; who, though in the whole course of his life he was *a man according to Gods own heart*, and *had respect to all his commandments*, does now, to avoid the deserved shame of his sin, and to hide it from the sight of men, draw his faithful servant not only into the bestial sin of drunkenness, but also into the impious sin of perjury, as much as in him did lie; but God by a strange and strong providence preserved him from it. The like example also we have in Peter, who being left by God to his own strength, or rather weakness, did for fear with cursing and swearing deny and abjure his Lord and Master. Which may teach the best to pray often and earnestly that God will *not lead them into temptation*, or at least that he will *deliver them from evil*.

*{but went not down to his house*} Notwithstanding all David’s devices and practices he remained firm and constant in his resolution.

**V. 14.** {*And it came to pass in the morning that David wrote a letter to Joab}* When all his plots and policies failed for the effecting of his design, which was to hide his adultery, he did by a letter call in Joab to his assistance, that by his help he might take away Uriah’s life by such means as might not come to light, and so might have his sin concealed. Observe here David’s fearful progress in sin, from one degree to another, and from a lesser to a greater. Having given way to sloth and idleness unbecoming a king, he falls into wantonness and lust; and this fire being kindled, and he using no means to quench it, it flames out to the act of adultery: and not repenting of it, he takes care only to hide his shame, not to be cleansed from his sin, and so seeks by wicked policies to attain his end. For first he draws Uriah his faithful servant to commit the sin of drunkenness, that he might forget and falsify his oath, and by lying with his wife might palliate his adulterous filthiness. And when this would not take, rather than he would fail of his end, he contrives his death, and that by such means as tended to the heartening of the enemy, by the ruin of divers others of the army in a treacherous way, their fellow soldiers and comrades deserting them in the fight. And now having lost his sense and understanding by running on in this labyrinth and maze of wickedness, and being grown stupid and hardened with the deceitfulness of sin, he lies sleeping and snorting in this spiritual lethargy a long time together, till God was pleased to use the ministry of the prophet Nathan to waken him out of it, and bring him to repentance. Thus David, who had such a tender conscience that *his heart smote him* for cutting off the lap of Saul’s garment, being left to himself, grows so hard-hearted and senseless in sinning that he scruples not to murder his faithful and innocent servant, (yea because he was innocent and simple-hearted) and together with him divers others; and also draws on Joab to partake with him in the same wickedness, as though it were not enough to go alone in the way of death and destruction, unless others by his persuasion did bear him company. So great cause we have to pray daily unto God, not to lead into, or at least not to leave us in temptation, seeing holy David being thus left sinned more fearfully than wicked Ahab: he coveted but the vineyard of his subject and neighbor, but David the wife, yea, even the life of his faithful and valiant servant and soldier; and that because he could not draw him from his singleness and simplicity to serve his own turn.

**V. 16.** {*And it came to pass when Joab observed the city}* To wit, that he might find out in what part of it the stoutest soldiers manned the walls. No sooner had he received commands from his king, but presently he puts them in execution, not caring whether they were right or wrong, nor how unpleasing to God, so he might please his sovereign, upon whose favor he wholly depended for worldly preferments. It may be David in his letter pretended that Uriah was guilty of some fault for which he deserved death, though for some reasons he was unwilling to have it known; but Joab knew well enough that no offender ought to die upon the king’s bare command without a legal trial, nor unless he were convicted by two or three witnesses; and, if Uriah deserved to die, yet not in this manner, wherein others that were innocent must die with him. And therefore it might well be thought that Joab herein had no other aim than to please the king, and (it may be) to recover his favor to the full height, which was lessened and abated by his murder of Abner, when himself should become as guilty as he of the like act.

**V. 17.** {*And the men of the city went out*} Those valiant men which Joab had observed sallied out of the gate of the city where assault was given, and charging with that part of the army in which Uriah was placed in the front, they slew some of them, and Uriah with the rest.

**V. 18.** {*Then Joab sent and told David*} i.e. He devised and put into the messenger’s mouth a form of speech, setting a fair color on a foul cause, and daubing over a rotten ruinous building with untempered mortar, to please David, if he found him to be displeased with the loss. But it seems that howsoever the messenger delivered the main substance of the matter, yet he left out the enlargements, as superfluous and needless, seeing David made no such objections against his service, but was well enough pleased with the slaughter of others, when as he heard that Uriah was slain with them.

**V. 21.** {*Who smote Abimelech the son of Jerubbesheth?*} Judg. 9. 53. i.e. the son of Gideon, called Jerubbaal Judg. 7. 1. but here Jerubbesheth, because the Hebrews in detestation of idols did expunge the word *Baal* out of their names, and put *Bosheth* or *Besheth* in the place of it, signifying *an infamous thing*, as the idol was, Hos. 9. 10. Compare 1 Chr. 8. 33. with 2 Sam. 2. 8. and 1 Chr. 8. 34. with 2 Sam. 4. 4. where Eshbaal and Meribbaal in the one place are called Ishbosheth and Mephibosheth in the other.

**V. 23.** {*Surely the men prevailed against us*} i.e. The valiant soldiers of Rabbah, that were besieged, sallied out upon us.

*{and we were upon them*} i.e. Fought with them, and forced them to retire, pursuing them to the very gates, and so in heat of war came too near the wall, and lost some of our men.

**V. 25.** {*say to Joab, Let not this thing displease thee*} Here he dissembles with the messenger, and slights the slaughter of his men, to the end that neither his bloody and cruel commandment, nor Joab’s wicked obedience might be discovered.

*{the sword devoureth one as well as another*} i.e. The event of war is various, and men are slain sometimes on the one side, and sometimes on the other: therefore we must be content, and bear with patience such accidents when they happen, seeing it is in vain to trouble ourselves too much with such things as cannot be prevented.

*{and encourage thou him*} i.e. As my messenger, and in my name, encourage and comfort him, that he be not too much dejected with this loss.

**V. 26.** {*And when Uriah’s wife heard that Uriah her husband was dead, she mourned for her husband}* So Gen. 50. 3. The time of mourning, as Josephus writes, lasted but seven days; so Gen. 50. 10. 1 Sam. 31. 13. Ecclus. 22. 12. But their more solemn mournings lasted thirty days, Deut. 34. 8. This of Bathsheba was perhaps a merry mourning, only in a formal, ceremonial and counterfeit show, to blear and blind the eyes of the people, because by her husband’s death she was not only freed from his wrath and rage, and the punishment which the law inflicted upon the adulteress, but also was in hope to become a queen: but she had cause enough of grief, and even of heart-bleeding and heart-breaking mourning, when she considered that by her sin she had caused his untimely death: although David’s sin was much more heinous, who, being a king and a prophet, had allured her to uncleanness, and had also plotted and contrived Uriah’s death by the sword of God’s enemies.

**V. 27.** {*And when the time of mourning was past*} Which no doubt was as short as might be, that David taking her to wife the sooner, she might be thought to be with child by him after they were married.

*{But the thing that David had done displeased the Lord*} i.e. His whole carriage in the matter of Uriah. For though David were a man after God’s own heart, and greatly in his love in respect of his person, yet he hated and abhorred his sin as heinous and abominable, and did severely correct him for it; teaching us hereby to follow his example, and in our greatest love of the person to hate the sin, and in our greatest hatred of sin to love the person.

## CHAPTER 12

**Verse 1.** {*And the Lord sent Nathan unto David*} Or, *wherefore the Lord, &c.* i.e. Because David, who was beloved of God and belonged to his election, had committed heinous sins which were displeasing unto him, and had now long continued in them without repentance, even for the space of ten months together, from the conception to the birth of his child, notwithstanding that all that time he did in a formal manner use and enjoy God’s ordinances and the means of his worship and service; and so would have lived still in this impenitency, to his perdition, if God had still left him to himself and the hardness of his own heart; for who fallen into a lethargy can awaken himself? Therefore the Lord of his free grace, when ordinary means prevailed not, sent his holy prophet to rouse him out of this dead sleep, and by convincing him of his sin to bring him to repentance; seeing all the care that David took for himself, was only to hide his sin, and to avoid the shame, and not to be cleansed and freed from the guilt of it; even as men sick of a bodily lethargy, apprehending no danger, and being insensible of any pain, are willing to continue sleeping still, and are loath to be molested by being awakened.

*{and he came unto him, and said unto him, There were two men in one city, the one rich, and the other poor*} Nathan’s God put into Nathan’s mouth this parable, as the fittest means to deal with such a person. For though in his absolute power he be able by plain and direct terms to do what he pleases, yet ordinarily he uses those means which are most likely to take effect with the persons to whom he applies them. Now parables usually stir men up to more attention, we being more apt to listen after new things, or things propounded in a new manner, than when they are spoken in a common and ordinary way: and they make us also more equal and indifferent in hearkening to what is said. Generally men are very partial judges in their own causes, being blinded with pride and self-love: and though they can see motes in other men’s eyes, yet they cannot discern beams in their own; or if haply they see them, yet they are hardly brought ingenuously to confess them, without mincing, excusing and extenuating, unless it be presented unto them in the persons of others. And this in a more special manner is the property of great men, princes and kings, who think it harsh and uncivil usage to be reproved for their faults and failings by their subjects and inferiors. But when sin is brought to the bar and arraigned before them in other men’s persons, they in a sort retain the power and privilege of judicature, belonging to their place, in their own hand; and when they have passed the sentence of condemnation impartially against others, then either conscience of itself, or, if asleep, being awakened by some Nathan, brings the matter home, and makes it their own case, as in this place. Thus God deals with the Jews, Isai. 5. 2. Jotham with the Israelites, Judg. 9. 8–16. and our Savior with the priests and Pharisees, Luke 7. 41, 43. Matth. 21. 33, 41. Now in expounding of a parable we are chiefly to look at the main scope and drift intended in it, and not to every circumstantial passage, which is as it were but the outward dress and ornament to deck and beautify it. As here in this parable Nathan’s chief aim and scope is, to awaken David out of his deep sleep of sin and security, by causing him unknowingly to pass an impartial sentence against himself in the person of another, that so he might be brought to the sight of his sin and to unfeigned repentance. See ch. 14. 6. 1 kings 20. 35, 41.

**V. 2.** {*The rich man had exceeding many flocks and herds*} By the rich man he understands David, whom God had enriched with abundance of blessings, and permitted him to enjoy many wives and concubines, which best pleased him, having power in his hand to make lawful choice where he would.

**V. 3.** {*But the poor man had nothing*} By him is meant Uriah, who was but poor in comparison of his king, and had but only one wife. But some write he had another before, by whom he had divers children, with whom Bathsheba his young and tender wife, whom he had lately married, did live, together with him, as the words following seem to imply; though I think rather they are added to set forth and formalize the parable. And she is here compared, *to a little ewe lamb*, or a young cade, which wanting a dam to suckle it is brought up by hand. This lamb he is said *to have bought*, because in those days they gave dowries to their wives, and had not (as our manner is) portions with them. The same also he is said to have *fed with his own meat*, or, as it is in the original, *with his own morsel*; whereby is meant, that he dearly and tenderly loved it, as it had been his own and only *daughter*; so as though he had so little that it was scarce sufficient to nourish himself, yet he would spare some of it, even as it were out of his own mouth and stomach, to nourish his dear-beloved lamb.

*{save one little ewe lamb*} This also did the more endear his love and affection, because he had only one. For as a stream divided runs more weakly, so is it with the affections. An only son, or an only wife, having the whole and sole interest in the love of the father or husband, are more dearly beloved than when many sons or wives have the same divided amongst them.

*{and lay in his bosom*} i.e. It was as near and dear to him as his own young and tender child. By which is set forth that Bathsheba being Uriah’s only jewel and chief delight, he made her partner and partaker with him in all the blessings he enjoyed.

**V. 4.** {*And there came a traveller to the rich man*} By the *traveler* some expositors understand fleshly lust and concupiscence, which David feasted with this lamb; others, the devil, who was as well pleased by David’s entertaining his temptations, and committing adultery, as if he had made him a feast. But it is not much material whether of them is meant, seeing they are both alike pleased with sinful entertainment: and besides, it is but a complement and formality of the parable, tending to set forth the main end of it; which was to show David’s merciless cruelty and oppression of Uriah his faithful servant, in taking and abusing his dear wife, to feast the devil, and to feed and foment his own filthy lust.

**V. 5.** {*And David’s anger was greatly kindled}* To wit, because with a just and impartial eye he looked upon this sin in another man’s person as horrible and hateful, though himself had committed it, and hugged it in his bosom, with much delight.

*{As the Lord liveth, the man that hath done this thing, shall surely die*} Or, *is worthy to die*. So Eph. 2. 3. This sentence some think David passed, being transported with some choler and indignation against such a covetous and griping oppressor. For by God’s law the thief was to restore five oxen, and four sheep, for one, Exo. 22. 1. and not to die for the offense. Or he thus speaks only to aggravate the heinousness of the fact, and not to determine the punishment; as if a judge, to show how greatly he abhorred the malefactor’s heinous crime, should say he deserved to be twice hanged, or many deaths, though it be not in his power to inflict more than one. But in the execution of positive laws it is in the judge’s power to alter the penalty and punishment; and though he may not (as some think) lessen it, lest he might seem to arrogate to himself more mercy than was in God that did appoint it; yet he may increase and heighten it according as the sin is aggravated, either in respect of its extraordinary degree, or else in respect of circumstances. And thus this rich man’s theft, being not barely so, a sin against the eighth commandment, but also joined with transcendent cruelty and oppression, which was a breach of the sixth, might be justly punished by the magistrate even with death itself.

**V. 6.** {*And he shall restore the lamb fourfold}* This word being of the dual number, some understand *twice four*, i.e. eightfold; doubling his penalty upon him, *because he had no pity*. And it seems the penalty of theft varied, seeing in Solomon’s time there was required *seven-fold restitution*, Pro. 6. 31. unless it may be meant there that the thief shall make full and perfect restitution; in which sense the number *seven* is often taken. Now if it be here meant, that he shall both die and also restore, it was to show David’s greater abhorrency of so foul a fact, seeing he adjudged him not only worthy of death, but also worthy to make restitution to the poor man; because his death, though it might satisfy his mind, yet could not repair his estate which was damnified by him; therefore there must be also made some considerable restitution.

**V. 7.** {*And Nathan said to David, Thou art the man*} i.e. Who have committed this heinous offense, and on whom you have passed this heavy sentence of death.

*{thus saith the Lord——I anointed thee King over Israel*} The Lord puts David in mind of all his former benefits, to aggravate his disobedience and unthankfulness, and to bring him to unfeigned repentance. Neither does this cross that Jam. 1. 5. *that God giveth to all men liberally, and upbraideth no man*: seeing that may well be understood of prayer, unto the frequent use of which the apostle there exhorts, saying, that God does not challenge any for their often asking, nor deny their suits because he has already done much for them; as it is the manner of men to upbraid those with former benefits who seem too bold in importuning them to grant new suits: thus God upbraids none; but the oftener men come, the better welcome; and the more importunate they are, the more like to speed. Or else, God does not upbraid men with his benefits to shame and reproach them, but only puts his servants in mind of them when they ungratefully forget them, that by this means he may bring them to repentance. And thus he deals with David in this place.

**V. 8.** {*And I gave thee thy master’s house}* i.e. The kingdom of Saul, who was your lord and master.

*{and thy master’s wives into thy bosom}* Not to marry them: for that had been incest, Levit. 18. 15. Seeing David having married Michal Saul’s daughter, his wives became his mothers-in-law. Neither do we read that David ever took any of Saul’s wives and concubines to become his: nor had it been wisdom in him to take any that were so near his enemy into his bosom. Neither is it likely he should desire it, in respect of their age. Nor do I think that by this phrase of *giving them into his bosom* is only meant that he had his choice of all the women that were under Saul’s subjection; for all the women in the land had been his subjects whilst he reigned; and so any other might in that sense have taken Saul’s wives as well as David, that is, such as had been under his government. The rabbis say that Eglah and Rizpah are here meant. But neither do we read that Eglah was Saul’s, or Rizpah David’s wife. I conceive the meaning is, that God had exalted David to such regal power and dignity, that all generally that belonged to Saul, were at his disposing; yea, even his wives and concubines, which were most near and dear unto him, were so in any lawful way; yea, in respect of his sovereign power, he might have taken them into his own bosom and bed, seeing there was none that could hinder him, if he in his own conscience could have dispensed with God’s law. Neither does God therein approve of such a fact as taking of Saul’s wives, no nor yet of polygamy; for it was not a gift of allowance, but of providence, tolerating for a time the plurality of wives. So Shimei cursed David, not by God’s allowance, but by his sufferance, ch. 16. 10, 11. though it be there said that *God had bidden him*.

*{And if that had been too little, I would moreover have given unto thee such and such things*} i.e. As I am able, so I would have been willing to have satisfied your heart’s desire to the full, by giving unto you, in a lawful way and by lawful means, more and greater things than all these, Psal. 37. 4.

**V. 9.** {*Wherefore hast thou despised the Covenant of the Lord*} By transgressing the second table of the law, the sum whereof is, that we should *Love our neighbours as ourselves*, Give everyone his own, and *Do to others as we would have them do unto us*. This law David had grievously transgressed by his sins of adultery and murder, and hoped by policies and shifts to have concealed them, though God, who sees all secrets, did behold him. And for this contempt he is here reproved.

*{thou hast killed Uriah the Hittite with the sword*} i.e. Not an ordinary man, or one worthless and wicked, but one of your great worthies, who feared God, and did you faithful service: him, instead of rewarding you have shamefully murdered. David was the chief contriver of his death; he was slain by his command: Joab was an accessory and a willing actor in this fearful tragedy; and the Ammonites only, and that unknowingly, their instruments.

*{and hast taken his wife to be thy wife*} Making way to the marriage by the murder of her husband, to conceal your adultery and cover your shame.

*{and hast slain him with the sword of the children of Ammon*} This is added as a great aggravation of David’s sin, both because Uriah was most treacherously betrayed into the Ammonites’ hands, and together with him divers others of the army; and also because hereby God’s people had some cause of discouragement, and their enemies being heartened with this victory, would the more stand on their strength, and make the more desperate resistance; yea, would take occasion hereby to insult over God’s people and their religion, seeing he had given them no better success.

**V. 10.** {*Now therefore the sword shall never depart from thine house*} Here God threatens to punish and correct David by retaliation or law of requital, like for like, that by the punishment he might be led as it were by the hand to the sight of his sins; as a dog that is beaten before the sheep which he has worried. He had sinned by killing Uriah with the sword, and therefore those of his family must with the sword kill one another; yea, the sword must never depart from his house; according to that of our Savior, Mat. 26. 52. *All that take the sword, shall perish with the sword.* So Gen. 9. 6. Rev. 13. 10. By *never*, some understand unto *the coming of Christ* the king of peace: and indeed that period of time is sometimes signified by this phrase. So this threatening should relate, not only to that blood which was shed in David’s time, but also to the wars which followed his days, between his posterity and the ten tribes, with many other enemies, as the Edomites, Moabites, Ammonites, Assyrians and Chaldeans. But I rather conceive that by *never* here is meant a long time, or as long as David lived, as the word is used Gen. 43. 9. 1 Sam. 1. 22. Deut. 15. 17. and that the threatening has reference to the sword threatened in David’s children, Amnon, Absalom and Adonijah, and to those civil and unnatural wars raised against him by treacherous Absalom, who came out of his own loins.

*{because thou hast despised me*} To wit, by willful violating of my law, not regarding my omniscience nor my justice, but only caring to hide your wickedness from the sight of men.

**V. 9.** {*Behold, I will raise up evil against thee out of thine own house*} i.e. For your sins I will afflict you with many evils by means of your own children, giving them over to commit rapes, murders and unnatural rebellions. This was verified in Amnon, Absalom, Adonijah, and others of David’s posterity, who perished by the sword. For though God was not the author of these evils as they were sins, yet he had a hand in them as they were punishments, Amos 3. 6. Isa. 45. 7. And as they were sins, the power of acting was from him; *for in him we live, and move, and have our being*, Act. 17. 28. though the malignity of the actions was from the agents; as the rider makes his lame horse go, but his halting is from himself. Yea, God’s providence has a hand in the evil of sin itself, not in acting it, (for that is impossible, seeing he is the supreme good, and the fountain of all goodness, from whom no evil can spring and flow) but in ordering and disposing of it, so as that which is evil in itself may tend to that which is good; as appears in the sin of Joseph’s brethren selling him into Egypt, whereby Jacob and his family were preserved in the time of famine; and in that grand sin of Judas, the priests, Pilate, and the soldiers, in crucifying Christ, which God made to be the means of man’s redemption. Yea, God sometimes punishes one sin with another, not by infusing of sinful corruption, but by an active permission, leaving men to the corruption of their own hearts, and withdrawing his grace: and so the objects of sin being presented unto them, they lay hold on them, and being ensnared, they fall into sin to their perdition. And this is the greatest punishment that God inflicts on wicked men in this life, and that which David wishes his desperate enemies, Psal. 69. 27.

*{And I will take thy wives*} i.e. Your concubines.

*{before thine eyes*} i.e. In your lifetime. And you shall as certainly know it, as if with your eyes you did see it, ch. 16. 22.

*{and give them to thy neighbour*} i.e. To one that is most near unto you, not only in habitation, but also in blood. Which did much aggravate David’s affliction, Psal. 55. 12.

*{And he shall lie with thy wives in the sight of this Sun*} i.e. In the open day and light of the sun, which shall discover his foul fact to the sight of all Israel. For Absalom did act this foul villainy, not in a secret corner, but in the open view, a tent being spread for him on the housetop for this purpose, ch. 16. 22. and it is not improbable, on the top of that very palace from whence David spying Bathsheba lusted after her, ch. 11. 2.

**V. 12.** {*For thou didst it secretly*} As fearing men more than me, and shame more than sin.

*{But I will do this thing before all Israel, and before the Sun*} i.e. Openly and at noonday, in the public view of all the people, that I may purposely bring upon you that shame and disgrace which you did more carefully avoid than my displeasure. And whereas he says, *I will do it*, it is as if he had said, I will justly give over your son to his own ambition and base lusts, and not restrain him, as I could, Hos. 4. 14. and will by providence so order his sins that they shall become a just chastisement on you; though he thereby shall be no whit excused, seeing he will act his own part freely, wickedly and rebelliously, and to his own wretched ends, without any respect at all to my secret counsel.

**V. 13.** {*And David said unto Nathan, I have sinned against the Lord*} He had also sinned against Uriah and Bathsheba, and, as the prodigal speaks, *against heaven*; but here he says *he had sinned against the Lord*, and Psal. 51. 4. *Against thee, against thee only, have I sinned*; because sin, against whomsoever it be committed, is the transgression of God’s law, which makes it to be sin; and also because, being the supreme magistrate, he had no superior judge to call him to account or punish him for his sin. This confession, though very short, yet was effectual and accepted of God, because it was joined with true contrition and unfeigned repentance in the sincerity of his heart, which God respects more than verbal expressions. Yea, it may be, David’s confession was therefore so short and abrupt, because his heart was so oppressed with grief that he could not utter more words: for small griefs speak and are talkative, when great ones are silent, or expressed in few words. But afterwards, when his heart was a little unburdened and eased, sorrow having found a vent, he at large expresses it, purposely penning the one and fiftieth Psalm to lay open his sin and sorrow, yea and to proclaim his shame: which he had formerly so carefully covered; and not contenting himself to pen a psalm on this subject, he also prefixes a title to show the occasion, *A Psalm of David, when Nathan the Prophet came unto him, after he had gone in to Bathsheba*. Neither does he think this enough, unless he commit it to *the chief Musician* to be sung publicly in the congregation as one of the penitential psalms. On the contrary, Saul’s confession, though more full and large in words, 1 Sam. 15. 24. was not regarded, because it proceeded not from a true penitent heart, but was in mere hypocrisy; and also because he was a man rejected of God, whereas David was beloved, and in the state of grace by free election.

*{And Nathan said unto David, The Lord also hath put away thy sin*} To wit, out of his sight; and has so covered it that it shall never be imputed unto you to hinder your eternal blessedness, Psal. 32. 1. yet howsoever he has fully pardoned it in respect of the guilt, he will notwithstanding chastise you for it as a loving father for your spiritual and everlasting good, Num. 23. 21. Mic. 7. 18. Rom. 8. 28.

*{thou shalt not die*} i.e. Neither everlastingly, nor by any sudden stroke of a temporal judgment, as you might have cause to fear, because the Lord has threatened that *the sword shall not depart from thy house*, v. 10. and also because your sin has deserved it according to your own sentence. And here we have a lively representation of a flat opposition between the sentence of the law and the gospel, not in themselves, but in respect of the subjects upon whom they are pronounced. The law says, to wit, to them that are under the law, *The Adulterer shall die the death*: but the gospel says, *He shall not die*; to wit, if by a lively faith he be under grace, and in Christ, who has paid his debt, and satisfied for his sin by dying for him.

**V. 14.** {*Howbeit, because by this deed thou hast given great occasion to the enemies of the Lord to blaspheme*} i.e. Not only to the Ammonites, whom you have made to triumph and insult over God’s people, as though he was unable to defend them, Isa. 36. 15. but also to other heathen nations, and even to all the wicked among your own people, who, when they shall hear or see that one whom I have chosen king, and so much favored, has committed as foul and heinous sins as the very heathens, or as Saul himself whom I rejected, thereupon will be ready to censure me as unjust and partial, conniving at those sins in mine own servants which I punish in others; yea, thereupon they will take occasion to blaspheme my true religion, and the professors of it, as though either it taught or favored such wickedness, or at least the professors are all hypocrites, making only a show of godliness and honesty in their lives, but denying the power of it: this, say they, such examples of their wickedness manifest, they are all alike. So Rom. 2. 24. And therefore by my corrections inflicted on you I will vindicate my justice and the truth of my religion, and first, by the death of your child begotten in adultery.

*{the child also that is born unto thee, shall surely die*} This is only to be understood of temporal death, seeing no child dies eternally for the sin of his parents, according to Ezek. 18. 4. *The soul that sinneth it shall die*. But God punishes children, *to the third and fourth generation of them that hate him*, with temporal punishments, and even death itself; not only because children are accounted parts of their parents, and their goods, which may justly suffer for the owner’s sake; but chiefly because parents are only the occasion of their children’s punishments, but the cause is in themselves, to wit their original sin and corruption of nature in which they were conceived, and which in itself deserving death, God may justly inflict it upon any occasion, when, and how he will. But in the death of David’s child there was mercy mixed with judgment, seeing had it lived, it would have been unto the parents a continual grief, representing daily unto them their sin and shame.

**V. 16.** {*David therefore besought God for the child*} i.e. He prayed, fasted and humbled himself with inward contrition of heart and outward afflicting of his body, that he might obtain the life of the child, not only because it was dear to him and his wife, but because they looked upon its death as the punishment of their sin. And herein he sought not to oppose and cross God’s will (for when by his work his will was clearly revealed unto him, he submitted unto it with all meekness and patience, v. 20.) but not knowing whether God’s threatening was absolute or conditional, if he did not use the means to avert the judgment, he thus humbled himself before God by unfeigned repentance. And that he did it with this mind, appears plainly by his own words, ver. 22. See Jer. 18. 8. Ezek. 33. 10, 11. according to the example of Hezekiah, and the Ninevites, Isa. 38. 1, 5. Jonah 3. 9, 10. And this David did, hoping for good success, because God in his rich mercy had pardoned his sin, and comforted him with his promise that he should not die.

*{and went in*} To wit, unto his inner chamber or closet.

**V. 17.** {*And the Elders of his house arose*} i.e. Like faithful subjects and servants, they addressed themselves to comfort him in his mourning; but he refused it.

**V. 18.** {*And it came to pass on the seventh day, that the child died}* Some understand the seventh day from the sickening of the child; others from the birth of it, and consequently, the day before it was to have been circumcised; from whence they gather, that since this child died without circumcision, and yet David was cheered and comforted after its death, therefore he was not of their mind who think that children dying before they receive the sacrament, the outward seal of the covenant, are in a dangerous and damnable condition, when as it is not neglected, but cannot, according to the institution, be rightly administered, the child being taken away by sudden or unexpected death. Indeed otherwise God is displeased when we neglect his ordinances, which we may enjoy in a right manner; as we see in the example of Moses, whom the Lord threatened to kill because he had neglected to circumcise his son, Exod. 4. 24.

*{we spake unto him, and he would not hearken*} i.e. If he were impatient of any comfort, when yet, the child being alive, there was some hope, how will he torment himself with grief when the case by the death of the child is quite become desperate?

**V. 19.** {*But when David saw that his servants whispered*} To wit, secretly one with another that he might not hear, he suspected that the child was dead, and that they kept it secret for fear of grieving him.

**V. 20.** {*Then David arose from the earth, and washed*} When he saw God’s will by his work, he patiently submitted unto it, and ceasing to mourn and pray against that which God had revealed to be his good pleasure, he changing his habit, and with it his heart, addressed himself to do God that service unto which he now called him.

*{and came into the house of the Lord, and worshipped*} i.e. The tent which he had erected for the ark. By the law it was provided, that when a man died in a tent, all that came into that tent, and all in it, should be unclean seven days, Num. 19. 14. and whilst he was unclean he might not come into the sanctuary. But David, who was a careful observer of the law, presently upon the death of his child washed himself, and went into God’s house, and worshiped. Whereby it appeared that either this uncleanness was not contracted by being in the same house wherein one died, if they were not also in the same room, or that this child died in some other house near the king’s, into which it was put, that the king might not be further grieved by being an eye or ear-witness of this poor infant’s pain and misery who was already too apprehensive of it.
