And so being free from legal uncleanness, he hastens to God’s house, as being the fittest place for receiving comfort in this great affliction, and that he might there worship God, (whom we are bound to bless with Job, for taking away his temporal blessings as well as for giving them, and receiving evil as well as good, Job 1. 21. & 2. 10.) and that for many causes, and in divers respects; as, to praise him for all his rich mercies which he still enjoyed, for the pardon of his great sins, and preserving his life from death, which he had justly deserved, for giving him patience to submit unto his correcting hand, and for working in him the grace of repentance, when as he was so fearfully hardened in sin and impenitency; and also to pray unto God, either that in his mercy he would lighten and lessen those punishments he had threatened against his family, or at least sanctify them to their use, and had given strength and patience to him, that he might be able to bear them.

*{then he came to his own house, and when he required, they set bread before him, and he did eat*} Though he had fasted seven days whilst the child lay sick, eating only some small repast in the evening, yet so earnest he was to perform these holy duties of God’s service, and to ratify his peace with him, that he would not taste of any food before he had first been at God’s house.

**V. 21.** {*Then said his servants unto him, What thing is this that thou hast done?*} Though David had done nothing but what was agreeable to reason and religion, yet being contrary to the corrupt custom of those times, (as it is also of ours) his servants question him for it, as a thing strange in their apprehension.

**V. 22.** {*While the child was yet alive, I fasted and wept*} In hope that God would reverse his sentence of death, if conditional: as Isa. 38. 1 John 3. 4.

*{For I said*} i.e. I thought within myself that it was not known to any man whether upon my repentance God would spare the child, or no; seeing it was doubtful whether the threatening were absolute or conditional, till God had revealed his will by his work.

**V. 23.** {*Can I bring him back again?*} i.e. Restore him to life.

*{I shall go to him*} i.e. Into the state of the dead; but *he shall not return to me*, into the state of the living. But why, if he did not think it fit to mourn for the death of his child, did he so lament the death of Saul, Jonathan, Abner, and Absalom? I answer, Because in some of them the commonwealth had loss by their death, and others of them died in their sins. See on 1 Sam. 28. 19.

**V. 24.** {*And David comforted Bath-sheba his wife*} As he had been a means to draw her to sin and uncleanness, and so to bring upon them this judgment of losing their son by death; so now, she being troubled and grieved with this great affliction, like a good and faithful husband he endeavors to communicate unto her such comfort as himself had been comforted with; as, That God in mercy had pardoned their sin and freed them from that death which by their fearful fall they had deserved, and that their child was in a state of blessedness, and freed from sin and sorrow.

*{And she bare a son, and he called his name Solomon*} That is, *Peaceable*; because he was to enjoy a peaceable reign, as a type of Christ *the Prince of peace*, Isa. 9. 6. This name David gave him by God’s own appointment, 1 Chr. 22. 9.

*{and the Lord loved him*} Or, *for the Lord loved him*. i.e. Of his free grace, before he had done either good or evil. Herein also Solomon was a type of Christ, the son of God’s love, in whom alone he is well pleased; by whom all his elect being reconciled unto him in the body of his flesh, through death, he has made peace with them, given them in this life peace of conscience, and will perfect their peace in his heavenly kingdom, Col. 1. 21. Rom. 5. 1, 15, 16. Luke 2. 14. Joh. 14. 27. & 16. 33.

**V. 25.** {*And he sent by the hand of Nathan*} God used the ministry of Nathan to comfort David, by bringing unto him this joyful tidings, whom he had formerly sent to convince him of sin, and to threaten against him and his family his heavy judgment; and so he cured and healed him with the same hand and lancet that had given the wound.

*{and he called his name Jedidiah*} That is, *Beloved of the Lord*. And thus he called him, *because the Lord* by Nathan appointed this name to be given him; and *because*, also *of the Lord* Christ, God’s best beloved, in whom alone Solomon was, and all the elect are beloved of God, Mat. 3. 17.

**V. 26.** {*And Joab fought against Rabbah of the children of Ammon*} i.e. Assaulted it fiercely that he might take it. He had now continued the siege the greatest part of the year, having had more slow success than the Israelites usually had in their expeditions: of which the cause might well be David’s sin; for how could his army prosper abroad, when he at home was guilty of so much wickedness? But he having now by true repentance made his peace with God, God begins to give him and his army good success in their undertakings.

*{and took the royal City*} i.e. That part of the city in which the royal palace stood, environed with waters both for safety and delight.

**V. 27.** {*And have taken the City of waters*} Hence some gather that this Rabbah, the chief city of the Ammonites, consisted of two parts, which were like two cities conjoined, as Ramathaim-Zophim, 1 Sam. 1. 1. And one of them (called *the Royal City*, because the king’s palace stood in it, and *the City of waters* because environed with the river Jabbok) Joab has now taken, and was also in good forwardness to take the other; and therefore sent unto David to come with an army, that he might bear the name of it. Others (and upon good grounds) think that he had not taken already the royal city, but that he was in taking it, and near the taking of it, as the vulgar Latin reads it, and as the words here used may signify: for if he had already taken the royal city, how should David’s coming prevent his having the name of that chief part which he had conquered? and if the royal city were taken before David’s coming, then it is very likely the king also was taken, and with him his crown, seeing his royal palace was there; whereas it is said that David at his coming took *the Kings crown from off his head*. v. 30.

**V. 28.** {*Now therefore gather the rest of the people together, and encamp against the city*} The strength of Joab’s army was able to have done it alone; for they had already taken the strongest piece, or at least were near the taking of it; but because he would have David to have the glory of the victory, he advises him to bring new forces to color his conquest. And this he did, because kings are jealous of their honor, and cannot with patience brook that their subjects should dazzle their glory, as David before had found by experience in Saul, 1 Sam. 18. 8. Hereby we also may learn, when we get victories over our spiritual enemies, not to arrogate the praise unto ourselves, but to ascribe the whole glory unto our king and sovereign Jesus Christ, in whose strength alone we overcome in deed and truth, and not only in semblance and show, as David in this place: for he is jealous of his honor, and will not give it to another, Isa. 42. 8.

*{and take it*} To wit, that you may have the honor of taking it, and not I, seeing the conqueror usually has the glory of the conquest.

**V. 29.** {*And David gathered all the people*} Having made his peace with God, he bethinks himself fit to fight against his enemies; and having recovered his former courage and valor, which was weakened by the guilt and filth of sin, he cheerfully undertakes this expedition, and acts the part of a valiant general in sacking of Rabbah.

**V. 30.** {*And he took their King’s crown from off his head}* Which either he had put on purposely upon David’s approach, that he might maintain as long as he could his royal magnificence and state, and that thus appearing like himself, as a king to a king, David might use him with more respect, or at least less disgrace; or else it was put on his head by others, and at David’s coming taken off his, and put on David’s, to show that he was now degraded of his royal dignity, and David invested therewith; a just punishment inflicted on this wicked king for his dishonoring David’s ambassadors in that disgraceful manner. It is very probable that his degrading was accompanied with his death, and that he being deposed and slain, his brother Shobi the son of Nahash was by David’s appointment substituted in his place: which was the cause why he afterwards showed such great kindness to David when he was pursued by Absalom, ch. 17. 27, 28.

*{the weight whereof was a talent of gold*} If we reckon according to the weight of the sanctuary, the crown here weighed above a hundred pounds, and so was as heavy as the golden candlestick, Exod. 25. 39. but if according to the ordinary talent, it weighed sixty-four pounds, eight ounces and a quarter, of Troy weight: either of which, especially the first, being too heavy to be worn ordinarily, we are to conceive that it was but a crown of state, or a royal crown, which was held or hanged over their heads, as they sat in their chair of state; or if at all it were set upon their heads as an ensign of state and royalty, it was only at their coronation, or some other great and extraordinary solemnity.

**V. 31.** {*And he brought forth the people that were therein*} i.e. So many of them as he thought fit to be made exemplary, i.e. their elders and rulers, which had been the chief ringleaders in all their wickedness. Josephus affirms the whole people were exposed to these severe punishments. But it is manifest that, though the most were thus used, yet many were exempted, as Shobi the son of Nahash, who was put in Hanun’s place; and if he were made king, he must have a people of that nation over whom to rule.

*{and put them under saws, and under harrows of iron*} Such examples of great severity exercised on God’s enemies we read of elsewhere in the Scriptures, as on Adonibezek, Judg. 1. 6. and on the men of Succoth, Judg. 8. 16. And they are not to be condemned as cruel, because either God commanded them, or their heinous crimes deserved them. As for the Ammonites, they were bitter and malicious enemies, and extremely cruel when they had the Israelites at advantage, as at Jabesh-Gilead, and had put many of them to the like cruel deaths when they fell into their hands, Amos 1. 3, 13. (So Heb. 11. 37.) and had now violated the law of nations in disgracing David’s ambassadors; and had stirred up and hired all the nations round about to make war against God’s people, with a purpose utterly to destroy them; and besides, were abominable idolaters, and sacrificed their own children.

*{and made them pass through the brickkiln}* Some think this was the furnace of Molech or Milchom the idol of the Ammonites, where they had made their children pass through the fire, 2 Kin. 16. 3. & 23. 10. Lev. 18. 21. and if so, then was this a fit punishment for such abominable wickedness.

*{And thus did he to all the Cities of the children of Ammon*} i.e. To almost all the elders of every city, who were the chief offenders and corrupted those that were under them by their wicked example.

## CHAPTER 13

**Verse 1.** {*Absalom the son of David had a fair sister*} Though she was sister to all David’s sons, and so to Amnon among the rest, they having all the same father, yet she is said to be Absalom’s sister in a special manner, because they were both born of Maacah, the daughter of Talmai king of Geshur, ch. 3. 3. and David had the rest by other wives. This Talmai being an idolatrous king, David did ill to match with his daughter, having choice of wives enough among his own people. And therefore it was no marvel he was so much crossed in the posterity which he had by her.

*{And Amnon the son of David*} Which Ahinoam the Jezreelitess bare unto him.

*{loved her*} i.e. Lusted after her, being ensnared with her beauty.

**V. 2.** {*And Amnon was so vexed, that he fell sick for his sister Tamar*} i.e. His heart was distracted and distempered, being set upon the rack of his passions, the grossest and most sensual parts of the soul; which being in their nature nearest to the body, do affect, and work upon it, and making the whole more restless, breaking the sleep and taking away the appetite to meat, do so cause lingering and languishing diseases.

*{For she was a virgin, and Amnon thought it hard for him to do any thing unto her*} i.e. He was out of hope of enjoying his love, and satisfying his lust with her, not only because, being a virgin, she was according to the commendable custom of those times and countries kept from gadding abroad, and strictly observed, (which discipline Dinah taking liberty to dispense with, was ravished by Shechem, Gen. 34.) but also because, being the king’s virgin-daughter, she had some appointed to take care of her, and to wait upon her daily, whithersoever she went; especially she being (for ought we know) his only daughter among so many sons, and withal very fair and beautiful, and therefore no doubt, most diligently attended, as a rare and precious jewel, and her father’s dearest darling.

**V. 3.** {*But Amnon had a friend*} i.e. A friend, in respect of near relation of kindred and intimacy of counsels, but not in respect of true friendship, which advises only to that which is good, and to compass it by lawful means.

*{and Jonadab was a very subtil man*} Politic and crafty; *wise to do evil, but to do good having no knowledge*, Jer. 4. 22.

**V. 4.** {*Why art thou, being the King’s son, lean from day to day?}* i.e. Discerning by his carriage that he was rather sick in mind than body, he asks him what should be the cause of it; or rather implied he could have no just cause at all thus to languish and pine away, seeing he being the king’s son, yea, his eldest son, and heir to the crown, his present condition and future hopes might make him easily dispense with matters which might be grievous to others of a low condition; and besides, he was of that power and authority, that if he would make use of it, there was no stop or grievance in his way that he might not easily remove, when he pleased.

*{wilt thou not tell me?*} i.e. Will you smother your griefs in your own breast, till they eat out your heart, and kill you? and not impart them unto me, who will be faithful to keep your secrets, and ready and willing to give you advice?

*{And Amnon said unto him, I love Tamar my brother Absalom’s sister}* q.d. Though it be a fault to love Tamar in a lustful manner, that is so near in blood to me, yet it is not so great as it may seem, seeing she is only my half-sister, by the father’s side, whereas she is Absalom’s sister both by father and mother. But afterwards, when he was upon another design, to persuade his father to send her unto him, he calls her his sister Tamar, v. 6.

**V. 5.** {*Let my sister Tamar come and give me meat, and dress the meat in my sight}* He advises him to call her *sister*, the rather, to avoid all suspicion of the foul fact intended by him; but withal implying, that she was such a dear sister unto him, that he conceived and fancied (as men are apt to do in a languishing disease) it would do him more good to have the meat of her cooking, and given unto him by her own hand, than if it should be dressed and brought unto him by any other.

**V. 6.** {*Amnon lay down, and made himself sick*} He was vexed before with a pining sickness, v. 2. but now he counterfeited that his sickness had so far prevailed that he was fain to keep his bed. Thus, like wax to the seal, no sooner had Jonadab given his wicked counsel but Amnon was ready to receive the impression, and to embrace and put it in practice.

*{Let Tamar my sister come and make me a couple of cakes*} i.e. Some hearty cordial, or pleasing dish, fit for one in a languishing condition, as the word may signify. Here we may observe the simplicity and humble plainness of those times, when the only daughter of a great king scorned not to put her hand to so mean an office as being a nurse to dress meat for her sick brother; which surely she could not have done, if she had not been taught and trained up, and sometimes exercised in such employments. See the like, Gen. 18. 6. A good precedent for great ladies in our times, who should not think themselves too good or great to act that part for which they were created, i.e. to be helpers unto men in sickness and health, and to play the part of good housewives, as is described by a great and glorious king, and (as he professes) taught him by his mother, who, herself also was a queen, Prov. 31. 1, 10, &c.

**V. 7.** {*Then David sent home to Tamar*} Being a most indulgent father to his sons, and now seeing him sick who was his eldest, he presently seeks to satisfy his longing desire; and not having the least jealousy (as there was just cause) that he had any ill intention in his suit, (for none are less suspicious of ill in others than those who are innocent, and have in their hearts no fraud or guile) he sends Tamar unto him, as an ox to the shambles, or an innocent sheep to a ravenous wolf. Which doubtless added much to his grief, when he saw what mischief came of it, and that his dear daughter fell into this great misery by obeying his commands.

**V. 9.** {*And she took a pan, and poured them out before him*} To wit, out of the baking or frying pan, into a dish.

*{And Amnon said, Have all men out from me*} Being resolved now to act that filthiness which he had before plotted, he would have no eye or ear-witness of his villainy. *For every one that doth evil, hateth the light*, Joh. 3. 20.

**V. 10.** {*And Amnon said unto Tamar, Bring the meat into the chamber*} i.e. Out of that room where they were, into an inner chamber, that he might act his villainy with more secrecy, and that none might hear and break in to succor her, if when the rape was in acting she cried out for help.

**V. 12.** {*Nay my brother*} She uses divers arguments which might have dissuaded him from his wickedness, had not blind lust been also deaf. First she calls him *brother*, both to convince him that the foul act he was going about was an incestuous rape, and such an unnatural filthiness as might work abhorrency in the heart of a brother, who should be so far from thus dishonoring his sister, that he should be ready, even with the hazard of his life, to protect her against any who would offer her such an indignity.

*{do not force me*} Here she uses another reason to deter him; q.d. It were a great sin to entice me, being your sister, to commit this filthiness, but a transcendent villainy to compel me against my will, seeing unto incest you will add rape, both which by God’s law are to be punished with death, Gen. 34. 7. Levit. 18. 6, 9. Deut. 22. 29.

*{for no such thing ought to be done in Israel*} Who are by profession God’s own peculiar inheritance, and a holy people; in which respect such an example of abominable wickedness committed amongst them, and that by one of such eminency as you are, would bring a great scandal upon the whole nation, and cause religion and even the name of God to be blasphemed by all the heathen round about us.

*{do not thou this folly*} Though you have used all your wit in the subtle contriving of it; yet as the greatest sin is the greatest folly in itself, as transgressing the law of a dreadful God, who is both able and willing to punish it with all severity; it will in the issue prove extreme foolishness to you, and make you ridiculous to all who shall hear of it, that you, who might have choice of wives where you pleased, with their love and liking, should force me with violence to commit this loathed act, who am your own sister.

**V. 13.** {*And I, whether shall I cause my shame to go?*} i.e. Which way, or by what means shall I clear myself of this dishonor and reproach? or how shall I be ever able for shame, to show my face in any place where I come?

*{and as for thee, thou shalt be as one of the fools in Israel*} i.e. Esteemed among all men notoriously infamous, a son of Belial, desperately vicious, and so branded and stigmatized with folly and wickedness that all men will think you most unworthy to succeed such a glorious and gracious father in the government of the kingdom, who are so unable to govern yourself. See on Josh. 7. 17.

*{I pray thee, speak unto the King; for he will not withhold me from thee*} This she says, not because there was any hope that the king who was a strict observer of the law, could ever be persuaded to give his consent to such an incestuous marriage, Lev. 18. 6, 9, 11. but only to allay the rage of his lust for the present, and to escape his violence, knowing that if this brunt were past, her father would take order to keep her from him.

**V. 14.** {*Howbeit he would not hearken to her voice*} The louder cry of his lust did so outcry her wholesome counsel, and the devil so hardened his heart, that he was stark deaf to her persuasions, and so pursued his wicked purpose.

**V. 15.** {*Then Amnon hated her exceedingly*} Thus it often happens to those who have sold themselves to work wickedness; when one lust is satisfied, they run into the contrary extreme. Now Amnon’s eyes being opened, which lust had blinded, he is filled with horror of conscience; and seeing plainly what reproach he had brought upon himself by his abominable filthiness, he hated his fair sister, because her beauty was the bait that had allured him to fall into the snare of shame and misery. Yet this did not befall him as a necessary concomitant of the satisfying his lust, (for we see the quite contrary effect in Shechem, after he had deflowered Dinah, Gen. 34. 2, 3.) but it came to pass by a special providence of God, so disposing of it, that his sin thereby might be discovered, and a way made for the inflicting of those heavy judgments which God had threatened to bring upon David’s family for his sins of adultery and murder.

*{And Amnon said unto her, Arise, be gone*} Here the rage of lust is turned into the fury of folly and desperate madness. He has no respect to his credit, nor care to shun shame. For whereas if he, after he had abused his sister, had used her civilly and kindly, keeping her still in his chamber, till her grief had been somewhat digested, she might have been persuaded, both for his and her own credit, to have concealed the injury she had received from him, now thrusting her out of doors into the open streets and the view of all men, what does he else but proclaim his own sin and shame to all the world? And that indeed was the end at which God aimed in thus giving him over to his impetuous folly and wild rage, that by the discovery of his filthiness a way might be made unto his slaughter, and the punishment therein of David’s sin.

**V. 16.** {*And she said unto him, There is no cause*} To wit, that I should be gone in this posthaste, being at the present in such a woeful condition, no course being yet taken to preserve my reputation, or to keep me from falling into much mischief and misery.

*{this evil in sending me away is greater than the other}* i.e. This injury of thrusting me out of doors is much worse than the other of deflowering me. So she might truly say in divers respects. For that, though a foul fact, proceeded from natural lust, unto which he was tempted by the alluring bait of beauty; but this proceeded from more than barbarous inhumanity and savage cruelty, against all sense and reason. That, being committed, might be concealed, and so the shame avoided; but this proclaimed his sin, and exposed her to open reproach, seeing she either might be suspected to have willingly yielded to his villainy, or at least, though she were a mere patient, yet she should be so stained and blemished in her reputation that none worthy of her would desire her in marriage. Besides, God’s name would be blasphemed amongst his enemies, when they should hear of so foul a fact committed among his peculiar people, his true religion and the professors of it scandalized, their father’s heart extremely grieved, Absalom her brother enraged and excited to take revenge. All which mischiefs might be avoided by concealing his sin, especially if hating and forsaking it he did turn unto God by unfeigned repentance.

**V. 17.** {*Then he called his servants that ministered unto him}* Who did presently execute his command, v. 18. For evil masters seldom want evil servants to humor them in their wicked courses and designs.

**V. 18.** {*And she had a garment of divers colours*} i.e. Of wrought and embroidered work. For as in all ages men and women have been by God differenced from one another in their estates and degrees, so also in the costliness and meanness of their habits and apparel. So Gen. 37. 3. Judg. 5. 30.

**V. 19.** {*And Tamar put ashes on her head*} So they used to express their bitter mourning and heart-rending grief. See on ch. 1. 2.

*{and laid her hand upon her head*} To express her sorrow and shame, as though she desired to hide herself that none might see her. So Jer. 2. 37.

*{and went on crying*} i.e. She addressed herself to Absalom, who was her only brother both by father and mother, and complained as she went along that she had been shamefully wronged and abused, that when the matter came to be discovered, it might appear that she had not willingly consented unto it. But for the present it may be she did not particularly express the fact to those she passed by, but only told it to Absalom upon his inquiry.

**V. 20.** {*hath Amnon thy brother been with thee*} i.e. Abused you in that foul manner you relate?

*{But hold now thy peace, my sister: he is thy brother*} And therefore this fact of his were best to be concealed, seeing the publishing of it would redound both to the dishonor of the king and the disgrace of our whole family.

*{regard not this thing*} i.e. Set not your heart too much upon it, to be thereby vexed and tormented, seeing it was forced upon you, and done against your will; which will make all men excuse you. What is done, cannot be helped; and that which is past cure, ought to be past care. Thus he seeks to pacify his sister, though at the same time his heart was enraged, and breathed after revenge.

*{So Tamar remained desolate*} i.e. In a forlorn and mournful condition, none being able to give her comfort.

**V. 21.** {*But when King David heard of all these things, he was very wroth*} Not only because his eldest son had committed such a sin as exposed him to shame and obloquy, but also because it trenched upon his own honor, who had bred him no better. But this was not enough for David, to be angry. He was the supreme magistrate, and ought to have seen the law of God executed upon the offender without partiality or respect of persons. And this he neglecting to do, showed himself an indulgent father, like Eli, and moved God in just displeasure to pull the sword out of his hand, for neglecting his duty, being his vicegerent, and himself to see justice executed upon him by such an executioner as afflicted his soul with double grief. This his neglect of justice proceeded (as it seems to me) not only from fondness towards his son, but also from inward guilt in himself. He was convinced in his own conscience that not long since he was defiled with the filthiness of lust, and with the blood of his innocent servant, and thereby had deserved death in the rigor of justice, had not God in mercy forgiven the sins and remitted the punishment; and this blunted the edge of his justice, that it was not so keen to cut off another in the like case with himself as otherwise it would have been. Neither will that serve for David’s excuse which some allege, that he saw that Amnon repented for his sin; seeing there is no warrant for it in the text: and though it had been so, yet it should not have exempted him from temporal punishment, though it might have been available for his everlasting salvation. For in executing justice, respect must be had not only to the offender, but to the whole commonwealth, to keep others from offending by exemplary punishments; unto which none would be brought, if it were sufficient to make show of repentance.

**V. 22.** {*And Absalom spake to his brother Amnon neither good nor bad*} i.e. Having out of inveterate hatred and unreconcilable grudge resolved upon revenge, he smothered his malicious purpose, that he might get the fitter opportunity to execute it; and knowing that professed hatred leaves no place for revenge, seeing the party forewarned is also forearmed against any intended mischief, he spoke neither less nor more unto him concerning the abuse offered to his sister, lest he should suspect that he intended revenge, and so should prevent it; neither was he sullenly silent, lest he should thereby suspect that he bare a grudge, but spoke occasionally unto him, as at other times, though not about this matter.

**V. 23.** {*And it came to pass after two full years}* This time is purposely noted to show the inveterate rancor and malice, that was all that while lodged in Absalom’s heart, seeing so long time could not wear it out, or mitigate it.

*{Absalom had sheep-shearers*} Upon this occasion he made a feast, as the manner was in those times, to entertain their friends, and to encourage their servants and workmen. So 1 Sam. 25. 36.

*{in Baal-hazor*} Or, *the plain of Hazor*, situate on the borders of Ephraim and Benjamin, Josh. 15. 25.

*{and Absalom invited all the King’s sons}* Because he had expected thus long, and saw that his father out of fondness towards Amnon neglected to execute justice, and punish his heinous crime, he was so much incensed that he would no longer wait, but resolved himself to take revenge. And to this end he laid hold of this opportunity, none suspecting after so long time past that he had any such bloody design.

**V. 24.** {*Let the King, I beseech thee, and his servants go with his servant*} He invites the king and all his sons, with their retinue, to avoid all suspicion of any ill intention against Amnon. Neither can I think (as some expositors imagine) that he in truth desired the king’s presence, because he would have him a spectator of his son’s murder, in regard that he himself had so long neglected to execute justice upon him; seeing he had little reason either to provoke him to wrath, who had power in his hand to punish this heinous crime, or to desire his presence, which would have been a hindrance to his intended flight after he had committed so foul a fact.

**V. 25.** {*Howbeit he would not go, but blessed him*} i.e. Wished he might have much joy in his feast, and gave him thanks for his kind invitation. So ch. 14. 22.

**V. 26.** {*If not, I pray thee, let my brother Amnon go with us*} This he desired under color that being the king’s eldest son, and by lineal descent to succeed him as heir to the crown, he might represent the king’s person, and so much grace and honor his feast; whereas otherwise his earnest pressing and importuning to have him in particular to bear them company might well have made both David and Amnon, in respect of former carriages, jealous and suspicious that he intended some mischief, had not God deprived them both of prudence and understanding, that so he might bring them both to punishment for their sins.

**V. 27.** {*But Absalom pressed him*} i.e. Was importunate, and would take no denial and so in the end prevailed.

**V. 28.** {*Mark ye now, when Amnon’s heart is merry with wine}* Of this time he makes choice to perpetrate his bloody design and treacherous villainy, that it might be the more secretly acted, when Amnon being intoxicated with excess of wine was destitute of counsel and strength, to make any resistance. And here David’s sin is punished by retaliation. For as he had made Uriah drunk, that he might work his design upon him: so does Absalom deal with Amnon to the same purpose.

*{and when I say unto you, Smite Amnon, then kill him*} He makes his will stand for law, yielding no reason for his bloody command. Howsoever it might well be judged that he did it in revenge of his sister’s wrong, whom Amnon had defiled and ravished, yet it may be he had a further end, being proud and ambitious, to make way to the crown by taking his eldest brother out of the way.

*{fear not: have not I commanded you?*} q.d. Is not my warrant sufficient to bear you out and excuse you, who am so potent in myself, and so highly in the king’s favor, that he will never question either me or you, seeing it is done in the just revenge of so foul a fact; and seeing also when he is removed out of your way, I shall be prince, and as heir to the crown, succeed my father, and sit upon his throne?

**V. 29.** {*And the servants of Absalom did unto Amnon as Absalom had commanded*} i.e. Like wicked men, they obeyed the command of a wicked master, being more willing to please him than either God or their king.

*{then all the King’s sons arose, and every man gat him up upon his mule, and fled}* i.e. Not knowing how far Absalom’s bloody treachery might extend, they hasted away to secure their own lives. And it is said that they *gat them up upon their mules*, which great men in that country used to ride upon, 1 kings 1. 33. For though the Israelites might not breed them, by causing beasts of divers kinds, as the horse and the ass, to gender together, Lev. 19. 19. yet they were not forbidden to make use of mules so engendered. See Gen. 36. 24.

**V. 30.** {*And it came to pass while they were in the way}* i.e. Posting from Absalom’s house to the court.

*{tidings came to David, saying, Absalom hath slain all the King’s sons}* Flying fame was increased in her flight, God so ordering it by his special providence, that David first might be the more thoroughly humbled by hearing the report of such a heavy judgment, and afterwards might be made more capable of comfort, when the loss proved far less than he expected.

**V. 31.** {*Then the King arose, and tore his garments*} See on ch. 1. 11. and 12. 16. And well might David thus express his great grief, being such a tender loving father as that he placed much of his happiness in his children, and now seeing one of them murdered by another, yea, (as it was related) all of them by one, and that in such a base treacherous manner; and withal looking back to his own sin as the fountain and cause of all this mischief and misery; and considering also that both by his sin and punishment he had given occasion to God’s enemies to blaspheme.

*{and lay on the earth*} As one dejected with extreme grief in the apprehension of God’s wrath upon his house, before threatened, and now inflicted, for his sin, ch. 12. 10.

**V. 32.** {*And Jonadab the son of Shimeah——said*} This subtle man was able above all others rightly to judge of this accident, because by his counsel and contrivance Amnon’s rape was committed, which was Absalom’s motive to this murder. And it may be, he being Absalom’s bosom-friend, and of his secret counsels, he had expressed to him his hatred towards Amnon, and his purpose of revenge; and therefore Jonadab limited the murder to Amnon alone. Here the impudence of this court-favorite, and his impenitency for his sin, notwithstanding he saw what mischiefs it had produced, appears, in that he could thus talk of Tamar’s rape, which himself had plotted, without any blushing, as if it had been a matter that nothing concerned him.

**V. 33.** {*Let not my lord the King take the thing to his heart*} i.e. To mourn excessively above that for which there is just cause.

**V. 34.** {*But Absalom fled*} He that before the sin committed was so courageous that he would have his servants act it without fear, now it is done, is so pursued with the guilt of an evil conscience that himself flees to escape the sword of justice ready to pursue him for his sin.

*{the young man that kept the watch, lift up his eyes, and looked*} And having discovered the people approaching, brought word of it to David.

*{there came much people by the way*} To wit, which did lie between Baal-hazor and Jerusalem.

**V. 35.** {*As thy servant said, so it is*} Heb. *according to the word of thy servant.* To wit, that all the king's sons are alive, and only Amnon is dead.

**V. 36.** {*The King’s sons came, and lifted up their voice and wept}* Bewailing the great loss that happened in the family of two of their eldest brethren, the one being lost by a horrid murder, and the other having forfeited his life to the hand of justice by his treacherous cruelty.

**V. 37.** {*But Absalom fled, and went to Talmai*} His mother's father, from whom he hoped for protection, or to get pardon from his own father by his mediation: ch. 3. 3.

*{the son of Ammihud*} Or, *Ammihur.*

*{and David mourned for his son every day*} i.e. For the death of his son Amnon, who was so treacherously murdered by his own brother, and who also was so dear unto him. His unnatural and untimely death he bewailed for the space of three years every day, having no intermission in his mourning, v. 38.

**V. 38.** {*went to Geshur, and was there three years*} He stayed so long there in hope that his father's displeasure would be worn out in process of time; as it also happened.

**V. 39.** {*And the soul of King David longed to go forth unto Absalom*} Or, *was consumed.* i.e. He so vehemently longed to see his beloved Absalom that even he languished in his desire. Or, he so much desired to have him return unto him that he could willingly have found in his heart to have gone for him himself, or to have met him in the way, or at least to have sent some other to fetch him home. But he could do none of these for fear and shame, seeing he was guilty of so foul a murder; for by showing favor to such a flagitious and wretched son he should not only expose himself to God's displeasure but to the common obloquy of all the people.

*{for he was comforted concerning Amnon, seeing he was dead*} i.e. Had ceased his mourning for Amnon's death; because mourning could not fetch him again, ch. 12. 23: and it may be because now he considered that his foul offense had justly deserved death, though Absalom had failed in the manner of execution.

## CHAPTER 14

**Verse 1.** {*Now Joab——perceived that the King’s heart was toward Absalom}* i.e. Longed to have him fetched home. Hereby it appears that all Joab's following endeavors to reduce Absalom into the king's favor did not proceed from any true love or friendship towards Absalom, but merely out of self-love, because he looked upon him as heir to the crown, who might do him either a pleasure or displeasure when he came to reign; and therefore perceiving he was now likely to recover the king's favor, he thought it good policy to purchase his love by showing himself forward and a chief means in working his reconciliation: and also he looked upon him as David's jewel and chief favorite, after whom his heart now yearned and longed, not being able any longer to endure his exile and absence; therefore like a cunning courtier he applies himself to please the king's humor, and to persuade that he might be sent for home, because he saw that David much desired it. Now this plainly appears by that which follows: For when after his return the king seemed displeased, and would not suffer him to see his face, Joab likewise would not for a long time give him so much as a visit, yea, would not come at him, though sent for, till he was constrained by the injury he did him in burning his barley.

**V. 2.** {*And Joab sent to Tekoah*} A city eight miles from Jerusalem, upon a hill on the north borders of Judah, 2 Chron. 11. 6: where the prophet Amos lived, Amos 1. 1.

*{and fetch thence a wise woman*} He made choice of one at such a distance, rather than of one dwelling at Jerusalem, or near hand, because coming with a feigned case, she might not be so easily known and discovered. And for this woman, he knew her to be prudent and politic, witty, active, and of ready speech, and thereby fit for such an employment, being able to deliver her mind and message, and to answer the king upon the sudden, as any occasion should be offered. And he thought fitter to use a woman than a man, and a widow rather than a wife or maid, because the parable was to suit with a woman, and with a woman of such a condition. And that sex being the more passionate, was the more apt to move compassion, and the more fit to act a mourner's part, having tears and lamentations at command. And being a widow, she might (being unable to help herself) the better crave and implore David's assistance, who being God's vicegerent should administer justice to all, especially to the fatherless and widows, with all lawful favor, seeing they had from God special promises of protection.

*{and anoint not thyself with oil}* In token of mourning. Otherwise the Jews used to anoint their faces with oil to make them seem more cheerful, Judg. 9. 9. Ruth 3. 3. Psal. 104. 15. Matth. 6. 17.

**V. 3.** {*So Joab put the words in her mouth*} i.e. The whole form and substance of speech which he would have her use to the king, and which he would have delivered, not plainly and directly, but darkly and by way of parable; which was usual in those times. This Joab thought fit for the present occasion, both because parables move much, and leave behind them a strong and deep impression, and besides, the cause being evil needed to be disguised under oblique and indirect colors and shows: otherwise it would have appeared an unlawful and dishonest suit, if she had bluntly desired that he who had murdered his brother should be protected.

**V. 4.** {*and said, Help, O King*} Heb. *Save.* i.e. Succor and relieve me, that I may be delivered out of the distress and danger in which now I am, according to your place, and that power which God has been pleased to put in your hand, and has appointed you to exercise for the relief of distressed and oppressed widows, Esai. 1. 17. Deut. 27. 19.

**V. 5.** {*And she answered, I am indeed a widow woman*} And therefore more need to be pitied and relieved in my distressed condition, seeing I am not able to help myself, and want a husband to assist me; which in itself is misery enough: But how much more intolerable would it be if I should lose also both my sons; as I am now in danger to do, unless you will be pleased to help me against those that are against me?

**V. 6.** {*And thy handmaid had two sons, and they two strove together in the field}* i.e. They quarreled with one another upon some sudden occasion accidentally offered, and not upon any pretended malice.

*{and there was none to part them*} And consequently any witnesses to prove the fact, as the law requires, Deut. 17. 6.

*{but the one smote the other, and slew him*} As the avengers say, but cannot prove.

**V. 7.** {*And behold, the whole family is risen against thine handmaid*} i.e. The kindred, who are appointed by the law to be the avengers of blood, are risen to prosecute justice against the offender, Num. 35. 19. Deut. 19. 12: This they pretend, as hot zealots of the law; but it may well be thought they do all out of self-love, knowing that my son's inheritance by his death shall come unto them; yea this their own words import, for they say *they will destroy the heir also.*

*{and so shall quench my coal which is left*} i.e. Kill my only remaining son, by whom my husband's name and family may be continued and preserved: even as a fire ready almost to go out, may yet be revived by a coal that lies hid under the ashes, being kept alive and blown upon; but if that also be put out and quenched, then no hope remains, Deut. 25. 6, 10.

**V. 8.** {*I will give charge concerning thee*} That your cause may be heard and examined, and your son preserved, if that which you say appear true.

**V. 9.** {*The iniquity be on me and on my father’s house, and the King and his throne be guiltless}* i.e. I am so well persuaded of my son's innocence, that I am content, if there be any fault in protecting him, to take it wholly upon myself and mine, that the king and his kingdom may not lie under the guilt of blood, for preserving my son that is alive. Or, if there be any iniquity in that which the king shall grant at my request, let the guilt be upon me and mine. So Matth. 27. 25. This was not a sound argument to move the king to grant her suit, because, if unjust, God would punish both her for making it, and him for giving way unto it; yet it might be some kind of motive to make him think her request good, seeing she was so confident of the goodness of it that she was willing to bear the whole burden and blame if it proved evil; and it might somewhat incline his heart to favor her suit, seeing out of her great care of his good she would rather suffer in herself and hers any evil, than that any harm should redound to the king or his kingdom. Thus Rebekah persuaded Jacob to hearken unto her motion of getting his father's blessing by deceit, Gen. 27. 13.

**V. 10.** {*He shall not touch thee any more*} i.e. Not put you to any more trouble.

**V. 11.** {*Then said she, I pray thee let the King remember the Lord thy God*} i.e. How the Lord by his law has provided that such as have slain a man at unawares should be preserved from the avengers of blood, Numb. 35. 12: And seeing there were many that sought her son's life, she desires the king would be pleased to take order that he might be preserved against the persecutions of them all. Others take it, and that very probably, that she in these words desires that he would remember his promise now made in the presence of God, which he might haply forget through the multitude of his weighty occasions, and secretly moved him to ratify what he had said by an oath; unto which he condescended in the words following.

*{And he said, As the Lord liveth, there shall not one hair of thy son fall to the earth*} i.e. I will take order that your son shall not be hurt in the least degree or manner. This oath was very unlawful, seeing by the law her son, who had slain his brother wittingly and willingly, though in heat of blood, ought to have died, Num. 35. 16, 17, 21. But it was now David's own case in respect of Absalom; and that makes him so favorable in passing this sentence, and so ready to ratify it by a solemn oath. See 1 Sam. 14. 45. Acts 23. 27.

**V. 13.** {*Wherefore then hast thou thought such a thing against the people*} Here she subtly applies the parable to the king's particular case, taking so much as served her turn, and leaving out all that made against her. For there were divers great differences between the case of her son and his brother (as she propounded it) and that of Absalom and Amnon. For, he slew his brother suddenly in the heat of blood; but Absalom killed Amnon after a long meditation, and in a treacherous manner. One of them assaulted the other in a duel or equal fight; but Amnon was slain at a feast, where love was pretended, and for the present no offense given. There, no witnesses were present; but here, all the king's children and their retinue. She pretended this was her only son, and that if he were cut off, there would remain no heir, nor any remembrance of her deceased husband, to preserve his house from being utterly extinguished; but Absalom had many brethren, and much better than he; and therefore, if he were taken away, there would have been no loss. But herein they agreed, that both the one and the other were willful murderers, and therefore could have no benefit or protection by the cities of refuge, which were appointed only for those that killed any at unawares.

*{for the King doth speak this thing as one that is faulty, in that the King doth not fetch home again his banished*} i.e. As one who having given a just sentence in the case of another, does fail in the execution of the same when it is his own. For David, who (as she charges him) would have no rigor used against her son for killing his brother, because it would extend to her great grief and loss, could be contented that rigor should be exercised towards his own son Absalom, though it redounded much to the grief and detriment of the whole commonwealth, which looked upon him as heir to the king, and as a light that should shine unto them, when as he, who was now their sun, should set; and all this for killing Amnon, who had deserved death, and highly provoked Absalom to inflict it, by ravishing his sister, and thereby dishonoring him and the whole family.

**V. 14.** {*For we must needs die, and are as water spilt on the ground}* i.e. We take his absence so much to heart that we can no longer subsist without him, seeing we shall be desperately lost (even as water spilt upon the ground, which cannot be recovered) if such a hopeful successor be cut off.

*{neither doth God respect any person*} To wit, to exempt him from death, of what degree soever he be.

*{yet doth he devise means, that his banished be not expelled from him*} Or, *because God hath not taken away his life, he hath also devised means,* &c. i.e. It is a sign that it is God's will he should succeed you, in that he has preserved him all this time of his banishment, and now uses means to persuade you that he may be restored. Others understand it of our state of mortality, and the necessity of death; seeing such is our condition as that we must all needs die, first or last, and when we are dead, there is no returning, as water spilt cannot be gathered up again, therefore it is in vain to take Absalom away before his time for killing Amnon, seeing by his death the other could not be revived and brought back into the world. Or it may thus be meant, that seeing all men are mortal it must needs be the case also both of David and Absalom; and if David should die, who was now while he lived, the light and life of Israel, yet if Absalom survived him, he would, as a living coal, again kindle the fire ready to go out; whereas if David should happen to die, (as there is no certainty in man's mortal condition) and Absalom also should miscarry through grief, before his time, by living in exile, then this coal being quenched, all their hope and happiness would be quite extinguished.

**V. 15.** {*It is because the people have made me afraid*} Either that you are so much incensed against him that you are fully resolved to continue him in his banishment, or that they will be so enraged to have their hope cut off of enjoying such a successor when you are gone, that they will rise up in rebellion, and bring him back against your will. Or it may be it is thus purposely to be understood, that she was afraid for the people's sake, that there might some attempts be made against them, in the way of invasion, by Absalom, assisted by his father-in-law the king of Geshur, and his allies; or else, lest Absalom living long among idolaters should be corrupted, and so corrupt the people in their religion, when he came to reign over them.

*{And thy handmaid said, I will now speak unto the King*} i.e. Fearing some mischief would ensue if the king's son continued in banishment, I resolved with myself to use the best means I could to prevent it, and to speak in his behalf to the king that he would cause him to return, hoping that when he hears what dangers will arise, both to him and the commonwealth, by his still remaining in exile, he will hearken unto me, and grant my suit.

**V. 16.** {*For the King will hear, to deliver his handmaid, &c.*} i.e. Knowing the king so just that he will be always ready to hear the cause of the oppressed, and to right their wrongs, I thought it best, in a parabolical way to propound it, and to make his case my own, hoping that when he saw the equity of the cause in my person, and had judged right concerning me and my son, that we ought to be delivered from those that pursue us, he would be no less equal and just to himself and his own son, when he should see the parable brought home and applied unto them, considering how dear he is to that people which are *Gods inheritance.*

**V. 17.** {*Then thine handmaid said, The word of my lord the King shall now be comfortable*} i.e. Upon this ground she concluded with herself that she should receive from the king a gracious answer to her suit concerning his son's return.

*{For as an Angel of God, so is my lord the King*} Here she insinuates herself into his favor for the obtaining of her suit, by commending and extolling him. And because there was none on earth to match him, she compares him to an angel of heaven, both for wisdom in discerning between right and wrong, good and evil, for dexterity in judging, and uprightness in judgment; persuading herself that, like an angel, he would judge without passion or partiality of her suit which she made unto him, and constantly observe his promise which he had made unto her under the name of her son, for the reducing of his own Absalom.

*{therefore the Lord thy God shall be with thee*} i.e. Cause your kingdom to flourish, and prosper you in your government, and give good success to all your undertakings, because you are wise and upright in judgment, and will now approve yourself so to be in this particular case between you and your son.

**V. 19.** {*Is not the hand of Joab with thee in all this?*} i.e. Have you not spoken and done all this by Joab's counsel and direction?

*{none can turn to the right hand or to the left from ought that my lord the king hath spoken*} i.e. You have judged so right that none can decline from that which you have spoken and not speak an untruth.

*{he put all these words in the mouth of thine handmaid*} i.e. He gave me special directions for all that I have spoken.

**V. 20.** {*To fetch about this form of speech*} i.e. To speak thus unto you in a parabolical manner, propounding the matter in my own case, that you might the more impartially judge of it being applied unto yourself.

*{and my lord is wise, according to the wisdom of an angel of God*} By these transcendent and superlative praises she insinuates herself into David's favor, that he might the more easily condescend unto her suit. q.d. You are of so deep a reach to dive into the secrets of all our counsels that you excel others in wisdom as far as angels excel men. See 1 Sam. 19. 9.

**V. 21.** {*And the King said unto Joab, Behold now, I have done this thing*} i.e. I have granted your suit, which by the woman of Tekoah you have moved me in.

**V. 22.** {*And Joab——thanked the King*} Heb. *blessed.* i.e. With all lowly reverence he gave him thanks for granting his suit, and wished him all happiness and prosperity.

*{To day thy servant knoweth that I have found grace in thy sight*} i.e. I know it experimentally, and am further confirmed and assured of your favor by your granting my suit.

*{in that the King hath fulfilled the request of his servant*} Joab well knew it was David's desire to have Absalom brought home; and that was the chief motive inducing him to make it his request: He knew it would be very pleasing to the king to have it pressed upon him by the importunate suit of the general of the army, who was in high place, and favor of the people; especially when he knew not how that which he so much desired could be better done for his own credit: Yet taking no knowledge of this, he makes it his own suit, and shows his thankfulness for having it granted, that he might gain the king's affection by expressing his love towards his children and showing himself careful of their welfare.

**V. 23.** {*So Joab arose*} i.e. Addressed himself with all speed to set upon his journey, that he might fetch Absalom home.

**V. 24.** {*And the King said, Let him turn to his own house, and let him not see my face*} i.e. Nor be admitted into my presence, as though he were reconciled and in my favor. For it is more than probable that in the space of two whole years Absalom saw his father's face: else he could not all that while have come at the place of God's public worship, from which it was not David's purpose to have him banished. Now David did this partly to bring him to the sight of his sin, and to keep him for the time to come from falling into the like, as in the pride of his spirit he would have been too apt to do, had he found his father's favor easily recovered; and to deter others also by his example; and partly to avoid the scandal of too easily remitting so foul a fact. And it may be also that he had respect unto Amnon's friends, and avoided to give them just offense by his easy admittance of him that had murdered him, lest they should thereby be provoked to seek revenge.

**V. 25.** {*But in all Israel there was none to be so much praised as Absalom, for his beauty*} This is here brought in to commend David's gravity and power over his affections, that could moderate and restrain himself from showing any countenance or love to so lovely a son. Or it is prefixed before the story of Absalom's rebellion, as being one special cause of it. His beauty made him insolent, proud and ambitious; and being admired of the people, he became thereby popular, and highly in their favor. In this respect one compares beauty to a goodly oak, which brings forth no other fruit but mast and acorns fit to feed and fatten hogs.

**V. 26.** {*at two hundred shekels after the King’s weight}* To wit, common and civil shekels, which weighed a quarter of an ounce, or two drams.

**V. 27.** {*And unto Absalom there were born three sons, and one daughter*} All which died before him; ch. 18. 18.

**V. 28.** {*And saw not the King’s face}* See on v. 24.

**V. 29.** {*Absalom sent for Joab, to have sent him to the King*} Namely to mediate on his behalf, that he might be admitted into his grace and favor.

*{And when he sent again the second time, he would not come*} Thereby showing himself a right courtier, whose love carried him no further on to act in his business than stood with the favor of the king. Therefore fearing (as it may be thought) that the king might suspect some secret counsels and practices between them, he shunned to come near him, that he might avoid such jealousies.

**V. 30.** {*Go and set it on fire*} To the end that he that would not come at his request, out of love, might be forced to do it for his own sake, to expostulate the injury.

**V. 31.** {*Then Joab arose*} i.e. Made all the haste he could to come unto Absalom.

**V. 32.** {*Wherefore am I come from Geshur?*} Thus he would have him with all earnestness expostulate with the king concerning his withdrawing from him his face and favor, as though he were so full of love towards him that he was now grown impatient of further delay; whereas contrariwise, being discontented at his restraint, he grew ill-affected towards his father, and entertained thoughts of rebellion against him, and aspiring to the crown; and not seeing any means of compassing his design while he was banished from the court, he vehemently desires that he might be restored thither, to raise a party, and make friends by his popular carriage.

*{It had been good for me to have been there still*} Or, *it had been better for me to have remained there still,* than come home to be banished out of the king's sight, and live out of his favor, which is the life of my life, and the want of it a living death. For the nearer I am to my father, the more sensible I am of my misery that I cannot enjoy him: And it more tends to my disgrace and reproach, when the people by my being here take notice of his disfavor in debarring me to come into his sight, which they would not have been so apt to do, had I still lived from him in a further distance. At Geshur also I had liberty to come to court, or go whither I pleased, whereas now I am confined by the king's command to mine own house, or at least banished from the court. And indeed it is not likely that the king restrained him from the place of God's public worship, though he in his profaneness makes no account of enjoying such a privilege, which he was wholly deprived of while he lived at Geshur.

*{And if there be any iniquity in me let him kill me*} Here he justifies himself, as though he had done nothing amiss in murdering Amnon, seeing thereby he had justly revenged that wrong and dishonor which he had offered his sister. Or, if he had failed in the manner of executing justice, he was content for that to suffer, because he had rather die than live in such disgrace: But then he desired that his father should sentence him to death, who was supreme judge, because he knew he had such interest in his favor that he was sure enough to be out of danger. Thus wicked men justify themselves in their evil actions, especially when they presume upon the favor of their judge.

**V. 33.** {*and told him*} i.e. Joab related unto him Absalom's speeches, by which he made show that he was so much grieved for his being excluded out of his father's presence that he chose rather to die than to live. And hereby David, deluded by his hypocrisy, and out of his own indulgence, being persuaded that he was now sufficiently humbled for his sin, yielded to his suit, and admitted him to come into his presence. But there was in him no true repentance, nor hearty desire to recover his father's favor, but only that he might come unto the court to work his own mischievous and ambitious ends by gaining the love and applause of the people.

*{And the King kissed Absalom*} To give him full assurance that now he had forgiven his offense, and was thoroughly reconciled unto him. See on Gen. 29. 11. & 31. 55.

## CHAPTER 15

**Ver. 1.** {*And it came to pass after this}* i.e. After David had thus reconciled himself to his wicked son, and again received him into grace and favor, neglecting to execute justice by inflicting upon him that punishment which God's law required and his bloody sin deserved, God in his righteous judgment began to execute upon David that heavy doom which he had threatened against him for his sins of adultery and murder, that he would *raise up evil against him in his own house,* ch. 12. 11. And to this purpose he gives up his graceless son, out of pride and ambition to plot and practice against him, that dethroning him he might reign in his stead. Being now his eldest son, he had fair hopes to attain his desire, if he would have stayed till his father's death, which now in respect of old age could not be far off; but he was so impatient of delay that nothing could content him but present possession, though he could no otherwise ascend into the throne than by making the dead carcass of his father a step unto it. Of this his haste (besides his restless ambition) one cause might be an old grudge conceived against his father, because he had dishonored him in the eyes of the people by his long banishment for killing his brother (though he approved it a just act) which might make him be thought unworthy to reign after his death, whom he thought unworthy to come into his presence while he was living. Another cause might be, the jealousy he had of Solomon's obtaining the crown, both because he was so well beloved of his father and of God himself, ch. 12. 24, 25, and also because he was a true Israelite both by father and mother; whereas himself was descended of Maacah, a stranger, and of a heathenish nation. Therefore to put the matter out of all doubt, he resolves, whatsoever it cost, to get present possession: And to this purpose he puts divers policies in practice to further his ambitious design. And first, he gets a princely retinue to attend him.

*{Absalom prepared him chariots, and horses, and fifty men to run before him*} This he did to the end, that taking upon him the port and state of a prince, the people might take notice of him as heir to the crown, and use him with suitable respect; and because such unusual pomp would much commend him to the esteem of the vulgar sort, who are much taken with novelties, and apt to admire and adore outward appearances of glory and greatness. Of this height of pride David takes no notice, nor uses any means to cross his son's ambition in the first rise, being much blinded with fondness of affection.

**V. 2.** {*And Absalom rose up early, and stood beside the way of the gate*} To wit, of the king's court, out of the city; which was appointed to be the place of judicature. This pains and diligence he used as another politic means to insinuate himself into the hearts of the people, seeing he was so zealous of their welfare that he spared no pains to inform himself in their causes and suits, that they might have right.

*{And Absalom called unto him*} i.e. He was so forward to gratify them in their suits, that he did not stay till they made their address unto him, but prevented them by showing his readiness to do them good.

*{Thy servant is of one of the tribes of Israel*} i.e. Of this or that city, situate in such or such a tribe.

**V. 3.** {*And Absalom said unto him, See thy matters are good and right*} i.e. Thus it was his manner to say unto any man that had a suit, that their cause was just and good, though it were never so bad: And he would make them fair promises of his assistance, though he never intended it, but only aimed by soothing flattery to gain their love; which was an easy purchase, costing him no more than the price of his breath.

*{but there is no man deputed of the King to hear thee*} Or, *None will hear thee from the King downward.* i.e. He has not appointed any of his judges or inferior officers to hear your cause, and do you right. Here he traduces the king, that he might work him out of the hearts of the people, by telling them of his ill government in his old age, seeing he had no care to execute justice himself, nor of having it done by others under him; either not appointing them to do it, or not calling them to account if they neglected it. Which was a shameless slander of a graceless son, seeing it is said, that *David reigned over all Israel, and executed judgement and justice unto all the people,* ch. 8. 15.

**V. 4.** {*Absalom said moreover, O that I were made Judge in the land*} So Gaal, Judg. 9. 29. Here he plainly shows that his end in traducing his father's government was to advance himself, though the eyes of the common people were so dazzled with his glossing language that they could not discern it.

**V. 5.** {*And it was so, that when any man came nigh to him to do him obeisance——he kissed him}* i.e. Seeing his plot was to inveigle all the people with his allurements, he put no great difference between one and another, rich and poor, but used the same humanity and affability to all alike. Putting forth his hand he pulled them to him, embraced and kissed them, which was the common complement that was used in those times for expressing of love. Now this he did to show how impartial he was, and that in all these courtesies he had no respect of persons, but only aimed at justice and the common good of all the people.

**V. 6.** {*So Absalom stole the hearts of the men of Israel*} To wit, from David his father, whose they were by right and duty, as being the lawful king and sovereign lord, unto himself to whom they belonged not in that relation. They might lawfully love and honor him as their prince, yet not so as to set the crown on his head, which was only his father's right. In this respect he is said to have *stolen their hearts;* because he sought to take away that which was due only to his father, and also in that he did it secretly and deceitfully, so that neither the people nor David himself did discover it. But it may be well admired that he who was so wise a man, and a king also (and kings are usually very jealous in matters which concern their crown) should suspect nothing, seeing Absalom's common carriage to all the people and his daily practice could hardly be kept from his ears, but he must hear of it, by the relation of one or other. But though love did not silence their tongues, David's fondness stopped his ears, that he had neither power nor will to hearken to any reports against his son. But this came to pass principally, because it was God's pleasure to use Absalom as his rod to correct his father, and to bring upon him those judgments, which he had by Nathan threatened against him, for his adultery and murder.

**V. 7.** {*And it came to pass after forty years}* From what period of time these forty years are to be computed, is a great question among expositors. For the determining whereof, I will let pass divers of their conceits which have no probable ground, and insist only upon two, which are most likely. The first is of those, who think that this account is to begin with David's reign. But seeing he reigned but forty years in all, it does not seem unto me very probable, that this reckoning can stand which takes up his whole time, from his first anointing at Hebron, to his death. And though it be said, that the things afterwards related from Absalom's rebellion, to the end of David's reign, were all transacted in a short time, and as they conceive, in the last year before his death; yet it seems to me to be far otherwise, and apparently contrary to the plain text. For besides the time required to the raising of the rebellion of Absalom and Sheba, and the suppressing of the rebels; we read of a three years' famine that followed in the days of David, inflicted upon the land for the sin of Saul and his bloody house; of execution done upon Saul's sons, and of Rizpah's attendance upon their dead bodies, which were hung up in the hill all the time of the harvest, chap. 20. ver. 10, mention also is made of wars raised by the Philistines three several times, and of David's victories over them; for the obtaining whereof, he went out and fought against them in his own person, and was in danger to have been slain, had not Abishai succored him, ch. 21. ver. 15, 16, which surely he could not have been able to do, when the same year natural heat was so wholly spent, that he could by no means have any warmth procured, but lay (as we say) bedridden. Besides, there were almost ten months taken up in numbering the people, chap. 24. ver. 8. And in this very time of Absalom's rebellion, it is related, that David was so strong and healthy, that he fled away on foot; yea, barefoot, and not only on plain ground, but by climbing up by the ascent of mount Olivet, ver. 30. And afterwards was resolved to have gone out with the people to battle, had they not dissuaded him, not because he was old, impotent and decrepit; but only because they would not have him to endanger his person, ch. 18. ver. 2, 3. All which things considered, I cannot but wonder that so many learned and godly expositors, should entertain such a groundless conceit, that all those things were done in a very short time before David's death. And therefore I much rather incline to their opinion, who reckon these forty years from the time of David's first anointing by Samuel, whereby he had from God just title and right to the crown and kingdom; though he had no actual possession for divers years after. Against which computation, there is but one only objection of any weight, which I do not doubt, but that I can easily answer; namely, that David's anointing being done privately, and in a secret manner, could not well be a rule of the public account; for though this action were secret and private when it was done, yet long before this rebellion of Absalom, in all the former parts of David's reign, it was famous and notoriously known to all men, and so now became a fit period of time, for the raising from it this present computation of forty years.

*{Let me go and pay my vow which I have vowed to the Lord in Hebron*} Or, *pay my vow in Hebron, which I have vowed whilest I abode at Geshur.* Where, like a cunning hypocrite he makes religion to be the cloak for his rebellion, because he knew that his pious father would not be a hindrance unto him in any act of devotion and religion. And he makes choice of Hebron, because it was a place famous, as being ancient, and one of the chief cities of Israel, where was the sepulcher of the patriarchs, Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, and a principal high place whereunto they resorted to offer sacrifices, especially gratulatory oblations and peace offerings, which might be lawfully done in other places besides the tabernacle, before the building of the temple. See 1 Sam. 9. 12 and the note upon it. The place also where his father was first crowned king, and where himself was born and bred, and where his kindred, friends, and acquaintance lived, which made him chiefly to desire to go thither, as being likely there to find the best assistance.

**V. 8.** {*If the Lord shall bring me again indeed to Jerusalem, then will I serve the Lord*} To wit, in a public manner, by offering unto him gratulatory oblations and peace offerings; of which service he makes choice to propound it to his father, not only because he knew that it would rejoice his heart to see his son so religiously devout to show his thankfulness, and so make him willing to condescend to his suit, but also, because at such sacrifices they used to make great feasts, unto which they invited their friends and acquaintance; under which color he had a fit opportunity to assemble as many as he thought meet to come together, for the raising of this rebellion.

**V. 10.** {*But Absalom sent spies throughout all the tribes of Israel*} To wit, to this end, that he might discover how the chief of every tribe stood affected towards him; and withal, that they might prepare a way for his rebellion, by persuading and drawing the people to his party, who were already too much inclined towards him, ver. 6.

*{As soon as ye shall hear the sound of the Trumpet, then shall ye say, Absalom reigneth in Hebron*} When the trumpets shall be sounded in every tribe, at the same time which shall be agreed upon and appointed by Absalom, then, at the hearing thereof, when the people wondered what it should mean, the spies and others, which were to be sent for this purpose, should inform them, that Absalom was anointed king in Hebron, with all regal ceremonies and solemnities, and was so accepted and proclaimed by the people. Or else, when these trumpets sounded in every tribe, they were appointed to tell them the cause of it; namely, that it was to summon them to resort unto Hebron, to give their approbation and acclamation to their new crowned king in Hebron, and to cry out, as it was usual in such inaugurations, *God save King Absalom.*

**V. 11.** {*And with Absalom went two hundred men out of Jerusalem, that were called, and they went in their simplicity, &c.*} That is, Absalom invited two hundred special men of note, to come to the feast of his peace offerings, whom he acquainted not with his conspiracy, and were therefore wholly innocent in the point of his rebellion. Of which he made choice, as being eminent men, and so fit to grace his action, and by their example, to draw on others to join with them; and because he had conceived good hope, that having drawn them from the king's party at Jerusalem, to come among his own friends at Hebron, they might easily be persuaded to be on his side, seeing they could not with any safety, now they were there, show their dislike, by forsaking the faction, and returning home; especially, considering how generally he was beloved of the people, ver. 6. But howsoever, though he could not so far prevail with them, as to withdraw them from David, yet now at least, they were in his power, and under restraint, that they could not give him their assistance, though they should much desire it.

**V. 12.** {*And Absalom sent for Ahithophel the Gilonite David’s counsellor}* Who it seems, though he had been formerly in great esteem with David for his wisdom; yet now there was some breach between them; either because David had, upon good experience, discovered him to be a crafty unconscionable politician, and a wicked man, or some other quarrel; and as some think, (though I see no reason for it,) for abusing Bathsheba, supposed to be his grandchild, seeing he had made her large amends, by taking her to wife, and making her a queen. But howsoever manifest it is, that he proved unfaithful to David, and became Absalom's counselor in plotting his conspiracy; though at this time he was gone home to his own house, that he might more easily disguise himself, and not be known to have his hand in this rebellion.

*{From his City even from Giloh*} Which was a city in Judah, Josh. 15. 51.

*{whilst he offered sacrifices}* To wit, his peace offerings, unto which he invited Ahitophel, as one of his guests.

*{the conspiracy was strong, for the people increased continually with Absalom}* Which was no small wonder, that a king so pious, just, and prosperous in all his undertakings, should be so ungratefully forsaken by his people, who notwithstanding made profession of God's true religion. But this came to pass, partly, from the fickle and unconstant disposition of the common people, who are soon weary of the present government, if at least they find any wants and weaknesses in it, which cannot wholly be avoided in the very best; and partly, because they were great with child of those swelling hopes, which Absalom, by his fair shows and flatteries, had begotten them; and partly, because the conspirators had blacked and blasted David, by divulging and aggravating all the errors of his life and government; as his adultery with Bathsheba, the murder of Uriah, with divers others of the army, his pardoning of Amnon for his incestuous rape; to which they might have added, his not punishing of Absalom, as a treacherous parricide, but that it would have made against their cause; which because it did not make for, but against them, therefore it is likely, that instead of that, they justified Absalom's fact, as being an act of his just revenge for the abuse of his sister, and contrariwise, laid it as a great fault to David's charge, that he had dealt harshly and rigorously with Absalom, in disgracing and discountenancing him so long a time for so good an action. But principally, this came to pass by God's special providence, who chiefly rules all the affairs of men, and their events, by ruling and inclining their hearts which way he pleases; and so here, the hearts of the people, in turning them from holy David, to impious Absalom; that thereby he might chastise his sins, by bringing upon him that punishment which they had deserved, and he had threatened.

**V. 14.** {*And David said unto all his servants——arise, and let us flee*} Though David were very valiant in himself, and now in a place of great strength, the fort of Sion, and had a strong party to make it good, and defend him against all the forces of his traitorous son, yet he resolved not to stay by it, but to flee away with all speed. Yet not out of cowardly fear, or distrust in God's power and providence; for it is manifest by the third Psalm, which is said to have been penned upon this occasion, that by faith he relied upon God as his shield; and resting upon his strength and protection, he professes, that he would not be afraid of ten thousands of people setting themselves against him round about, Psal. 3. ver. 3, 6.
