But out of his wisdom, he uses his flight, as a means to serve God's providence, as being the likeliest way to escape Absalom's fury; and not knowing certainly how those in the city stood affected towards him, nor whether there might not be many, in this fearful distraction and rebellion, that would desert him, and adhere to his son, seeing it is usual with the common people to adore the sun rising, rather than the sun setting; he thought it his wisest course to decline the present danger, by departing for a time, till he saw to what party they were inclined; and in the meanwhile, might use the best course to allay the rage of his rebellion; and either to regain them that were disaffected, or to strengthen his own party, that they might subdue them. Besides, his care of preserving the city from being spoiled and plundered, if they stood on their defense, by the rebels of Absalom's army abroad, assisted by the malignant party within, made him willing to secure it by his departure, preferring (like a good king) the common weal before his own personal interest. But chiefly, this came to pass by a powerful providence overruling him, and inclining his heart to take this course, that thereby a way might be made, for the bringing that judgment to pass, which God had threatened, of giving his wives to be defiled by his graceless son, ch. 12. 11.

**V. 16.** {*And the King left ten women which were concubines, to keep the house*} Which he did, thinking that they would not be so barbarously inhumane, as to offer any violence to them, being of the weaker sex, and unable to make any resistance; and much more, that they would out of reverence forbear to do it to them, who were in so near a relation to their king. But principally, this came to pass, by a special providence overruling his heart, that being thus left behind in the house, they might become the subjects of Absalom's filthy lust.

**V. 17.** {*And the King went forth, and all the people after him*} i.e. Not only his household, with his courtiers and officers, but also the Cherethites, and Pelethites, and Gittites, that were his chief guard, and with him all the citizens that were known to be his faithful friends and subjects, who would have been endangered to the rage of Absalom's party, if they had stayed behind him.

*{and tarried in a place that was far off*} That is, when he had gotten himself in some good distance out of the city, he made there some stay for a time, both to rest and repose himself and his weary followers; and also as a rendezvous for his friends to resort unto, that being collected into one body, they might be the better able to stand in their own defense, if they were attempted by their enemies; and also, that he might observe what course and counsels Absalom and his party would take, that he might accordingly use means to prevent or frustrate their proceedings.

**V. 18.** {*And all his servants passed on besides him, and all the Cherethites, and all the Pelethites*} See ch. 8. ver. 18, and the note upon it, and 1 Kin. 11. 38.

*{and all the Gittites*} Which were (as Junius thinks,) the garrison soldiers, who were appointed to keep Gath, which David, not long since, had taken from the Philistines, ch. 8. 1, which were valiant men, and long experienced in warlike affairs, and so the fitter to guard David's person in a time of danger.

*{Then said the King to Ittai the Gittite*} Who was captain over the six hundred Gittites that came from Gath; and as some suppose the king of Gath's son, who was become a proselyte to the Jewish religion; and being highly in the king's favor, as one of his great worthies, and a wise and valiant captain, he committed unto his conduct, a third part of his army in that battle against Absalom, ch. 18. 2. Others think, that he was a native Israelite, but called a Gittite, and a stranger, as likewise all the six hundred over whom he commanded, because of their habitation, they living at Gath, as the garrison of that city, and Ittai, their captain. But this will not stand with the text, seeing he calls him not only a stranger, but also an exile, who (as it may seem) was fled from the Philistines, and left all his lands and means in their country; and (it may be) for conscience' sake, hating their idolatry, and desiring to become a proselyte to the true religion; to which purpose he came to live with David, and for the same cause was much esteemed of him.

**V. 19.** {*Wherefore goest thou with us? return to thy place*} As if he had said, you have no reason to expose yourself to such troubles and dangers, as you are likely to meet with, if you do accompany us in our flight; but it were your wiser course to return to Jerusalem, and to endeavor your advancement, by complying with the new king.

**V. 20.** {*Whereas thou camest but yesterday*} That is, seeing you have as yet reaped so small benefit by my favor, as coming but very lately to my court, there is no reason why you should put yourself to so much trouble, as to partake with me, I know not where nor how long, in all my miseries and afflictions. Whereas, if you return to the new anointed king, he will receive you, and not suspect, or hurt you or your followers, seeing you are strangers.

*{and take back thy brethren*} i.e. Your countrymen and followers who are under your command.

*{mercy and truth be with thee*} i.e. As you have showed yourself merciful in commiserating my miseries, and a faithful and true friend, who is best known in adversity; so the God of mercy and truth recompense it unto you and all your followers, with his chief and choicest blessings.

**V. 21.** {*As the Lord liveth, and as my lord the King liveth*} See 1 Sam. 20. 3, and the note upon it.

*{surely in what place my lord the King shall be*} That is, nothing shall make me leave you, prosperity, or adversity; life or death.

**V. 22.** {*And David said to Ittai, Go and pass over}* i.e. The river Kidron, ver. 23, which lay between the city and the mount of Olives.

**V. 23.** {*And all the country wept with a loud voice*} i.e. All the people inhabiting about Jerusalem, lamented the woeful and afflicted condition of their good and gracious king.

*{and all the people went over*} To wit, which accompanied David in his flight.

*{the King also himself passed over the brook Kidron*} Called also Cedron, John 18. 1, which was a small river or torrent, running in a dark shady valley near mount Olivet, and without Jerusalem, into which the good kings of Judah, in the times of reformation, used to cast idolatrous reliques, and over which our Savior is said often to have passed, when he with his disciples resorted into that garden, in which he was betrayed and apprehended, John 18. 1, 2.

*{towards the way of the Wilderness}* Which was eastward from Jerusalem, between it and Jordan.

**V. 24.** {*And lo, Zadok also, and all the Levites with him bearing the Ark*} i.e. He, who was second priest, and next the high priest Abiathar, joined with the Levites, in carrying the ark, as they used sometimes to do in weighty and solemn occasions, Josh. 3. 6. (For otherwise, ordinarily the Levites were appointed to bear it, Num. 4. 4. 1 Chron. 15. 15.) And to Zadok's turn, it seems, this service now fell, according to the distribution of attendance, which David had made on his holy ministry, 1 Chr. 9. 22. & 15. 11. And the cause of this service of carrying the ark with David at this time, was, that in his troubles and dangers they might pray to God, and consult with him before it, to clear their doubts and difficulties, and to crave his direction.

*{and they set down the Ark of God*} To wit, in the place where David purposed to rest and repose himself, with the rest of his company.

*{And Abiathar went up*} That is, to the top of the mount of Olives; (as it is likely) to this end, that they might from thence take a view of the people, as they passed by with David out of Jerusalem over the brook Kidron, to the place where they had set down the ark; and that also from thence he might discover when they left coming out of the city.

**V. 25.** {*And the King said unto Zadok, Carry back the Ark of God into the city*} Though David highly esteemed the ark of God, as being the visible sign of his presence; before which such great wonders had been wrought in former times, as the drying up of Jordan, and the falling flat of the walls of Jericho, and never more needed the comfort of his presence, than in this his great affliction and distress; yet he commands it to be carried back, as more relying upon God's powerful presence and assistance, which was the substance, than upon the ark, which was but a sign and shadow of it. And this he did, partly, because he could not enjoy the ark, unless he had also the priests and Levites to attend it, whom he was loath to expose to Absalom's fury, for deserting him, having had former experience what had befallen them in the days of Saul upon his displeasure, for adhering to David. And partly, he was willing to leave them behind him, because he knew none so faithful and able as they, to do him service in his absence, by observing all the passages, counsels, and courses of the rebels, and acquainting him with them from time to time, as they got any fit opportunity. And this cause is expressed, ver. 35, 36.

*{if I shall find favour in the eyes of the Lord, &c.*} Where David sweetly and meekly submits to God's good pleasure; and wholly denying himself and his own will, he resigns up his person, crown, and state, to be disposed of as it should seem good in his eyes, knowing that he is infinitely wise, and understood what was best; omnipotent, and able to do what he pleases, and incomprehensibly good and gracious to his children that love and fear him; and therefore will convert all to their everlasting good. Wherein he left himself an example to all future ages, of reliance upon God, and of sweet and comfortable contentation in all conditions, unto which he shall be pleased to call us.

*{and shew me both it and his habitation*} i.e. His tabernacle, or tent, in which the ark stood, the visible sign of God's grace and presence.

**V. 26.** {*But if he thus say, I have no delight in thee*} i.e. If he does not accept of me, because of my sins and great unworthiness, but is pleased to correct and try me further, I wholly submit to his good pleasure.

**V. 27.** {*And the King said to Zadok the Priest, Art not thou a Seer?*} 1 Sam. 9. Whereby is meant, either that Zadok had the extraordinary gift of prophecy; or rather, in respect of his near approaches unto God in his office and ministry, he saw more into God's secrets than ordinary men, and so accordingly was able to teach and instruct the people in the knowledge of his will, Mal. 2. 7. Who more needed at this time his presence and guidance, than at others; and therefore he could not now be well missed, especially, considering, that it was (as it is very likely,) his course to give his public attendance at the tent or tabernacle, according to that order *which David and Samuel the Seer, had ordained,* 1 Chr. 9. 22. And in this respect, he being a public minister, who was bound to attend the ark for the service of the whole church and people, David chose rather to dispense with his absence from him, than he would withdraw him from his public service; seeing also in some respects he might by his staying behind, be very serviceable unto him.

**V. 28.** {*Until there come word from you to certify me}* i.e. Till you give notice of what shall be done in the city, and in the court of the new king.

**V. 29.** {*Zadok therefore and Abiathar carried the Ark of God again*} Not taking their sons Ahimaaz and Jonathan with them, as David directed them; but causing them to stay, upon some special occasion, at Enrogel, in some distance from Jerusalem, that lying there in obscurity, they might receive their fathers' instructions, and so acquaint David with them, as occasion was offered, ch. 17. 17.

**V. 30.** {*And David went up by the ascent of the mount of Olives, and wept as he went up*} Not so much bewailing his present misery and affliction brought upon him by his ungracious and unnatural son, whom he tenderly loved, as because he had moved God in his just displeasure, thus to correct him for his grievous sins of adultery and murder.

*{and had his head covered*} In token of mourning and deep humiliation, according to the manner of close mourners, ch. 19. 4. Hest. 6. 12. Jer. 14. 2. Who used to cover their heads, that men might not see their faces blubbered with tears, and to keep their own eyes from wandering, and gazing after such outward objects, as would divert and hinder them in their mourning.

*{And he went barefoot*} As a sign of sorrow and shame, Isai. 20. 3, 4.

*{And all the people that was with him, covered every man his head*} i.e. Partaking with the king in his afflictions and sorrows; they conformed themselves to his example, in the like expressions of their mourning.

**V. 31.** {*And one told David, saying, Ahithophel is amongst the Conspirators}* Which report was brought to him by a special providence, the more to humble David, and to make him to seek unto God with more fervent prayer, to defeat his policies, because he was well acquainted with his deep reach, and great wisdom.

*{O Lord, I pray thee, turn the counsel of Ahithophel into foolishness}* Which prayer of faith and fervency God heard and granted, ch. 16. 21. & 17. 14, 23.

**V. 32.** {*When David was come to the top of the mount—he worshipped God*} i.e. The mount of Olives, from whence he took this present occasion of worshiping God, not only because he might from thence turn himself towards mount Sion, where the tent was pitched; the which was the place of the ark, the sign of God's presence and type of Christ, to which, when they prayed, they used to turn their faces, 1 king. 8. 44, 48. Dan. 6. 10. But because also, he might at this time behold the ark in its passage, as it was carrying thither, and thereby be moved to pray with more fervency of affection, seeing he knew not how long it would be, ere he should again enjoy this sign of God's presence, of which by his flight he was now deprived.

*{Behold Hushai the Archite came to meet him*} So called from the place, either of his birth or habitation, which was Archi, in the borders of the children of Joseph; of which, mention is made, Josh. 16. 2. Whereby David had the first return of defeating Ahitophel's counsel, according to his prayer, which was no sooner made, but God presently provided and sent Hushai to meet him, whom he had qualified with such wisdom, that he was able to do it.

*{with his coat rent, and earth upon his head*} See ch. 1. 2, and the note upon it. By which ceremonies, they used to express their deep humiliation, Josh. 11. 6. 1 Sam. 4. 12, and ch. 13. 19.

**V. 33.** {*Unto whom David said, If thou passest over with me, thou shalt be a burden unto me*} i.e. Altogether unprofitable and troublesome; to wit, because you and your company will but add to my care, in protecting you from danger; and it will be but an unnecessary charge also, to provide for you victuals and necessaries, of which I am as yet scanted, having little enough for the company that already attend me.

**V. 34.** {*But if thou return to the City, and say unto Absalom*} Where he teaches Hushai to dissemble himself to be Absalom's friend and servant, though it were his chief intention to defeat all his counsels, and to hinder him in all his traitorous courses and proceedings. Wherein he discovers human frailty, which puts men, in their extremities, to use unlawful means, as dissimulation, lies, and equivocations, to compass their end and desires, as not thinking the simple truth so sufficient, as they may safely rely upon it, without these shifts.

**V. 35.** {*And hast thou not there with thee Zadok and Abiathar?*} That is, you have surely them there to assist you with their counsel, and all their endeavors. See Judg. 4. 6.

**V. 36.** {*Behold, they have there with them their two sons*} To wit, as David thought, who sent them away to the city together, not knowing that upon their fathers' command, they stayed at Enrogel, ch. 17. 17.

## CHAPTER 16

**Verse 1.** {*And when David was a little past the top of the hill*} That is, the mount of Olives, where he had worshiped God, ch. 15. 32, and was going on towards the wilderness.

*{Ziba the servant of Mephibosheth met him*} Though David for the present, were in an afflicted condition, and dethroned by his son, yet Ziba was willing to make an adventure, by presenting him in the time of his extremity and necessity with gifts of some value and more use, in hope of his restitution to his former state and dignity, because he had long experience of David's piety and justice in all his reign; and how God had prospered him in all his enterprises, and considered also Absalom's wickedness and rebellion, in which it was not likely that God would give him good success. Which if it came to pass, he then assured himself, that he would bountifully requite all his kindness in his prosperity, who had thus helped and relieved him and his followers in their adversity and want. Wherein (as I conceive) the adventure was not great, being compared with his hopes; seeing it is very probable, that he, who had the managing of all his master's estate, and all his goods at his disposing, did furnish this present out of Mephibosheth's means and store, and took advantage by thus using his goods, to betray his master, and enrich himself. Seeing I look upon him as a false, faithless, and treacherous servant, who being destitute of the fear of God, made conscience of nothing, but only aimed at his own gain.

**V. 2.** {*And the King said unto Ziba, What meanest thou by these?*} Though he might easily perceive, that Ziba brought these things as a present; yet because he was but a servant, with whom it was not usual to present things of such value, he supposed that he might be sent unto him by his master, and that he only was appointed to bring them, as his messenger; and therefore he propounds this question, that he might be more fully resolved from whom they came.

*{And Ziba said, the asses are for the King’s household to ride upon, &c.}* Though he intended them chiefly for David's use, yet comparing the smallness of the gift with his greatness, he does in a seeming modesty, extenuate his present as far unworthy so great a king; though upon such a sudden occasion, they might be of some use for his followers, in which respect, he might be pleased, graciously to accept of his humble love and duty, though the things themselves were but of small value.

**V. 3.** {*And the King said, And where is thy master’s son?}* i.e. Mephibosheth, the son of Jonathan. Formerly he had spoken nothing of him, as he might expect, in answer to his former question, but took upon himself alone, the present which he had brought, he takes occasion to inquire further of him, what was become of Mephibosheth in these troublesome times.

*{And Ziba said——he abideth at Jerusalem; for he said, To day shall the house of Israel restore me the Kingdom of my father}* The which was a shameless and improbable slander, both because of this poor cripple's unfitness to reign, as king; and also, because all the people's hearts were set upon Absalom, whom they had already chosen to be their king, and applauded him and themselves in their choice. And yet David, out of a kingly jealousy, (which makes them very tender and credulous in things that concern their crown) does over-readily, and easily, believe this base slander of this faithless sycophant, upon the weakest surmises, and slightest grounds; as that he aspired to sovereignty, because he was the next heir to the crown, of Saul's line; though it were apparent, that God rejected him and his posterity from being kings, and had established David on the throne; and though that race had not been cut off, yet this poor impotent man was far unlikely to lay any claim unto it; seeing he was so uncapable and unfit, that he was passed by, and neglected before; and Ishbosheth (though a man of no great worth, and having no just title,) was preferred before him. And another poor ground of his suspicion might be, because, at this time of his trouble, he had neglected to see him, when as those that were his faithful friends, had resorted unto him, to do him service; whereas he was a poor cripple, who could not come on foot, and his servant had abused and hindered him from coming, by depriving him of the use of his ass, ch. 19. 26.

**V. 4.** {*Then said the King to Ziba, Behold, thine are all that pertained to Mephibosheth*} Which was a most unjust sentence, far unworthy to be passed by so just a king, and that so rashly, upon the bare suggestion of one single man; yea, a servant against his master, in his absence, not so much as hearing what he could say for himself, without examining the cause, or producing of two or three witnesses, according to the law. But much more unworthy David was it, thus to censure the only son of his dearest Jonathan, to whom he had been so deeply and strongly engaged by his true worth, incomparable love, and singular favors; yea, by covenant and oath, solemnly made again and again in the presence of God. All which David now forgets, being transported with passion and high displeasure conceived against Mephibosheth, because he had neglected those many and great favors which he had formerly showed him; but especially, out of his jealousy towards him, as one that aspired to the crown and kingdom, which admits not of co-partners, or co-rivals. The which fault of David, is much aggravated by the circumstance of time, that he should now so much forget himself, contrary to all justice and common humanity, as to pronounce such an unjust sentence, when the hand of God did lie heavy upon him. But this plainly shows, that the best men are but men, and so frail and feeble in their own strength, that they are ready to faint, fail, and fall, if the Lord leaves them to themselves never so little in the time of temptation; and withal, it discovers how dangerous a thing it is, for men in place of judicature, to receive gifts and bribes, *which blind the eyes of the wise, and perverts the words of the righteous,* Exod. 23. 8.

*{And Ziba said, I humbly beseech thee, that I may find grace in thy sight*} As if he had said, though I humbly acknowledge, that I am much bound unto my lord the king for his bounty, in conferring upon me so great a gift, for such a small service, and do with all humble thankfulness accept of it; yet I do not so much desire to be enriched with my master's goods, as to deserve the continuance of your grace and favor towards me, in which I most rejoice.

**V. 5.** {*And when King David came to Bahurim*} A city situate on the frontiers of Judah, in the tribe of Benjamin, an hour's journey beyond Bethania, ch. 3. 16.

*{thence came out a man of the family of the house of Saul*} Who therefore maligned David, because Saul was rejected, and he chosen king in his stead.

*{he came forth and cursed still as he came*} i.e. Being enraged, and even mad with malice, he was content to vent his spleen and hatred against David, when he saw him in this affliction, as thinking he would wholly sink under it, though it were with the extreme hazard of his own life, whereas being but one single man, he incensed all David's friends and followers, by his opprobrious speeches, and malicious slanders against him, 1 king. 2. 1.

**V. 6.** {*And all the mighty men were on his right hand, and on his left*} Which is added, to note his desperate fury and folly, in thus provoking David, when he had such a strong guard about him, who were able, at their pleasure, to revenge upon him these horrible wrongs and indignities.

**V. 7.** {*And thus said Shimei——come out, come out, thou bloody man*} Where he redoubles his words, to express his rage and indignation. Where (according to his sense) he charges him most falsely with cruelty and bloodshed, as imputing unto him the death of Ishbosheth and Abner, and the blood that was shed in the wars against them, that he might get the kingdom, of all which David was most guiltless and innocent, seeing they were slain without his privity, and sore against his will; and the wars begun by Abner's provocation, when Joab did not intend it, ch. 2. 14, 27. For no other blood had been yet spilt of the house of Saul, unto which only, his speech had reference, v. 8. But God, in his secret providence, otherwise intended it, namely, to direct those shafts, shot at rovers, home to the wounding of David's heart with true grief, for the innocent blood of Uriah, and the rest of the army slain with him; of which David was guilty, and desired to be cleansed from it, Psalm 51. v. 14. *Deliver me from blood guiltinesse, O God.*

*{and thou man of Belial*} i.e. One, who being desperately wicked had shaken off the yoke of government, and would be under no law. See Judg. 19. 22, wherein he shamelessly slandered David, who being a holy and just man, was a strict observer of all God's commandments, Psal. 119. 6.

**V. 8.** {*The Lord hath returned upon thee all the blood of the house of Saul*} i.e. Does now justly call you to account for all the blood of Saul's family, which you have unjustly spilt, that you might aspire to the kingdom. Where he most falsely charges him, that he had gotten the kingdom by ambitious usurpation, and made way unto it by slaughtering those of Saul's house, who had right unto it. Whereas David never aspired unto it; but when he least thought of it, was called and chosen by God, and anointed king by Samuel, and did afterwards patiently wait upon God, till Saul's death, for the possession of it, though he had divers times opportunities put into his hand, of cutting him off.

*{And the Lord hath delivered the Kingdom into the hand of Absalom thy son*} So calling him, the more to sting and vex his heart, in that his own son should be appointed by God to be the means and instrument to supplant him; which had some show of truth, in respect of the secret acting of God's will and providence, and the present event; but utterly false, in respect of God's revealed will, which condemned Absalom's act, as horrible and unnatural rebellion.

*{Behold, thou art taken in thy mischief*} i.e. By God's just judgment, punished for all your wickedness, and that in a way of retaliation; for as you have rebelled against your father-in-law, and usurped his kingdom, so your son has rebelled against you, and usurped yours.

**V. 9.** {*Then said Abishai——Why should this dead dog curse my Lord?*} i.e. Why should this base and contemptible wretch be suffered to go unpunished, for reproaching the Lord's anointed to his face, and before all his followers, seeing he has justly deserved death, and we have power sufficient to revenge these wrongs upon him? See 1 Sam. 24. 15. & chap. 3. 8. & 9. 8.

**V. 10.** {*What have I to do with you ye sons of Zeruiah?*} That is, meddle not with my matters which belong not unto you, for I regard not your counsel in this way of revenge. Where he speaks in the plural number, though Abishai only had offered his service, because, as it is likely, his brother Joab had given the same counsel, though Abishai alone had offered to execute it. The which counsel David rejected, not as unjust in itself, seeing he well deserved death for reproaching the Lord's anointed; but because it was unseasonable, seeing now it was no time to revenge his personal quarrels, when he was in this afflicted condition, and greatly humbled under God's correcting hand; and because, in this time of commotion and rebellion, this private revenge might haply exasperate the minds and hearts of some of the people, especially the tribe of Benjamin, of which Shimei was, which now needed to be rather composed and won unto David by his humanity and clemency.

*{because the Lord hath said unto him, Curse David*} To wit, not by his word or law, which expressly forbids, *To revile the Magistrate, or to curse the Rulers of the people,* Exod. 22. 28, but by a secret command of providence, so overruling and disposing of Shimei's malice, that it might be a rod of correction, to chastise David for his sins, and for the time to come might be a cause of his own ruin. So 1 king. 22. 21, 22, compared with 2 Sam. 24. 1. & 12. 11, 12.

*{Who shall then say, Wherefore hast thou done so?*} That is, who can then expostulate with the Lord, and call him to account, seeing he has justly corrected David for his sins, ch. 12. 10, 11. Though Shimei sinned heinously in cursing and reviling the Lord's anointed, and was afterwards by Solomon called to account, and justly punished for it, 1 king. 2. 9. & 44, 46.

**V. 11.** {*Behold, my son which came forth of my bowels, seeketh my life*} That is, if my son which had his being from me, and of whom I have so much deserved by my fatherliness of love and innumerable favors, does not only ambitiously usurp my kingdom, but sticks not, for the compassing of his desire, to take away my life, which heavy cross God has given me some strength to bear it; how much more may I with patience endure these reproaches, which are offered unto me by a stranger; yea, in respect of his relation to Saul's family, an enemy, from whom I might expect such indignities.

*{Let him curse, for the Lord hath bidden him*} That is, has so disposed of it by his providence, that he should out of his own malice, thus curse and rail, to correct me for my sins.

**V. 12.** {*It may be the Lord——will requite good for his cursing this day*} That is, turn his cursings into blessings, when, by his fatherly chastisements, he has humbled me, and fitted me for them. The which his hope is not grounded on anything in himself, as though, by his patience in bearing this affliction, he had deserved it; but on the observation of God's ordinary dealing with his children, whom he usually exalts after he has humbled them, as appears in the examples of Joseph, Job, and many others. The which he speaks not out of an infallible confidence, but doubtingly, as having no express promise from God of these temporal blessings.

**V. 13.** {*Shimei went along on the hill’s side——and cursed as he went}* Where is showed Shimei's desperate and indefatigable malice, in that he continued to provoke David and his followers with such reproaches and indignities, notwithstanding it was with the extreme hazard of his own life; and David's admirable patience being now supported by God, who being lately in the cause of Mephibosheth and Ziba (which was a far lighter trial) left to himself, discovered much impatience and impotent weakness.

**V. 14.** {*and refreshed themselves there*} That is, at Bahurim, ver. 5.

**V. 15.** {*And all the people, the men of Israel*} That is, all that adhered unto him in his conspiracy and rebellion.

**V. 16.** {*Hushai said to Absalom, God save the King*} Heb. *let the King live.* Where Hushai, to curry favor with Absalom, to bring about his design, wishes unto him all health and happiness, but, in the meantime, deludes him with amphibologies and doubtful speeches which he applied to Absalom with his tongue, but to David with his heart, *God save the King;* though spoken to Absalom might be intended to King David, and so in the rest; but such equivocations and mental reservations, howsoever they may silence the cry of a carnal conscience, yet they are but shifts and shuffles, which have no warrant out of the word of God.

**V. 17.** {*Is this thy kindness to thy friend?}* As if he had said, if you be no more faithful to David your friend, who has done you so many favors, how should I expect fidelity from you, who have not so much deserved it? Where it is observable, that he does not say, is this your kindness to my father, but to your friend; both because his mind was alienated from him, that he would not so much as acknowledge him for his father; and also, because he should have secretly taxed and condemned himself of far fouler disloyalty than that which he reproved in him, for if it were a heinous fault to revolt from a friend in his adversity: how much more for a son to rebel against such a father as had been so indulgent unto him, and thereby to bring him into such affliction and misery, as made his other friends to desert and leave him? All this he slips over, and frames only such an expostulation, as might try him, whether he would be true to him, or no, in this present cause, and then not caring whether he loved or loathed true friendship, so he would serve his present turn.

*{And Hushai said——nay, but whom the Lord and his people shall choose, his will I be}* And that was none but David and Solomon after him; but he pretended this of Absalom, that he had made choice of him, and would adhere to his cause, upon this reason, because it appeared by the event, that God had chosen him, and the people also, to be king, and therefore no private obligation ought to hinder him from yielding unto him all due subjection and service, as his liege lord, seeing if he did otherwise, he should, being but a private man, resist God's ordinance, and the public decrees and sanctions of the whole commonwealth.

**V. 18.** {*Should I not serve in the presence of his son?*} As if he had said; I have greater reason to serve you, than any other, seeing the kingdom is translated by common consent of the people, not to a stranger of another family, but unto you, who are his son and next heir to the crown, as being the eldest of your brethren. And thus Hushai, to avoid the suspicion of treachery, upon these reasons, renders unto him his service, that so being near unto him, he might dive into his secrets, and defeat his counsels and intendments.

**V. 20.** {*Give counsel among you what we shall do*} i.e. How so to carry on this whole business, as that we may promise unto ourselves prosperous success.

**V. 21.** {*And Ahithophel said to Absalom, Go in unto thy father’s concubines}* i.e. Lie with, and defile them in a public manner, so as all men may take notice of it.

*{and all Israel shall hear that thou art abhorred of thy father*} i.e. That you have made yourself odious unto him by this fact, and utterly without hope of being ever reconciled.

*{then shall the hands of all that are with thee be strong*} That is, they will, with all resolution, go on with you in this rebellion, when they are assured, that you have hereby cut off all hope of any reconciliation between you. Whereas, if the least hope should remain of making your peace with him, if your war should prove unprosperous, they would be fearful to proceed, lest being deserted by you, they should be in danger to be punished by your father, for rising against him.

**V. 22.** {*So they spread Absalom a tent upon the top of the house*} i.e. The flat roof, or terrace of the king's palace. See Deut. 22. 8. From which place, it is very probable, that he espied Bathsheba washing herself, and lusted after her, ch. 11. v. 2.

*{and Absalom went in to his father’s concubines, in the sight of all Israel}* Wherein, though Absalom committed an act most abominable; yet was his sin so ordered and overruled by God's providence, that thereby his righteous judgment was executed, which he had by Nathan denounced against David for his adultery and murder, ch. 12. 11, 12. But this did not at all lessen Absalom's sin, seeing it was on his part committed, not by any compulsion of his will, by this divine providence, but freely and voluntarily; and that not so much to testify his impetuous lust, as in the case of Amnon, but out of a malicious and politic design, which did much aggravate his sin. And yet, though this wicked man seemed so much to abhor Amnon's rape, that nothing but his blood could quench his inflamed rage; does here himself make nothing of it, to commit, even in the sight of the people, an higher degree, and much more heinous incest with his own father's concubines; and only to satisfy his proud ambition, and to strengthen himself in his rebellion and usurpation.

**V. 23.** {*And the counsel of Ahithophel——was, as if a man had enquired at the oracle of God}* i.e. Was highly esteemed and applauded, as if his counsels had been of divine authority and truth, because the event commonly answered with suitable success, to his counsel and advice; and not because it was in itself divine and good; for here it was most damnably wicked, but yet, in the policy of it, most perilous and pestilent.

## CHAPTER 17

**Vers. 1.** {*Ahithophel said to Absalom, let me now choose out 12000 men}* Where this arch-rebel shows himself so forward in Absalom's cause against his lord and king, that he is not content to be only his counselor to plot his mischievous designs, but also offers himself, in his own person, to act and execute them; as a captain and commander, to draw out, and lead an army to surprise him.

*{and I will arise, and pursue after David this night*} He does not entitle him his king, for so his mouth should have proclaimed himself a rebel, but calls him only David, as a man deposed by the people, who had now no right to the kingdom. Neither would he have the execution of his design deferred, but it must be presently undertaken that night, both because he would prevent David by his speed, from gathering more forces, or from entering into some city and place of strength, where he could not be easily surprised; and also, because, setting upon him suddenly in the night, he might, by striking their hearts with terror and amazement, obtain a more easy victory, v. 2.

**V. 2.** {*And I will come upon him whilst he is weary and weak handed}* i.e. When he and his company are tired and spent, with their sudden and long march, which they have been forced unto, to get out of reach, that they might not be supplanted with your army, and have neither counsel nor courage to make any resistance. See ch. 4. v. 1, and the note upon it.

*{and will make him afraid*} i.e. I shall strike terror and astonishment into him and all his followers, coming upon them suddenly and unexpected.

*{and I will smite the King only*} i.e. When the people possessed with sudden fear, are put to flight, I will let them go, and spend no time in pursuing them, but only my work shall be to kill their king, which is the thing chiefly to be desired, for the settling you in the quiet possession of the kingdom.

**V. 3.** {*And I will bring back all the people unto thee*} i.e. The king being slain, I will sound a retreat, and proclaim a cessation of arms, and peace, and so reduce them to be under your government; for the man whose life you seek, being slain and taken out of the way, it is all one, as if all were done, seeing all the people being destitute of a head to lead and guide them, will peaceably submit themselves unto your government.

**V. 4.** {*And the thing pleased Absalom*} Whereby it appears that this bloody rebel and graceless son, desires not only the kingdom, but also the death of his father, who had ever been so loving, and even too indulgent towards him. Where also the wise and powerful providence of God, is clearly manifested, in defeating and bringing to naught their wicked counsels, by overpowering and inclining Absalom's heart to mislike afterwards this counsel of Achitophel's, which now, for the present much pleased him.

**V. 5.** {*Then said Absalom call now Hushai,——And let us hear likewise what he saith*} Though the counsel of Achitophel much pleased him, yet because, in a weighty business, in the multitude of counselors there is safety, he would not rest upon it alone, but would, at least, hear what Hushai could say, whom he reputed to be a wise man; that so according to his pleasure he might make his choice. God so overruling his heart, by his providence, that he might turn Achitophel's wise counsel into foolishness, according to David's prayer.

**V. 6.** {*Shall we do after his saying? if not, Speak thou*} That is, do you think this the best course unto which he advises, that if it be so we may follow it; or if otherwise, speak you your mind, and let us know the reasons, why you dissent from them.

**V. 7.** {*And Hushai said to Absalom, The counsel which Ahithophel hath given is not good at this time}* Where, though it were his main drift to cross Achithophel's politic, and pernicious counsel; yet considering how great and gracious he was with Absalom, he carries on the matter with as much respect towards him, as would stand with his purpose; as if he had said, though I acknowledge Achithophel to be so prudent and politic a man, that he, commonly, gives such good counsel as is accompanied with prosperous success; yet at this time, and in this particular case, he is, as I conceive, much mistaken.

**V. 8.** {*Thy father and his men are mighty men, and they be chafed in their minds*} That is, so wise and valiant, that they will not be easily daunted and dismayed with any danger; and besides their ordinary courage, they are at this time so bitterly enraged with this affront of their being forced to flee, and to leave their wives, children and habitations, that they will redouble their strength and resolution to recover what they have lost; and like a bear robbed of his whelps, will put themselves upon the extremest hazards and uttermost of their endeavors, to recover those things that are so dear unto them.

*{and thy father is a man of war, and will not lodge with the people*} That is, a prudent and old experienced soldier, who will not, especially in the civil wars, wherein men of all conditions and dispositions are mingled together, entrust himself to lodge in the camp, because among many friends, there may be some enemies and traitors, in so great a number, that will watch for an opportunity to betray him, and do him mischief: So as it will not be so easy a matter to surprise the king, as Achithophel would make it.

**V. 9.** {*Behold, he is hid in some pit, or in some other place*} Which either may have reference to that which went before, and then the meaning is, that he would be hid in some secret cave, well known unto him, ever since he used to hide himself when Saul pursued him; and therefore would not be so easily found out and surprised, as Achithophel supposed; or, it may be related to that which follows, and then it is thus to be understood, that David being an old experienced soldier, and well acquainted with warlike stratagems, would lay an ambush in some cave or secret place, and from thence, suddenly assault Absalom's men, as they marched by them, and give them some defeat; which if it should happen, then presently it would be divulged among the people, that a part at least of the men of Absalom's 12000 army were overthrown, from whence would arise such a general discouragement, as would leave no hope of any good success.

**V. 10.** {*whose heart is as the heart of a Lion, shall utterly melt}* That is, those of your followers that are now most valorous, and courageous, will grow fainthearted, and will be dissolved with fear, even as the hardest and strongest metal, grows weak, when it is melted with the fire. See Josh. 2. 11.

*{For all Israel knoweth that thy father is a mighty man*} And therefore will be the more apt to receive such a report, and to entertain any jealousy or fear, which is grounded upon his prudence and valor.

**V. 11.** {*Therefore I counsel that all Israel be generally gathered together, from Dan even to Beersheba*} That is, from the utmost limits of the land in the length of it, from the north to the south. See Judg. 20. v. 1. From which his following speech, we may in the general observe, that he fits his counsel to the proud humor of a vainglorious prince, stuffing and filling up the emptiness of it with the bombast of lofty and hyperbolical flourishes; and first, he puffs him up with the vain thought of being king and general over a numerous and invincible army, and that he himself should go out in his own person, and so have the honor, both of command and victory. The main end of which his counsel was, to make him to spend time by delays, that David, in the meanwhile, might gather strength and increase his forces, by his friends coming and joining with him, seeing such a numerous army, as he speaks of could not in any short time be gathered together.

**V. 12.** {*And we will light upon him as the dew falleth upon the ground*} That is, as the dew falls at once and in all places, so you with your numerous army, will overspread the whole country, so as he shall not be able to escape whithersoever he goes.

**V. 13.** {*Then shall all Israel bring ropes to that city, and we will draw it into the river*} That is, with the engines of war fastened with ropes, they shall draw it, either into the river, because cities usually had their rivers running along by their walls: Or, ditches and trenches filled with water, which encompassed the walls of the city for their defense: Which is nothing else but a hyperbolical and glorious expression used by soldiers boasting of their own strength, in respect of their multitude. So 1 king. 20. 10.

*{until there be not one small stone found there}* That is, until it be utterly destroyed, Psal. 83. 14. Matth. 24. 2.

**V. 14.** {*For the Lord had appointed to defeat the good counsel of Ahithophel}* It was not good in itself (for no counsel is good, that is not honest and agreeable to God's word) yea, contrariwise it was evil and wicked, as tending to the strengthening of graceless Absalom in his rebellious course, and the ruin of his holy father; but it is called good, that is, very subtle, politic and effectual, if it had been followed, for the attaining of Absalom's end, to wit, the overthrow of David, if he had presently pursued him, and had given him no time to raise and gather an army; but God, in his wise and overruling providence, had otherwise appointed it, using Hushai's counsel to defeat Achithophel's. Which is here given as the only reason, why it took no effect, 1 Cor. 1. 20, and 3. 19.

**V. 15.** {*Then said Hushai to Zadok and Abiathar*} That is, having by his counsel defeated Achitophel's design, he takes present care, as before David and he had agreed, to send him word of what was done, that he might make use and have the benefit of it.

*{lodge not this night in the plains of the wilderness, but speedily pass over, &c.}* Which advice Hushai prudently gives David, because he observes the fickleness of Absalom, who, though he had applauded the counsel of Achitophel, yet afterwards, misliked and fell from it, and hearkened unto his; and therefore, thinking that he might do so again, when Achitophel, being a politic man, and holding it a great disgrace to have his counsel rejected, would, in all likelihood, press it harder on with new arguments and persuasions, and discover the weakness of Hushai's advice, he now for more certainty and safety, counsels David to be speedily gone out of his reach, over Jordan. Where it pleased God to exercise David, by bringing him into these afflictions and troubles. For after he and his company had tired themselves all the day with a long and tedious march, when night comes he must have no time to rest; but with very much anguish of mind, he must go on to Jordan, and spend the whole night in passing over himself with his army, even to the dawning of the next morning.

*{Now Jonathan and Ahimaaz stayed by Enrogel*} Signifying the fuller's fountain, situate in the borders of Judah and Benjamin, near unto Jerusalem, Josh. 15. 7, and 18. 16, where they stayed by their fathers' appointment, to hear all the news, and bring it unto David, ch. 15. 27.

*{and a wench went out and told them*} i.e. Because they dared not be seen in the city, as being known to be of David's party, and so Zadok and Abiathar could not come to speak immediately with them, they sent by a maid their message, that they might report it unto David, because she might safely go out of the city, under the color of going to the well, either to wash clothes, or fetch water.

**V. 18.** {*Nevertheless, a lad saw them, and told Absalom}* Who knowing them to be of David's party, and missing them in the city, laid wait for, and inquired after them; this lad takes notice of it, and casually spying them, reports it to Absalom.

*{But they went both of them away quickly*} i.e. Perceiving by some signs, that they were observed and in danger to be discovered, they haste away, that they might hide themselves.

*{and came to a man’s house in Bahurim}* See ch. 4. 16.

**V. 19.** {*And the woman took and spread a covering over the well’s mouth}* That is, the man's wife, and mistress of the house, being quick of wit, (a thing usual with many of that sex, being put to it on the sudden, as we see in the example of Rahab, in the like case, Josh. 2. 6.) used this means to hide them, by letting them go down into a well, in which there was little or no water, and covered the mouth of it with a cloth, on which she spread ground corn or meal, under pretense that it needed drying, being moist, or airing, being musty.

**V. 20.** {*And the woman said, they are gone over the brook of water*} Where she tells an officious lie to preserve them from danger of being apprehended by Absalom's messengers, which though it were unlawful and unnecessary, because God needs not a lie to preserve whom he pleases; yet he is pleased to accept of the bent of the mind and heart to do his will, though there be a failing in the use of the means, as we see in the example of the Egyptian midwives, and Rahab, Exod. 1. 21. Josh. 2. 4.

**V. 21.** {*and pass quickly over the water}* To wit, Jordan.

*{for thus hath Ahithophel counselled}* That is, to pursue you in all haste, even in the night.

**V. 22.** {*By the morning light there lacked not one of them*} Which was a singular comfort to David in these his extreme troubles, that by God's special providence, they were all thus preserved in such a dangerous passage, and that in the night.

**V. 23.** {*And when Ahithophel saw that his counsel was not followed}* That is, when he perceived that Absalom neglected his advice, and preferred Hushai's before his, he grew desperate and discontented; partly, out of pride, when he saw another better esteemed than himself; and partly, because he saw this neglect tended to the ruin of Absalom and all his friends, and himself chiefly among the rest.

*{And gat him home——to his city*} That is, Giloh, chap. 15. 12.

*{And put his household in order}* That is, disdaining that Hushai's counsel should be preferred before his, which if it succeeded would be a dishonor to him, who had opposed it, and make him to live ingloriously for the time to come, who had been so highly magnified for his wisdom and policy; but chiefly, because he thought that Absalom and his followers, for neglecting his advice, would be utterly ruined, and come to naught; and so fearing, that if David prevailed, he should not escape condign and exemplary punishment, he resolved rather to die, than he would expose himself to this hazard; and hereupon he made his will, and disposed of his estate, like a wise politician, that he might not, after he was dead, be censured of folly and improvidence; in the meantime, discovering his carnal folly and madness, in taking no care for eternity, and the everlasting salvation of his soul.

*{and hanged himself*} Fearing, that if David prevailed, (as it was most like he would) he should be put to exemplary shame and punishment for his base treachery; wherein he was a type of Judas, who hanged himself, after he had betrayed his master, despairing of mercy.

**V. 24.** {*And David came to Mahanaim*} See ch. 2. ver. 8, and the note upon it.

*{And Absalom passed over Jordan*} Yet not immediately after David, but after he had gathered a great army, according to Hushai's counsel; in the meantime, giving him opportunity of collecting a strong party of his faithful friends and subjects, to encounter Absalom in a set battle.

**V. 25.** {*which Amasa was a man’s son whose name was Ithra}* Who is also called Jether, of whom Absalom made choice to be general of his army, in Joab's stead, 1 Chron. 2. v. 17.

*{an Israelite*} Who is said, in Chronicles, to have been an Ishmaelite. And so either he was an Ishmaelite by birth, and called an Israelite, because he was a proselyte to the Jewish religion, and so by profession and life; or else he was by birth an Israelite, and called an Ishmaelite, because he had lived among them.

*{that went in to Abigail*} Whereby is implied, that he was spurious, and not lawfully begotten in marriage.

*{the daughter of Nahash*} Whom some think to have been the wife of Jesse, and called her son, as Joab and his brethren were called the sons of Zeruiah, taking their denomination from the mother's side. And others suppose that this Nahash was Jesse himself called here by another name, as it was usual with other men to be called by two divers names. But howsoever manifest it is, that they were both of them David's sisters, and consequently the daughters of Jesse, whose sons were cousin-germans one to the other.

**V. 26.** {*So Israel and Absalom pitched in the land of Gilead*} In which also Mahanaim was situate, where they pitched, to environ David with their numerous army, or to provoke him to battle.

**V. 27.** {*And it came to pass when David was come to Mahanaim}* To wit, by God's good providence, who raised up friends to relieve him in his extremity and necessities, and to supply all his wants of necessaries, for the comfort of him and his followers. Wherein they approved themselves to be most faithful friends, by performing such duties of love, in such a manner, at such a time, and when he was in such a condition. For they did it voluntarily, out of their true love, when as their help was not required. They did it in a most seasonable time, when they were in necessity, hungry, thirsty, weary, harborless in the wilderness, and quite unfurnished of all necessaries; and that when they could not thus express their love, without incurring much danger from Absalom's strong party, who were at hand, and ready to fall upon them for relieving their enemies.

*{Shobi the son of Nahash of Rabbah*} Who was (as some probably think) the brother of Hanun, the son of Nahash, whom David, after he had deposed Hanun for abusing his messengers, whom he sent to comfort him, made king of Ammon in his stead. Who now, in token of his thankfulness, shows his kindness to David by supplying his wants, ch. 10. 1, 2, and 12. 30.

*{and Machir the son of Ammiel of Lodebar*} Who secretly entertained Mephibosheth when David came to the crown, fearing lest he would not deal well with him, because he was of Saul's family, and by lineal descent, next heir to the kingdom, ch. 10. 4. But finding afterwards, when he was discovered and called to the court, that David used him with all kindness, he was so much taken with his unexpected love and bounty, that with all willingness he ministers to his present necessity, and supplies his wants, ch. 9. 4.

*{of Lodebar*} See ch. 9. v. 4, and the note upon it.

*{and Barzillai the Gileadite*} Of whom, read ch. 19. 31, 32. 1 Kin. 2. 7. These three, as principal men, are mentioned; whom God, watching over his children in all their extremities, by his special providence, raised up to relieve and comfort David in his great affliction, even when his own son most unnaturally persecuted him, even to the taking away of his life, had not God prevented him. Wherein David was a type of Christ; for as these who were strangers unto him, and of other tribes, ministered to his wants, when as his own kindred of the tribe of Judah, yea, his own son pursued him; so did it fare with Christ, who, though he were the great prophet, was not esteemed in his own country, and hardly censured by his own kindred; when in the meantime, God raised up strangers, highly to esteem and minister unto him.

**V. 28.** {*Brought beds, and basons, and earthen vessels, &c.*} That is, all manner of furniture and household stuff, with necessary food fit for their present use, and for the relieving and satisfying his hunger, and of the rest of his followers. Where we have a notable example of the mutability of earthly things. He that, not long since, commanded a kingdom, and abounded with plenty, befitting a regal state and dignity, is now deprived of all, wanting a bed to lie on, basins and earthen vessels, yea, even bread to put into his mouth; wanting the help of strangers to supply these necessaries. Which is a fit mirror, wherein princes and potentates may behold the fickleness and uncertainty of their condition, and how easy a thing it is for God, when they are in their highest pitch of pomp, pleasure, and plenty of all things, to pull them down, and bring them into such a state of indigency, that they may need the help of their friends for supplying of necessaries; yea, also, (if so he please) may deprive them of friends, or, their friends of hearts to administer to their wants.

## CHAPTER 18

**Vers. 1.** {*And David numbered the people that were with him}* That is, mustered all his forces, which were much increased by those that resorted unto him out of the two tribes and a half, which inhabited beyond Jordan; who being far distant from Jerusalem and the court, had not been inveigled with Absalom's flatteries, nor alienated in their affections from David, by those aspersions which were unjustly cast upon his government; and looking upon his great afflictions, together with his innocence, and the horrid and unnatural rebellion of his wicked son, by which he was brought into all these miseries, they pitied his present condition, and adjoining themselves to that small army which he had brought with him, they became such a considerable number, as were fit to take the field, and put their just cause (in hope of God's protection) upon the trial of a pitched battle. What David's number was is not expressed, and therefore men have their various opinions about it. Josephus says, that they were in all but four thousand; others hold that they were seven thousand; others somewhat more. But for my own part, I conceive, that (though victory consists not in the multitude of an army) yet there were many more of David's party; seeing it is said, that he set captains over them by hundreds and thousands; and the people with him would not let him go out to battle, because he was worth ten thousand of them; the former words seeming to imply, that this number was but one half of their army; to say nothing of his dividing the army into three parts, and placing over them three generals. Which reasons, though they be not demonstrative and unanswerable, yet they make it probable, that his army was not so small as many expositors make it.

**V. 2.** {*And the King said——I will surely go forth with you myself also}* The which offer he makes, (though he were now grown in years,) partly, to approve himself a wise and valiant general, whose office it is to go forth with his army, both to govern and direct them, and also to hearten and encourage them by his presence and good example; partly also, because they fought, and ventured their lives in his quarrel, and therefore he thought it not fit that they should run all the hazard, and that himself, in the meanwhile, should be exempted from all danger; but especially (as it may be probably thought by that charge and motion which he made to deal gently with Absalom) it might be a chief cause why he desired to be present at the battle, because he would take order, being there in person, to have him favored and spared.

**V. 3.** {*But the people answered, Thou shalt not go forth*} Which they do not speak, and seeking to cross as overpower his will, but out of their love and reverence towards his person, which made them careful to provide for his safety; and by way of counsel and advice, which they grounded upon good reasons.

*{For if we flee away they will not care for us*} As if they had said, you are the only mark at which they aim in this rebellion; and if they could cut you off, they have their desire, which is to set up Absalom in your stead, ch. 17. 12. And therefore if they should know you to be in the field, they would bend all their strength against you, 1 king. 22. 31. In which respect it is necessary, that you should stay at home to avoid this danger. Whereas though half of us should flee away or be slain, they will not much regard it, if you be in safety, seeing as long as you live, you will be able to raise new forces, and to make a strong party against them.

*{But now thou art worth ten thousand of us*} That is, in respect of true worship, dignity and excellency, as being our king and general, protector and director of the whole army and kingdom; by whose death the commonwealth would sustain more loss; and the common enemies, who chiefly aim at your ruin, would more rejoice and triumph, than if ten thousand of us were slain.

*{therefore it is better that thou succour us out of the city*} That is, Mahanaim, to wit, as there shall any occasion be offered, according to the several occurrents and casualties of war; as chiefly, by your prayers which are prevalent with God; by your direction and counsel, as we shall have occasion to desire it; by commanding men, ammunition, and victuals to be sent unto the army, as there shall be need; by receiving and relieving the army; if being too weak to hold out, they should be forced to make a retreat, and so by encouraging the soldiers, and recruiting the army, to renew the battle. Besides, that it will also be a great disheartening to the enemies, when as you staying in the city, they will be apt to conceive, that you have a strong guard about you, and will be able and willing to send fresh supplies, if we stand in need of your help.

**V. 4.** {*and the King stood by the gate side*} To wit, that he might observe their marching out of the city, their order and discipline; and (as it is likely) to speak unto them, as they passed by, and to encourage them to go on in this expedition, valiantly, in assurance of good success, though this in particular be not expressed.

*{And the King commanded Joab——saying, Deal gently, for my sake, with the young man, even with Absalom*} Where, relying upon God by faith, in assurance of victory; he gives them this charge, as if it had been already gotten, that they should not pursue their victory against his son with too much rigor; seeing though his fault were heinous, yet it was but the error of a young man, out of the heat of youth; and if he were spared, that he might live till he came to be older, age and experience would make him wiser, and to repent of what he had done. But howsoever though his crime were so horrid, that it would admit of no excuse or extenuation, nor suffer him to be in any capacity of receiving favor in himself, yet it was his earnest request, that they would spare him, and save his life for his sake, seeing his death would be very grievous unto him. Which earnestness David uses, not only because he was so indulgent a father, that his excess of love would make him dispense with the greatest fault, in one that was so dear unto him, but also because he had respect to his soul's eternal salvation, of which he was like to make an utter and hopeless forfeiture, if he should be taken away in his sins without repentance. And not only so, but because also he looked upon his own sin of adultery and murder, as the prime cause which had moved the Lord to give Absalom up to his own lusts and wicked courses, that he might take occasion thereby to inflict upon his house those heavy judgments which he had formerly threatened, chap. 12. 10, 11.

*{and all the people heard*} That is, many of them immediately by themselves, and all the rest, by the report of these, who were ear-witnesses of it, when he gave this charge to his captains and commanders.

**V. 6.** {*So the people went out into the field against Israel*} That is David's army went out of the city to fight with that army which Absalom had gathered out of all Israel.

*{and the battle was in the wood of Ephraim}* Lying on the east side of Jordan, in that part of Gilead which belonged to the tribe of Gad, not far from Mahanaim; but was called Ephraim's wood, from that great defeat which was given to this tribe there, Judg. 12. 5, 6, though it was no part of the land belonging to Ephraim. Others, (though not so probably) think that it was so called, because lying on the other side of Jordan, right over against the tribe of Ephraim, the Ephraimites fed there their cattle which they did drive over Jordan.

**V. 8.** {*And the wood devoured more people that day than the sword devoured}* That is, being disordered and routed in the battle, they fled into the wood among the bushes and trees, hoping there to hide and secure themselves; but being pursued thither, by their enemies, they were easily slain there in that disorder and fear; and besides all those that fell by the sword, there were many of them that perished by falling, in their hasty flight, into marishes, pits and ditches.

**V. 9.** {*And Absalom met the servants of David*} That is, while in great fear he sought to flee from them, he suddenly and unawares fell upon them; and seeking to decline the danger, riding on his mule, he left the open and common way, (as it seems) and rode amongst the bushes and trees, till at last he was carried under the thick boughs of a great oak.

*{And his head caught hold of the oak}* i.e. Either striking among the thick boughs, his head was caught among them in some crotch, and so he hung by the neck, between the heaven and the earth, his mule going from under him; or else, the long, thick hair of his head, being (as may be) parted into locks, (like Samson's, Judg. 16. 13.) did catch and wind about the thick boughs of the oak, God making his pride to be the means of his ruin; and his hair, in which he so much gloried, to be his halter; and so reserved him from falling among others by the sword, to a more ignominious and cursed death, Deut. 21. 23, causing him to hang between the heaven and the earth, as unworthy to live in either of them.

**V. 10.** {*And a certain man saw it, and told Joab*} Not daring himself to hurt him, because of the king's command, verse 5, 12.

**V. 11.** {*And Joab said——Why didst thou not smite him there?*} Being desirous that Absalom should die for this rebellion, whereby he had caused the death of many; but willing withal, that another, rather than himself, should run the hazard of David's displeasure, who had laid a straight charge upon him and all the rest, that they should deal gently with him, he blames him, being a professed soldier, for not killing him, as by law of arms he might justly have done, seeing he was an enemy and a rebel.

*{and I would have given thee ten shekels of silver*} That is, the common shekel, which was in weight and worth but half so much as the shekel of the sanctuary, that is, a quarter of an ounce of silver, which amounts to about 14 or 15 pence sterling.

*{and a girdle*} i.e. A military belt, to be worn by him, as an honorable ensign and reward of his valor.

**V. 12.** {*Beware that none touch the young man Absalom*} Where he gives the meaning of David's words, *deal gently with the young man,* to wit, that they should not use him with rigor, nor do him any hurt, and then much less kill him.

**V. 13.** {*Otherwise I should have wrought falsehood against my own life}* i.e. I should have been false to myself, in betraying my life to David's revenge; or if I had made a lie to save my life, the king is so wise, that examining the matter, he would soon have found it out, and so have punished me for transgressing his command. Others understand it thus, that if he had used falsehood, in hope, by his lying, to conceal the fact, it would have been vain and bootless, tending to his own ruin, seeing the king by his wisdom would have discovered it, and so have punished both his fact, and his falsehood in denying it.

*{and thou thyself wouldst have set thyself against me}* i.e. You would have been so far from standing for me, that you would have been one of the first to hasten my ruin, laying the whole guilt and blame upon me, to clear yourself, and avoid the king's displeasure. Others read the words by way of interrogation, thus, *Wouldst thou have strove against me?* i.e. Have stood up in my defense, and pleaded my cause, opposing yourself against the king's sentence? as thinking that an ordinary soldier would not so unmannerly have charged his general to his face.

**V. 14.** {*Then said Joab, I may not thus tarry with thee*} i.e. I may not thus fruitlessly spend my time in arguing with you about this business and so let slip this opportunity which is now offered of putting an end to the war, seeing the matter in hand more needs execution than consultation or dispute.

*{and he took three darts in his hand*} To wit, to make sure work for his quick dispatch, that if one failed or broke, lighting on some rib or pithy coat, he might make use of another.

*{and thrust them through the heart of Absalom*} i.e. The middle of the body near the heart. For there was still some life remaining in him after these wounds received, which Joab's ten young men afterwards did wholly take away, v. 15. This fact of Joab's some condemn, as being expressly against the king's strait charge and command, and think that he did it out of spleen, either upon an old quarrel he had against Absalom for burning his corn, or, which is more likely, because he had lately cashiered him out of his generalship, and put Amasa in his room, ch. 17. 25. But others justify and commend him, for though he did that which was contrary to the king's personal command, yet it was not against his legal authority, which required that justice should be done and executed even in the field according to martial law. And for this Joab had good grounds and reasons: For I. He had no hope of Absalom's amendment, seeing after he was reconciled to his father, and that by his mediation, he had satisfied his faith, and was now become a dangerous traitor against king and state. II. Absalom had murdered Amnon, and was now the chief cause of slaughtering twenty thousand of the Lord’s people, whom he had first led into rebellion; and therefore Joab, as general of the army, might lawfully put him to death, being fallen into his hands, and execute martial law upon him. III. He knew that in all likelihood Absalom, if he lived still, would be a dangerous enemy to the whole state; and therefore he thought it best to cut him off, preferring the safety of the commonwealth before the private affection of the king. Upon these grounds it seemed David was so convinced of the lawfulness of Joab’s fact, that he did not after the battle blame him for it, nor make it any part of his charge, when afterwards he accused him to Solomon for killing Abner and Amasa; although it may be probably thought it was not the least part of his quarrel against him, in that, contrary to his express command he had slain his beloved Absalom, that through his fondness of affection was too dear unto him.

**V. 15.** {*Smote Absalom, and slew him*} i.e. Killed him outright, who had before received divers mortal wounds by the hands of Joab.

**V. 16.** {*And Joab blew the trumpet, and—held back the people*} i.e. After the arch-rebel was cut off, who had misled all the rest, Joab like a good general, pitying their seduction, and being now loath and sparing, like a wise commonwealthsman, to have any more blood unnecessarily spilt, caused a retreat to be sounded, and a cessation of arms.

**V. 17.** {*And they took Absalom—and laid a very great heap of stones upon him*} As it was their manner when they meant to suit an ignominious death with the like burial. So they did to the king of Ai, Josh. 8. 29. and the five kings, Josh. 10. 27. and Achan. See on Josh. 7. 26. and Lam. 3. 53.

**V. 18.** {*Now Absalom in his lifetime had taken and reared up for himself a pillar}* Like one of the pyramids of the kings of Egypt, that he might serve after his death as a monument to eternize his memory. This is here inserted to show his pride and vainglorious ambition to perpetuate his name, and how God was pleased to cross his proud humor, by causing him to be buried in a great pit under a heap of stones, as a malefactor, in an ignominious manner.

*{which is in the king’s dale*} Called also the valley of Cedron, or of Jehoshaphat, Joel. 3. 2. lying between Jerusalem and the mount of Olives. It was named the king’s dale, as some conjecture, because being very pleasant and fruitful it was a place fit for kings, and accordingly used by them for their sports and recreations. And therefore it was made choice of by Absalom, as the fittest place for his monument, in respect of the great resort of noble personages that did frequent it. Some think it to be the same with that which is called the valley of Shaveh, where the king of Sodom and Melchizedek the king of Salem met Abraham, when he returned victorious, having vanquished the four kings, and recovered the prey, Gen. 14. 17. and that upon this occasion it had first its name; though it might be continued, or renewed, in respect of that use which the kings of Judah did afterwards make of it.

*{For he said, I have no son to keep my name in remembrance*} It seems those sons mentioned ch. 14. 27. were now all dead, God justly cutting off his posterity that should have continued his name, and in whom after his death he should in a sort have lived, because he dishonored his father by his rebellion, and sought to take away his life, from whom he had received his.

**V. 19.** {*Then said Ahimaaz the son of Zadok, Let me now run, and bear the king tidings*} Because he and Jonathan were appointed by the king to bring him the news from time to time, how things went and were carried with Absalom and his followers, he now offers himself to do him this service; and having joyful tidings to relate, he desires to be the first man that should report it, because he thought it would be very acceptable to David, who all the time, while the fight lasted could not but hang in suspense, and therefore would needs long to hear the issue.

**V. 20.** {*And Joab said unto him, Thou shalt not bear tidings this day, &c.*} Joab out of his love to him denies to let him go, because there was in the news a mixture of joy and grief, and he would have him to be the messenger of glad tidings only, and promises he should be the messenger before any other, when such an occasion should be offered.

*{but this day thou shalt bear no tidings, because the king’s son is dead*} Joab out of much experience well knew, that such was David’s indulgency and extreme fondness over his son, that the news of his death would so embitter the joy of the victory as he would have but a cold welcome that should report it unto him; and therefore loving Ahimaaz, he was unwilling he should for so great pains be rewarded with no better entertainment.

**V. 21.** {*Then said Joab to Cushi, Go tell the King what thou hast seen*} Who this Cushi was, it does not appear. Some think he was not only the king’s servant, v. 29. but also his footman, and so fit for such an employment: And by his name they guess he was a Cushite or an Ethiopian by nation, as the word is taken Jer. 13. 23. but a proselyte to the Jewish religion; Him because of his blackness Joab made choice of, as the fittest to be the messenger of such sad and mournful news, as he supposed this would be to David, concerning Absalom’s death.

**V. 22.** {*Then said Ahimaaz—But howsoever, let me, I pray thee, also run, &c.*} Heb. *Be what may*. i.e. Come on it what will. This earnestness he uses, as desiring to be the first messenger of David’s victory, and now upon his second thoughts being able to take away Joab’s objection about his running, he resolved to report the best part of the news himself, and to leave the worst to be related by Cushi.

*{Wherefore wilt thou run, my son, seeing that thou hast no tidings ready?*} To wit, none that will be pleasing to the king; and besides that, Cushi who is gone before will relate all before your coming.

**V. 23.** {*Then Ahimaaz ran by the way of the plain, and overran Cushi}* There were two ways from the wood of Ephraim to Mahanaim, the one mountainous and unfit for a race, the other fair and plain: that Cushi chose, as being the shorter; this rather Ahimaaz in his prudence chose, though the longer, knowing that sometimes the nearest way is furthest about.

**V. 24.** {*And David sat between the two gates*} To wit, of the city Mahanaim. For more strength, use and ornament the gates of cities use to be double, one outward to the highways, the other inward towards the city; between which they had a fair and large entrance, and a chamber over it, where was their place of judicature and transacting public businesses. In such an entrance David sat waiting to hear the news, and having heard it with much grief, could stay there no longer, but ascended into the chamber, that he might take his full liberty to vent his sorrow. So 1 Sam. 4. 13.

*{and the watchman went up to the roof over the gate*} i.e. The turret, appointed to the watchman, that from thence he might discover all comers.

**V. 25.** {*And the King said, If he be alone there is tidings in his mouth*} For they that are beaten in battle do flee by troops, whereas the victors use to dispatch one or two to carry news, being otherwise busied in pursuing their victory, and taking the spoil.

**V. 27.** {*And the King said, He is a good man*} i.e. One who uses to bring me good tidings. And besides, as he is a valiant soldier, and therefore would not be one of the first that did flee, if the battle were lost, but purposely comes after it is won, and all danger past, to bring news of the victory.

**V. 28.** {*All is well*} Heb. *Peace*; or, *Peace be unto thee*. Under this phrase the Hebrews signify all prosperity and happiness.

*{Blessed be the Lord thy God, which hath delivered up the men*} i.e. The men that did rise up in rebellion against you, to be slain by the king’s servants. So 1 Sam. 24. 19. and 26. 8. Here he ascribes the whole glory of the victory unto God, and no part of it to Joab, the other commanders, or the whole army.

**V. 29.** {*And the King said, Is the young man Absalom safe?*} He was not so much rejoiced to hear of the victory, nor thankful for it, as solicitous for Absalom’s safety; therefore discovering his extreme indulgency and fondness over an ungracious son.

*{when Joab sent the King’s servant*} i.e. Hasted Cushi away with all haste to carry the news of the victory.

*{I saw a great tumult, but knew not what it was*} Ahimaaz no doubt knew well enough of Absalom’s death; for Joab gave it as a reason why he would not have him run and carry the news; but through human frailty he speaks an untruth, that he might conceal it from David, upon these reasons; if at least any reason can be rendered for lying: I. He was loath to grieve him by telling him ill news; the end of his coming being to rejoice his heart with glad tidings. II. He desired to be accepted of David, and therefore would only relate so much of the news as he knew would please him; leaving what was bad to be reported by Cushi. III. He thought it not fit that the king all at once, on the sudden, should be told of his death whom he so dearly loved, lest he might be oppressed with grief, but held him in suspense, that he might by degrees be the better prepared to hear it, and bear it with patience.

**V. 32.** {*The enemies of my lord the king—be as that young man is*} i.e. Overthrown, and dead.
