**V. 33.** {*And the King was much moved*} To wit, upon these grounds touched in the note upon v. 5. as Absalom’s desperate and lost condition, dying in his sin without repentance; and withal, his own sins of adultery and murder, which had occasioned his son’s misery: Both these, and other motives, were set on, as the greatest aggravation of his grief, by his excessive love and unbounded affection towards his dear Absalom, which does so wholly engross and take him up that he does not so much as mention other and far greater causes of grief, as the slaughter of twenty thousand of his subjects, who perished also in their rebellion without time of repentance, being misled and seduced by his flattery and hypocrisy.

*{and went up to the chamber over the gate*} i.e. He was so full of grief that it must needs either have a vent, or else break his heart; and therefore being utterly unable any longer to refrain and restrain his passion, and loath also that those in the gate should discover his weakness, he went not home to his house, but into the chamber over the gate, there to vent his grief; yea, so was he transported with violence of passion that he could not hold out so long till he came thither, but in his very passage up the stairs to that room he broke out into such woeful lamentations that those in the gate were ear-witnesses of them.

*{would God I had died for thee*} David could not choose but know what miseries and mischiefs would have befallen the whole state, those especially that were godly and religious, if Absalom had outlived his father; but either the violence of his passion makes him forget this at the present, or else in this comparison he has only respect to his own death as temporal, seeing he was sure of God’s favor, and dying so, should have no loss, but gain; and to Absalom’s, as eternal, seeing in all likelihood he died in God’s displeasure. In this regard he was willing (if it had been possible) to have prevented and redeemed his Absalom from the danger of everlasting misery by dying for him a temporal death.

## CHAPTER 19

**Ver. 1.** {*And it was told Joab*} It is not expressed by whom; but is likely that Ahimaaz and Cushi reported it when they returned unto him; and so it came to be noised throughout the whole army that the king immoderately lamented the death of his son, v. 2.

**V. 2.** {*And the victory that day was turned into mourning*} i.e. Whereas they expected joy and triumph for so great deliverance by so glorious a victory, contrariwise it came to pass that the king’s mourning caused all the people to mourn, the body sympathizing with the head, and not enjoying its health when the head aches, Prov. 16. 15. & 19. 2.

**V. 3.** {*And the people gat them by stealth that day into the city*} i.e. They came not like a victorious army with joy and triumph, but scattering themselves here and there secretly conveyed themselves into the city, as they could, not desiring either to see the king, or to be seen of him. Others take it in another sense, therein following (as they conceive) the Hebrew, thus, *The people stole away in entering*; and so the vulgar Latin reads it, *The people declined that day to enter the city, even as people fleeing before their enemies*, i.e. they came not into the city at all, but being about to enter they withdrew themselves into other places, through shame, grief and discontent, when they heard how heavily and heinously the king took the death of his son. Which Joab observing, told the king that if he did not forthwith cease his mourning, and go out and speak comfortably to the people, there would not any one of them tarry with him that night.

**V. 4.** {*But the King covered his face*} After the manner of close mourners. See on ch. 15. 30.

**V. 5.** {*And Joab came into the house to the king*} i.e. Either into the chamber into which he ascended when he first heard the news of Absalom’s death, or into the house in the city whither he had now retired himself.

*{and said, thou hast shamed this day the faces of all thy servants*} Though this speech of Joab’s were very harsh and saucy, proud and peremptory, and in respect of the manner much to be condemned, as far unbeseeming a subject to use unto his sovereign, and in part also very false, and wrongfully applied to David; yet thus far it admits of excuse and extenuation; It was for the most part true, in respect of the matter and substance of it; and though he failed in the manner, yet what he spoke was out of love and faithfulness, to prevent the great mischief which might have followed if David had held on his course of mourning: And it was uttered by a friend whom David had always found faithful, true and trusty to his person and cause; and was no more (as it seems, all circumstances considered, compared with the event and issue of it) than was profitable, and even at this time almost necessary. For David was so deeply and dangerously sunk into a gulf of sorrow, that he could have been no otherwise pulled out of it, and preserved from sinking deeper, than by such a rough and rude hand. So that though the sauce was over-tart and sour, and unfit for the palate of a king, yet the food itself was wholesome, and fit to strengthen and recover him out of his languishing passion. And first he tells him that he had shamed the faces of all his servants, i.e. had so discountenanced them by disrespecting their service, that not daring to come into his presence, they were in great discontent ready to leave and forsake him, as not deserving their future service, seeing he had no better rewarded their former, by which both himself and all his sons and daughters, wives and concubines, had been preserved from utter ruin. So also he had confounded their faces with shame, by disappointing them of their hopes, in not accepting of their faithful service, which they had performed with extreme hazard of their lives, nor any ways encouraging them with praise or reward, as they expected.

**V. 6.** {*In that thou lovest thine enemies, and hatest thy friends*} i.e. Have expressed so much love to Absalom, who was an enemy and traitor, by your bitter lamentation for his death; and so much hatred to your friends and followers, by withdrawing your person, and discountenancing their service. Here he charged David with that which was untrue, and sinisterly misinterprets both his passions and actions. For he bemoaned Absalom, not as an enemy, but as a son; nor withdrew himself from his friends and followers out of any hatred to them, but because he would not discover unto them his weakness in his excessive mourning, which for the present he was unable to temper and moderate, being transported by the vehemence and violence of his inordinate affection and unruly passion.

*{for this day I perceive, that if Absalom had lived, and all we had died—then it had pleased thee well*} These words, as they were extremely harsh and bitter to be uttered by a subject to his sovereign, especially being wounded already in his spirit, and dejected with grief, so also they were utterly false: For David desired the life and welfare of all his friends, but of Absalom also among the rest, though he had showed himself his mortal enemy. Neither does Joab moderate and qualify this his speech, by saying that if he went on in this course he would seem to his princes and people to be thus ill affected towards them, but positively affirms out of his own knowledge that it was so indeed.

**V. 7.** {*Now therefore arise, go forth, and speak comfortably to thy servants*} i.e. Congratulate their victory; give unto them due honor and praise for their well deserving in fighting and venturing their lives in your cause, and encourage them by giving or promising rewards for their good service. Here he gives him wholesome and faithful counsel, but embitters it in a proud and peremptory manner, not sweetening it with humble entreaties, but making it distasteful by insolent commands not beseeming a subject to his king; like a good potion or pill profitable for cure, but not so prepared and sweetened that the patient might take it without great loathing and reluctancy.

*{for I swear by the Lord, if thou go not forth, there will not tarry one with thee*} i.e. All your people will forsake you as unfit to govern them, who are not able to rule your own passions; and think of choosing another king, who is more moderate in himself, and just to them; and then your case will be miserable, though you retain the title of a king, seeing *in the multitude of the people is the king’s honour; but in want of the people is the destruction of the prince*, Prov. 14. 28. And this Joab confirms by a solemn oath, the more to startle and move the king to follow his counsel, though he had no certain knowledge that it would so fall out, but only guessed that so it would be by probable conjecture, which was no sufficient warrant for an oath.

*{and that will be worse unto thee than all the evil that befell thee from thy youth until now*} i.e. Though you have gone through many troubles, and endured great afflictions in former times, especially through Saul’s persecution, and Absalom’s late rebellion, yet this will exceed them all, when in your old age you shall be deposed, and exposed to contempt and desperate dangers.

**V. 8.** {*Then the King rose, and sat in the gate*} i.e. He addressed himself to follow and act Joab’s counsel; because though he disrelished it as harsh and tart, yet he saw it was profitable and necessary. And therefore for the present he shook off sorrow; and that he might win the people’s hearts by showing himself graciously unto them, he sat in the gate, the ordinary place of judicature and public assemblies.

*{and they told unto all the people*} i.e. David’s friends and followers appointed for this service gave notice to all the rest of the people, to wit, all those who were of the king’s party: for those who followed Absalom and had escaped out of the battle, were fled, and returned to their own houses.

**V. 9.** {*And all the people were at strife*} Recounting now in their second thoughts the great and manifold benefits they had long enjoyed by David’s government, and their unworthy requital of him by rising against him in that horrid rebellion, God working a great change in their hearts, they begin to repent and condemn that course they had begun; yet none being willing to bear the whole blame, every man was willing to put it off to another: And now misliking their own slackness in making him some amends, they began to think and speak of his return with honor to his own home at Jerusalem, that he might be reestablished on his throne; and to this end provoke one another, and call upon their magistrates and elders to hasten the work.

*{The King saved us out of the hand of our enemies*} To wit, the Moabites, Ammonites, Idumeans, Syrians and especially the Philistines; and therefore little reason had we to rebel against such a king, by whom we have had so many and mighty deliverances: But seeing we have done it, great cause we have to make him some amends by hastening his return, that we may peaceably submit under his government.

**V. 10.** {*And Absalom whom we anointed over us is dead*} i.e. Though there had been reason that we should, if Absalom had lived, have liked our course, and proceeded in it; as it seems there was none, seeing God has showed his dislike, by rendering it unprosperous and fatal both to him and us; yet now it were madness to proceed in this rebellion, and not to submit to David as our king, seeing he in whom we trusted is taken away.

**V. 11.** {*And King David sent to Zadok and Abiathar the Priests*} Whom he had left at Jerusalem, as most able and faithful to negotiate his business.

*{Speak unto the Elders of Judah, saying, Why are ye the last to bring the King back*} Because they were the first and chief that had strengthened Absalom in his rebellion, they were more apt to despair of pardon and reconciliation, and so were (as it seems) afraid, and loath to address themselves to the king, seeing they were so highly in his displeasure. Therefore to take off this fear and jealousy, he sends to the Priests and gives them commission to acquaint them with his mind, and tenderness of affection towards them, notwithstanding their former error, if they now repented of it, seeing they were in a nearer relation to him than any other tribe, as being his friends and kinsmen, who had anointed him for their king, while the other tribes adhered to another. Herein David was a lively type of Christ, who stays not till his enemies seek unto him, but sends his ministers unto them, to seek and sue that they will be reconciled, 2 Cor. 5. 20.

*{seeing the speech of all Israel is come to the King*} To wit, expressing their resolution of coming to fetch him back to his house from Mahanaim.

**V. 12.** {*Ye are my brethren, ye are my bone, and my flesh*} i.e. Stand in a most intimate relation to and near conjunction with me; and therefore no more possible it is that I should remember former wrongs, and revenge myself upon you, than I should bear to hurt my own flesh and bones. And so this phrase is used, Gen. 2. 22. Judg. 9. 2. Eph. 5. 29, 30.

**V. 13.** {*And say ye to Amasa, Art not thou of my bone, and of my flesh*} He was David’s nephew, being son to Abigail David’s sister, 1 Chr. 2. 16, 17. And Absalom had made him general of his army, ch. 17. 25. Unto him therefore David sends, as being desirous to gain him, and make him firm to his service, because he was potent with the people, having been their general, and able to draw a great party after him. And because having so highly offended him, he might despair of pardon, or at least of being received into his favor, that this might not hinder his design of bringing him in, he assures him that he was not only ready to forgive and forget all that was past, but also willing in respect of the near relation that was between them, to advance him to the highest honor.

*{God do so to me and more also*} See on Ruth 1. 17.

*{if thou be not Captain of the host, &c.*} Though Joab had incurred David’s just displeasure by divers acts, as the killing of Abner and Absalom, and by his harsh and insolent speeches, and peremptory expostulations unbeseeming a subject, yet considering how faithful he had always been unto him, what good and successful service he had ever done, and that in those particular failings his main intention was to promote the good of the king and the whole commonwealth, and contrariwise how faithless and rebellious Amasa had been unto him, I cannot see how it could stand with David’s justice to advance Amasa, who had so ill deserved, and to turn Joab out of that place which of right belonged unto him both by David’s promise and his own purchase, in hazarding his life in such a dangerous service, as was the assaulting and taking the strong fort of Zion. Neither can I think his policy justifiable in using such a means to reduce that party that had stood out against him, to their obedience, as he had formerly done to Abner with like success. Nor can I but (as I conceive) justly suspect that his chief quarrel against Joab was the death of his dearest Absalom, though it brought so much good both to him and the whole commonwealth, seeing he pardoned greater faults in Amasa and others, yea, instead of punishing did reward and advance them. But though David were a holy and just man in his ordinary carriage, yet he was but a man, and had his failings, and this among the rest. Nor can I think (as some do) that he did this by a secret instinct of God’s Spirit; for what were this, but to make it the veil to hide men’s failings? And it is like that if he had done this by an inward spiritual motion, he should have had better success both in Abner’s case, and in this of Amasa.

*{before me continually*} i.e. As long as you and I live.

**V. 14.** {*And he bowed the heart of all the men of Judah*} i.e. David by his friendly message and gentle speech, delivered by the Priests from him to the men of Judah, inclined their hearts with unanimous consent towards him. Some understand it of Amasa, that he having received from David this comfortable message concerning himself, persuaded the men of Judah to fetch him home.

**V. 15.** {*So the King returned, and came to Jordan*} i.e. The other side of Jordan towards Mahanaim.

*{and Judah came to Gilgal*} i.e. A place on this side Jordan, near Jericho, where Joshua first pitched his camp after he came over and circumcised the people, from whence it had its name. See on Josh. 5. 9. Hither now the men of Judah came; and passed over the river to fetch the king homewards from the other side.

**V. 16.** {*And Shimei—hasted and came down with the men of Judah*} Taking this opportunity of reconciling himself to the king, and obtaining pardon for those gross abuses which he had offered unto him, hoping to speed the better because the men of Judah also came to make their peace, and to obtain pardon for their rebellion.

**V. 17.** {*And there were a thousand men of Benjamin with him*} These he brought to intercede for his behalf for the king’s favor. Hereby is implied that he had some relation to the house of Saul, and that he was a potent man in that tribe.

*{And Ziba the servant of the house of Saul*} Ziba being guilty of treachery in slandering his innocent master, came among others, to see how things would go, and to make the best shift he could for himself, to retain the king’s favor if he were questioned; though David’s partiality made this care needless.

*{and they went over Jordan before the King*} i.e. To meet him on the other side, and to conduct him over.

**V. 18.** {*And there went over a ferry-boat to carry over the king’s household, and to do what he thought good*} i.e. To bring over some of the chief of his family, as his wives, children, and whom he pleased, and to be employed for such other uses as he thought good; the rest and gross of his company making shift as they could to pass over by the flats and fords, or as Josephus will have it, by a bridge made of boats.

*{And Shimei fell down before the King*} Not to excuse his heinous fault, which was altogether unexcusable; but to make an humble acknowledgment of it, crave pardon, and deprecate deserved punishment.

**V. 19.** {*Let not my Lord impute iniquity unto me*} i.e. Though my fault be heinous and capital, yet do not, as in justice you may, lay it to my charge, but pardon it, seeing nothing more adorns a king than clemency. Neither do you only forgive, but also forget it, that you may not still harbor hard thoughts in your heart against me, as my fault deserves.

**V. 20.** {*For thy servant doth know that I have sinned*} i.e. The guilt of my sin so overburdened my conscience that it pressed from me this humble acknowledgment, not any ways extenuating or excusing my fault, but wholly relying upon your mercy and clemency.

*{therefore, behold, I am come*} i.e. Not despairing of grace, but presuming on your goodness, I have not run away, but taken the boldness to press into your presence, that I may obtain forgiveness.

*{the first this day of all the house of Joseph*} i.e. Out of an earnest desire to regain your favor, I have by care and diligence prevented others far nearer to this place than I am, to wait upon you, and to find grace in your sight. He names the house of Joseph, not because he was of that family, either Ephraimite, or Manassite; for it is plainly said he was a Benjamite; but his meaning is, that he came first of all the tribes of Israel, Judah only excepted. For it is usual in the Scripture to divide all Israel into these two, Judah, and Ephraim; Judah having Benjamin joined to it, and Ephraim comprehending the other ten tribes, which are all called by its name, because it was the chief, and had still the preeminence above the rest, all the rights and privileges of primogeniture or birthright by Jacob’s blessing being divided between it and Judah. So Zach. 10. 6. Isa. 7. 2. and 11. 13. Jer. 31. 9. Hos. 5. 13. Or else he might be said to have come first, or, before all the house of Joseph, because he came now with the men of Judah, before any of the ten tribes came. Judah and Benjamin were so joined and mixed together that they were accounted but as one tribe. Now Shimei makes use of this precedency, as an effectual argument to move David to pardon him, seeing by his coming in, submission, and reliance upon David’s mercy he had given a good example to all others; and if he, in so doing, found good success, it would be a singular encouragement for them that had risen against him to do the like; whereas if he should deal with him in rigor of justice, it would deter and dishearten others that had been in the rebellion, from coming in and submitting themselves, seeing there was no hope to find mercy.

**V. 21.** {*Shall not Shimei be put to death for this?*} q.d. Surely he cannot in justice be spared, seeing the law of God condemns his offense as a great sin, Exo. 22. 28. 1 King. 21. 10, 13.

**V. 22.** {*What have I to do with you, ye sons of Zeruiah*} q.d. Why do you so over-busily intermeddle with my regal affairs, hindering me from exercising my kingly prerogative, in pardoning and showing mercy to whom I please? See on ch. 16. 1.

*{that you should this day be adversaries unto me*} i.e. Howsoever you would seem friends, and thus advise me out of zeal to mine honor; yet your counsel is so ill at this time, all circumstances considered, that you could not have given me worse, had you been my enemies; hindering me in that which is good, and exciting me to evil, to wit, unseasonable revenge.

*{shall there any man be put to death this day in Israel?*} Seeing God, by giving me victory over mine enemies, and restoring me to my kingdom, has made this day unto me and all that love me, a day of rejoicing and thanksgiving, should I cause it, by shedding of blood, to be unto any a day of mourning and lamentation? See on 1 Sam. 11. 13.

*{for do not I know that I am this day King over Israel?*} I am, as if I were newly created king, my lost kingdom being restored: And what better beseems and more adorns a king, or wins and knits the hearts of his subjects unto him, than clemency? especially in the day of his inauguration, or restitution to his kingdom, when the eyes of all are upon him, and read his disposition in his actions? or what more alienated their affections from him than rigor and severity, which are at this time so unseasonable?

**V. 23.** {*Therefore the King said unto Shimei, Thou shalt not die; And the King sware unto him}* This is not simply and absolutely to be understood, but in reference to his former faults, for which now he craves pardon. For, if he committed any new offense, this oath would not privilege him from deserved punishment. Neither could David undertake for future times after his death, but only for the time of his own reign. Yes, but how could it stand with David’s oath to give order to his son Solomon to put him to death when he came to reign, 1 King. 2. 9? I answer, His oath did not bind him to restrain his son from doing justice in punishing this offense, which was so heinous and scandalous that he did not think it just, it should be wholly remitted, lest others should be encouraged by his impunity to do the like; and therefore he leaves it to his successor to call him to an account for it, and not to hold him guiltless. For though respecting seasonable circumstances and the prudent carriage of things, with reference to state policies and purposes, he had sworn, that he would not put him to death with the sword, as he speaks in his charge given to Solomon, 1 King. 2. 8, 9. yet he leaves him at liberty to execute justice: Yet not directly to punish him for that fault which he had remitted, but upon the committing of some new transgression, into which he foresaw (knowing the malice and wickedness of his heart) that he would readily fall, if in his prudence he would lay any law or injunction upon him, and strictly hold him to the keeping of it; And if so, then he might take occasion thereby to put him in mind of his former wickedness, and punish him for one as well as the other.

**V. 24.** {*And Mephibosheth the son of Saul*} i.e. The nephew or grandson of Saul, father of his father Jonathan.

*{and had neither dressed his feet, nor trimmed his beard, nor washed his clothes*} i.e. He came in all respects of habit and deportment as a true mourner for David’s long absence and many troubles.

**V. 25.** {*When he was come to Jerusalem to meet the King*} It seems this passage in the story is transposed, seeing it is afterwards said that the king was not come to Jerusalem, but was still at Barzillai’s near Jordan; unless we will read words thus, as others do, *when the king was coming to Jerusalem, Mephibosheth met him*, wanting as it seems opportunity of coming sooner to him through Ziba’s treachery.

*{Wherefore wentest thou not with me, Mephibosheth?*} As the rest of my friends and followers did, being willing to partake with me in my condition? This question he uses to see how he would answer to those things which Ziba had laid to his charge, ch. 16. 3.

**V. 26.** {*My lord, O King, my servant deceived me*} Though he knew that Ziba had most unjustly slandered him, yet he does not charge and accuse him for doing him that great wrong, until he was necessitated by David’s question to make his just apology.

*{For thy servant said, I will saddle me an ass*} i.e. I made known my purpose to Ziba, and commanded him to make ready my ass, and he neglected to do it, carrying him away for his own use, and so disappointed me.

**V. 27.** {*But my lord the King is as an Angel of God*} Of excellent wisdom to discern truth and falsehood, good and evil. See on ch. 14. 20.

*{do therefore what is good in thine eyes*} i.e. If you judge me faulty, punish me how you please; for you may justly do it, seeing all my father’s house have deserved to be punished with loss of goods and life, by rising against you our lawful king, and siding with Ishbosheth.

**V. 28.** {*What right therefore have I yet to cry any more to the King?*} i.e. To make further complaints of my servant’s ill dealing with me, though thereby I should lose both my honor and lands; seeing you who have given them unto me of your free love and bounty without any of my deserts, may at your pleasure justly take them away, and dispose of them as you think good.

**V. 29.** {*And the King said unto him, Why speakest thou any more of thy matters? I have said, Thou and Ziba divide the land*} This sentence some excuse, and justify as if this were the meaning, That he should not need to trouble the king or himself in making any long apology, seeing he did accept of his excuse, and was content howsoever upon Ziba’s information he had passed an hard doom, now to revoke it; and that his first sentence should stand, to wit, that Mephibosheth should enjoy the right and inheritance of all the lands of Saul wholly to himself, and that Ziba should till and husband them to the halves, i.e. for his care and pains should have an equal moiety with him in the profits, according to ch. 9. 9, 10. though no such order of husbanding the lands to the halves appears in that place, but only that as a servant he should do his business, and for his pains have a servant’s reward. But howsoever, to justify David, I would willingly subscribe to this exposition, yet I see not how I can do it, seeing it cannot (as I conceive) stand with truth; For it is manifest that David does not here speak of dividing the fruits for tillage, but the land itself; as also Mephibosheth’s answer plainly implies, *Yea, let him take all*, i.e. both lands and fruits. And therefore those words, *I have said*, cannot have any relation to his former order, but to his present sentence of dividing the land, as it is plainly expressed. q.d. Trouble me no more with your matters, being at this time full of weighty business that concerns the state; for I have passed my sentence, and am resolved to have it stand, that the land shall be divided between you. Thus David, though a most just king, passes and ratifies a most unjust sentence, being now blinded with Ziba’s lies, sycophancy, flattery and bribes, and with a kingly jealousy of his crown and kingdom, and rewards a perfidious villain, who for his treachery deserved severe punishment. And as contrary to law he formerly had taken the single testimony of a servant against his master, and that in his absence; so now he ratifies it, though there were no color for it, that a poor cripple should aspire to the crown; and though he might appear to David by Mephibosheth’s habit, deportment and utter neglecting of himself that he had out of love to him grieved and mourned all the time of his absence and trouble. Neither would he now, though the cause were so important, vouchsafe to give it a new hearing for the rectifying of his judgment, but slubbers it over, as resolving to stand to his sentence, whether right or wrong; yea, though he might plainly see Ziba’s extreme faultiness, since standing by and hearing his master’s accusation he had nothing to say in his own excuse. This injustice of David is much aggravated, if we consider the person upon whom he did exercise it, even the only son of his dearest Jonathan, who to the hazard of his life had done him so many favors; all which he now ungratefully forgets, and with them his covenant and oath also which in God’s presence he had made with him that he would forever show kindness both to him and all his posterity after him. So frail and infirm are the best men, if in the day of temptation God leaves them to themselves, there being no sin so foul into which they are not apt to fall, if they stand only in their own strength.

**V. 30.** {*Yea, let him take all, for as much as my lord the King is come again in peace*} i.e. Both fruits and lands; and not a part only, but the whole: For seeing it goes well with the king and state, it joys me so much that I can easily dispense with my own private loss.

**V. 31.** {*And Barzillai—came down from Rogelim*} A city beyond Jordan where he lived.

**V. 32.** {*For he was a very great man*} i.e. In power and riches, seeing he was able so long to sustain the king and all his numerous followers, ch. 17. 27.

**V. 33.** {*Come thou over with me, and I will feed thee*} Here David shows his humanity and gratefulness by his readiness to requite Barzillai’s bounty and kindness, though he in respect of his wealth, needed it not.

**V. 34.** {*And Barzillai said to the King, How long have I to live?*} i.e. I cannot, in respect of my great age, live long, and therefore have no reason to change my habitation and course of life for so little a time; especially being unfit, by reason of age, to enjoy the pleasures of the court.

**V. 35.** {*I am this day fourscore years old*} And therefore it is high time for me to prepare for death, rather than delight and solace, whereof I am not at this age capable.

*{can I discern between good and evil?*} To wit, in meats, drinks, and other delights?

*{wherefore then should thy servant be yet a burden to my lord the King*} Being unable to do you any service in this my decrepit age.

**V. 36.** {*And why should the King recompense it me with such a reward?*} i.e. My poor kindness in supplying your late wants with so rich a reward, whereas all I did was but my duty.

**V. 37.** {*Let thy servant return that I may die in my own country}* q.d. It becomes men of my age to think rather of their death and burial than of enjoying courtly delights: and therefore the best way to gratify me, if so you are pleased, is to give me leave to return unto my own home, where, for the short remainder of my life, I may enjoy rest and retiredness, fit myself for death, and after I am dead may be buried in the sepulcher of my father and ancestors, which is to all a thing acceptable.

*{Behold thy servant Chimham, let him go over with my Lord*} That he may not slight the king’s kind offer, he propounds a thing which would be to him more acceptable and profitable, namely, that Chimham one of his sons, might be taken into his favor, and preferred by him as he should think good. Which motion David entertained; and in his old age, when he could not live to see it done, he commends him to Solomon his son, 1 King. 2. 7. Of this Chimham we read Jer. 41. 17.

**V. 38.** {*Chimham shall go over with me*} To the other side of Jordan, and so to the court.

**V. 39.** {*The King kissed Barzillai, and blessed him*} i.e. Taking leave of him, he gave him thanks, and wished him all prosperity. So ch. 14. 22.

**V. 40.** {*and also half the people of Israel*} i.e. Not precisely the just half, but either those that had not rebelled, or had reconciled themselves to the king, or so many of them as in that great haste could make themselves ready to go out to meet him.

**V. 41.** {*Why have our brethren, the men of Judah stolen thee away?*} i.e. Fetched you homewards unawares to us, and not expecting our company, thereby engrossing you, and appropriating to themselves all the glory of the action.

*{and have brought the King and his household*} Here they change their speech from the second person to the third, which is usual with the Hebrews when the inferior speaks to his superior, that he might exalt him, and abase himself.

**V. 42.** {*Because the King is near of kin unto us}* i.e. Of our tribe, and dwells among us.

*{have we eaten of the King’s cost?*} i.e. Have we done him this service for our own advantage, and not rather in the former respects?

**V. 43.** {*And the men of Israel answered—We have ten parts in the King*} q.d. If David be considered as a private man, you have more interest in him than we; but consider him as a king over the whole land, and then we have a greater share in him than you, being ten tribes; and you only two, though Benjamin be also reckoned with you; and so we are the far greater part of this kingdom. In this contention between Judah and Israel the mutability and inconstancy of the common people is very observable, seeing they who lately rejected David and rebelled against him, do now in a hot contention strive who should have the greatest interest in him. So the Jews dealt with our blessed Savior, one day singing *Hosanna* unto him, and blessing him, and the next day crying out, *Crucify him*, Matth. 21. 9. and 27. 22.

*{And the words of the men of Judah were fiercer than the words of the men of Israel*} i.e. They were more rough and stout, presuming upon their privileges, because the king was of kin to them, and the royal dignity settled on their tribe, and because they were the greatest and richest, and in wars had precedency before the other tribes. This contention the king hearing, and saying nothing to compose it, being loath to displease either party, it afterwards broke out into a further and greater inconvenience: For seeing Judah challenged the greatest part in the king, Israel impatient of such a disparagement, refused to have in him any interest at all, ch. 20. 1.

## CHAPTER 20

**Ver. 1.** {*And there happened to be there*} i.e. When the ten tribes contended with Judah, which is mentioned as the occasion of Sheba’s rebellion and commotion.

*{a man of Belial*} See on Judg. 19. 22.

*{whose name was Sheba the son of Bichri, a Benjamite*} He being a chief man of that tribe, and, it is like, of Saul’s family, having gotten this opportunity, out of hatred to David, and hope to reduce the crown back again to that tribe and family, and particularly to himself, became the chief head and captain in this rebellion.

*{he blew a trumpet*} Namely to assemble the Israelites, who were now discontented, and to stir them up to rebellion ch. 19. 41, 43. Unto which they were the more apt, because as yet they were not well settled in their allegiance; like a broken bone newly set, before it had time to knit together. Thus God was pleased to correct David for his sins, and to exercise his faith and patience, with a new affliction coming in the neck of the former, as one billow follows another, not allowing him any time of rest or breathing.

*{and said, We have no part in David*} This was a form of speech used by the Hebrews, whereby they professed that they would have no commerce with those who had displeased them, 1 King. 12. 16. Act. 8. 21. but here occasionally taken up, because Judah had claimed a chief share in the king above Israel; q.d. Seeing Judah challenges the whole interest in the king, therefore let them wholly have him, and let us make choice of another, unto whom we may have as good right as they.

*{Neither have we inheritance in the son of Jesse*} So he called him by way of contempt, in respect of his mean parentage. And hereby he discovered the spleen and scorn of a proud Benjamite; who had high thoughts of themselves, because formerly the crown and scepter had been worn and wielded by Saul a man of their tribe.

*{every man to his tents, O Israel*} i.e. Desist from prosecuting your purpose of bringing David home, and return back to your own houses and tribes, that you may there advise and consult what is to be done.

**V. 2.** {*So every man of Israel went up from following David*} i.e. Embracing Sheba’s counsel they forsook David, not accompanying him any longer or further, but leaving him wholly to Judah, who still unanimously adhered unto him, and conducted him the whole way from Judah to Jerusalem.

**V. 3.** {*And the King took the ten women his concubines*} To wit, which Absalom had defiled, ch. 16. 22.

*{and put them in ward, and fed them*} i.e. Provided nourishment and all necessaries fitting for them. Wherein he dealt graciously with them, if they were defiled by voluntary consent, seeing when they had justly deserved death, yet he favored and spared them, because they had been his wives, and contented himself only with their confinement. Yea, they deserved little less than death, though they were in some sort unwilling to be thus abused, if they did not to the uttermost of their power make resistance, choosing rather to die than become the subjects of such an horrid and shameless villainy. Yea, if they were wholly innocent, and mere patients to Absalom’s violence and wicked lusts, yet David dealt piously and prudently with them (especially if it were done by their consent) to put them in ward, and never more to company with them, because they had in an incestuous manner been defiled with his own son, and could not go abroad without shame to themselves, and some disgrace to him, and without renewing the memory of Absalom’s filthiness and wickedness, which he out of his tender affection desired should be rather forgotten and buried with him.

*{living in widowhood*} For though their lord and husband still lived, yet they were in a sort widows, because they were divorced and perpetually separated from him, and so he was to them, as if he had been dead.

**V. 4.** {*Then the King said to Amasa, Assemble me the men of Judah*} Having appointed him to be general instead of Joab, to ratify this grant and confirm him in his place, he put him into present employment suitable hereunto, namely to raise an army to pursue Sheba, and prevent his design.

*{within three days}* This was a great work to be done in so short a time; but David thought expedition necessary, lest Sheba by longer leisure should have increased his number of mutineers, and grow too strong.

**V. 5.** {*So Amasa went—but he tarried longer than the time*} Either the time was too short for such a dispatch, or most of the people were so addicted to their old general, under whom they had formerly such good success, that Amasa could hardly persuade them to follow him, who of late had prospered no better. But whatsoever was the cause, this delay might work some jealousy and suspicion in David, that he being but a reconciled enemy was not true to him and his cause, and so make him think to employ another in that service which required such quick dispatch.

**V. 6.** {*And David said to Abishai*} David was impatient of Amasa’s long stay, yet being resolved not to restore Joab to the generalship, as fearing that if he should use him in his service he would encroach upon him, and by gaining the people’s favor would usurp it into his hands, he chooses rather to employ Abishai in this expedition.

*{Now shall Sheba—do us more harm than did Absalom*} He will raise up against us a more dangerous rebellion, if he be not speedily prevented; seeing many that would not adhere to Absalom, because he was David’s son, would now follow Sheba, who was of Saul’s tribe and family, and David’s professed enemy.

*{Take thou thy lord’s servants, and pursue after him*} This some understand of Joab, who had been general of the army; and others of Amasa, who was now in his place. See ch. 11. 11. But most probably it is meant of David’s own servants, and chiefly of the Cherethites and Pelethites, who were of his guard with others of his faithful followers, that were mighty men, and now about him as it is expressed v. 7.

**V. 7.** {*Joab’s men*} So called because they had been under his command.

*{and the Cherethites, and the Pelethites*} See on ch. 8. 18.

**V. 8.** {*When they were at the great stone*} When Abishai and Joab with the rest of their company were come to this great stone, which was a landmark stood near Gibeon, they discovered Amasa and part of the army going before them; and so Joab hastened to overtake him, with a full resolution to kill him, as afterwards it appeared.

*{And Joab’s garment that he had put on, was girded unto him*} i.e. His soldier’s coat or cassock was tied close to him, that it might not hinder him in acting his design.

*{and upon it a girdle, with a sword fastened upon his loins*} i.e. Hanged on the outside of his coat in a soldier’s belt, that he might readily use it upon any sudden occasion.

*{and as he went forth, it fell out*} i.e. Purposely going with Abishai, with a full resolution to kill Amasa, as he had formerly done Abner for the same cause (being encouraged, it seems, by his impunity) he put his sword into a wide scabbard, that being glib in the sheath it might upon the moving and bending of his body fall out, and that so falling he might without Amasa’s suspecting any thing stoop to take it up, and in his rising might deadly wound him, while he embraced him and took him by the beard to salute and kiss him.

**V. 9.** {*And Joab said to Amasa, Art thou in health, my brother?*} i.e. My dear kinsman. For he was his cousin-german, or sister’s son; and it was the manner of the Hebrews to call kinsmen brethren, 1 Chro. 2. 16, 17. Mark 6. 3. Matth. 12. 47. Joh. 7. 3.

*{and Joab took Amasa by the beard—to kiss him*} As their manner was in saluting. See Gen. 29. 11.

**V. 10.** {*But Amasa took no heed to the sword that was in Joab’s hand*} i.e. He suspected nothing, when, slipping out of the scabbard, he stooped to take it up, thinking that it had slipped out by accident, and not by any design.

*{So he smote him therewith in the fifth rib*} i.e. He stabbed him into the body under the fifth rib, near his belly, so that his bowels came out. See on ch. 2. 23.

*{and struck him not again*} Heb. *doubled not his stroke*. Because he had made sure to kill him already, and had given him his death’s wound with the first blow. See 1 Sam. 26. 8.

*{and he died*} i.e. Received a mortal wound.

*{so Joab and Abishai his brother pursued after Sheba*} Joab having thus taken Amasa out of his way, resumed this office of being general over the army, and as their captain led them out against Sheba.

**V. 11.** {*And one of Joab’s men stood by him*} i.e. By the body of Amasa, appointed by Joab (as it seems) to stand there to make the following proclamation to the soldiers as they passed by.

*{He that favoureth Joab*} i.e. Who loving him, desires to restore him to his place of general, Amasa being dead.

*{and that is for David, let him go after Joab*} i.e. Whoso desires that David and his party should have good success in this expedition against the common enemy and the traitors that join with him, let not this accident hinder him from pursuing them.

**V. 12.** {*And Amasa wallowed in his blood*} Being in the pangs of death.

*{And when the man saw that all the people stood still*} When Joab’s friend and follower, v. 11. observed that, notwithstanding his proclamation and encouragement, many of the soldiers, as they were passing by, stood still to gaze upon Amasa’s body, as a strange and formidable sight, he removed it out of their way, that they might march on under Joab’s conduct.

**V. 13.** {*When he was removed—all the people went on after Joab*} Hereby it appears in what high favor Joab was with all the militia and army, in that notwithstanding David’s displeasure against him, and his bloody and treacherous murdering of Amasa, none of the soldiers, no not those who were lately under Amasa’s conduct and command, refused to follow him. And this may somewhat excuse David for not punishing him for his foul fact, seeing it was not in his power to do it; though he may justly bear the blame, in that after his return he did not so much as sharply reprove him for it, if at least the story report wholly all that was said and done.

**V. 14.** {*And he went through all the tribes of Israel*} i.e. Joab and his army pursued Sheba with his followers, who went through all the tribes to stir them up to join with him in this rebellion; that is, all those that lay between Jerusalem and Abel, to wit, Ephraim, Manasseh, Issachar, Zebulun, and Naphtali.

*{unto Abel*} Situate in the tribe of Naphtali, in the north border of Canaan towards Syria, where were valleys called Maachah. Hence it is named Abel-beth-maachah here, and 2 King. 15. 29. and Abel-Maim, 2 Chr. 16. 4. to distinguish it from another city of this name not far from Gadara, in the tribe of Judah. Some think this Abel-Maachah was in the tribe of Manasseh, and so named from the wife of Machir, who was called Maachah, and was of Manasseh, 1 Chro. 7. 16.

*{and all the Berites*} i.e. The inhabitants of the land and city of Beeroth in the tribe of Benjamin, near Abel, Josh. 18. 25.

*{and they were gathered together*} i.e. The Israelites which inhabited the forementioned places, not hearkening to Sheba, followed after Joab.

**V. 15.** {*And they came and besieged him*} i.e. Sheba the rebel.

*{and they cast up a bank*} That from or upon it they might fight with and beat off those that defended the city wall.

*{and it stood in the trench*} Or, *it stood against the outmost wall*.

*{and all the people that were with Joab battered the wall to throw it down*} Heb. *marred to throw down*. To wit, with engines; and, as the word implies, under-mined the wall.

**V. 16.** {*Then cried a wise woman out of the city}* i.e. From the city wall.

**V. 18.** {*They were wont to speak in old time, saying, They shall surely ask counsel at Abel: and so they ended the matter*} Or, *They plainly spake in the beginning, saying, Surely they will ask of Abel, and so make an end*. According to the former reading some understand it thus. The inhabitants of this city in former times being reputed very just and prudent, were in weighty affairs resorted unto for counsel; whence it grew into a proverb, That they who needed advice should ask at Abel. And when they had heard it, they so rested in it, that the controversy was ended and determined, and all well and peaceably concluded. Which course if Joab had taken before the siege, he might have saved that labor, and the city have been preserved from danger. Others think, according to the second reading, that she puts him in mind of the old law, which commanded that before they besieged a city, they should summon it, and offer unto it conditions of peace, to which if the citizens yielded, there should be a cessation of arms, Deut. 20. 10, 11. Wherein tacitly she charged Joab that he had failed; as in truth he had done, and therein offended by transgressing God’s law; for if by his messengers he had sent such conditions of peace unto Abel before he laid siege to it, they had yielded to them, and so the war had ceased.

**V. 19.** {*I am one of them that are peaceable and faithful in Israel*} i.e. I, with many others in this city are of a peaceable condition, and faithful to the king, and not apt to rise up against him in rebellion; though it may seem otherwise, seeing Sheba, a rebel and a seditious person fled hither for harbor, is here sheltered, and the gates shut against you and your army. But he suddenly came in upon us, and we did not know his end and aim in resorting to us. And the shutting of our gates was not caused by any disaffection to the king or your self, but by a sudden fear, when we heard that an army approached near unto us, and had from you no intimation of the cause: And if the gates should have been left open, there being in every army some unruly soldiers given to plunder and prey, they might have done us a mischief, and we should have had the amends in our own hands.

*{Thou seekest to destroy a city and a mother in Israel*} This she speaks in the name of the city, that it was a mother or head city, having towns and villages under it, which had ever been peaceable and loyal to the king. And this she uses as another argument to dissuade Joab from destroying or hurting it, seeing it would be an act of great injustice, and very disserviceable to the state, which would be much weakened by the ruin of such a famous city.

*{Why wilt thou swallow up the inheritance of the Lord?*} q.d. If you use unjust rigor in ruining this city and the inhabitants of it, you shall not only much prejudice and endamage the whole commonwealth by cutting off such a helpful member, but also deal impiously and injuriously against God himself by destroying a city in Israel which he accepts and loves, as his inheritance and by robbing him of his right commit abominable sacrilege, and so make yourself obnoxious to much guilt and punishment. She uses this expression of swallowing up the Lord’s inheritance to intimate unto him that, if in fury and compassion he proceeded in his course, he would not show himself a man, who leisurely chews what he eats, but resemble a ravenous wild beast, which out of greediness and hunger devours the prey at once, and as it were swallows it down whole.

**V. 20.** {*Far be it from me that I should swallow up or destroy*} Joab wholly disclaims all that the wise woman had laid to his charge, and protests in a redoubled speech, that his aim was not to destroy the city and endamage the commonwealth, but to preserve both, by cutting off and taking from among them a common pest, which would infect all that came near him with the contagion of his sedition and rebellion, if we were suffered to live any longer.

**V. 21.** {*But a man of mount Ephraim—Sheba by name*} Sheba is said to be a man of mount Ephraim, because he had dwelt in the confines of Ephraim. But properly the Beerothites, of which he was, were Benjamites. See v. 1. and ch. 4. 2. Josh. 18. 25.

*{And the woman said unto Joab, Behold, his head shall be thrown to thee over the wall*} This wise woman thus confidently promises, either because she already knew the minds of the elders and chief magistrates of the city, who had sufficient power in their hands to effect it, and it may be had put her upon this employment, as being wise, and so fit to manage it, and a woman, and therefore more likely to be heard with pity and compassion; or else because she thought it a thing so reasonable that they should cut off a rebel to preserve the whole city, which was now in extremity of danger, that she doubted not but that, wisely and prudently pressing the matter, she should easily persuade them all to hearken unto her.

**V. 22.** {*the woman went to all the people in her wisdom*} She persuaded the citizens generally, as well as the elders, with her wise reasons, to give Sheba’s head to Joab, as she had promised. See Eccl. 9. 15.

*{and he blew a trumpet, and they retired from the city every man to his tent*} Here again Joab showed his wise moderation, and that he was a lover of the peace and welfare of the commonwealth, and a prudent general, keeping his soldiers in good order and discipline, seeing when they were so near the taking of this mother city, upon his bare command they were content to retire themselves, and so after all their labor to lose their expected prey.

*{and Joab returned to Jerusalem unto the King*} David is not here said to have reprehended Joab either for killing Amasa, or re-estating himself in the generalship against his order and without his consent. And such a matter, it is likely, would not have been omitted, if it had been done seriously and severely as it ought. But David did not think it fit to be done at this time, partly, because Joab had such success in this expedition as gained the hearts of the soldiers; and partly because he saw he could not well want his service for the time to come, especially now that Amasa was taken out of the way; and partly because Abishai would have been incensed against him if he had offered any hard measure to his brother; and partly also, because he dared not displease Joab, who was now so potent and powerful: Therefore in a prudential and politic way he thought it in vain to reprehend that which he was not able to reform or punish, and chose rather a better opportunity; and what he could not do himself, he thought it best to give in charge to his son.

**V. 23.** {*Now Joab was over all the host*} Here the names of David’s chief officers are expressed, as they were when he began to reign over all Israel, ch. 8. 16. because this reestablishment was as if he had been again anointed and crowned. And first, Joab is said to have been still general over the army, being restored to his place after the death of Amasa; not that David desired it, but because another could not be substituted without danger of commotions. See ch. 3. 39.

*{and Benaiah—was over the Cherethites*} He also was formerly named, but here again repeated, with divers of the rest, because upon David’s restoring to the kingdom though there might happen many great alterations, yet these officers for the most part remained still the same. See on ch. 8. 16.

**V. 24.** {*And Adoram was over the tribute*} This office and officer were not mentioned ch. 8. but now were added to the rest, because David had use of them, there being many nations by his conquests become tributaries unto him.

**V. 25.** {*And Sheva was Scribe*} Called Seraiah ch. 8. 17. as some think, the same man having two names, as was not unusual with the Hebrews.

*{and Ira also the Jairite*} Of the country of Jair in Gilead, Num. 32. 41. Judg. 10. 4.

*{was a chief Ruler*} Or, *prince*. David’s sons mentioned ch. 8. are not here spoken of, because divers of the chief of them, as Amnon, Absalom, and (as it is very likely) Chileab, were now dead.

## CHAPTER 21

**Ver. 1.** {*Then there was a famine in the days of David}* i.e. After the things related in the former story, concerning the rebellion of Absalom and Sheba, were done and ended, God sent a grievous famine upon Israel, which continued three years one after another. Some expositors think this story of the famine, and that following, of David’s victories against the Philistines, are transposed, and that these things happened before those rebellions, grounded their conceit upon the forty years mentioned ch. 15. 7. which they reckon from the beginning of David’s reign in Hebron: But seeing the account may be better taken from his anointing by Samuel, as I have showed in my notes upon that place, and so the things which followed might be done in that order as they are set down in the story, there is no reason to admit of such a transposition in the Scriptures, seeing it is never safe to allow it but when it is necessary and cannot be avoided.

*{And David enquired of the Lord*} i.e. When by the long continuance of the famine he conceived there was some cause of it above ordinary course of nature, in the fourth year, like a good king, careful of the welfare of his subjects, he inquired (as is most likely) by the high Priests, with the breastplate and Urim and Thummim, what was the cause of God’s wrath, that he might use means to appease it, and so the famine might cease, Numb. 27. 21.

*{It is for Saul, and for his bloody house, because he slew the Gibeonites*} This was an heinous sin in God’s sight, both because they violated their oath made in his presence, Josh. 9. 3, 15, 18. and also because they used this bloody cruelty towards them who were proselytes to the Jewish religion, and by the long prescription of divers hundreds of years were invested into the same privileges with God’s people, being of the same faith, and by their calling and profession serviceable to the sanctuary. The sin is imputed to Saul and all his house, and punished both in them and all the people, because they were accessory to Saul’s bloody fact, either by their advice and counsel, or in that they liked and approved it, or at least did not seek to hinder and dissuade him from it.

**V. 2.** {*Now the Gibeonites were not of the children of Israel*} To wit, by nation and birth, though they dwelt among them.

*{but of the remnant of the Amorites*} They were not Amorites properly, but Hivites, Josh. 9. 7. but because the Amorites were the chief and most potent and warlike people of all those cursed nations, and because, though they had a peculiar country to themselves which they principally inhabited, many of them were dispersed and intermingled with the rest on both sides Jordan, therefore usually all the inhabitants of Canaan are in the Scripture called Amorites, as Gen. 15. 16. Josh. 5. 1. & 10. 5, 6. and 24. 8, 15. as in many other places they all go under the name of Canaanites, though they who were properly so called did inhabit but one part of the country, lying upon the coast of the Mediterranean sea, Gen. 13. 7. and 24. 3. Numb. 14. 25.

*{and Saul sought to slay them*} i.e. Utterly to destroy and root them out of the land.

*{In his zeal to the children of Israel and Judah*} Not in a true and holy zeal, aiming at God’s glory; for it tended much to his dishonor to have them destroyed who were now proselytes, and professed his true religion, and had put themselves under his protection; but in a blind and bloody, and hypocritical and preposterous zeal, aiming, as he pretended, at the welfare of the commonwealth of Israel; though indeed his main end was popular applause, and to gain their favor, by seeming to be so zealous in promoting their good. In which respect he is said to have done it *in his zeal*, and not inspired by God. He would seem to envy these Gentiles and cursed nations, that they should be incorporated into God’s people, and enjoy all their privileges; and thinking to rectify the error of Joshua and other rulers, and to bring the kingdom into a right frame, when none enjoyed any part of it but only Israelites, to whom it was given, he endeavored to have all others rooted out from amongst them. And this is called zeal, because he seemed herein to respect the law of God, which commanded that these cursed nations should be utterly destroyed, Exod. 23. 32, 33. Deut. 7. 16. And here by the way we may observe Saul’s gross hypocrisy, in that being expressly commanded to destroy the Amalekites, he spared Agag out of a seeming pity, but here uses barbarous cruelty in seeking to root out the Gibeonites, who were to be preserved by oath and covenant, for no other fault but because they were strangers; and this he did under pretense of zeal.

**V. 3.** {*Wherefore David said to the Gibeonites, What shall I do for you?*} i.e. What satisfaction will you require, that you may not complain unto God of his people’s cruelty, and so draw down his judgments upon them; but contrariwise, being satisfied, may pray for their peace and prosperity? Hereby it may seem that when David consulted with God about the cause of the famine, he not only told him what it was, but also required that he should use this means to give satisfaction for the wrong which the Gibeonites had received: For otherwise he would have done what himself thought best, and not have put it to their choice to demand what they pleased.

**V. 4.** {*We will have no silver nor gold of Saul, nor of his house, neither for us shalt thou kill any man in Israel*} To wit, saving those only whom we will now name unto you. And here they show that their ends and aims in the satisfaction required were not base and covetous; for they desired no money, nor cruel and unjust; for though many of them had been murdered and massacred, yet they would rest satisfied with the death of some few, and of those only who were chief actors in their destruction, namely, Saul’s bloody family; and that not so much for private revenge, as that by their death an atonement might be made unto the Lord, and so the famine might be removed.

**V. 5.** {*The man that consumed us, and that devised against us*} i.e. Who destroyed a great number of us, and not so contented, plotted still and contrived to root out utterly all the rest.

**V. 6.** {*Let seven of his sons be delivered unto us*} i.e. Of his posterity; for he had none of his own sons living, immediately descended. In this demand there was an overruling hand of providence that inclined and directed them, that Saul’s posterity being rooted out, David’s kingdom might be established, according to God’s promise. For these, if they had lived, would upon all occasions have been ready to disturb the peace by aspiring unto the crown.

*{and we will hang them up unto the Lord*} i.e. Devote them unto him, to appease his wrath incensed against the land of Israel. So Numb. 25. 4. Such execution was sometimes used to make atonement for some public misdeeds Deut. 21. 22, 23.

*{in Gibeah of Saul*} To wit, for their great dishonor, Gibeah being the place of their habitation, among their kindred and friends; and to the end also that by their example all others, especially their family and tribe, might be deterred from attempting the like bloody design.

*{whom the Lord did choose}* Namely, to be the first king of Israel. This they added as an aggravation of his bloody sin, in that being advanced above all others out of God’s mere grace to be king, he turned tyrant, and imbrued his hands in innocent blood. Others read the words thus, *O elect of Jehovah*, and apply them unto David, who was elect and chosen of God both in respect of the kingdom, and his own person, with his posterity after him; and also in respect of the Messiah, of whom David was a figure, and in him eternally elected.

*{And the King said, I will give them*} Being appointed by God to give them satisfaction, he promises to deliver seven of Saul’s sons into their hands to be hanged by them. Here it may be demanded first, how this could stand with David’s justice, to punish the children for the father’s sin, contrary to the practice of Amaziah 2 Kings 14. 6. which was grounded on God’s law, Deut. 24. 16. I answer, David’s fact was lawful, yea necessary, seeing he had God’s warrant and direction for it, who has power, when he pleases to dispense with his own laws. But secondly, how will this stand with God’s justice, to have Saul’s posterity punished for his sin, contrary to what he protests Ezek. 18. 20. I answer, Though it be said that *God will visit the sins of parents upon their children unto the third and fourth generation*, that is not to be understood simply and absolutely without any reference to the sin of the children, for principally in his punishments he has respect to a man’s own sin: As in this place, Saul’s posterity are punished for his bloody sin; but it is not simply for his sin alone, they being innocent, but because they were accessory and guilty of the like cruelty; in which regard they are here called *his bloody house*. And it was just, in way of retaliation and requital, that as Saul had slain the Gibeonites and their posterity, so they also should slay his. Yea, but God often punishes the sins of parents upon their children, though they be not guilty of them. I answer though they be innocent of their parents’ sins, yet they have sins enough of their own which God may justly punish, when and how he pleases, though he may take occasion of so doing by calling to remembrance the sin of the parents. And this he may do justly in little infants, who are often involved in the same punishment with their parents, though they never committed any actual transgression, seeing they are under the guilt of original sin, which deserves death. Again, punishments are to be distinguished into temporary, and eternal. In the former, children, and such as are innocent of those sins for which the punishment is inflicted, are usually involved, because they live in the same community and corporation, and are as it were members of the same body; and therefore they rejoice and mourn together; and as they participate and communicate in temporal blessings and benefits, so also in outward crosses and calamities, like the members of the same body, which though they be sound and healthy in themselves, yet sympathize with their fellow members, if they be diseased and pained. But as for those punishments which are eternal, they are never inflicted upon any but only for their own sins. And therefore of these chiefly the prophet is to be understood, Ezek. 18. 4, 20.

**V. 7.** {*But the King spared Mephibosheth—because of the Lord’s oath that was between them*} He had likewise sworn to Saul that he would not cut off his seed after him, 1 Sam. 24. 21, 22. But when God by his command had dispensed with that oath, and it was at David’s choice to appoint to death which seven he would of all Saul’s posterity, he spared Mephibosheth above all the rest, because of that special covenant that was between him and Jonathan, which was contracted and grounded on that great mutual love which they bore one to another, 1 Sam. 18. 3. and 20. 8, 16, 42.

**V. 8.** {*But the King took the two sons of Rizpah*} i.e. Saul’s concubine, ch. 3. 7. and v. 11.

*{and the five sons of Michal the daughter of Saul*} i.e. Of Merab the sister of Michal, and wife of Adriel, 1 Sam. 18. 19. Her sons being educated and adopted by Michal are called here Michal’s sons. See Gen. 16. 2. and 30. 3. and 50. 23. Ruth 4. 17. Some think here is used the figure ellipsis, which in a curt and short manner of speaking according to the Hebrew idiom cuts off the name of the one sister, and expresses it by the other. So v. 19. *Goliath* for *the brother of Goliath*: and Jer. 32. 12. *Jeremiah’s uncle* is put for *his uncle’s son*. Howsoever, it is manifest that Merab, and Michal, was married to Adriel; and that Michal was first married to David, and afterwards to Phalti, and then again resumed by David, but had no children to the day of her death, and if she had borne any to him, they must have been reckoned rather the posterity of David than of Saul 1 Sam. 18. 19. 1 Sam. 6. 22.

*{whom she brought up for Adriel*} Heb. *bare to Adriel*. This makes me prefer the ellipsis, Michal for *Merab Michal’s sister*: For otherwise it should rather have been said that she adopted them to herself or her husband, than for Adriel, who was their natural father: unless we would say that she only educated them for Adriel; and then they could not be called her children, seeing they were so neither by nature nor adoption.

*{the son of Barzillai the Meholathite*} So called, because he was of Abel-Meholah in the tribe of Benjamin, Judg. 7. 22. 1 King. 4. 12. and 19. 16. And it is here used to distinguish him from Barzillai the Gileadite, ch. 19. 13.

**V. 9.** {*and they hanged them in the hill*} i.e. In some eminent hill Gibeah-Saul, v. 6. that all might look upon them as a fearful example of God’s high displeasure against Saul and his bloody family for massacring the Gibeonites, and to deter all others from attempting the like cruelty.

*{before the Lord*} i.e. To appease God’s wrath, and to make an atonement for the people, that the famine might cease.

*{fell*} i.e. Were executed and put to death.

*{in the days of harvest, in the first days, in the beginning of barley harvest}* Which in Canaan was in the month Nisan, part whereof was in March, and part in April. See Ruth 1. 22. They were to hang from this time till God by sending rain did show that his wrath was appeased. Whereby it appears that the cause of the famine was a great and long drought, the usual cause of famine in the land of Canaan, as we see 1 King. 17. 1. and 18. 1, 2. Deut. 11. 12, 14. and 28. 23, 24. And though it does not appear how long the bodies did hang, yet it is manifest that it was much longer than the ordinary time, seeing they were to hang from the beginning of barley harvest till the Lord by sending rain should testify that his wrath was appeased. And though this were not deferred, as some guess, till the usual time of their latter rain, i.e. September, (for in an extraordinary case God had sent rain in an extraordinary time, or else it would not have been so manifest that his wrath was pacified) yet it appears by Rizpah’s preparation and attendance that they hanged much longer than one day, the time appointed by God in the law for the hanging of dead bodies on a tree, Deut. 21. 23. This David could not have done without manifest transgressing God’s express commandment, but that he had herein special direction from God, who when he pleases may dispense with his own law.

**V. 10.** {*And Rizpah—took sackcloth, and spread it for her upon the rock*} Some understand that she spread the sackcloth upon the dead bodies to preserve them from birds and beasts: But seeing it is said that she spread it not for them but *for herself*, and not upon their bodies but *upon the rock*, I rather with others conceive that she spread it as a tent to defend her from the heat of the day, and from malignant influences in the night, whilst she watched these bodies; and that for this purpose she used sackcloth rather than other cloth, to testify her mourning. And it seems the bodies were not covered, but hung in the open air, seeing it is said that they remained there *until the water dropped upon them from heaven*, and that Rizpah watched them to keep off the birds and beasts from preying upon them.

*{from the beginning of harvest, till water dropped upon them*} Though by God’s law bodies were to hang but to the setting of the sun the same day, Deut. 21. 23. yet because the famine came by drought, the king by God’s direction would have them hang till rain came, that so it might be a sign that God’s wrath was appeased, and mercy obtained; for which end they were hanged up.

**V. 11.** {*And it was told David what Rizpah—had done*} David no doubt approved of her natural and pious affection towards her sons, manifested in her taking such care and pains herself to preserve their dead bodies from being rent and devoured, when in all likelihood she had servants whom she might have employed in that service.

**V. 12.** {*And David went and took the bones of Saul—and Jonathan*} i.e. Commanded it to be done by his servants and officers; being (as it seems) put in mind to do it by the example of Rizpah.

*{from the men of Jabesh-Gilead*} See on 1 Sam. 31. 10, 11, 12.

**V. 13.** {*And he brought up from thence the bones of Saul, &c.*} To wit after they had been long buried. See 1 Sam. 31. 12.

*{and they gathered the bones of them that were hanged*} That hereby he might give some comfort to their afflicted mother, and by an honorable burial make some amends for their ignominious death. Now whereas it is said *their bones* were gathered, it is implied either that this was not done soon after their death, but some good space of time after, or rather that they used their bodies as the men of Jabesh-Gilead formerly had used the bodies of Saul and his sons, which being so putrefied that they could not be embalmed, they burnt off the flesh with sweet odors, and buried the bones: See on 1 Sam. 31. 12, 13.

**V. 14.** {*Of Benjamin in Zelah*} Josh. 18. 18. where was the sepulcher of Kish Saul’s father.

*{and they performed all that the King commanded*} i.e. All before spoken of, and concerning their funeral, with all the solemnities belonging unto it.

*{and after that, God was intreated for the land*} This he testified by taking away the famine, and returning plenty, by sending rain. So ch. 24. 25.

**V. 15.** {*And David waxed faint*} Being now aged, and not able to hold out with that vigor, as he had in the time of his youth and strength.

**V. 16.** {*And Ishbi-benob, which was of the sons of the Giant*} Called Rapha, who dwelt at Gath, from whom other giants had the name Raphites, v. 22. Of giants. See on Gen. 6. 4.

*{the weight of whose spear*} Heb. *the staff*, or, *the head*. The iron of his spear’s head. See 1 Sam. 17. 5.

*{three hundred shekels*} i.e. Twelve pounds and an half. See on 1 Sam. 17. 5.

*{he being girded with a new sword, thought to have slain David*} Some think he was then called to some place of military honor and command, as to be a centurion, tribune, or captain, and in token thereof had, as the custom was in this case, put on a new sword. And he now adventured to press into the army to assault David, that he might give an eminent proof and trial of his valor.

**V. 17.** {*Thou shalt no more go out with us to battle}* This oath they make not in a compulsory way to restrain the king against his will, but as faithful and loyal subjects, out of a reverential love and prudential care, to preserve him from falling again into the like danger.

*{that thou quench not the light of Israel*} Heb. *candle*, or, *lamp*. q.d. Lest the glory and splendor of Israel perish with you. Good kings by their wisdom guide and counsel the people, and go before and direct them by the light of their holy and just conversation, and are the very joy and life of the whole nation: In which respect they are called lights and *lamp*s, as David here, and others in other places, 1 Kings 11. 36. & 15. 4. Psal. 132. 17. 2 Chr. 21. 17.

**V. 18.** {*there was again a battle with the Philistines at Gob}* Was situate by Gezer in Ephraim, upon the borders of Benjamin, towards the country of the Philistines. Whereupon it is said 1 Chr. 20. 4. that this battle was fought at Gezer, because Gob was near unto it.

*{Then Sibbechai*} One of David’s worthies. 1 Chr. 11. 29.

*{slew Saph*} Another giant, called also Sippai, 1 Chr. 20. 4.

**V. 19.** {*Where Elhanan*} Another of David’s worthies, 1 Chr. 11. 26.

*{the son of Jaare Oregim*} Called also Jair, 1 Chr. 20. 5. To which name (as some would have it) Oregim was added, because the spear of the giant whom Elhanan slew was *like a weaver’s beam*, as the word signifies. The which might have been probable, if the name Oregim had been added to Elhanan after he had slain this giant; and not to Jair, who was his father, and had his name before this battle was fought.

*{slew the brother of Goliath*} Heb. Goliath. Here by ellipsis *the brother* is left out, and in our translation rightly supplied. So v. 8. Michal for *Merab Michal’s sister*. For David himself slew Goliath, 1 Sam. 17. But this giant here was his brother, and called Lahmi, 1 Chr. 20. 5. See on Josh. 11. 22. and the Appendix after Genesis.

**V. 20.** {*And there was yet a battle in Gath}* i.e. Near unto Gath, one of the five famous cities of the Philistines, near unto the midland sea.

*{that had on every hand six fingers, &c.*} And so was not only a giant of a vast growth and stature, but also a monster, and therefore the more terrible.

*{and he also was born to the Giant*} Or, *Rapha*. So called in way of eminency, because the other giants descended from him.

**V. 21.** {*And when he defied Israel*} i.e. Reproached, dared and challenged them with scorn and despite, as Goliath had formerly done. See on 1 Sam. 17. 10, 25, 36.

*{Jonathan*} Some think he was the same man who is before called Jonadab, that gave that wicked counsel to Amnon, ch. 13. 3, 5. And if so, then it appears that he was a man of more wit, strength and valor, than of piety and honesty, as the most are of his profession.

*{the son of Shimea*} Named also Shammah 1 Sam. 16. 9.

**V. 22.** {*These four—fell by the hand of David and—his servants*} To David as king and general, the victory of his soldiers and commanders is ascribed. Or we may add to these Goliath, who was slain by David himself. 1 Sam. 17. All these were sons to this giant. And so taking one with another, they may be said to have *all fallen by the hand of David and his servants*.

## CHAPTER 22

**Ver. 1.** {*And David spake unto the Lord the words of this song*} i.e. Composed and sang unto the praise and glory of the Lord this psalm, to testify his thankfulness for his many and great deliverances, victories over his enemies, and singular blessings and benefits conferred upon him.

*{in the day that the Lord had delivered him out of the hand of all his enemies*} i.e. In his old age, and the last year of his reign, when having vanquished all his enemies, both foreign and domestical, he enjoyed a settled and secure peace; Not spending the remainder of his days in ease and idleness, pleasure and voluptuousness, as has been the manner of many kings and conquerors in the like case and condition, but devoting himself to pious and religious exercises to the glory of God, who had been the chief cause and author of all his deliverances and victories.

*{and out of the hand of Saul*} Him he specially names above others, because his persecutions of him were most fierce and cruel, dangerous, and of longest continuance.

**V. 2.** {*And he said*} i.e. In penning this song of thanksgiving and praise he expressed himself in the following words. This is all one with the eighteenth psalm, saving that there is some variety of expressions here and there, and some small passages left out or added in the one which are not in the other. And this some conceive was done, by reason this here, recorded in the Book of Samuel, was the first draught, and that it was by David afterwards revised, and so put into the volume of his Psalms, and committed by him to the Levites to be sung by them. But I cannot see how this can well stand with the immediate inspiration of God’s Spirit; unless we will say that the Holy Ghost chiefly respected the matter of the Scriptures, and not the expressions; as seems to appear plainly by comparing parallel places as the books of Samuel, kings and Chronicles, and the four evangelists, all which agree in a sweet harmony in respect of sense and substance, though they vary in words and some other circumstantial differences. And first here those words are omitted in the beginning, *I will love thee, O Lord my strength*, Which are the proposition of the whole psalm; all the rest that follow being but reasons of the Psalmist’s ardent and intent affection towards God, who had vouchsafed him so many and great deliverances, with such miraculous victories over so malicious and potent enemies.

*{The Lord is*} Here David uses great variety of expressions to set forth in a more lively manner the experience he had found of God’s all-sufficiency in the many kinds of his deliverances from innumerable evils and enemies, and to raise up and even ravish his spirit with unfeigned thankfulness to God, who had so multiplied his favors towards him that he could not find words enough sufficiently to express them.
