*{my rock*} i.e. A sure, strong and immovable foundation of all the good which I enjoy or expect, and a place of safety against all the machinations of my enemies, either hiding me in its caverns when they seek after me to destroy me, as in the days of Saul; or securing me when I stand upon my defense against their assaults and fury, as being a place impregnable and inaccessible, 1 Sam. 13. 6. Judg. 6. 2. By this allusion David shows that though he had formerly used rocks to secure himself from his enemies, yet he had not relied chiefly on them, but on God his Savior, who had used them as means of his safety. Now this in a peculiar manner may relate unto Christ, who is the rock upon whom his church is built, Matth. 16. 18. and the sole foundation of all his comfort and salvation, unto whom that alone may rightly be applied, v. 32. *Who is a rock, save our God?*

*{my fortress, and my deliverer*} i.e. My stronghold and place of protection, to defend me against all the assaults of my enemies; and my Savior, to deliver me in time of danger from falling into their hands.

**V. 3.** {*The God of my rock, in him will I trust*} Psal. 18. 2. *My God, my strength, in whom I will trust*. i.e. Both as *he girdeth me with strength*, as he speaks Psal. 18. 32. and endues me with such valor and fortitude as that I am able to resist all the encounters of my enemies; and also as he is my strength, who supports and sustains me when in my own strength I am ready to faint.

*{he is my shield*} i.e. A strong defense, to repel all the encounters and assaults of my enemies.

*{and the horn of my salvation*} Whereby I do not only defend myself, but also offend my enemies. A metaphor borrowed from horned beasts, to whom their horns serve not only for beauty and comeliness, but only for defensive and offensive weapons against those that oppose them. So Numb. 23. 22. and 24. 8. Deut. 33. 17. Psal. 92. 10. Thus Christ is called *the horn of our salvation*, Luk. 1. 69.

*{my high tower and my refuge*} i.e. An invincible fort strong both by art and nature, unto which retiring myself in time of danger I am safe from the violence of all my adversaries.

*{my Saviour, thou savest me from violence*} When I was so circumvented and ensnared with the plots and power of my foes that there appeared no way nor hope of escaping, you contrary to the opinion of all, and mine own expectation, did provide a means of my deliverance from the violence of mine enemies.

**V. 4.** {*I will call upon the Lord, who is worthy to be praised, &c.*} Seeing all my safety is from God, who, whether I recount his goodness in himself, or his graciousness towards me, deserves all praise, therefore in all my difficulties and dangers I will have recourse unto him by prayer, being assured that he is both able and willing to save me out of the hands of my most potent and malicious adversaries.

**V. 5.** {*When the waves of death compassed me*} Or, *the pangs*, or *sorrows*, Psal. 18. 4. i.e. When I was beset on all sides with many and malicious enemies, who threatened imminent death and destruction without any hope of escaping, calling upon you, I found deliverance. He compares his enemies, and the evils they threatened to bring upon him, to huge waves ready to drown and overwhelm him, and even to swallow him up quick, as Psal. 124. 3, 4, 5. both in respect of their multitude which came against him from all parts of the kingdom, and of their sudden and frequent attempts, which like waves and billows came one in the neck of another, and of their unresistible strength and violence, like a raging torrent bearing all down before them; but withal intimating that they were like land floods, but of short continuance. For the time they lasted, they put him in fear, as land floods and torrents do passengers, when they see and hear them with an hideous noise coming against them from the mountains, upon the melting of snow, or some sudden and hideous storm of rain; But these fears lasted not long; and whilst they continued, they were turned to his good, driving him unto God by fervent and effectual prayer for help and deliverance. Others read it thus, *When the cords of death compassed me*, i.e. When I was in great consternation and fear, like those that have present death before them, and being bound fast with cords are led out to execution.

*{the floods of ungodly men made me afraid*} Heb. *Belial*. i.e. The outrageous attempts of graceless and lawless men struck such terror into me that I could see no hope of escaping their might and malice if you in your goodness did not send deliverance. See Judg. 19. 22.

**V. 6.** {*The sorrows of hell compassed me*} i.e. I was enclosed on all sides with the sorrows and fears of death and the grave, like a man who, lying on his deathbed, has no hope of recovery; yea, like one carrying to the grave, and bound with graveclothes, who has no strength to come forth.

*{the snares of death prevented me*} i.e. My enemies had so cunningly and closely plotted and contrived my death and ruin, (like cunning fowlers, or foresters, that lay secret snares to catch and kill birds or beasts) that I should have been easily circumvented by their plots and policies, and so have fallen into their mischievous gins, unless you had discovered them, and so made way for my escape.

**V. 7.** {*In my distress I called upon the Lord, and cried unto my God}* By this repetition he shows that he prayed unto God not seldom, but frequently, again and again, in the time of his straits and troubles, and that not slackly and slightly, but with all fervency and earnestness, both calling and crying for help and deliverance.

*{and he did hear my voice out of his holy Temple*} i.e. When David prayed unto God out of the place of his public worship, where in a special manner he is present. Or rather it is to be understood of the place from whence God heard his prayers; i.e. heaven the place of his habitation Which is called his temple, because the temple was a type of it, both in respect of God’s special residence there above all other places, and also of its purity and holiness, seeing no unclean thing may enter into it; and finally, because God had made special promises that he would hear the prayers of his servants calling upon him from his temple, the place upon which his name was called, and where his honor dwelt, 1 King. 8. 29. 2 Chr. 7. 15. And hence it was that, though they were absent from the temple, yet they prayed with their faces towards it, Dan. 6. 10. And herein the temple was not only a type of heaven, but also of Christ himself, in reference to whom all our prayers are heard and granted.

**V. 8.** {*Then the earth shook and trembled*} From this verse to v. 17. he sets forth the vengeance which God exercised upon his enemies, in destroying them with sudden, fearful and inevitable plagues and punishments, by comparing them to an horrible tempest raised suddenly by his almighty power, which in a lively manner he here describes. And herein he alludes to God’s former wonders manifested in delivering the Israelites out of the Egyptian bondage, at the Red sea, on mount Sinai, in bringing upon the cursed nations in Joshua’s time swift and sudden destruction; all which were evident demonstrations of God’s presence, providence and power in protecting his church and destroying his enemies. First he says that *the earth shook and trembled*, alluding to that terrible earthquake at the giving of the law, which caused both the men and the mount also to shake and tremble Exod. 19. 16, 18. By such manifestations of his power God so terrified David’s mighty and malicious enemies that they dared not proceed in their mischievous intentions against him.

*{the foundations of heaven moved and shook*} i.e. The high mountains, which seemed to touch the heavens, as though they were founded upon them: Even as Atlas, an high hill in Barbaria, by the poets is said to be an huge giant sustaining the heavens with his shoulders.

*{because he was wroth*} i.e. God’s power and displeasure in destroying of my enemies was as plainly evidenced as when he suddenly sends such horrible storms and tempests.

**V. 9.** {*there went up a smoke out of his nostrils*} Here he alludes to the manner of men, who when they are inflamed with great wrath, do, as we use to say, puff and huff their breath out of their nostrils. By this smoke he understands thick vapors and clouds raised up by the sun and carried with the winds, which darken the heavens, and hide the sun and stars from the earth.

*{and fire out of his mouth devoured*} He alludes to a man inflamed with rage, who is said to spit fire out of his mouth, when he threatens ruin and destruction to those at whom he is offended. And here in the metaphor is meant hot and fiery exhalations causing thunders and lightnings, which are signs of God’s power and wrath.

*{coals were kindled at it*} Hereby is signified the durableness and continuance of God’s wrath against his enemies, which did not suddenly vanish, like a flash of lightning, but was like that fulgurous and gross lightning which takes hold on solid substances, rending trees and houses, and setting them on fire.

**V. 10.** {*He bowed the heavens also, and came down*} He alludes to a thick and foggy mist or cloud which makes the heavens seem to descend and come near unto our heads. And hereby is meant that God did so visibly execute his vengeance against David’s enemies that he seemed to come down from the heaven to the earth for that very purpose. Not that God properly can move from place to place; for being infinite and immense, he fills at once all places; but he is said so to do, when he does manifest his power and presence in one place more than in another, as he did here in the destruction of David’s enemies.

*{and darkness was under his feet*} i.e. Thick and black clouds; whereby is signified his secret and invisible ways, in which he came to rescue David out of all his troubles, and to destroy his enemies.

**V. 11.** {*He rode upon a Cherub, and did fly*} i.e. On the wings of the wind. Here he alludes unto the Cherubims over the mercy seat in the Holy of holies, which had wings, to signify the speed of the holy angels in executing God’s commands. And he resembles God to a triumphant king, riding in a winged chariot, descending as it were with a swift motion to bring deliverance to his servants and destruction to his enemies; and withal he shows that he was the same God who dwelling in the sanctuary, as a chief place of his residence, was in covenant with his people, and ready, according to his promise, to give speedy help and deliverance to his servants, and to bring destruction upon their adversaries.

*{and he was seen upon the wings of the wind*} i.e. Did manifest his power and providence in his speedy coming in to the rescue of his servants when their enemies did bend all their strength against them.

**V. 12.** {*And he made darkness pavilions about him*} Either to keep men from prying into the secrets of his judgments, or to show his displeasure against his and his servants’ enemies, like a king that retiring himself into his secret closet when as he will not vouchsafe so much as to be seen of those who are in his displeasure. Or else the Psalmist may seem to allude to the cloud which went before the Israelites, which was darkness to the Egyptians, but gave light to God’s people.

**V. 13.** {*Through the brightness before him were coals of fire kindled*} This is otherwise expressed Psal. 18. 12. *At the brightness that was before him his thick clouds passed, hailstones and coals of fire*. 13. *The Lord also thundered in the heavens, and the Highest gave his voice; hailstones and coals of fire*. Which is a description of the lightning breaking out of dark and thick clouds; and here resembles God’s judgments against his enemies, which are sudden, speedy and unresistible.

*{were coals of fire kindled*} See v. 9.

**V. 14.** {*The Lord thundered from heaven, and the most high uttered his voice*} i.e. By thunder and lightning God daunted Pharaoh, Exod. 9. 28. and the Israelites on mount Sinai, Exod. 19. and 1 Sam. 12. 17, 18. discomfited the Philistines, 1 Sam. 7. 10. according to Hannah’s prophecy, 1 Sam. 2. 10. So Psal. 77. 18. Thunder is called the voice of God, to show the terribleness of it, and to admonish us that we do not wholly ascribe it to natural causes, but acknowledge that it comes by God’s special providence and command, for the terror of his proud enemies, when they exalt themselves against him.

**V. 15.** {*And he sent out arrows, and scattered them: lightning, and discomfited them*} Or, *he shot out lightning*, Psal. 18. 14. i.e. Thunderbolts and hailstones; as upon the Canaanites, Josh. 10. 11. These are called *God’s arrows*, Hab. 3. 11. These the Lord shot and sent, to scatter and discomfit his enemies, and to comfort and deliver his servants, particularly David, out of their hands, as is here expressed, and v. 17, 18. And hereby is implied that those creatures which are terrible to the wicked, be comfortable to God’s servants, as being a means of their good. And therefore, when, seeing these things, *men’s hearts shall fail them with fear*, it is said that the faithful *shall look up, and lift up their heads, because their redemption draweth near*, Luke 21. 26, 28.

**V. 16.** {*And the channels of the sea appeared*} i.e. By reason of this raging tempest the waters and waves were so raised up, even as it were from the bottom of the sea, that the very earth was discovered, which is the foundation and center of the world. It is an emphatical and hyperbolical expression of a terrible tempest wherein withal David seems to allude to the drying of the Red sea, and of Jordan, that the Israelites might pass over.

**V. 17.** {*He sent from above, he took me, he drew me out of many waters*} i.e. He delivered me in a miraculous manner out of deep dangers and desperate calamities, which threatened to drown and destroy me, even as it were with his hand stretched out from heaven to pull me out of them. Thus it is usual in the Scriptures to call great calamities waters. Psal. 32. 6. and 69. 1, 2. and 130. 1. Job. 22. 11. And here the Psalmist compares the rage of his enemies to deep waters, from which he was delivered, not by his own or any human prudence, policy or power, but only by God’s omnipotent hand.

**V. 18.** {*He delivered me from my strong enemy*} This may be understood either indefinitely, of any of his greater enemies, as Goliath, Doeg, Achitophel, &c. or more particularly, of Saul, who as he matched any of the rest in malice: so he exceeded them all in power. And here David begins plainly and particularly to apply and explain those things which before he had spoken figuratively concerning his enemies, their rage and malice, and God’s wonderful deliverances of him out of their hands.

*{for they were too strong for me*} This he adds to magnify God’s almighty power, who was able to conquer and quell such potent enemies, and takes delight to interpose his might in assisting his servants when they are overmatched, and to manifest his power in their weakness when they are ready to faint and sink.

**V. 19.** {*They prevented me in the day of calamity*} i.e. They had so suddenly and subtly plotted my destruction, before I had so much as any time to think of it, and much less to prevent it, that notwithstanding all human counsel and strength they had surely supplanted me, unless the Lord had succored and sustained me.

**V. 20.** {*He brought me forth also into a large place*} i.e. Whereas I was heretofore in many straits and troubles, difficulties and dangers, like a man in prison bound and hampered with fetters and chains, he has freed me from my sorrows, and set me at liberty out of my restraint, giving me a time of breathing, and enlarging my heart with joy and gladness. And all this he did, not for any cause in me, but out of his free grace and good pleasure.

**V. 21.** {*The Lord rewarded me according to my righteousness*} Here we are not to understand a reward of merit; for so he should contradict his last words, that God did all because he delighted in him, out of his good pleasure; but a reward of mere grace, there being no proportion between the work and the reward. Neither does he speak of his righteousness in reference to God; for in this sense he prays, *Enter not into judgement with thy servant, O Lord; for in thy sight shall no man living be justified*, Psal. 143. 2. but of his innocency in respect of his enemies, as being clear of those faults and crimes which they slanderously laid to his charge, as rebellion against his king, ambitious aspiring and affecting the kingdom by unlawful means, disturbing the state and commonwealth, wars, murders, spoiling of the country to relieve his followers, and such like; in all which he appeals to God, to justify his cause against their false calumnies and imputations, who being the searcher of all hearts did know his integrity and innocency in all those things which they laid to his charge. So Psal. 7. 3, 4, 8.

*{according to the cleanness of my hands*} i.e. The purity of my actions, and mine innocency in all my courses and dealings, in reference to my enemies. In all which he shows that God had justified and approved him, in that he had so richly rewarded him, notwithstanding all their oppositions, out of his rich grace and bounty.

**V. 22.** {*For I have kept the ways of the Lord*} i.e. I have framed and squared my life and actions according to the rule of God’s word, and ordered my conversation according to his law, making choice of it as my ordinary way in which I have walked.

*{and have not wickedly departed from my God*} i.e. Howsoever I have had many slips and failings, and as it were errors and digressions out of God’s ways, yet I have not resolvedly proceeded in them; but still the bent of my will and my constant endeavor have been to keep myself close with God, and to please him, by walking constantly in the ways of his commandments.

**V. 23.** {*For all his judgements were before me*} I have set the whole law of God before me, as the rule of my life and all my actions, and have not wittingly and willingly dispensed with any one of his commandments, but endeavored to observe them all, one as well as another.

**V. 24.** {*I was also upright before him*} Howsoever I have had many failings and defects even in my best actions, yet what I have done in God’s service has not been in hypocrisy and dissimulation, but in the sincerity and uprightness of my heart, in singleness and simplicity, as he best knows who tries and searches the secrets of the heart and reins.

*{and have kept myself from mine iniquity}* I restrained myself from committing that sin unto which otherwise I was by nature prone to fall, as revenging myself upon mine enemies in an unjust way, when they fell into my hand, and particularly upon Saul, both in the cave, and in his camp, 1 Sam. 24. 26.

**V. 25.** {*Therefore the Lord hath recompensed me according to my righteousness*} Seeing I did not seek to right myself in a way of private revenge, but left it to the Lord to plead my cause, to whom alone vengeance belongs, therefore he has rewarded me abundantly, according to my uprightness and just dealing, by bringing mine enemies to destruction, and giving me deliverance.

*{according to my cleanness in his eye sight*} According to that innocency and uprightness which he saw in me, who judges all things according to truth; though mine enemies judged otherwise of me, and condemned me to be ambitious, seditious, ungrateful, turbulent and cruel.

**V. 26.** {*With the merciful thou wilt shew thyself merciful}* Here he rises from the hypothesis to the thesis; and from his own particular experience he gathers a general observation, That God would deal thus graciously with all his sincere and faithful servants, showing mercy to those who were merciful to others, and dealing righteously and faithfully in rewarding those according to his gracious promises, who had walked before him in the uprightness of their hearts; and contrariwise would punish and pull down those who had showed themselves hypocritical, unjust and cruel; as it now appeared in the example of himself, whom he had exalted, and of his enemies, whom he had cast down and destroyed.

**V. 27.** {*And with the froward thou wilt shew thyself unsavoury}* Or, *froward*, Psal. 18. 26. i.e. Those that walk perversely and cross unto God in all his ways, profane contemners of holiness and purity, either wholly neglecting his service, or not serving him in sincerity and integrity but in formality and hypocrisy, with such unsavory salt, as are so distasteful unto God, he will show himself unsavory, and not such as he is in his own nature, or such as they would have him to be, but such as they have deserved to find him, that is, unpleasant and distasteful to their appetite. Or, he will show himself *froward* to those that deal *froward*ly and perversely with him, i.e. not according to his own nature, who being good, does good to all that are in a capacity to partake of his goodness, Psal. 119. 68. but as a just Judge he will punish them according to their demerits, and laying aside the exercise of his mercy, which is his own most natural work, will do *his strange work*, as the prophet expresses it, Isa. 28. 21. The like phrase we have Lev. 26. 23, 24. *If you walk contrary to me, I will walk contrary to you*.

**V. 28.** {*And the afflicted people thou wilt save: but thine eyes are upon the haughty, that thou mayst bring them down*} Or, *thine eyes will bring down the proud looks*, Psal. 18. 17. i.e. You will preserve those who are unjustly oppressed by their mighty and malicious enemies; but will take special notice of their proud oppressors, to bring them to confusion and destruction in due time. Here he seems to meet with a secret objection, That in the ordinary dispensation of God’s providence it may seem that the godly are afflicted, and the godless flourish. To which he answers, That howsoever it may so fall out for a time, yet it shall not continue so forever; but in the end God will approve himself to be true and just in his promises and threatenings, delivering and exalting the godly, and punishing and destroying the wicked. So Psal. 37. 25, 36, &c. and 73. 17.

**V. 29.** {*For thou art my lamp, O Lord; the Lord will enlighten my darkness*} Or, *thou wilt lighten my candle*, Psal. 18. 28. Here he applies the general doctrine to his own particular, showing by his own example that God is true in his promises to his faithful servants, in supporting and comforting them in their troubles and afflictions, and giving seasonable deliverance, and restoring them to a prosperous condition, and joy and comfort in it. For in the Scripture light, and a candle or lamp, metaphorically signify prosperity and felicity, and joy and gladness in the fruition of it; because naturally light does clear and comfort the heart, even as contrariwise darkness makes it sad and lumpish. So Prov. 13. 9. *The light of the righteous rejoiceth, but the lamp of the wicked shall be put out*. Job. 18. 5. Esth. 8. 19. So that his meaning is, that God did both comfort him in his afflictions whilst they lasted, and by delivering him out of them, and bringing him into a prosperous condition, did replenish his heart with gladness.

**V. 30.** {*For by thee have I run through a troop; by my God have I leaped over a wall*} He ascribes all his good success, his valor and victories unto the Lord alone, as the chief author and giver of them. By his assistance he had prevailed against his enemies, both in the open field, in the day of battle, breaking through their troops, and so routing and discomfiting them, and also assaulting them in their fortifications, cities and walled towns, which though they seemed impregnable, yet he easily scaled them, and that with much celerity and speed, as he implies by that phrase of leaping over their walls.

**V. 31.** {*As for God, his way is perfect*} He returns from his own particular experience to a general doctrine, showing that God was not only to him, but also unto all the faithful, who put their trust in him, perfect in all his ways, not failing in any of his promises, nor dealing at any time deceitfully with them, but showing himself upright, sincere and just in all his performances.

*{the word of the Lord is tryed*} Or, *refined*. Not so much in itself; for so it is most pure, and needs no trial or refining, Psal. 19. 8, 9. but it is said to be pure and *refined* in respect of us, when in our experience we find it to be certain and constant without any dross of deceit, fickleness or unfaithfulness.

*{he is a buckler to all them that trust in him*} i.e. Protection and a sure defense in all difficulties and dangers, who repels all the assaults of their enemies that trust in him, reposing themselves under the shelter of his power, promises and providence.

**V. 32.** {*For who is God, save the Lord?*} i.e. Though there be many false gods among the nations, 1 Cor. 8. 5. yet there is no true God, who is able to help, or to give being to his promises, saving Jehovah only, Isai. 45. 22. and therefore none but he alone in whom we may put our trust.

*{and who is a rock, save our God?*} There is none save he who is a sure foundation on whom we may build our hopes, nor any else but he unto whom we may retire for safety and defense when we are pursued by our enemies.

**V. 33.** {*God is my strength and power*} Or, *it is God that girdeth me with strength*, Psal. 18. 32. i.e. It is he alone from whom I have all my strength and valor, whereby I have been victorious. He alludes to the soldier’s belt or girdle, wherewith he girds his loins for the strengthening of them; or to the loins and reins themselves, in which the Scriptures do sometimes place a man’s strength, which are said to be girt, when as men put forth their strength to achieve some noble exploit, Job. 38. 3. and 40. 16. Isai. 45. 1, 5. Nah. 2. 1. Prov. 31. 17.

*{and he maketh my way perfect*} i.e. God by his blessing prospers me in all my attempts, and gives good success to all my actions and proceedings, so as I find no rubs to hinder them.

**V. 34.** {*He maketh my feet like hinds’ feet, and setteth me upon my high places*} i.e. He has endued me with much agility and celerity to act all martial affairs, ch. 1. 23. 1 Chron. 22. 8. and enables me with much speed and ease to scale and master high castles and impregnable holds herein like unto hinds and goats, which with much facility and safety climb up the craggy rocks, which are unaccessible to other beasts.

**V. 35.** {*He teacheth my hands to war*} i.e. All my skill in managing martial affairs I have learned from him.

*{so that a bow of steel is broken by my arms*} i.e. He has endued me with such singular strength that I am able not only to draw the stiffest and strongest bow against my enemies, but also, if I be so pleased, to break it in pieces. Here he does not, like other great soldiers and commanders, arrogate the glory of his strength to himself, but ascribes it wholly to God alone, from whom he had received it.

**V. 36.** {*Thou hast also given me the shield of thy salvation*} i.e. All the means whereby I am saved and secured, are not of myself, but of your gracious and free gift.

*{and thy gentleness hath made me great*} Psal. 18. 35. *And thy right hand hath holden me up, and thy gentleness hath made me great*. i.e. You have strengthened and supported me in the day of battle, that I have not fainted and failed in any of my warlike enterprises. Neither do I ascribe my prosperous success whereby I am exalted, unto any thing in myself, but to your free grace and tender favor, which you have sweetly and graciously extended towards me.

**V. 37.** {*Thou hast enlarged my steps under me*} i.e. When I was brought into such straits and difficulties that there was no hope of escaping by any human help, then you did free me out of those desperate dangers, and set me at liberty, that I might go whither I pleased. He seems to allude to the time when Saul caused his to be watched and beset, and his wife let him out of a window; or, when being pursued by Saul, and almost apprehended, Saul was taken off by the news of the Philistines’ invasion, 1 Sam. 19. 11, 18. and 23. 26, 27.

*{so that my feet did not slip*} i.e. I had a fair safe and sure passage in all my undertakings, effecting what I took in hand, without incurring much danger.

**V. 38.** {*I have pursued mine enemies, and destroyed them*} Here he shows that God had made him prosper in all his warlike expeditions, even to the utter ruin of his enemies; which though he had executed with all severity, yet he was not to be taxed with injustice and cruelty, seeing he had done nothing herein but that which God had appointed and called him unto, and not out of malice and private revenge, but chiefly aiming at God’s glory and the preservation of his people, by rooting out their desperate and implacable enemies, whom he was bound to hate with a perfect hatred, as he protests Psal. 139. 21, 22.

**V. 39.** {*And I have consumed them and wounded them, that they could not arise; yea, they are fallen under my feet*} i.e. I have utterly destroyed them, they not being able to make any resistance, and have trampled them under my feet, which is a sign of their extreme dejection and unrecoverable ruin. Josh. 10. 24.

**V. 41.** {*Thou hast also given me the necks of mine enemies*} To wit, that I might behead them, slay and cut them off; Or, as others, that I might subdue and subjugate them, bringing their necks under the yoke of tribute.

**V. 42.** {*They looked, but there was none to save*} Or, *they cried, but there was none to save them*, Psal. 18. 41. i.e. In their troubles and miseries they cried unto the Lord, expecting from him help and deliverance; but he regarded not their prayers, because they were not of faith, nor accompanied with true repentance, but in hypocrisy, and extorted from them with pain and anguish. Not crying unto God with their hearts, when they howled on their beds, Hos. 7. 14. which seems not to be meant of his heathenish and idolatrous enemies, but of the hypocrites of his own nation, who called upon God with their lips, when their hearts were far from him. Like those spoken of Isai. 29. 13.

**V. 43.** {*Then did I beat them as small as the dust of the earth*} Or, *the dust before the wind*, Psal. 18. 42. *I did stamp them as the mire of the street, and did spread them abroad*, or, *I did cast them out as the dirt in the streets*, Psal. 18. 42. By these hyperbolical expressions he shows that he had not only wholly subdued and ruined them, but also had in a disgraceful manner trodden and trampled upon them, as filth and dirt, because of their abominable wickedness. This we see verified in the destruction of the Moabites and Ammonites.

**V. 44.** {*Thou also hast delivered me from the strivings of my people*} From the plottings and conspiracies, the insurrections and rebellions of mine own people, over whom you have set me as their king, and that both in the time of Saul’s reign, and those wars afterwards raised against me by Abner and Ishbosheth, Absalom, and Sheba.

*{thou hast kept me to be head of the heathen*} You have not only established me in a peaceable government in mine own nation, but also have extended my dominion over all the heathen round about me. And whereas formerly they had been grievous oppressors of your own people, now being vanquished, they have submitted themselves to live in subjection, and become my tributaries. Such were the Moabites, Ammonites, Philistines, and Syrians.

*{a people which I knew not shall serve me*} i.e. Strangers and aliens, whom I never owned as mine, or any ways belonging to me, are now become my subjects and servants. And herein David was a notable type of Christ, whose dominion was to the ends of the earth over Jews and Gentiles, Psal. 2. 6, 8. Gen. 49. 10. Hag. 2. 7.

**V. 45.** {*strangers shall submit themselves unto me: as soon as they hear, they shall be obedient unto me*} Psal. 18. 44. *As soon as they hear of me, they shall obey me; the strangers shall submit themselves unto me*. i.e. The fame of my many victories shall so spread itself abroad that many nations against whom I never warred shall voluntarily submit themselves unto my government. This most fitly agrees to Christ, of whom David was a type, unto whose scepter the nations and Gentiles have submitted, and received him for their king, only by the hearing of the ear, and the preaching of the Gospel by his faithful ministers and ambassadors, Rom. 1. 16. and 10. 17. 1 Cor. 5. 20. Ephes. 2. 13. 2 Cor. 10. 4, 5. But as many nations submitted themselves to David’s yoke not freely and cordially, but out of fear and self-respects, so also is it with Christ’s subjects: Among these there are many carnal worldlings and hypocrites, who profess that they receive him as their king, but do it only in outward show, for their earthly ends; as is implied by the words here used, which may be thus read, as it is in the margin of our Bibles, *they shall yield feigned obedience*. And thus the word is used Psal. 66. 3. and 81. 15.

**V. 46.** {*strangers shall fade away*} i.e. Hearing the fame of my victories they shall be so amazed and terrified that their hearts shall faint and fail them; and all confidence in their own strength to make resistance being taken away, they should molder and come to nothing, like flowers, or untimely and blasted fruits.

*{and they shall be afraid out of their close places*} Though they have retired themselves for their safety into secret dens and caves, or else into their castles, fortifications and strongholds, yet all this shall not free them from fear, till they have submitted themselves unto me, and covenanted to become my subjects. This was typified in David, but verified in Christ, from whose rod of iron none living in sins can secure themselves from being crushed in pieces as a potter’s vessel, with any shifts and evasions, but are still possessed with fear and the spirit of bondage, till thereby they are wholly brought out of themselves, and willingly acknowledge that he is their king, and they his subjects.

**V. 47.** {*The Lord liveth, and blessed be my rock*} Here he concludes the Psalm with thanksgiving, and praising God for all the benefits formerly rehearsed, especially because he had been a rock of refuge in all his difficulties and dangers, and a sure foundation whereupon he had built all his hopes.

**V. 48.** {*It is God that avengeth me*} i.e. Who has made me victorious over all mine enemies, and delivered them into my hands, giving unto me courage and power to execute upon them such punishments as they have deserved for carrying themselves as malicious enemies to God and his people. And here he again repeats what he had often before said, that he might ascribe the whole glory of this good success unto God alone, and not any part thereof to his own prudence, power and skill in military affairs.

**V. 49.** {*And that bringeth me forth from mine enemies*} i.e. Delivers me out of their hands, notwithstanding all their policy and power.

*{thou hast lifted me up on high*} i.e. To a kingly state and strength, out of the reach of their malice and might, like a man seated in an impregnable fort, on the top of an high and inaccessible rock.

*{thou hast delivered me from the violent man*} i.e. Either indefinitely, from the violence of my mighty enemies; or particularly, from Saul’s powerful violence.

**V. 50.** {*Therefore I will give thanks unto thee, O Lord, among the heathen*} I will publicly declare your mercies and favors even unto the heathen nations that have not known you, that they taking notice of your grace and goodness towards your people, and to myself in particular, may be brought into your church, and the profession of your true religion; and so being made partakers of the like mercies, may magnify your glorious name, and together with your own people sing aloud your praises. And this David foretells by the spirit of prophecy, having respect to the calling of the Gentiles, and their joining to the church of the Jews; to which purpose this place is cited by the Apostle, Rom. 15. 8, 9.

*{I will sing praises unto thy name*} That I may glorify you by magnifying your favors and mercies towards me and all your people.

**V. 51.** {*He is the tower of salvation for his King*} i.e. The king by his power and protection, as by a strong tower, has strength and defense from all his enemies. He names himself under the title of God’s king, to justify himself against the obloquies and calumnies of his adversaries, seeing he was a king of God’s own calling and anointing, and not an ambitious usurper, as they charged him.

*{and sheweth mercy to his anointed, unto David, and to his seed for evermore*} Here he magnifies God’s favor and goodness, in that it is extended not only to himself, but also to his seed unto all generations; and more especially and chiefly unto Christ, of whom David was a type, ch. 7. 12. though his father according to the flesh, Rom. 1. 3. Act. 13. 23. In whom God’s favor and love is firmly established forever; and not only to himself, but in and through him to all the faithful, who are his seed, and by the ministry of the Word and Spirit are begotten unto him, and united into one body, whereof he is the head, by a true and lively faith. For so the faithful are called his seed, Isai. 53. 10. and *his children*, Isa. 8. 18. which prophecy the Apostle particularly applies to our Lord and Savior, Heb. 2. 13.

## CHAPTER 23

**Ver. 1.** {*Now these be the last words of David*} Not the very last he spoke before he died; for he spoke many other words after these, concerning civil domestical affairs, and the administration of the kingdom, ch. 24. and 1 Kings 1 and 2. but the last which he spoke by the inspiration of God’s Spirit, as a penman of holy Scriptures, after his Psalms and other works, for the public use and edification of the church.

*{David the son of Jesse said*} He describes himself by divers titles, and first by his stock and parentage, that he was the son of Jesse. And this he does both to show that he was not, though a great king, ashamed of the meanness of his family and pedigree; and also because the kings of Israel, and the Messiah himself, by God’s special appointment, were to come out of the stock of Jesse, Isai. 11. 1.

*{the man who was raised up on high*} Here he describes himself by his advancement from a low and mean estate to sovereign dignity. This he mentions to magnify God’s free grace and mercy towards him.

*{the anointed of the God of Jacob*} Here he describes himself by his calling, which he had by God himself, who chose and appointed him to be king over his own peculiar people Israel, and herein to be a type of the Messiah anointed by God to an everlasting kingdom.

*{the sweet Psalmist of Israel*} This he sets down last, as being the highest of his titles, That God had inspired him by his Spirit to be a penman of holy Scripture, not only inditing Psalms and spiritual hymns, and singing them for his own comfort, and solace, but also publishing them for the service of God in the church, and withal appointing singers, and music of instruments, and tunes fitting for them, Amos 6. 5. 1 Chro. 16. 4, 5. This commendation of himself might well stand with David’s modesty, because what he spoke was truth, and his intention therein was not to praise himself but to glorify God, who had of his free grace endued him with these gifts; and because he spoke it not in an ordinary manner, out of his own private spirit, but as he was, being herein a penman of Scripture, immediately inspired by the Spirit of God, as appears in the words following.

**V. 2.** {*The Spirit of the Lord spake by me*} i.e. God the Father, and God the Son, who is called the rock of Israel, v. 3. Spoke unto him by his Holy Spirit. So that here we have expressed the Trinity of persons; and also, that they are all three but one God, of the same nature and substance, because the same action of speaking unto David is ascribed to them all.

*{and his word was in my tongue*} i.e. I spoke nothing of that which follows but by the motion of God’s Spirit. Hereby he implies the excellency and dignity of this speech, as also of his other Psalms, which proceeded not from private motion, but from the Holy Ghost, 2 Pet. 1. 21. In which respect the Apostles say in their prayer to God, that *he had opened the mouth of his servant David*, Act. 4. 25.

**V. 3.** {*The Rock of Israel spake unto me*} i.e. God, and his Son Christ, who is the immovable foundation upon which all the hopes and comforts of his church and people are built, and the only place of refuge unto which they retire themselves in all dangers. So Deut. 32. 4. Psal. 18. 2.

*{he that ruleth over men must be just, ruling in the fear of God*} It is fit that he who gives laws and commands to order and guide others should himself be just and straight; and that not for outward and civil respects, but from an inward and true principle, the fear of God, in whose right and place he rules. This David propounds as a maxim and general rule both to himself, and all his kingly posterity that should succeed him. Yet some think this is meant of our spiritual king, the Lord Christ, of whom David and Solomon were types, Psal. 2. 8. and 110. 2. and 72. 1, 2, 8. Jer. 23. 5. Zach. 9. 9.

**V. 4.** {*And he shall be as the light of the morning*} i.e. He that rules justly and in the fear of God shall still increase in glory and all prosperity; like herein to the morning light, *which shineth more and more unto the perfect day*, Prov. 4. 18. having no clouds to obscure and hide it from the sight of men.

*{as the tender grass springing out of the earth or clear shining after rain*} i.e. He shall grow and flourish like the grass and herbs which have the benefit of the sun and seasonable showers. This is to be understood of David and the good kings that succeeded him, and of the fruits and benefits that did accompany their just reign, as the types; but principally of the Messiah our Lord Christ, who was typified by them Psal. 110. 3. Isai. 60. 1, 2. Hos. 6. 3. Mal. 4. 2. Luk. 1. 78.

**V. 5.** {*Although mine house be not so with God*} i.e. Though I have not been myself, nor my successors will be, so exact and perfect in our rule and government as we ought, and as God requires, v. 3. but are subject to many errors and failings, and have often broken covenant, and through human frailty are like to do so still.

*{yet he hath made with me an everlasting covenant*} i.e. Durable and immutable, being not grounded on our worthiness and perfections, but on his own free grace and goodness, ch. 7. 15, 16, Jer. 31. 32, 33, 34. and on the promised Messiah, as the only sure foundation, which is to come out of my loins.

*{ordered in all things and sure*} i.e. By his eternal and gracious decree so disposed, both in respect of the end and means, that it cannot fail; but he will, notwithstanding our unworthiness, continue firm in all his promises both to me and mine house, even for his own name’s sake, and for his Christ, the foundation of the covenant.

*{for this is all my salvation, and all my desire*} i.e. The only ground of all my hope concerning salvation, and all that I can wish and desire.

*{although he make it not to grow*} i.e. Although for our sins and unworthiness he do not grant unto us that flourishing prosperity, expressed v. 4. yet all this will not frustrate his free promises grounded on the Messiah, and make them of none effect, Rom. 3. 3.

**V. 6.** {*But the sons of Belial shall be all of them as thorns thrust away*} i.e. Wicked and reprobate men, who being quite opposite to the just and faithful, have shaken off the yoke of Christ’s kingly government. See Judg. 19. 22. These because they are untractable, and cannot be dealt with, nor amended by any fair means or more gentle corrections, but rather are ready to prick and hurt them who come near, or have any conversation with them, shall be used like thorns, i.e. cut down, or stubbed up by the roots, by God’s vengeance.

**V. 7.** {*But the man that shall touch them must be fenced with iron*} i.e. Must either have his hand armed with some iron glove or gauntlet, or hold it in some iron instrument, to preserve it from being hurt by them.

*{and the staff of a spear*} i.e. Must thrust them from him and keep them aloof, that they may not prick and hurt him, with a staff or pole, like the staff of a spear.

*{and they shall be utterly burnt with fire in the same place*} i.e. Destroyed by the hand of the magistrate or by some extraordinary judgment; or at least, being cut off by the hand of God before their time, shall be cast into the everlasting fire of hell, like thorns which are burnt and consumed in the same place where they grew and were rooted up. Here some think he prophetically alludes to the destruction of the obstinate Jews, who were by the Roman army destroyed in the same place, where they by wicked hands had crucified the Lord of life, and their city was burnt and utterly ruined.

**V. 8.** {*These be the names of the mighty men whom David had*} i.e. Of his chief worthies, captains and commanders, who as they were men of great valor and fortitude, so also were they most faithful to him, both in his afflicted condition before he came to the crown, coming in to him, and giving him their assistance for making him king, 1 Chro. 11. 10. and afterwards also doing him service in all his wars against his enemies both foreign and domestical. And having so well deserved of David for their faithful service, they were worthy to have their names registered and eternized by having them kept in this sacred record. Now these worthies are said to have been, as they are here recorded, *thirty and seven in all* v. 39. And they are divided into two ranks. The first six were chief colonels and commanders over so many brigades it may be of the whole army, each containing several regiments, all under the conduct and command of Joab the Lord general; who though not here named, yet must necessarily be understood and supplied to make up the full number of thirty seven. The other thirty were it may be captains over thousands, or the like number. Again, the six great worthies are divided into two ternions; the first three being most eminent and famous for strength, valor and noble exploits; the other three, though exceeding valorous, yet not matching the first in height of fortitude and magnanimity, or at least not in strength and warlike achievements.

*{The Tachmonite that sat in the seat*} Or, *Joshebbasshebeth the Tachmonite, head of the three*. He is also called *Jashobeam an Hachmonite*, or *son of Hachmoni*, 1 Chro. 11. 11.

*{the same was Adino the Eznite*} He is described I. by his proper name, Adino. II. by the country where he was born or bred, the Eznite. III. by his parentage, the Tachmonite, or Hachmonite, as being the son of one Tachmoni or Hachmoni. IV. He is called Jashobeam, or Joshebbasshebeth, from the honorable place he had in the army, being next unto Joab the general, and the chairman or chief president in the council of war, unto which his name alludes.

*{chief among the Captains*} To wit, among the six colonels here spoken of, who in David’s army were set over the thirty captains mentioned v. 23, 24.

*{he lift up his spear against eight hundred, whom he slew at one time*} i.e. He fought against eight hundred, or set upon them to slay them. Though he slew only three hundred with his own hand upon the place, as it is 1 Chro. 11. 11. yet it may be said that he slew eight hundred, not only because he was the chief commander and principal actor, unto whom the glory of the victory is usually ascribed, but also because he exposed them to be slaughtered by others, when as by his valor slaying three hundred of them he discomfited and put to flight all the rest. Others think this place and that in the Chronicles speak of two several victories; but, as I conceive, it is not so probable.

**V. 9.** {*And after him was Eleazar the son of Dodo the Ahohite*} He is here described I. by his name, Eleazar; II. by his parentage, the son of Dodo, who was an Ahohite, descended of Ahoah a Benjamite, 1 Chr. 8. 4. III. by the honorable place he had in David’s army, he was the second of his three chief worthies. IV. by his famous exploit in slaying the Philistines himself alone, when the rest of the army had left him and were fled away. This story is somewhat enlarged 1 Chro. 11. 13. where it is said that at this battle he was with David at Pasdammim and in *a parcel of ground full of barley*.

*{when they defied the Philistines}* i.e. He was one of David’s worthies who defied the Philistines, afterwards smote and subdued them, when the men of Israel were fled away. Others think the word *charaph* here used is thus to be taken, that *they exposed their life to danger* in this service, as it signifies Judg. 5. 18.

**V. 10.** {*and his hand clave unto the sword*} i.e. Either because it is blistered with his fast holding it, or clammy with the blood of those whom he had slain, or so benumbed and stupefied by gripping it long and fast that he could not open it to take it from the hilt.

*{and the people returned after him only to spoil*} i.e. The people that had fled returned, not to fight, (for he himself had got the victory) but only to take the spoil.

**V. 11.** {*And after him was Shammah the son of Agee the Hararite*} This was David’s third worthy of the first rank, called also Shammoth, 1 Chr. 11. 27. But others think that Shammoth was not the same with this Shammah; and that Shammah is not there mentioned at all, but that the same exploit which is here ascribed to Shammah is there attributed to Eleazar, the defending of a cornfield against a troop of Philistines, whom he routed and slew after his men were fled. But this is said to have been in a field of lentils, that in a field of barley. Whence some expositors probably conjecture that this exploit was achieved jointly by these two worthies at the same time, and that therefore it is said in the plural number, that *they set themselves in the midst of that parcel, and delivered it*, 1 Chr. 11. 14. and that there being both barley and lentils in the same field, Eleazar is said to have defended the barley field from the Philistines, and Shammah that part of the field in which the lentils were.

**V. 13.** {*And three of the thirty chief*} Or, *the three captains over the thirty*; i.e. Those three set over the thirty; which it is probably thought were the first three chief colonels, Adino, Eleazar, and Shammah, because these only are hitherto mentioned, and the other brought in after this story, which 1 Chr. 11. 19. is also concluded with these words, *These things did these three mightiest*, which being expressed in the superlative must in all probability point out the three first and greatest worthies. So v. 17.

*{came to David in the harvest-time unto the cave of Adullam*} Some conjecture that this action there related was done in Saul’s time, when David hid himself from his persecutions in this cave of Adullam. But this cannot be, seeing the Philistines then had no wars with David, but with Saul only. Therefore this is rather to be referred to the beginning of David’s reign over all Israel, when as the Philistines are said to have *come up to seek him*, and to have *pitched in the valley of Rephaim*, ch. 5. 17, 18. and in this place. And this is said to have happened in the time of harvest, when kings usually go out to war, in respect that then they may have plenty of forage. And the hot season might be the cause of David’s thirst, and his thirst put him in mind of the well of Bethlehem, which by reason of his old acquaintance with it he might think fittest to refresh him.

**V. 14.** {*And David was then in an hold*} Probably that mentioned ch. 5. 17. which it may be was that strong fort of Zion, or rather the cave of Adullam, seeing that it is here named, v. 13.

*{and the garrison of the Philistines was then in Bethlehem}* Or, *in the country adjoining unto it*; through which those who would come to the well must necessarily pass. This is added to intimate the great difficulty of the following action.

**V. 15.** {*And David longed*} Being much afflicted with heat and thirst he earnestly desired to drink of the water of this well.

*{and said, Oh that one would give me drink of the water*} i.e. Some one or other. He only hereby signified his desire to have it, if it could be had with a wish, but did not require, or so much as intend, that any should fetch it with the hazard of their lives. But the wishes and desires of good and gracious kings have in them the strength and virtue of a law or command with their loving and loyal subjects; as appears in this place.

**V. 16.** {*And the three mighty men brake through the host of the Philistines}* To wit, which lay spread abroad in the valley of Rephaim.

*{and drew water out of the Well of Bethlehem*} Notwithstanding the garrison of the Philistines there. So vehement and venturous these worthies were to give David satisfaction, even in the least wish, though it were by running the extremest hazard of their lives.

*{nevertheless he would not drink thereof, but poured it out unto the Lord*} In a religious manner, to testify by this oblation his thankfulness unto God for giving such valor unto these worthies, and for preserving them in that great danger; and also to testify how unwilling he was that they should have run such an hazard for pleasing of his appetite.

**V. 17.** {*Is not this the blood of the men that went in jeopardy of their lives?*} i.e. Be it far from me, O Lord, to drink this water, which was purchased by the hazard of their blood and lives. Herein he sets a good example to princes, teaching them that they should not buy their pleasures by endangering the lives of their subjects. And if David might fitly call the water blood that was purchased by the hazard of the blood of his worthies, why might not Christ call the wine in the sacrament his blood, seeing it is a sign of his blood, which he was to shed for our redemption?

*{These things did these three mighty men*} The first three great worthies.

**V. 18.** {*And Abishai the brother of Joab—was chief among three*} 1 Chr. 11. 20. Not the first three, but the second ternary of colonels, whereof two only are here named, Abishai, and Benaiah. Who the third was is not certainly known. But some conjecture it was Asahel. Others think it was one of those before mentioned, who slew the giants, to wit, Sibbechai, Elhanan, or Jonathan, ch. 21. 18, 19, 21. So do men vary in their opinions when they have not the Spirit of God to lead them.

*{and he lift up his spear against three hundred, and slew them*} i.e. He assaulted and fought against three hundred, and vanquished and slew them. This is here mentioned as his chief act of valor; though he had done many others; as namely, his going with David into the midst of Saul’s camp, 1 Sam. 26. 6. and his rescuing him when he was in danger, and slaying the giant, ch. 21. 16, 17. which are here omitted, because they were before related.

**V. 19.** {*Howbeit he attained not to the first three*} i.e. To that esteem of strength and valor.

**V. 20.** {*And Benaiah the son of Jehoiada, the son of a valiant man, of Kabzeel*} A city in the south border of Judah upon the coast of Edom, near the dead sea, Josh. 15. 21.

*{he slew two lion-like men of Moab*} Which did resemble lions in courage and strength.

*{he went down also and slew a lion in the midst of a pit in time of snow*} The lion it seems was at unawares slipped into the pit being covered with snow: And this is added to commend Benaiah’s valor, both in that he voluntarily adventured himself to fight the lion in a place of great disadvantage, in the midst of a pit, where being in a narrow strait he must necessarily miscarry, if he did not overcome and master the beast by his strength; and also in that he did it in time of snow, when as lions wanting their prey (because cattle are shut up commonly by their owners, and fed in their folds and stalls, when they cannot graze) are above all other times most fierce and furious. This adventure Benaiah did not desperately undertake out of a foolhardiness, to show strength and valor, (for then it had not been commendable in him, as being a rash and an unnecessary act) but because he was necessitated unto it for the good of the country, this lion being known to have done much mischief in destroying their cattle.

**V. 21.** {*And he slew an Egyptian, a goodly man*} 1 Chr. 11. 23. It is said that he was *a man of great stature, five cubits high*, and that *his spear was like a weaver’s beam*: and yet Benaiah adventured to encounter him, and by clean strength overcame him, though he fought upon great disadvantage, having only a staff in his hand, and the giant this great spear, which Benaiah pulled from him, and slew him with his own weapon. It is also added, that David *set him over his guard*, or, as the Hebrew has it, *over his audience*. By which is not meant that he was his secretary, notary, or master of requests; for Jehoshaphat and Sheva had those places, ch. 20. 24, 25. but that he was *set over the Cherethites and Pelethites*, ch. 20. 23. who were the king’s guard that daily attended upon his person, and so were ready at the first hearing to execute his commands.

**V. 24.** {*Asahel the brother of Joab was one of the thirty*} And, as some think, the last of the second order of David’s chief commanders. And it is the more probable, because in the following catalogue there are thirty named besides him; which were under the command of the six principal colonels, five under each colonel.

*{Elhanan the son of Dodo*} By comparing this catalogue with that 1 Chr. 11. there appears great difference both in the names and number of David’s worthies partly because divers of them had two names, as was usual among the Hebrews; and partly because divers are reckoned 1 Chr. 11. which were either added to this number, or else substituted in the places of such as were dead, all which notwithstanding David’s worthies were still called thirty, in respect that there were just so many in his first constitution and choice, though afterwards they might be fewer, or more. As they were called still *the twelve Apostles*, when Judas was taken from them, and when Matthias, Paul and Barnabas were added to them, Joh. 20. 24. 1 Cor. 15. 5.

**V. 25.** {*Shammah the Harodite*} See Judg. 7. 1. This Shammah, and Shammah the Hararite, v. 33. as they were diverse one from the other, so both of them from him that was the third man of David’s first three worthies, who was the son of Agee, v. 11.

**V. 26.** {*Helez the Paltite*} Or, *the Pelonite*, 1 Chr. 11. 27.

**V. 27.** {*The Anethothite*} See Josh. 21. 18.

*{Mebunnai*} Called also Sibbecai, 1 Chr. 11. 29.

*{the Hushathite*} Of the tribe of Judah, 1 Chr. 4. 4.

**V. 39.** {*Uriah the Hittite*} The husband of Bathsheba, whom David caused to be slain by the sword of the children of Ammon.

*{thirty and seven in all*} To wit, if to the thirty one worthies here mentioned we add the five colonels, and withal either Joab, who was general of the army, or else the third man of the second rank of the great worthies, who is not named, but conjectured to be one of those spoken of in the note on v. 18.

## CHAPTER 24

**Ver. 1.** {*And again the anger of the Lord was kindled against Israel*} i.e. After the judgment of three years’ famine, whereby in his just displeasure he had punished Israel, he was again by their sins incensed against them. Here anger improperly is attributed unto God: For he is in his own nature free from all passions; but by a speech borrowed from men he is said to be angry, when he does that which they use to do when they be angry, that is, punish those who justly have incurred displeasure by trespasses and sins. Now what these sins were, is not here expressed, but it is conjectured they were pride, carnal security and self-confidence in respect of their strength and number and the plenty and prosperity which they enjoyed after the famine, besides their rebellions and insurrections against their lawful king, under the conduct of Ishbosheth, Absalom and Sheba.

*{and he moved David against them*} He let loose Satan upon David, to tempt him to number the people, that he might take occasion thereby to bring upon them that judgment which he intended and their sins justly deserved. And therefore it is plainly said 1 Chr. 21. 1. *Satan stood up against Israel, and provoked David to number Israel*. Whereby we are not to understand that Satan had any absolute power in himself to make David commit this sin, but that God, to the end that he might take occasion to punish David’s and the people’s former sins, did give him over to be tempted by Satan, and withheld his grace, whereby he might have been enabled to withstand the temptation and so being left to himself he committed this sin of numbering the people, which did arise out of pride and confidence in his own strength, no necessity pressing him unto it, as is plainly implied v. 2. Otherwise in a civil use the action had been lawful, as appears by divers examples both in former and following times, Exod. 30. 11. Num. 1. 3. Now though this was a sin in David, yet it may be truly said that God moved him against them, to say, *Go number Israel*, because he had a special hand in disposing of it for his own ends. For we are not to conceive that God has only an inspection and permissive providence about sins which are committed, but that he has also in divers respects a special hand therein. For I. the power whereby the act of sin is done is from him alone, *in whom we live and move and have our being*; though the malignity and evil of the action be from the wicked agent. II. He punishes one sin with another, not infusing wickedness and corruption; but withdrawing his grace, whereby only we stand in the day of temptation, and which he is not bound to give unto any, much less to those who by their former sins have deserved to be left by him. III. He permits wicked instruments to tempt, delude and seduce; as he did when Satan offered himself to be a lying spirit in the mouths of the false prophets, to bring Ahab to destruction for his former wickednesses, 1 King. 22. 22. IV. He presents unto them objects and occasions of wickedness, which are not simply evil in themselves, and so leaves them to their own corrupt and wicked choice. V. Lastly and chiefly, He so orders the most wicked actions through his infinite wisdom and goodness that he makes them serve his providence for producing great good, namely his own glory, the good of his church, the exercise of his graces in his children, and the just punishment of the wicked; as we see in the example of Joseph’s brethren selling him into Egypt, Shimei’s cursing of David, and the Jews’ crucifying of our Lord and Savior Jesus Christ, which last God so ordered that the greatest good that ever was, came of it, even the eternal salvation of all the elect. See ch. 16. 10. 1 Sam. 26. 19. Gen. 45. 5. Act. 2. 23. and 4. 10, 12. Isai. 9. 14. and 63. 17. Exod. 7. 3. and 10. 20, 27. and 9. 16. 2 Thess. 2. 10, 11.

**V. 2.** {*For the King said to Joab, &c.*} Not inquiring of God, or having his command to warrant him in so weighty a matter, nor upon any just cause, either ecclesiastical or civil, but merely out of his own curiosity, that he might know their number.

*{from Dan even to Beersheba*} i.e. From north to south. See on Judg. 20. 1.

*{and number ye the people*} i.e. From twenty years old and upward, all the males that were able to bear arms; and were not disabled by age, and superannuated, Num. 1. 2, 3. and 26. 2. So v. 9. Others think he commanded all to be numbered that were able to go to war, without respect to this age, contrary to the law, Num. 1. 3. and that this might be the cause of the difference between the number ver. 9. and that 1 Chron. 21. 5. the one speaking of them only above the age of twenty, and the other of all in general.

**V. 3.** {*And Joab said unto the King, Now the Lord thy God add unto the people—an hundred-fold*} This was a wish to express how much his heart desired the good and prosperity of David and the people, and not implying possibility in nature, especially David’s old age being considered; though to God all things are possible.

*{but why doth my Lord the King delight in this thing?*} He implies there was no just cause of this tedious and troublesome work, but only the king’s will and pleasure, to satisfy his curiosity. Other reasons are mentioned 1 Chr. 21. 3. which Joab used to dissuade the king from putting him to this task; as 1. That they were all the king’s servants and subjects, and therefore always ready, how many soever they were, to be at his command; and 2. That he would be a cause of trespass to Israel; to wit, either by drawing them through his example to partake in the same sins of pride and self-confidence, or seeing the vanity of the action, to murmur against their king for doing of it.

**V. 4.** {*Notwithstanding the King’s word prevailed*} Notwithstanding all that Joab could do or say the king would have his will to stand for reason, and his bare command to go for law.

*{And Joab and the Captains of the host*} These it seems, at this time were all together attending upon the king in their course with their bands of twenty four thousand men, which came in and went out month by month, in their several turns throughout all the months of the year, to be employed in the king’s service as he pleased, as is plainly expressed 1 Chr. 27. 1. And now, as it seems, they were sent with Joab to countenance the action to prevent mutinies and insurrections (which might happen, either because the action was vain and troublesome, or chargeable and burdensome to the people, by reason of the officers’ exactions for the king by some poll money, or themselves for fees) and to assist him in this tedious and troublesome business.

*{went out from the presence of the King to number the people*} This act of obedience to the king’s command was, as I conceive, justifiable in Joab, though others think otherwise. For though he might very probably suspect that it was evil in the king, yet because had no certain knowledge of it, seeing *the Lord only trieth and pondereth the hearts* Prov. 17. 3. and 21. 2. and *the hearts of kings especially are unsearchable*, Prov. 25. 3. and seeing also that the action itself of numbering the people was not unlawful, therefore it seems to me that Joab’s acting of the king’s command was not in itself simply evil, though he might sin by doing of it in a sinful manner. For he did it doubtingly, and therefore not of faith, yea, against his own reason and conscience, the king’s word seeming abominable unto him, because he feared it would bring some judgment upon the land, 1 Chron. 21. 3, 6. and yet he would do it to avoid the king’s displeasure; which is to be condemned as a sin in him. But yet seeing all this was grounded but on his own jealousy and suspicion, and not on any certain knowledge; and it was possible that he might otherwise have been persuaded of better intentions in David in this action, out of his own charity, and the experience he had of his king’s piety and integrity; and if so, then it had been apparently sinful to have resisted his commands; therefore in such a doubtful case it was best to take the surer side. And something also it is, that this action of Joab passes in Scripture without any censure, though in David it be deeply condemned as a great sin. Howsoever, I confess, it had been much better for Joab if he had not suffered himself to be acted with an erroneous conscience, either in obeying or disobeying his king’s command, but had humbly searched out the depth of the matter with further expostulation, so far as might have stood with his duty and allegiance; seeing if we have only the guidance of an ignorant and erroneous conscience, we shall easily fall into sin, whether we do an action, or leave it undone.

**V. 5.** {*and they passed over Jordan*} i.e. They first began their journey towards the east side of Canaan.

*{and pitched in Aroer*} A city of the Gadites, Numb. 32. 34. situate between the two rivers of Arnon, the one of which runs into Jordan, the other into the Dead sea, Deut. 2. 36. whose coast belonged to the tribe of Reuben, Josh. 13. 15, 16. and so lies between both the tribes: and hence Arnon is here called the river of Gad.

*{and toward Jazer*} Situate on Arnon.

**V. 6.** {*And to the—of Tahtim-Hodshi*} Or, *to the Nether-land newly inhabited*. It had been recovered in the days of Saul from the Hagarites by the Reubenites, 1 Chr. 5. 10.

*{and they came to Dan-jaan*} Some think this was the city Dan, the north limit of Canaan, named also Laish and Leshem, Josh. 19. 47. Judg. 18. 7. but commonly called Dan without any addition.

*{and about to Zidon*} i.e. By the grounds and fields of Zidon. For the city itself the Canaanites possessed still, as also they did Tyrus, Josh. 19. 29.

**V. 7.** {*And came to the strong hold of Tyre*} i.e. They compassed all the west coast along from Tyre by the cities of the Hivites and Canaanites.

*{and they went out to the South of Judah*} i.e. They turned southward to Beersheba, and so came to Jerusalem, and finished their journey. In this survey Joab spent, as it is said v. 8. nine months and twenty days; whereas the Spies in their search spent but forty days, because they went only to discover the land, but Joab was forced to make many stays in this troublesome business of numbering the people. Joshua’s surveyors were seven months about their discovery. See on Josh. 18. 9.

**V. 9.** {*And Joab gave up the sum of the number—and there were in Israel eight hundred thousand valiant men*} In 1 Chr. 21. 5. are mentioned eleven hundred thousand. And for reconciling the seeming difference in these two places some think that David’s ordinary soldiers or trained bands are not here computed, because serving in ordinary every month in their turns there was no need that Joab should take an account of them, their names being always kept upon the roll; and yet they are reckoned 1 Chr. 21. 5. Now the number of these in ordinary was two hundred eighty eight thousand. For there were four and twenty thousand that served every month, and twelve times this number amounts to two hundred eighty eight thousand, as appears 1 Chr. 27. 1—15. Over these were set so many colonels, tribunes, centurions, captains, commanders and chief officers as made up the number of twelve thousand; So that in all the train-soldiers were three hundred thousand. See on 1 Chr. 21. 5. Others say that in the Chronicles all are expressed which were numbered by Joab, i.e. eleven hundred thousand; and that here is set down only the number which Joab gave up to David, i.e. eight hundred thousand; which came so far short of the other sum, because whilst he was making up the account, the plague was begun in Jerusalem, and that made him have little list to follow his business exactly. And this reason is implied 1 Chr. 27. 24. But against this it is objected that it is expressly said 1 Chr. 21. 5. that Joab gave up to David the sum of eleven hundred thousand, and therefore this reason is rejected, and the former way of reconciliation preferred before it.

*{that drew sword*} Hereby is implied that only those men were numbered which were of years and strength to manage weapons and to be serviceable for war, because David chiefly intended to know the strength of his kingdom, and how many able fighting men he could bring into the field, if he had occasion to use them. This was that in which he prided himself.

*{and the men of Judah were five hundred thousand men*} The men of Judah in this numbering were set apart from the men of Israel, (as they are in many other places of Scripture) as exceeding any other of the tribes both in number and dignity, the crown and scepter being entailed upon them. Now in 1 Chr. 21. 5. the men of Judah are said to have been four hundred threescore and ten thousand, that is, thirty thousand less than here. For reconciling of which difference it is said that here are reckoned the ordinary soldiers of David’s army in Judah, which are thought to have been thirty thousand, every one of those thousands having one of these colonels or commanders mentioned ch. 23. 8, 13, 23. set over them. Now these are left out in the computation 1 Chr. 21. 5. Others think, that according to the number related there, Joab did not at his return reckon the tribes of Benjamin and Levi, and Jerusalem itself, because he was weary of his work, and the king’s command was abominable unto him, 1 Chr. 21. 6. and 27. 24.

**V. 10.** {*And David’s heart smote him after he had numbered the people*} i.e. After he had lain in his sin nine months and twenty days, it pleased God to give him a true sight of pride and carnal confidence: For these his conscience accused and condemned him, as liable to punishment. And this was done either by the immediate operation of God’s Spirit in him, or by the ministry of Gad purposely sent by God to convince him of his sin, as may be probably gathered from v. 11, 12. and 1 Chr. 21. 9, 10, 11. at least being read according to our translation; especially considering that it is God’s more usual way to convince men of sin and to bring them to repentance by the ministry of his prophets than by the immediate working of his Spirit.

*{And David said unto the Lord, I have sinned greatly in that I have done*} No sooner is David by the testimony of his conscience, acted by God’s Spirit, thoroughly convinced of his sin, but laying aside all excuses he humbly confesses it with all aggravations, as knowing it to be the only way of obtaining mercy and forgiveness, Prov. 28. 13. 1 John. 1. 9.

*{take away the iniquity of thy servant*} Forgive and forget it, as a thing put out of sight, or cast into the bottom of the sea, Mic. 7. 18, 19. See ch. 12. 13.

*{for I have done very foolishly*} i.e. Howsoever in this action of numbering the people I seemed to myself very prudent and politic, yet now I plainly see that it was no better than extreme folly, seeing I swerved out of the way of your commandments through pride and carnal confidence.

**V. 11.** {*For when David was up in the morning*} And also had received from Joab the account of the people which he had numbered; then God sent his prophet unto him when his sin was now fully completed.

*{the Prophet Gad David’s Seer*} See on 1 Sam. 9. 9.

**V. 12.** {*I offer unto thee three things; choose thee one of them}* i.e. I propound unto you three judgments, with all the calamities which usually accompany them, that you may make your choice which of them you would have me inflict. Here God, like a gracious father, being resolved to correct his son, does yet out of his pity and compassion give him leave to make choice of his rod; because we bear those crosses and afflictions with more ease and patience in which we have some liberty and choice than those which are pressed upon us by violence and necessity. And though God had decreed and purposed that of all these three judgments he would send the pestilence, and neither famine nor sword, yet he leaves David to his choice, as he should be directed by his wisdom and prudence, but so as that in the meantime both it was enlightened and his will inclined by the secret motion of his Spirit. So that the determination of our wills by God’s decree may well stand with their liberty as it was with David’s in this place, who freely chose that which God had decreed and determined.

**V. 13.** {*Shall seven years of famine come upon thee in thy land?*} 1 Chr. 21. 11, 12. there is mention only of three years’ famine; but here, of seven. For reconciling of which seeming difference we are to reckon in this number the three years of famine, ch. 21. 1. inflicted for Saul’s bloody sin, now already past, and the present year, wherein God had sent seasonable rain and some plenty; unto which three years of famine more being added make up the seven here mentioned. Some by seven understand many, according to the usual Hebrew phrase. But it is not probable, that putting David to his choice, he would speak of an indefinite number, which should end he knew not when.

*{or wilt thou flee three months before thine enemies, while they pursue thee?}* To wit, they also overtaking you, and slaughtering you and your people in the flight. See 1 Chr. 21. 12.

*{or that there be three days’ pestilence in thy land?}* Or, as it is expressed and enlarged 1 Chr. 21. 12. *three days the sword of the Lord, even the pestilence, in the land, and the angel of the Lord destroying throughout all the coasts of Israel*. i.e. The sword of the Lord in the hand of his angel. This is added to show that this pestilence came not in an ordinary manner from the infection of the air, or other natural causes, but immediately from God by the ministry of his angel; that so, when this judgment was inflicted, they might not ascribe it to any secondary means, but look to him that smote them, and be humble under his mighty hand.

**V. 14.** {*And David said unto Gad, I am in a great strait*} Not so much in respect of making my choice, as that I am so encompassed with these three heavy judgments that there is no hope, God having determined it, that I should escape them all.

*{let us now fall into the hand of the Lord*} i.e. Seeing I with my people must necessarily be corrected for our sins, I desire that God will do it immediately by himself. For though the other two punishments, sword and famine, come also from God, 2 Chr. 6. 24. and 7. 13. yet that more immediately, and these by the mediation of secondary causes, as men, locusts, caterpillars, immoderate rain, or drought. And therefore David being to be corrected, and put to his choice what punishment to choose, like a loving child, chooses to be chastised by his father himself, rather than by the hand of a slave or servant.

*{for his mercies are great*} So great and infinite that even in his wrath he will remember mercy, and will correct us in measure, and not according to the merit of our sins, as a gracious and loving father uses to do with his son who has displeased him.

*{and let me not fall into the hand of man*} Who, when God permits them, do execute his judgments without mercy, and with all rigor and cruelty, Isai. 47. 6. Zach. 1. 15. like an executioner, who being to inflict death according to the sentence of the Judge, does in his acting of it, out of his malice, add torments unto it. But here withal we may observe David’s candor, impartiality, and love to his people, in making choice of such a punishment as he had no privilege or exemption from more than the meanest of his subjects. For whereas he might against famine have provided for himself by laying up store of provisions, and against war by retiring himself to some impregnable castle, or the strong fort of Zion, against the judgment of the pestilence he had privilege above others, especially this, which was to pass through the whole land; and therefore he could not secure himself by flight, nor have any benefit of antidotes, preservatives and cordials, seeing the people were immediately smitten with the sword of the angel.
