But if this be so, that David made such a wise and pious choice, how does this agree with that of the Apostle, that it is *a fearful thing to fall into the hands of the living God*, because *he is a consuming fire*? Heb. 10. 31. and 12. 29. To this I answer, that it is a fearful thing to fall into the hands of God as a just Judge, incensed with those grievous and unpardonable sins of fearful apostasy, despiting the spirit of grace, and trampling under foot the precious blood of Christ, of which the Apostle speaks; but not to fall into his hands as a gracious and loving Father, who corrects in mercy and measure, and that for amendment.

**V. 15.** {*So the Lord sent a pestilence upon Israel, from the morning even to the time appointed*} i.e. From the morning in which he had sent the prophet Gad unto him, until it was the latter part of the third day, which was the time that God had appointed, to wit, three days, v. 13. Yet it did not continue the full term of three days: for it could not have been said that *the Lord repented him of the evil*, if nothing of the former sentence had been revoked; neither should the Lord have needed to bid the angel to hold his hand, if the term of three days had been fully expired, seeing his commission extended no farther, beyond which he might not go. But the Lord, to show that in wrath he remembered mercy, taking compassion on the people, and as if he had been sorry for their calamities, moderated the rigor of his former sentence, and suffered not the angel to proceed in punishing throughout the whole third day, but stayed him perhaps about the time of the evening sacrifice. Now here was no change in God, but only in his action; nor any alteration in the truth of his word and sentence, seeing it was not absolute but conditional, like all other temporal judgments threatened upon the neglecting of repentance, as it is expressed Jer. 18. 8, 10. and was verified in the example of the Ninevites, Jon. 3. 10.

**V. 16.** {*And when the Angel stretched out his hand*} i.e. When the angel whom God had appointed to destroy, 1 Chr. 21. 15. addressed himself to persist in the execution of his commission, for which end God had sent him, and to destroy the inhabitants of Jerusalem, as he had done of other parts of the land. See on Psal. 78. 49. 1 Sam. 21. 13.

*{the Lord repented him of the evil*} i.e. Did as men do when they repent, ceasing to proceed in his action of punishing the people. For otherwise that is true, that *God is not as man that he can repent*, Num. 23. 19. Neither was there any change in him or his decree, seeing he was purposed to cease punishing, when by his fatherly chastisement he had brought David and the people to unfeigned repentance. See on 1 Sam. 15. 11.

*{And the Angel of the Lord was by the threshingplace of Araunah the Jebusite}* Called Ornan 1 Chr. 21. 15. where this story is enlarged. He is said to be a Jebusite, i.e. by birth descended from one of the cursed nations, the Jebusites, who were of old the inhabitants of Jebus, at this time called Jerusalem, Judg. 1. 21. and 19. 20. but now, being converted, a proselyte, and professor of God’s true religion.

**V. 17.** {*And David spake unto the Lord when he saw the Angel*} To wit, in the visible shape of a man, between heaven and earth, with his sword drawn in his hand. Together with David were the elders, who were clothed with sackcloth, and being thoroughly humbled with this terrible sight, were all fallen upon their faces, as it is to be supplied out of 1 Chr. 21. 16.

*{Lo, I have sinned, and I have done wickedly: but these sheep, what have they done?*} David knew well that the people slain by the pestilence (whom he calls sheep, because God had placed him over them as their shepherd) had committed many sins whereby they justly deserved this and far greater punishments; yet taking notice that this particular judgment was inflicted for his sin, he does as much as in him lies acquit them, and take the whole blame upon himself, yearning in his bowels with bitter grief that he should be the cause of bringing such a great calamity upon his people, who were so dear unto him.

*{let thine hand, I pray thee, be against me, and against my father’s house*} i.e. Let me, and those that belong unto me and my family, bear the punishment which I have caused by my sin, rather than this poor people, who had no hand in it. Here David, like a good shepherd offers to lay down his life for his sheep: And herein he was a type of the great shepherd of our souls, Jesus Christ; saving that he came far short of him; for he not only offered but freely gave his life for his sheep, and that when they were his enemies, and had by their sins deserved everlasting death, he himself being wholly innocent, John 10. 11.

**V. 18.** {*And Gad came that day to David, and said unto him, Go up, rear an altar unto the Lord*} i.e. The prophet Gad by God’s command came unto him when he saw him and the people thus humbled, and directed him to the means whereby an atonement might be made, and the plague stayed, as is expressed 1 Chr. 21. 18. David and the elders had humbled themselves, and prayed unto the Lord for the removal of this judgment, yet he would not stay the plague till David had reared an altar, and offered sacrifice. And this was done, to teach him and us that there is no merit in our humiliations and repentance, which may move the Lord to pardon our sins and remove our punishments, but only in the sacrifice of our Lord Christ, typified by those legal sacrifices, the virtue and efficacy thereof being applied unto us by a true and lively faith. David is commanded to build an altar, yet forbidden to build a temple, 1 Chr. 22. 15.

*{in the threshingfloor of Araunah the Jebusite}* This place was designed by God’s special appointment, chiefly because it so pleased him, though divers things might concur which are subject to our apprehensions. It was upon the mount Moriah, where Abraham offered to sacrifice his son Isaac, who was a type of Christ who offered himself as a propitiatory sacrifice even in the same place for the taking away both the sins and punishment of all his elect. It was the place also where the temple was afterwards to be built, 2 Chr. 3. 1. which also was a type of Christ; and was now to be consecrated to this sacred use, by having this altar there built, and hereby to be brought into an higher esteem among the people, as being the place where they received such a singular benefit, as the staying of this fearful plague, which threatened total ruin and destruction. And being now in the possession of Araunah the Jebusite, God would hereby typify the calling of the Gentiles, amongst whom after the coming of the Messiah he would have his church and place of residence, where he would be worshipped and served. And finally, God would have this altar erected in this place, because the Tabernacle was at this time in Gibeon, whither David dared not go to inquire of God, and so intermit or defer the means of making atonement, for fear lest this angel, which he saw with his sword drawn, should in the meanwhile proceed in his dreadful execution; and this reason is rendered of his not going to Gibeon, 1 Chr. 21. 29, 30. Neither could there be a fitter place to make intercession in, than this; chiefly, because it was of God’s appointing, and was also the place where it pleased God the angel should appear with his sword in his hand, that so there David might by his intercession thrust himself as it were between the living and the dead, for the staying of the plague, as sometimes Aaron had done in the like case, Num. 16. 47, 48.

**V. 20.** {*And Araunah went out and bowed himself before the King*} Giving him that civil adoration which was due unto him, as being his king and sovereign, 1 Chr. 29. 20.

**V. 21.** {*Wherefore is my lord the King come to his servant?*} q.d. It is very strange that a sovereign should so far condescend from his greatness as to come to his subject; yea, the king of Israel to one that was by birth of an heathen nation.

*{to build an altar to the Lord*} i.e. Both by the Lord’s appointment, and for his service, and likewise for the welfare of the whole commonwealth, as being the means ordained for the staying of the plague.

**V. 22.** {*And Araunah said—Behold, here be oxen for burnt-sacrifice, &c.*} Hereunto is added 1 Chr. 21. 23. wheat also for a meat-offering. All this Araunah offered (knowing to what use they were to be employed) to hasten the work of making an atonement, because they were all ready at hand; the oxen to tread out the corn, and the wooden instruments which were used about them for this service, which being dry and sear were fit to take fire, and so to expedite the business; and the wheat itself all ready trodden out for the meat offering. All these he presses upon David, though he knew that he had himself store of all these things; not only to show his homage by this great bounty, but also to expedite the business of staying the plague, which he could not choose but fear when he saw the angel with his slaughtering sword standing by his threshing-floor, where he and his four sons hid themselves for fear, 1 Chr. 21. 15, 20.

**V. 23.** {*All these things did Araunah, as a King, give unto the King*} i.e. Offered to bestow upon him out of a kingly bounty and munificence. Hence some collect that he had been formerly king of the Jebusites before David subdued them; and the rather because the Hebrew text thus has it, *Araunah a king did give unto the king*. But this is the less probable, because it is manifest by the story that David and Joab took it by assault, and had it not surrendered by this king, as they suppose, for which cause he was with David in this special favor.

*{The Lord thy God accept thee*} i.e. Become propitious by this sacrifice, and remove the present judgment from you and your people.

**V. 24.** {*And the King said to Araunah, Nay, but I will surely buy it of thee at a price*} This David speaks, not only out of kingly equity and courtesy, because he would not be overburdensome to a willing and freehearted subject, but also out of piety and conscientiousness, because he would offer unto God of his own substance, according to that Prov. 3. 9. *Honour the Lord with thy substance*; i.e. Your own, and not another man’s which cost you naught, which would disparage the sacrifice, and make it less acceptable, seeing it is to be ascribed rather to the bounty of the donor, which is at the cost, than to the devotion of him that offers a gift that cost him so cheap. And this reason David himself renders in this place.

*{So David bought the threshingfloor and the oxen, for fifty shekels of silver}* i.e. The very floor itself in which they threshed, and the oxen and threshing instruments, he bought for fifty shekels of silver. But for the whole place or plot of ground, wherein the threshing floor and the houses about it, with the gardens, yards and courts stood, in which was sufficient space and circuit for ground for the building of the temple upon it, with the courts and lodgings belonging unto it, he gave a far greater sum, even six hundred shekels of gold, as appears 1 Chr. 21. 25. where this story is enlarged, from v. 18. to v. 30.

**V. 25.** {*And David built there an altar*} As he might lawfully do, even for burnt offerings as well as meat offerings, though the ordinary altar was to be only in the Tabernacle, because he had God’s special command for his warrant.

*{So the Lord was intreated for the land, and the plague was stayed from Israel*} i.e. He accepted the sacrifice, as he made it appear by sending fire from heaven to consume it, as it is 1 Chr. 21. 26. (as he did Aaron’s sacrifice, Lev. 9. 24. and Gideon’s by fire out of the rock, Judg. 6. 21. and the sacrifice of Elijah by fire from heaven, 1 King. 18. 38.) and also by the success and issue of it, which was the staying of the plague. This came to pass, not out of any worth or merit that was in David’s sacrifice of itself, but because it was a type of Christ, and offered by faith in him, who is our alone high priest, altar and sacrifice, in whom God is well pleased, and all sacrifices services and prayers are accepted of him.
